ABOUT THIS VOLUME

This is volume 4 of five, and it continues the catalogue at Chapter 180.

The front matter and Part One, the general account of the neuron, are given in Volume 1 and are not repeated here.

The tags and flags used throughout are explained in Volume 1, and are summarised again in one line each below.

• (PUC) marks a Previously Unnamed Construct: a neuron type that is real, functionally necessary and relied upon in the literature, but which had no accepted proper name until this work gave it one.

• (COG) marks a type involved in cognition, and (NON-COG) marks a type that is not.


PART TEN: SPINAL CORD (CONTINUED FROM VOLUME 3)

CHAPTER 179. V1 (Pace-Quickening Damping Neuron) (NON-COG)

Lift your toes and the muscle at the back of your calf must fall silent at the exact instant the muscle on your shin pulls, and the cell that enforces that silence is a V1.

V1 is a birth-domain code.

Pace-Quickening corrects the intuition that inhibition must slow things down, since silencing these cells in the neonatal mouse cord slows the step cycle severalfold, meaning fast stepping depends on each phase being cut short on time.

Damping names the chemistry and the action, because every member of this lineage is inhibitory and it supplies the cord with its best known brakes, including the cells that damp motor neuron output through recurrent feedback and the cells that silence the opposing muscle at a joint.

Neuron marks it as the lineage supplying the well-timed inhibition that makes a quick step possible.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

V1 neurons are the spinal cord's professional interrupters: every one is inhibitory, every one keeps its axon on its own side, and all carry the embryonic mark of the gene engrailed 1.

The family contains the Renshaw cell, which feeds a copy of each motor command back as inhibition, and the Ia inhibitory interneuron, which silences a muscle's opponent whenever that muscle is told to pull.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Small compact multipolar cells with three to six dendrites confined largely to one lamina.

Renshaw cells sit in a thin band at the lamina VII and lamina IX border, close enough that motor axon branches reach them within a millimetre; Ia inhibitory interneurons sit slightly more dorsally.

SIZE:

Cell bodies 10 to 20 micrometres across, Renshaw cells at the larger end near 15 to 20.

Dendritic trees span 200 to 400 micrometres and axons are short, usually under a centimetre.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Renshaw cells, earliest born, marked by calbindin and MafB, about 9 percent of V1, the only spinal cells receiving a direct branch of a motor neuron's own output axon.

• Foxp2 V1 cells, late born, 59 to 66 percent of V1, tightly wired onto motor neurons and containing many Ia inhibitory interneurons, which are themselves about 13 percent of V1.

• Pou6f2 V1 cells, early born and only loosely connected to motor neurons.

• Sp8 V1 cells, late born and also weakly connected to motor neurons; across the clan roughly 19 transcription factors combine to give about 50 distinguishable subclasses.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Lamina VII at every level from cervical to sacral, concentrated in the two limb enlargements, with the Renshaw subgroup pressed against the ventral and medial edge of the lamina IX motor nuclei in the region called the Renshaw area.

The critical interface is the lamina VII to lamina IX border, where inhibitory interneuron territory abuts motor neuron territory and V1 axons cross it in enormous numbers.

NUMBER:

Two anchors disagree and both deserve stating.

The classical ratio of one Renshaw cell per five motor neurons gives about 40 thousand Renshaw cells in a human, and at 9 percent of V1 that implies only about 450 thousand V1 cells.

Scaling instead from the finding that V1 cells are over a third of all ventral inhibitory interneurons gives 1.5 to 3 million.

Confidence is low; the true figure is very likely between half a million and three million, or 0.25 to 1.5 percent of the roughly 200 million neurons in the human cord.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Renshaw cells are among the fastest firing spinal cells: one motor axon volley drives a burst at several hundred spikes per second that decays over 30 to 50 milliseconds.

Rest is near minus 60 millivolts, threshold near minus 45, and the membrane time constant is a short 5 to 15 milliseconds, which is what makes the burst so tight.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Renshaw cells receive motor axon collaterals releasing acetylcholine onto nicotinic receptors together with glutamate, and send inhibition back to the same and synergist motor pools, to other Renshaw cells, and to Ia inhibitory interneurons.

Ia inhibitory interneurons receive Ia afferents, the fast fibres reporting muscle stretch, and inhibit the antagonist muscle; both types receive corticospinal and reticulospinal input, so the brain presets reflex gain before a movement.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin, 0.5 to 1.5 micrometres, conducting at 1 to 5 metres per second, but so short that the whole recurrent loop closes within 1 to 3 milliseconds.

Terminal arbors are dense, so one V1 cell contacts dozens of motor neurons.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glycine and GABA, frequently from the same vesicle.

Renshaw cells carry exceptionally large clusters of gephyrin, the scaffold protein that anchors receptors, holding glycine and GABA type A receptors side by side, and their inhibitory currents are larger than those of other spinal interneurons.

MYELINATION:

Minimal, because these are short grey matter axons that are usually bare or given only a few myelin turns.

Speed is not what V1 sells; proximity is.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High for their size during activity, since a burst at several hundred spikes per second is one of the most expensive things a small neuron can do, and low between bursts.

Mitochondria cluster at the motor collateral synapses where the sodium load is greatest.

PLASTICITY:

Renshaw inhibition onto motor neurons is set by activity in development and stays adjustable in adults, which is part of how training changes reflex gain.

In ALS models V1 synapses are lost first, then the engrailed 1 marker, then the cells, all before motor neurons and V2a cells die, making V1 an early warning system rather than a bystander.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

No glial cell is unique to V1, and that should be said plainly.

What is locally special is chemistry: ventral horn astrocytes run the glycine cycle, recapturing glycine and returning its raw material, and in ALS models microglia actively strip V1 terminals from motor neuron surfaces.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove V1 and the ventral horn keeps its power and loses its precision.

Opposing muscles co-contract, bursts stop terminating crisply so the locomotor rhythm slows, and flexor activity comes to dominate extensor activity.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Renshaw cells are electrically coupled to each other through connexin 36 gap junctions in early life, so a population can synchronise with no chemical synapse at all.

They co-release two inhibitory transmitters onto physically co-clustered receptors, getting a fast and a slower inhibition from one vesicle, and their input synapse is itself a hybrid of acetylcholine and glutamate.

Gephyrin scaffold size is regulated by how much excitation the cell receives, a structural feedback loop running over days.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: motor axon collateral rate, 0 to 100 spikes per second; Ia afferent rate, 0 to 200 spikes per second; corticospinal and reticulospinal drive, 0 to 1 normalised.

• OUTPUTS: inhibitory conductance onto homonymous and synergist motor neurons, 0 to 8 nanosiemens, reversal near minus 70 millivolts; inhibition onto other V1 cells.

• STATE: membrane voltage; burst adaptation; short term depression at the collateral input; gephyrin cluster size as a slow structural variable.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 60 millivolts; threshold minus 45 millivolts; membrane time constant 5 to 15 milliseconds; loop delay 1 to 3 milliseconds; recurrent gain 0.1 to 0.5.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 5 to 15 milliseconds; burst decay 30 to 50 milliseconds; depression 100 to 500 milliseconds; structural change days.

• FAILURE MODE: loss gives co-contraction, flexor bias and a slowed rhythm; in ALS terminals vanish before motor neurons die; failure of the glycine receptor at these synapses causes hyperekplexia, in which a small surprise stiffens the whole body.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to advance one time step of the Renshaw recurrent inhibition loop for a single motor pool.
Create a number variable called descendingDrive with initial value 40.
Create a number variable called motorRate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called renshawRate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called collateralGain with initial value 3.
Create a number variable called recurrentGain with initial value 0.35.
Create a number variable called delayedMotorRate with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called delayLine with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called netMotorInput with initial value 0.
Add motorRate to the end of delayLine.
Check if the total number of items in delayLine is greater than 7.
If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set delayedMotorRate to the item at position 0 in delayLine.
    Remove the item at position 0 from delayLine.
End the conditional block.
Set netMotorInput to the difference of descendingDrive and the product of recurrentGain and renshawRate.
Check if netMotorInput is less than 0.
If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set netMotorInput to 0.
End the conditional block.
Set renshawRate to the sum of renshawRate and the product of 0.04 and the difference of the product of collateralGain and delayedMotorRate and renshawRate.
Set motorRate to the sum of motorRate and the product of 0.01 and the difference of netMotorInput and motorRate.
Display motorRate to the console.
Return motorRate to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C: a recurrent loop with a hard millisecond delay is exactly the tight fixed
   step inner loop C makes cheap. Compile: cc -std=c99 -O2 renshaw.c -lm */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>
#define DT 0.0002      /* 0.2 millisecond step, in seconds */
#define STEPS 25000    /* five seconds of simulated time   */
#define BUF 64

int main(void) {
    double mn = 0.0, rc = 0.0;   /* motor pool and Renshaw rates, per second */
    double drive = 40.0;         /* descending command, spikes per second    */
    double w_mn_rc = 3.0, w_rc_mn = 0.35;   /* forward and return gains      */
    double tau_mn = 0.020, tau_rc = 0.005;  /* Renshaw cells are the fast one */
    int dsteps = (int)(0.0015 / DT);        /* 1.5 millisecond loop delay    */
    double buf[BUF] = {0.0};
    int head = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < STEPS; i++) {
        double rc_in = w_mn_rc * buf[(head + BUF - dsteps) % BUF];
        double mn_in = drive - w_rc_mn * rc;
        if (mn_in < 0.0) mn_in = 0.0;       /* rates cannot go negative */
        rc += DT / tau_rc * (-rc + 200.0 * tanh(rc_in / 200.0));
        mn += DT / tau_mn * (-mn + mn_in);
        buf[head] = mn;
        head = (head + 1) % BUF;
        if (i % 5000 == 0)
            printf("t=%.3f s  motor=%6.1f Hz  renshaw=%6.1f Hz\n", i * DT, mn, rc);
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A V1 is the only spinal neuron that is inhibitory, stays on its own side, and carries the engrailed 1 mark.

Find a small cell under a motor nucleus that answers a motor axon within two milliseconds with several hundred spikes per second and replies in glycine, and you have found a Renshaw cell, and therefore a V1.

CHAPTER 180. V2 (Step-Strength Steadying Neuron) (NON-COG)

The clock that sets the speed of your walking is not in your brain, and a large part of it is a column of V2 neurons running the length of your lower spinal cord.

V2 is another progenitor address.

Step-Strength names the excitatory half's contribution, which is the robustness and reliability of each burst of motor neuron firing, so that a step is delivered with full force rather than fading.

Steadying names the inhibitory half's contribution, holding bending and straightening muscles of the same limb in alternation so that the limb does not stiffen with both acting at once.

Neuron marks a lineage that splits almost evenly into an excitatory half and an inhibitory half, plus a small third group, the two halves working on the same problem from opposite directions.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The V2 family comes from the p2 progenitor stripe and, uniquely among the cardinal classes, splits immediately into excitatory and inhibitory siblings that both keep their axons on their own side.

The excitatory half, V2a, marked by the gene Chx10, is the ipsilateral engine of walking; the inhibitory half, V2b, marked by Gata3, works with V1 to make a joint's bending and straightening muscles take turns.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

V2a cells are chunky and multipolar, with dendrites reaching both towards the central canal and out to the white matter.

The axon leaves ventrally, joins the ventrolateral funiculus and runs up or down the cord, branching into motor nuclei several segments away; V2b cells are smaller and stay local.

SIZE:

V2a cell bodies are large for interneurons at 20 to 30 micrometres, V2b smaller at 10 to 18.

Dendritic fields span 250 to 500 micrometres, and long propriospinal V2a axons run from cervical to lumbar levels, 30 to 50 centimetres in an adult.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• V2a, excitatory and glutamatergic, Chx10 positive, about 35 percent of embryonic V2 cells, arranged in a positional gradient that maps onto forelimb versus hindlimb control.

• V2b, inhibitory, Gata3 positive, about 25 percent of embryonic V2 cells, releasing glycine and GABA.

• V2c, marked by Sox1 and lacking Gata3, a late arriving relative of V2b.

• V2d, marked by Shox2 and derived from V2a, part of the machinery that generates the rhythm rather than relaying it; a further Arid3c population has been reported with no function yet assigned.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Lamina VII, the intermediate grey, is the heartland, spilling into lamina VIII and the medial edge of lamina X, at every spinal level and also in the hindbrain, where V2a cells help drive breathing.

The consequential interface is the boundary between lamina VII grey and the ventrolateral funiculus, because that is where a local interneuron becomes a long distance one.

NUMBER:

Roughly 2 to 4 million V2 neurons in the human cord, of which perhaps 1 to 2 million are V2a, so about 1 to 2 percent of the cord's approximately 200 million neurons.

Confidence is low to moderate and no human count exists; the firm statement is that V2a cells outnumber alpha motor neurons by at least an order of magnitude, because each motor pool receives convergent input from many of them.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Adult V2a cells are unusually excitable and fire tonically for as long as they are depolarised.

They show serotonin dependent bistability: with brainstem serotonin present a V2a cell can latch into a self-sustaining plateau that outlasts its input and is switched off by a brief inhibition, supported by a persistent sodium current and L type calcium channels.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from reticulospinal fibres, from Shox2 rhythm cells, from proprioceptive afferents reporting muscle length and force, and from other V2a cells.

Outputs go to ipsilateral motor neurons, to commissural V0V cells, to inhibitory interneurons and back onto other V2a cells, and it is this recurrent excitation that lets a V2a population sustain a burst; V2b output lands on motor neurons and V2a cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

V2a axons are thick for interneurons at 1 to 3 micrometres, conducting at 5 to 25 metres per second, and one axon commonly innervates three to eight segments.

V2b axons are thinner and stay within a segment or two.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

V2a and V2d release glutamate onto AMPA and NMDA receptors; V2b and V2c release glycine and GABA.

Serotonin from the raphe nuclei acts on V2a cells through 5-HT2 receptors to enable the plateau, a case of a brainstem chemical changing what a spinal computation is able to do.

MYELINATION:

Long propriospinal V2a axons in the ventrolateral funiculus are properly myelinated, supporting up to about 25 metres per second, while local V2b axons in grey matter are essentially bare.

This is a family whose excitatory half is insulated for distance and whose inhibitory half is not.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High and sustained, because tonic firing is expensive and a held plateau is more expensive still, running the sodium and potassium pumps continuously.

Long propriospinal axons add a permanent bill for axonal transport, the motorised delivery of proteins and mitochondria down a fibre half a metre long.

PLASTICITY:

V2a cells are the great opportunists of spinal repair.

After a high cervical injury severs the direct pathway to the phrenic motor neurons that drive the diaphragm, Chx10 positive cells are recruited into the surviving circuit within two weeks in mice, and the same recruitment underlies much of the hand and arm recovery seen after incomplete injury.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing about V2 glia is anatomically exotic, and that is worth saying plainly.

What is locally distinctive is that ventrolateral funiculus oligodendrocytes maintain very long internodes and feed lactate to those axons through monocarboxylate transporters, a partnership that becomes limiting after injury, while lamina VII astrocytes clear glutamate fast through the transporter GLT-1, which is what stops recurrent V2a excitation running away.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove V2a and lamina VII stops being an engine and becomes a relay: the brain can still drive movement, but the cord loses its own ability to sustain and speed a rhythm, and left and right alternation collapses first at fast gaits.

Remove V2b and the same region loses the ability to make a joint's opposing muscles take turns.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The persistent sodium current is a genuine computational device, converting steady input into rhythmic output with no oscillator anywhere in the circuit.

Bistability means the cell holds a memory of recent input in its membrane rather than in its synapses, serotonin and noradrenaline reach these cells partly by volume transmission that retunes the whole population at once, and dendritic L type calcium channels let a single branch amplify its own inputs.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: reticulospinal drive, 0 to 1 normalised; recurrent V2a excitation, 0 to 1; proprioceptive rate, 0 to 200 spikes per second; V1 and V2b inhibition, 0 to 8 nanosiemens; ambient serotonin, 0 to 1 normalised.

• OUTPUTS: glutamatergic conductance onto ipsilateral motor neurons and V0V cells, 0 to 10 nanosiemens; V2b glycinergic conductance onto motor neurons, 0 to 8 nanosiemens.

• STATE: membrane voltage; persistent sodium inactivation, 0 to 1; plateau on or off; adaptation level.

• PARAMETERS: capacitance about 21 picofarad; persistent sodium conductance 2 to 6 nanosiemens; leak conductance 2 to 4 nanosiemens; sodium reversal 50 millivolts; leak reversal minus 62.5 millivolts.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 30 milliseconds; persistent sodium inactivation 1 to 6 seconds, which sets burst duration; step cycle 0.4 to 2 seconds; serotonergic modulation seconds to minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: loss leaves a cord unable to hold a fast alternating gait or to recruit alternative respiratory drive after injury; V2a cells degenerate after V1 and before motor neurons in ALS models; excess persistent sodium current after injury contributes to spasticity, which is why sodium channel blockers reduce spasms.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to advance one time step of a V2a rhythm generating unit driven by a persistent sodium current.
Create a number variable called membraneVoltage with initial value -60.
Create a number variable called slowInactivation with initial value 0.4.
Create a number variable called stepMilliseconds with initial value 0.05.
Create a number variable called capacitance with initial value 21.
Create a number variable called sodiumConductance with initial value 4.5.
Create a number variable called leakConductance with initial value 2.8.
Create a number variable called sodiumReversal with initial value 50.
Create a number variable called leakReversal with initial value -62.5.
Create a number variable called activation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called sodiumCurrent with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called leakCurrent with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called inactivationTarget with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called inactivationTimeConstant with initial value 1200.
Set activation to the steady state activation of persistent sodium at membraneVoltage.
Set sodiumCurrent to the product of sodiumConductance and the product of activation and the product of slowInactivation and the difference of membraneVoltage and sodiumReversal.
Set leakCurrent to the product of leakConductance and the difference of membraneVoltage and leakReversal.
Set membraneVoltage to the difference of membraneVoltage and the product of stepMilliseconds and the quotient of the sum of sodiumCurrent and leakCurrent divided by capacitance.
Set inactivationTarget to the steady state slow inactivation at membraneVoltage.
Set slowInactivation to the sum of slowInactivation and the product of stepMilliseconds and the quotient of the difference of inactivationTarget and slowInactivation divided by inactivationTimeConstant.
Check if membraneVoltage is greater than -45.
If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
    Display membraneVoltage to the console.
End the conditional block.
Return membraneVoltage to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: a two variable burster fits on one screen and the units stay auditable.
# millivolt, nanosiemens, picoamp, picofarad, millisecond.
import math

C, G_NAP, G_L, E_NA, E_L = 21.0, 4.5, 2.8, 50.0, -62.5

def m_inf(v):   # instantaneous persistent sodium activation
    return 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp(-(v + 40.0) / 6.0))

def h_inf(v):   # steady state of the slow inactivation gate
    return 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp((v + 48.0) / 6.0))

def tau_h(v):   # slow inactivation time constant, milliseconds
    return 1200.0 / math.cosh((v + 48.0) / 12.0)

def run(i_app=0.0, ms=8000.0, dt=0.05):
    v, h = -60.0, 0.40
    for i in range(int(ms / dt)):
        i_nap = G_NAP * m_inf(v) * h * (v - E_NA)
        v += dt * ((-i_nap - G_L * (v - E_L) + i_app) / C)
        h += dt * ((h_inf(v) - h) / tau_h(v))
        if i % int(500.0 / dt) == 0:
            print("t=%5.1f s   V=%7.2f mV   h=%.3f" % (i * dt / 1000.0, v, h))

run()

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A V2a cell is the only spinal interneuron that combines a large intermediate grey cell body, a serotonin switchable plateau, a persistent sodium current strong enough to make a rhythm by itself, and an axon that carries that rhythm from neck to lower back without crossing the midline once.

CHAPTER 181. V3 (Two-Sided Rhythm Balancing Neuron) (NON-COG)

Take away a mouse's V3 neurons and it still alternates its legs, but the steps become uneven and the gait wobbles, because V3 cells are the shock absorber of walking.

V3 is a code.

Two-Sided names the anatomy, because this lineage is excitatory and largely crossing, spreading excitation from each half of the cord into the other half rather than keeping it local.

Rhythm Balancing names the measured effect, since silencing these cells leaves a mouse still able to step but with an irregular, unbalanced gait in which cycle durations vary and the two sides become unevenly driven.

Neuron marks the lineage that supplies the shared excitatory glue keeping a fast rhythm even, favouring simultaneous rather than alternating activity of the straightening muscles, which is what a fast bounding gait requires.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

V3 neurons come from the p3 stripe, the lowest ventral progenitor domain, carry the mark of the gene Sim1, release glutamate, and cross the midline in roughly 85 percent of cases.

Their job is not rhythm and not left and right alternation, both of which survive their removal; their job is robustness, making the rhythm regular, balanced between the two sides and hard to knock off course.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Born at the very bottom of the cord, they migrate outward and upward, so by birth they sit in scattered clusters rather than one band.

Cell bodies are round to oval with four to seven dendrites, and the axon crosses in the ventral commissure then branches widely across several segments on the far side.

SIZE:

Cell bodies 12 to 25 micrometres across, with the ventral cluster at the larger end.

Dendritic fields span 200 to 450 micrometres and crossed axons distribute over three to six segments, two to five centimetres in an adult lumbar cord.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Ventral V3 cells in lamina VIII, which fire tonically, have low rheobase, meaning very little current makes them fire, and receive strong excitatory drive.

• Dorsal V3 cells in the deep dorsal horn, laminae IV and V, which fire phasically and are placed to receive skin and deep tissue input.

• Ipsilateral V3 cells, about 15 percent of the class, whose axons never cross and which work locally.

• An intermediate group in medial lamina VII whose properties fall between the ventral and dorsal patterns.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Lamina VIII and medial lamina VII hold the main cluster, laminae IV and V of the deep dorsal horn hold a second, and both run from cervical to sacral levels, densest in the enlargements.

Two interfaces matter: the ventral commissure, where the axon crosses, and the deep dorsal horn, where sensory processing territory meets motor territory and the dorsal V3 group lets the state of the skin and joints reach the crossing motor circuit.

NUMBER:

Roughly 500 thousand to 1.5 million in the human cord, about 0.25 to 0.75 percent of its approximately 200 million neurons.

Confidence is low and no human count has been published, but the p3 domain is the thinnest ventral stripe, so V3 should be the smallest of the four cardinal classes, and that ordering is far more reliable than the absolute figure.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Ventral V3 cells fire tonically with low rheobase and little adaptation, translating sustained excitation into sustained output.

Dorsal V3 cells adapt quickly and often fire only at stimulus onset, a pattern usually produced by a fast-inactivating A type potassium current; rest is near minus 60 millivolts, threshold near minus 44, and locomotor firing rates fall between 5 and 50 spikes per second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from ipsilateral excitatory rhythm circuitry, reticulospinal fibres, load-sensing proprioceptors and other V3 cells.

Outputs land on contralateral motor neurons directly and on contralateral V0, V2a and Renshaw cells, so one V3 population raises the excitability of the whole opposite half at once, and that broad non-selective excitation is exactly what makes it a stabiliser rather than a patterner.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Diameter 0.7 to 2 micrometres, conducting at 2 to 15 metres per second.

The signature feature is the branching: after crossing, the axon divides into ascending and descending limbs with many collaterals, giving a wide diffuse terminal field rather than a focused one.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate only, acting on AMPA receptors for the fast component and NMDA receptors for a slower one that summates across a step cycle.

A subset adds neuropeptides, and the complete absence of glycine and GABA is what separates V3 sharply from V0D and V1.

MYELINATION:

The crossing and funicular portions carry thin to moderate myelin while terminal branches in grey matter are bare.

The cell therefore gets its signal across the midline quickly and then loses speed as it spreads, which suits a stabilising rather than a timing role.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate to high, since tonic firing means a continuous pump load and a widely branched axon is a large membrane surface to maintain.

Broad excitatory terminal fields also carry a heavy glutamate synthesis cost, met partly by borrowing glutamine from astrocytes.

PLASTICITY:

V3 cells are central to what goes wrong after spinal cord injury.

In mice with a sacral injury, tail spasms with clonus, hypertonus and hyperreflexia appear weeks later, and optogenetic work shows V3 neurons initiate and coordinate them, so the circuit does not merely lose inhibition, a surviving excitatory commissural population takes over.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

There is no glial specialisation unique to V3, and that should be stated plainly rather than dressed up.

The relevant local point is that astrocytes surrounding these broadly branched glutamatergic terminals carry a heavy clearance burden through GLT-1 and supply the glutamine that refills the transmitter pool, and when that clearance fails after injury the same network that stabilises walking becomes the network that produces spasms.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Without V3 the ventral horn keeps its pattern and loses its poise.

Concretely, the rhythm still alternates but the duration of each cycle varies far more from step to step, the two sides become unequal in strength, and the animal wobbles and stumbles on uneven ground.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

V3 cells demonstrate something integrate and fire models miss entirely: recurrent excitation between two oscillators does not add signal, it removes variance.

NMDA receptors on their targets give a slow voltage dependent conductance lasting hundreds of milliseconds, letting the population average over several cycles; their broad fields make the code a field rather than a line; and their activity helps instruct myelination of their own crossing segment.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: ipsilateral rhythm drive, 0 to 1 normalised; reticulospinal drive, 0 to 1; load-sensing group Ib afferent rate, 0 to 150 spikes per second; recurrent V3 excitation from the opposite side, 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: broad glutamatergic conductance onto contralateral motor neurons and interneurons, 0 to 6 nanosiemens per target, delivered to tens of targets per cell.

• STATE: membrane voltage; A type potassium inactivation for the dorsal subtype; NMDA conductance history; the running estimate of contralateral activity the cell effectively averages.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 60 millivolts; threshold minus 44 millivolts; rheobase 10 to 40 picoamps for ventral cells; crossing delay 2 to 8 milliseconds; coupling gain 0 to 1.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 15 to 35 milliseconds; AMPA decay 2 to 5 milliseconds; NMDA decay 50 to 200 milliseconds; step cycle 0.4 to 2 seconds; post-injury reorganisation weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: loss gives unstable gait with high cycle to cycle variability and unbalanced sides; after injury the same cells drive spasms and clonus; unchecked recurrent excitation with failed glutamate clearance produces synchronous self-sustaining discharge.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to measure how much V3 commissural coupling reduces the step to step variability of a locomotor rhythm.
Create a number variable called nominalPeriod with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called currentPeriod with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called couplingGain with initial value 0.6.
Create a number variable called cycleCount with initial value 200.
Create a list variable called periodRecord with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called noise with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called runningTotal with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called meanPeriod with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called squaredError with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 200 times.
    Set noise to a random value from a normal distribution with mean 0 and standard deviation 0.18.
    Set currentPeriod to the sum of currentPeriod and the sum of noise and the product of couplingGain and the difference of nominalPeriod and currentPeriod.
    Check if currentPeriod is less than 0.25.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set currentPeriod to 0.25.
    End the conditional block.
    Add currentPeriod to the end of periodRecord.
    Set runningTotal to the sum of runningTotal and currentPeriod.
End the loop.
Set meanPeriod to the quotient of runningTotal divided by cycleCount.
For each recordedPeriod in periodRecord, perform the following operations.
    Set squaredError to the sum of squaredError and the product of the difference of recordedPeriod and meanPeriod and the difference of recordedPeriod and meanPeriod.
End the loop.
Display meanPeriod to the console.
Display squaredError to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: the point is statistical, and the standard library gives reproducible
# random numbers with no dependencies at all.
import random
import math

def variability(v3_gain, cycles=200, seed=7):
    """V3 commissural excitation pulls each side's cycle period back towards
    the shared nominal period, turning a random walk into a tight process."""
    random.seed(seed)
    nominal, period, record = 1.0, 1.0, []      # period in seconds
    for _ in range(cycles):
        noise = random.gauss(0.0, 0.18)         # cycle to cycle jitter
        period += noise + v3_gain * (nominal - period)
        period = max(0.25, min(2.50, period))   # physiological limits
        record.append(period)
    mean = sum(record) / len(record)
    var = sum((p - mean) ** 2 for p in record) / len(record)
    return mean, math.sqrt(var) / mean

print("V3 gain   mean period (s)   coefficient of variation")
for gain in (0.0, 0.3, 0.6, 0.9):
    m, cv = variability(gain)
    print("  %.1f          %.3f              %.3f" % (gain, m, cv))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A V3 neuron is the excitatory cell that crosses the midline, a combination that rules out V0D, which crosses but inhibits, V1 and V2b, which inhibit and never cross, and V2a, which excites and never crosses.

Add the Sim1 mark, the scattered clusters left by its migration, and a terminal field that is deliberately broad rather than precise, and nothing else fits.

CHAPTER 182. LAMINA I PROJECTION NEURONS (Pain-Reporting Brain-Bound Neuron) (COG)

Somewhere between a hundred thousand and a few hundred thousand cells stand between a burn and the feeling of being burned, and if you destroy them the injury still happens but the suffering does not arrive.

The old label is an address, a numbered sheet of grey matter, and it says nothing about what the cell does.

Pain-Reporting names the message these cells carry, because they are the top of the dorsal horn and are driven by nociceptors, the nerve endings that signal tissue damage.

Brain-Bound names the destination, because unlike their interneuron neighbours these cells send a long axon out of the cord, most of them to the lateral parabrachial area of the brainstem.

Neuron marks it as a projection cell, and about seventy to eighty percent of them carry the neurokinin 1 receptor, the docking site for substance P, killing off that group abolishing the exaggerated pain of chronic injury, which is the clearest proof that this really is the pain report line.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Lamina I is a sheet only two or three cells thick draped over the top of the dorsal horn, and while most of its cells talk only to neighbours, a minority send an axon out of the spinal cord entirely.

These projection neurons are the second link in the chain carrying pain, temperature, itch, muscle ache, air hunger and the general state of the body into conscious experience, which is why they are flagged as cognitive: they are the point where a bodily event becomes something the brain can feel.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is flattened into the plane of lamina I, like a leaf lying on the surface of the dorsal horn.

Three shapes recur: fusiform, spindle-shaped with two long head to tail dendrites; pyramidal, triangular with three; and multipolar, which makes up about 58 percent of lumbar spinothalamic cells while fusiform cells dominate in the neck.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are large for the dorsal horn, 20 to 35 micrometres along the long axis, and soma size is in practice how you tell a projection neuron from a neighbouring interneuron carrying the same receptor.

Dendrites reach 200 to 400 micrometres head to tail but only 10 to 30 micrometres up and down, while the axon runs about 45 centimetres from sacral cord to thalamus in an adult.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Neurokinin 1 receptor positive cells, 75 to 80 percent of the population, responding to substance P, the peptide released by injury-signalling fibres.

• Two distinct neurokinin 1 receptor groups differing in AMPA receptor subunit composition and in how densely they are innervated.

• Neurokinin 1 receptor negative cells, 20 to 25 percent, selectively innervated by thinly myelinated A-delta nociceptors rather than peptide-releasing C fibres.

• Sorted by destination: spinoparabrachial cells, the large majority, spinothalamic cells, and cells reaching the periaqueductal grey, with heavy overlap because most branch to several targets.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Lamina I, the marginal zone, at every level from the first cervical segment to the coccygeal tip, continuing into the brainstem as the outer layer of the spinal trigeminal nucleus caudalis, which serves the face.

Axons cross in the anterior white commissure and ascend in the contralateral anterolateral system to the lateral parabrachial nucleus, the periaqueductal grey, the posterior ventral medial thalamic nucleus known as VMpo, the ventral posterolateral and medial dorsal thalamus, the nucleus of the solitary tract and the medullary catecholamine cell groups; the defining interface is the boundary with Lissauer's tract, the band of incoming pain fibres lying directly on top of lamina I.

NUMBER:

In the rat there are about 400 lamina I projection neurons per side in the fourth lumbar segment and about 215 per side at the seventh cervical, of which only about 15 and about 90 respectively reach the thalamus.

Summed across segments that is roughly 20 to 30 thousand in a rat, so a human figure of 100 to 300 thousand is defensible, under 0.2 percent of the cord's approximately 200 million neurons; confidence is high for the rat counts and low for the human extrapolation.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Four firing patterns recur: tonic, delayed by an A type potassium current, phasic and single spike.

The defining behaviour is wind-up, in which identical C fibre volleys at about one per second produce steadily more spikes, because a slow depolarisation built from substance P and NMDA receptor currents accumulates over tens of seconds; rest is near minus 65 millivolts, threshold near minus 40, and the chloride reversal potential is normally about minus 70.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are peptidergic C fibres releasing glutamate with substance P, A-delta nociceptors, cooling and itch afferents and local excitatory interneurons including lamina II vertical cells, with inhibition from lamina I and II interneurons and from descending noradrenergic and serotonergic fibres.

Outputs are exclusively excitatory, and the parabrachial target closes a loop back down through the rostral ventromedial medulla to the dorsal horn, so these cells sit inside a circuit that can amplify its own input.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Under 2 micrometres in diameter and slow for such a long projection, roughly 1 to 15 metres per second, so a signal from the foot takes tens of milliseconds to reach the brainstem.

Most axons give collaterals to the parabrachial nucleus and periaqueductal grey on the way to the thalamus, so one cell reports to several places at once.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate is the output transmitter without exception.

The interesting chemistry is on the receiving side: neurokinin 1 receptors for substance P, NMDA receptors rich in GluN2B, AMPA receptors that in one subpopulation lack GluA2 and therefore admit calcium directly, and TrkB receptors for brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF).

MYELINATION:

The ascending axon is myelinated once in the anterolateral white matter but thinly, which is why conduction is slow, while within lamina I it is bare.

The thin sheath is a design decision rather than an oversight: pain does not need to be fast, it needs to be unignorable.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high for a spinal cell, because an axon up to 45 centimetres long requires continuous transport of proteins and mitochondria and these cells fire for hours during injury or inflammation.

They are among the most mitochondria-dependent neurons in the cord, which is one reason chemotherapy drugs that damage mitochondria produce neuropathic pain.

PLASTICITY:

They support long-term potentiation at their C fibre inputs after high-threshold stimulation, a cellular mechanism of pain memory, and substance P pulls the neurokinin 1 receptor inside the cell within minutes of a noxious stimulus.

After nerve injury microglial BDNF acting through TrkB reduces the potassium chloride cotransporter 2 (KCC2), the pump keeping internal chloride low, and the chloride reversal potential rises until GABA and glycine stop inhibiting the cell and start exciting it.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here behave as they do nowhere else.

Superficial dorsal horn microglia carry the ATP-sensing P2X4 receptor and, after nerve injury, drive p38 signalling and BDNF release aimed specifically at these projection neurons, so a glial cell reprogrammes a named neuron's chloride handling, and astrocytes here release GABA tonically through monoamine oxidase B, which paradoxically raises neuronal activity and glucose use in neuropathic pain.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and lamina I becomes a sense organ with no wire.

When they are destroyed by substance P linked to the toxin saporin, animals still respond normally to mild noxious stimuli but lose their responses to strongly noxious ones and lose hyperalgesia almost entirely, so the layer keeps its detection and loses its escalation.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Wind-up is a real temporal integrator built from substance P acting through a G-protein-coupled receptor and NMDA receptors released from their magnesium block, with a memory measured in tens of seconds.

Calcium-permeable AMPA receptors let synaptic input trigger biochemistry without waiting for a spike, back-propagating action potentials spread into the flat dendritic sheet and drive gene transcription over hours, the chloride reversal potential is itself a plastic variable under glial control, and the parabrachial loop means their own output eventually returns to their own input.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: peptidergic C fibre volleys, 0 to 20 spikes per second, carrying glutamate plus substance P; A-delta volleys, 0 to 100 spikes per second; cooling afferents, 0 to 50 spikes per second; local inhibition, 0 to 5 nanosiemens; descending monoamine modulation, 0 to 1 normalised; microglial BDNF, 0 to 1 normalised.

• OUTPUTS: spike trains, 0 to 60 spikes per second, on a thinly myelinated ascending axon to the lateral parabrachial nucleus, periaqueductal grey, VMpo, ventral posterolateral thalamus and nucleus of the solitary tract.

• STATE: membrane voltage; slow neurokinin 1 and NMDA facilitation, 0 to 1; chloride reversal potential, minus 70 to minus 40 millivolts; receptor internalisation fraction; potentiated weight at the C fibre synapse.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 65 millivolts; threshold minus 40 millivolts; membrane time constant 15 to 30 milliseconds; tonic inhibitory conductance 0.5 to 1.5 nanosiemens; conduction velocity 1 to 15 metres per second; KCC2 activity 0 to 1.

• TIME CONSTANTS: AMPA 2 to 5 milliseconds; NMDA 100 to 200 milliseconds; substance P depolarisation 5 to 30 seconds; wind-up envelope tens of seconds; potentiation minutes to hours; KCC2 loss hours to days.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of KCC2 turns inhibition into excitation, giving allodynia, where light touch hurts, and hyperalgesia; destroying the population abolishes hyperalgesia while sparing basic nociception; runaway wind-up plus descending facilitation produces chronic pain that outlasts the injury.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to reproduce wind-up in a lamina I projection neuron and show that a weakened chloride pump makes it start earlier.
Create a number variable called membraneVoltage with initial value -65.
Create a number variable called slowFacilitation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called pumpActivity with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called chlorideReversal with initial value -70.
Create a number variable called spikeCount with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called spikesPerVolley with initial value empty list.
Set chlorideReversal to the sum of -70 and the product of 30 and the difference of 1 and pumpActivity.
Repeat the following steps exactly 16 times.
    Set spikeCount to 0.
    Set slowFacilitation to the sum of slowFacilitation and 0.1.
    Check if slowFacilitation is greater than 1.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set slowFacilitation to 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Repeat the following steps exactly 1000 times.
        Set membraneVoltage to the result of one millisecond of leak, tonic inhibition towards chlorideReversal, and synaptic drive scaled by slowFacilitation.
        Set slowFacilitation to the difference of slowFacilitation and the quotient of the product of 0.001 and slowFacilitation divided by 8.
        Check if membraneVoltage is greater than -40.
        If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
            Set membraneVoltage to -60.
            Set spikeCount to the sum of spikeCount and 1.
        End the conditional block.
    End the loop.
    Add spikeCount to the end of spikesPerVolley.
End the loop.
Display spikesPerVolley to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: wind-up is bookkeeping over one slow variable, and Python keeps the
# accumulation, the threshold and the reset all in view.
DT = 0.001                               # one millisecond steps, in seconds

def windup(pulses=16, hz=1.0, kcc2=1.0):
    """Identical C fibre volleys at 1 Hz. kcc2 is chloride pump activity,
    1.0 healthy and lower after nerve injury."""
    v, s = -65.0, 0.0                    # millivolt, and slow facilitation
    e_cl = -70.0 + 30.0 * (1.0 - kcc2)   # chloride reversal, millivolt
    tau_m, tau_s = 0.020, 8.0            # seconds
    g_inh, amp = 0.8, 1200.0             # tonic inhibition, volley drive
    counts = []
    for _ in range(pulses):
        fired = 0
        s = min(1.0, s + 0.10)           # each volley loads the slow term
        for i in range(int(1.0 / hz / DT)):
            drive = amp * (1.0 + 2.0 * s) if i < 50 else 0.0
            v += DT * ((-65.0 - v) / tau_m + g_inh * (e_cl - v) / tau_m + drive)
            s -= DT * s / tau_s
            if v > -40.0:                # spike, then reset
                v, fired = -60.0, fired + 1
        counts.append(fired)
    return counts

print("healthy chloride pump :", windup(kcc2=1.0))
print("pump reduced to 30 pc :", windup(kcc2=0.3))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is a large flat cell in the topmost sheet of the dorsal horn whose axon leaves the spinal cord altogether, and nothing else in lamina I does that.

Stain for the neurokinin 1 receptor, inject a tracer into the parabrachial nucleus of the pons, and the handful of cells lit by both are these and only these.

CHAPTER 183. ISLET CELLS (NON-COG)

Imagine a neuron stretched like a thread of chewing gum, running head to tail through the pain layer of your spinal cord for the better part of a millimetre while staying almost perfectly flat in every other direction.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An islet cell is an inhibitory interneuron of lamina II, the layer beneath lamina I known since the nineteenth century as the substantia gelatinosa because fresh tissue there looks translucent and jelly-like.

Gobel named it in the 1970s for the self-contained island its dendrites form in stained sections, and its job is to hold down traffic in the pain layer, receiving the C fibres that carry burning, aching and itching and releasing GABA onto the neighbours those same fibres are trying to excite.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small spindle-shaped cell body with exactly two main dendritic trunks, one heading towards the head and one towards the tail, each branching into a flat sheet lying in the plane of lamina II.

The axon arborises inside the same territory the dendrites occupy, and the dendrites themselves contain synaptic vesicles and make outgoing contacts, so a dendrite here is not only an ear but also a mouth.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are small, 10 to 15 micrometres across.

The head to tail dendritic span is the signature measurement, typically over 400 micrometres and sometimes beyond 800, longer than any other lamina II cell, while dorsoventral spread stays under about 40 micrometres and mediolateral under about 100, making the cell a ribbon roughly a millimetre long and a twenty-fifth of a millimetre thick.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Classic islet cells, firing tonically, showing a voltage sag on hyperpolarisation caused by the current called Ih, and using GABA.

• Nitric oxide producing islet cells, carrying neuronal nitric oxide synthase and releasing a diffusible gas from the dendritic tree.

• Peptide-expressing subsets carrying galanin or neuropeptide Y alongside GABA.

• Central cells, a shorter-armed neighbour with the same plan but a span of only 100 to 300 micrometres, treated by several authors as the small end of one islet to central continuum.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Lamina II, both outer and inner divisions, at every spinal level, and in the medullary dorsal horn as the outer layers of the spinal trigeminal nucleus caudalis, which handles the face.

The interface that matters most is the boundary between lamina II outer and lamina II inner, where peptide-releasing C fibres end above and non-peptidergic C fibres marked by the plant lectin IB4 end below, because an islet cell's ribbon lies along that boundary and samples both.

NUMBER:

Older Golgi studies put islet cells at about 30 percent of substantia gelatinosa neurons, but modern work finds only about a quarter of lamina II neurons are inhibitory at all, so the classical figure must have included cells now classed differently.

A defensible modern range is 10 to 25 percent of lamina II neurons, giving perhaps 3 to 8 million in a human cord, about 1.5 to 4 percent of its approximately 200 million neurons; confidence is low, and the definitional disagreement is currently larger than the counting error.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The most reliable electrical signature in the dorsal horn: a tonic discharge lasting the whole of a depolarising step rather than adapting away, with resting potentials often more negative than minus 60 millivolts.

Hyperpolarising the cell produces a characteristic sag as Ih turns on through HCN channels, named for hyperpolarisation-activated cyclic nucleotide-gated, and the low-threshold T type calcium current is distributed unevenly across lamina II classes with islet cells at one end of that distribution.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are dominated by direct C fibre contacts, with some A-delta input plus local vertical and radial cells and descending noradrenergic and serotonergic fibres.

Outputs go three ways: onto other lamina II neurons, onto the dendrites of lamina I projection neurons, and onto the central terminals of primary afferents inside synaptic glomeruli, which is presynaptic inhibition, turning the volume down before the message is even released.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin, under 0.5 micrometres, unmyelinated, and confined to the same ribbon as the dendrites, never leaving lamina II.

Conduction is well under 1 metre per second, but with distances under a millimetre the whole cell operates within a millisecond or two of itself.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA acting on GABA type A receptors, which open chloride channels, is primary, and some islet cells co-release glycine.

Subsets add galanin or neuropeptide Y as slow modulators, and the nitric oxide subset releases a gas that is never packaged in vesicles and simply diffuses out of the dendrite in all directions.

MYELINATION:

None, and this is not a deficiency: an axon confined to a ribbon shorter than a grain of rice gains nothing from insulation, and myelin would only add bulk to the most crowded neuropil in the spinal cord.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest in absolute terms but continuous, because tonic firing never lets the sodium and potassium pump rest.

Mitochondria run all along the long dendritic trunks, and the vesicle-filled dendrites carry their own release and recycling machinery, so the dendrite is expensive per micrometre in a way most dendrites are not.

PLASTICITY:

Inhibitory tone in lamina II falls after nerve injury, and this disinhibition is a main engine of neuropathic pain.

Whether islet cells actually die is disputed: in the spared nerve injury model, tactile allodynia appeared without any selective loss of GABA or GABA type A receptors from lamina I and II synapses, so changed function rather than lost cells is better supported, and glycinergic transmission is selectively impaired in a subpopulation of dorsal horn interneurons.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here do things they do nowhere else.

Superficial dorsal horn astrocytes take up GABA through GAT-3 and, in neuropathic pain, run it in reverse, synthesising GABA through monoamine oxidase B and releasing it tonically, which unexpectedly raises rather than lowers neuronal activity and glucose use, while microglia carrying P2Y12 and P2X4 receptors alter excitatory transmission onto lamina II neurons; unlike the ventral horn this layer has essentially no perineuronal nets, the lattice cages that stabilise synapses, which is part of why it stays so plastic in adulthood.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove islet cells and lamina II loses its ruler.

The specific loss is restraint along the head to tail axis: a pinprick that should feel like a point begins to feel like a band because nothing holds down the neighbouring stretch of the layer, and a brief stimulus outlasts itself because nothing terminates the local excitation, so the jelly layer stops being a filter and becomes an amplifier.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Islet cell dendrites are presynaptic as well as postsynaptic, making dendrodendritic contacts so the cell can compute and output in the same branch with no spike at all.

The nitric oxide subset does something genuinely counterintuitive, releasing a gas from its dendrites that diffuses through lamina II outer and increases substance P and calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) release from C fibre terminals, so an inhibitory cell exerts an excitatory volume effect on its own inputs; they also produce primary afferent depolarisation at axoaxonic contacts, and Ih gives the membrane a pacemaker-like tendency to return towards threshold after inhibition.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: C fibre volleys distributed along the ribbon, 0 to 20 spikes per second per contact; A-delta input, 0 to 100 spikes per second; local excitatory interneurons; descending monoamine modulation, 0 to 1 normalised.

• OUTPUTS: GABAergic conductance onto neighbouring lamina II somata and lamina I projection dendrites, 0 to 3 nanosiemens; axoaxonic conductance onto primary afferent terminals; a nitric oxide field spreading roughly 100 to 200 micrometres.

• STATE: membrane voltage per dendritic compartment; Ih activation, 0 to 1; nitric oxide concentration per compartment; presynaptic dendritic vesicle pool.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 62 millivolts; threshold minus 45 millivolts; membrane time constant 10 to 20 milliseconds; electrotonic space constant about 150 to 250 micrometres; compartment spacing 40 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; GABA type A decay 10 to 30 milliseconds; Ih activation 100 to 500 milliseconds; nitric oxide lifetime a few hundred milliseconds; disinhibition after injury days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: reduced GABA or glycine output gives mechanical allodynia and spreading, poorly localised pain; astrocytic reverse GABA release converts the layer's inhibition into tonic excitation; total loss removes presynaptic inhibition so every arriving volley releases its full transmitter load.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to spread one C fibre input along an islet cell's head to tail dendrite and find how much of that dendrite releases nitric oxide.
Create a list variable called compartmentVoltage with initial value empty list.
Create a list variable called nitricOxide with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called restingVoltage with initial value -62.
Create a number variable called releaseThreshold with initial value -45.
Create a number variable called releasingCompartments with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called thisVoltage with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 21 times.
    Add restingVoltage to the end of compartmentVoltage.
    Add 0 to the end of nitricOxide.
End the loop.
Repeat the following steps exactly 20000 times.
    For each position in compartmentVoltage, perform the following operations.
        Set thisVoltage to the result of leak towards restingVoltage plus axial flow from both neighbours plus any synaptic drive at this position.
        Set the item at position in compartmentVoltage to thisVoltage.
    End the loop.
    For each position in nitricOxide, perform the following operations.
        Set the item at position in nitricOxide to the result of diffusion from both neighbours plus synthesis when the local voltage exceeds releaseThreshold minus breakdown.
    End the loop.
End the loop.
For each position in compartmentVoltage, perform the following operations.
    Check if the item at position in compartmentVoltage is greater than releaseThreshold.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set releasingCompartments to the sum of releasingCompartments and 1.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display releasingCompartments to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C: a fixed grid cable plus a fixed grid diffusion, where the cost and the
   stability of each step stay visible. Compile: cc -std=c99 -O2 islet.c */
#include <stdio.h>
#define N 21           /* dendritic compartments, 40 micrometres apart */
#define DT 0.0001      /* time step, seconds                           */
#define VREST -62.0    /* resting potential, millivolt                 */
#define VNO -45.0      /* voltage above which nitric oxide is made     */

int main(void) {
    double v[N], no[N], drive[N];
    double tau = 0.015, gc = 25.0, dno = 30.0;  /* seconds, coupling, per s */
    int i, t;
    for (i = 0; i < N; i++) { v[i] = VREST; no[i] = 0.0; drive[i] = 0.0; }
    drive[10] = 16000.0;   /* a C fibre volley lands mid ribbon, mV per s */
    drive[11] = 11000.0;
    for (t = 0; t < 20000; t++) {              /* two seconds of model time */
        double vn[N], non[N];
        for (i = 0; i < N; i++) {
            double vl = (i > 0) ? v[i - 1] : v[i];        /* sealed ends */
            double vr = (i < N - 1) ? v[i + 1] : v[i];
            vn[i] = v[i] + DT * ((VREST - v[i]) / tau
                    + gc * (vl + vr - 2.0 * v[i]) / tau + drive[i]);
            double nl = (i > 0) ? no[i - 1] : no[i];
            double nr = (i < N - 1) ? no[i + 1] : no[i];
            non[i] = no[i] + DT * (dno * (nl + nr - 2.0 * no[i])
                    + ((v[i] > VNO) ? 3.0 : 0.0) - no[i] / 0.5);
        }
        for (i = 0; i < N; i++) { v[i] = vn[i]; no[i] = non[i]; }
    }
    for (i = 0; i < N; i++)
        printf("compartment %2d  %+5.0f um   V %7.2f mV   NO %5.3f\n",
               i, (i - 10) * 40.0, v[i], no[i]);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Fill a lamina II neuron with dye and look at it from the side.

If the dendrites run head to tail for more than four hundred micrometres and hardly at all up, down or sideways, if the cell fires steadily rather than in a burst, if hyperpolarising it produces a slow sag, and if staining shows GABA, it is an islet cell, and no other neuron in the human nervous system is that long, that flat, that steady and that quiet all at once.

CHAPTER 184. VERTICAL CELLS, ALSO CALLED STALKED CELLS (NON-COG)

Picture a tiny tree growing upside down, branches hanging into the layer where burning pain arrives and one trunk climbing up to pass it on.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A vertical cell is a small excitatory neuron, one that makes its partners more likely to fire, in lamina II of the dorsal horn, the sensory back wing of the spinal grey matter.

It hands signals of burning and tissue damage to the lamina I neurons that carry pain to the brain, so it is the last local relay before pain leaves the cord.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The soma sits atop lamina II and sprays dendrites, the branches that collect signals, downward in a narrow cone, while one axon climbs into lamina I.

SIZE:

The soma is 10 to 15 micrometres across, and in rat the excitatory form spreads 200 to 546 micrometres head to tail and 122 to 552 top to bottom.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• The common glutamate using excitatory form, 12 of 15 cells in one rat study.

• The rare inhibitory form using gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA), the main calming chemical, 3 of 15.

• A somatostatin carrying subset, about half of those tested.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They occupy lamina II outer, the top half of lamina II, in the dorsal horn of all 31 spinal segments, densest in the cervical enlargement, C5 to T1, and the lumbosacral enlargement, L3 to S2.

Many sit on the lamina I to lamina II interface, and the class continues into the brainstem's spinal trigeminal nucleus, which serves the face.

NUMBER:

A defensible estimate is 1 to 3 million in the human cord, 2 million as the working figure, with low confidence since nobody has counted them in a human.

That is about 1 percent of the roughly 200 million spinal neurons and about 0.002 percent of the roughly 100 billion in the whole nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Rest is near minus 64 millivolts and the signature is delayed firing, a hesitation imposed by the A type potassium current of Kv4 channels, which must inactivate before threshold near minus 40 millivolts.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input comes directly from C and A delta fibres and indirectly from A beta touch fibres through protein kinase C gamma and central cells, and output is glutamate onto lamina I neurons bearing the neurokinin 1 receptor.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is 0.2 to 0.5 micrometres thick and unmyelinated, running 50 to 150 micrometres up into lamina I at under 1 metre per second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases glutamate packaged by the vesicular glutamate transporter 2 (VGLUT2), often with somatostatin, and carries AMPA, N methyl D aspartate (NMDA), neurokinin 1 and often mu opioid receptors.

MYELINATION:

Essentially none, because this axon crosses a fraction of a millimetre in a layer so poor in myelin that it looks translucent, the old substantia gelatinosa.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest per spike because the cell is small, but the steady bill is high because lamina II holds one of the densest synapse fields in the central nervous system.

PLASTICITY:

Its synapses are a main site of spinal long term potentiation, and after nerve injury microglial brain derived neurotrophic factor lowers the chloride pump KCC2 so touch begins driving the cell hard.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here are exceptionally fine, wrapping synapses a micrometre apart with high GLT 1 and GLAST glutamate transporter levels, while microglia, quiet in health, answer nerve injury through the P2X4 to BDNF pathway.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete it and a pinprick still hurts, but the region loses emphasis: the build up during a sustained burn, the tenderness around an injury, and the ability of a brush to feel like fire.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The dendritic A type current filters out single weak inputs while several arriving together outrun it, making the cell a coincidence detector rather than an adder.

Somatostatin diffuses to bias neighbours it never touches, called volume transmission, and the endocannabinoid 2 arachidonoylglycerol travels backwards to silence the terminals inhibiting it.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ C fibre glutamate and substance P 0 to 5 hertz per fibre, 20 to 200 fibres; A delta 0 to 30 hertz; A beta 0 to 100 hertz; GABA tonic 1 to 10 hertz.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Glutamate onto lamina I neurokinin 1 neurons, 0.5 to 2 millivolts per event; somatostatin above 10 hertz, diffusing 20 to 50 micrometres.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential; A current activation and inactivation, each 0 to 1; dendritic calcium; synaptic weights.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Rest minus 64 millivolts, threshold minus 40, capacitance 0.08 to 0.12 nanofarads, leak 0.004 to 0.006 microsiemens, peak A conductance 0.04 to 0.08 microsiemens.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 15 to 25 milliseconds, A activation 2, A inactivation 30 to 60, NMDA decay 100 to 200.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss of inhibitory gating after nerve injury lets touch drive the cell, producing allodynia, which is pain from stroking the skin.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide when a vertical cell relays a nociceptive message to lamina I.
Create a number variable called potential with initial value -64.
Create a number variable called gate with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called elapsed with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called fired with initial value false.
Repeat the following steps exactly 200 times.
    Calculate the product of gate and 0.34.
    Calculate the difference of 0.38 and the result.
    Calculate the product of the result and 1.2.
    Calculate the sum of potential and the result.
    Set potential to the result.
    Calculate the product of gate and 0.978.
    Set gate to the result.
    Calculate the sum of elapsed and 1.
    Set elapsed to the result.
    Check if potential is greater than -40.
    Set fired to the result.
    If fired is true, then perform the following actions.
        Display elapsed to the console.
        Break out of the current loop immediately.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Language chosen: Python, because the delayed first spike is pure arithmetic and Python keeps every step visible.

import math
DT = 0.1                              # milliseconds per step
def vertical_cell(drive_na, steps=2000):
    v, a, b = -64.0, 0.0, 1.0         # millivolts; A current activation, inactivation
    cm, gl, el = 0.10, 0.005, -64.0   # nanofarads, microsiemens, millivolts
    gka, ek = 0.060, -90.0            # microsiemens, millivolts
    spikes = []
    for i in range(steps):
        a += DT * (1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp(-(v + 40.0) / 8.0)) - a) / 2.0
        b += DT * (1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp((v + 75.0) / 6.0)) - b) / 45.0
        i_a = gka * a * a * a * b * (v - ek)   # A type potassium current
        v += DT * (drive_na - gl * (v - el) - i_a) / cm
        if v >= -40.0:                         # spike threshold
            spikes.append(round(i * DT, 1))
            v, b = -70.0, b * 0.9              # reset; spikes shave the A current
    return spikes
s = vertical_cell(0.35)
print("first spike after", s[0], "ms;", len(s), "spikes in 200 ms")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A soma pinned to the roof of lamina II with a cone of dendrites beneath and one axon climbing back into lamina I, in a cell that pauses a tenth of a second before firing at all.

CHAPTER 185. RADIAL CELLS (NON-COG)

If the vertical cell is an upside down tree, the radial cell is a dandelion clock, with short branches shooting out equally in every direction.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A radial cell is a small excitatory interneuron of lamina II, an interneuron being a cell whose axon never leaves its own region, and its compact tree radiates in all directions at once.

It amplifies pain and itch signals locally, and every radial cell ever examined has proved excitatory.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Three to six primary dendrites leave a small rounded soma at wide angles and branch heavily nearby, giving a dense roughly spherical tuft like a burr.

SIZE:

The soma is 8 to 14 micrometres across and rat dendrites spread 147 to 271 micrometres head to tail and 59 to 157 top to bottom, a ratio near 1.7 where an islet cell has one near 7.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Lamina II outer radial cells, where C fibre nociceptors end.

• Lamina II inner radial cells, where itch and low threshold inputs arrive.

• A somatostatin carrying group, which in one study included every radial cell tested.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Radial cells occur throughout lamina II, both lamina II outer and lamina II inner, densest in the cervical enlargement, C5 to T1, and the lumbosacral enlargement, L3 to S2.

Cells at the lamina II inner to lamina III interface bridge two worlds, since lamina II handles noxious and itch signals while lamina III handles innocuous touch.

NUMBER:

A defensible estimate is 0.8 to 1.6 million in the human cord, 1.2 million as the central figure, with low confidence since it extrapolates a rodent share of 10 to 16 percent of lamina II neurons.

That is about 0.6 percent of the roughly 200 million spinal neurons and about 0.001 percent of the roughly 100 billion in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Rest is near minus 62 millivolts, and every radial cell in one rat study fired in an A type potassium current pattern: delayed, meaning a pause before the first spike, gap, meaning spike then silence then a train, or reluctant.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input comes from C fibre nociceptors including the MRGPRD class that reports pinch, from A delta fibres and from local excitatory cells, with inhibition from islet cells, and output reaches vertical, central and lamina I neurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is 0.2 to 0.4 micrometres thick and unmyelinated, stays within a few hundred micrometres of the soma, and never projects out of the region.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate packaged by VGLUT2 in every case examined, usually with somatostatin, on a membrane carrying AMPA, GluN2B rich NMDA, GABA A, glycine and sometimes mu opioid receptors.

MYELINATION:

Not applicable, and the reason matters: myelination speeds conduction over distance, and this axon crosses a fraction of a millimetre, so the cell is bare by design.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low per cell and concentrated in the tuft, where synapse density is very high and vesicle refilling dominates the bill, while reluctance to fire keeps spike costs low.

PLASTICITY:

C fibre conditioning strengthens their inputs through NMDA receptors and calcium, and after nerve injury the A type current shrinks, turning reluctant firing into ready firing.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Because the tuft is so compact, astrocyte processes here squeeze between contacts a micrometre apart with a high density of the glutamate transporter GLT 1, and supply the D serine without which these NMDA receptors cannot open.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete radial cells and pain still reaches lamina I, but as a private line rather than a signal that recruits neighbours, so the ache spreading outward from a bruise is lost.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Each dendrite subtracts with its own A type current, yet because the tree is symmetric and compact the cell approximates a true summing junction, a rarity where most neurons weight inputs by position.

Somatostatin release is volume transmission reaching cells it never contacts, and back propagating spikes travelling out into the dendrites tag recently active synapses with calcium.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ C fibre glutamate including MRGPRD nociceptors 0 to 5 hertz per fibre; A delta 0 to 30 hertz; local excitatory cells 0 to 50 hertz; GABA tonic 1 to 10 hertz.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Glutamate onto vertical, central and lamina I projection neurons; somatostatin above about 10 hertz, diffusing 20 to 50 micrometres.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, A current activation and inactivation gates, per sector dendritic charge, dendritic calcium.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Rest minus 62 millivolts, threshold minus 40, capacitance 0.07 to 0.11 nanofarads, leak 0.004 to 0.006 microsiemens, peak A conductance 0.2 to 0.4 microsiemens, 4 to 8 equal sectors.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 15 to 20 milliseconds, A inactivation 30 to 50, recovery 100 to 300, somatostatin action 1 to 30 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Shrinkage of the A type current after nerve injury turns a reluctant amplifier into an eager one, feeding central sensitisation and mechanical hypersensitivity.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to make a radial cell sum equal inputs from all directions and fire once its A type current fades.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called potential with initial value -80.
Create a number variable called gate with initial value 0.79.
Create a list variable called sectors with initial value empty list.
Add 0.15 to the end of sectors.
Add 0.10 to the end of sectors.
Add 0.07 to the end of sectors.
For each sector in sectors, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the sum of drive and sector.
    Set drive to the result.
End the loop.
Repeat the following steps exactly 500 times.
    Calculate the product of gate and 0.36.
    Calculate the difference of drive and the result.
    Calculate the product of the result and 1.1.
    Calculate the sum of potential and the result.
    Set potential to the result.
    Calculate the product of gate and 0.985.
    Set gate to the result.
End the loop.
Display potential to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Language chosen: C, because a tight fixed step loop is what a large scale spinal simulation will actually run.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>
#define SECTORS 6
#define DT 0.1                                  /* milliseconds per step */
int main(void) {
    double in[SECTORS] = {0.10, 0.00, 0.09, 0.00, 0.11, 0.02};  /* nanoamps */
    double v = -80.0, h = 0.79;  /* held hyperpolarised, which frees the A current */
    double drive = 0.0, act, i_a;
    int k, step, spikes = 0, first = 0;
    for (k = 0; k < SECTORS; k++) drive += in[k];   /* equal weights: radial symmetry */
    for (step = 0; step < 5000; step++) {
        act = 1.0 / (1.0 + exp(-(v + 45.0) / 7.0));          /* A current activation */
        h += DT * (1.0 / (1.0 + exp((v + 72.0) / 6.0)) - h) / 40.0;
        i_a = 0.30 * act * h * (v + 90.0);                   /* nanoamps */
        v += DT * (drive - 0.005 * (v + 62.0) - i_a) / 0.09;
        if (v >= -40.0) {
            v = -68.0; h *= 0.9; spikes++;
            if (!first) { printf("first spike at %.1f ms\n", step * DT); first = 1; }
        }
    }
    printf("spikes in 500 ms: %d\n", spikes);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A small round lamina II cell whose dendrites shoot out in every direction with no favourite axis, always glutamate using, always showing the delayed or gap firing signature of the A type current.

CHAPTER 186. CENTRAL CELLS (Close-Range Relay Neuron) (NON-COG)

Central cells are the shortened cousins of the long islet cells, and unlike almost every other class in the layer they can be either accelerator or brake.

Central teaches nothing, since it names neither a shape a student could draw nor a job, and it is easily misread as an address in the middle of the cord.

Close-Range records the one solid fact, that their dendrites and axons stay inside a very small head-to-tail column of lamina II.

Relay records the other solid fact, that their main known output is onto other local interneurons, transient central cells preferentially innervating the substance P radial cells, which in turn receive about half of their excitatory input from interneurons rather than from the skin.

Neuron marks it honestly, because this class is not one function, since central cells come in both excitatory and inhibitory flavours and no single physiological name would be true.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A central cell is a small lamina II interneuron whose dendrites run head to tail like an islet cell but cover a far shorter stretch, which is why Grudt and Perl named them separately in 2002.

About half are excitatory and half inhibitory, and among the excitatory ones the transient central cells are the best documented relay in the gate control circuit for pain.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Two or three sparsely branching dendrites leave opposite poles of a small rounded soma and run head to tail with little spread otherwise, making the cell a short thin rod lying along the cord.

SIZE:

The soma is 8 to 13 micrometres across, and in rat the inhibitory form spans 191 to 385 micrometres head to tail and the excitatory form 189 to 324, about a quarter of an islet cell's 466 to 1069.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Tonic central cells, firing steadily while depolarised, usually inhibitory and using GABA, glycine or both.

• Transient central cells, bursting at onset then falling silent, sitting at the lamina II outer to inner boundary.

• Transient central cells that also carry an A type potassium current, adding a delay before the burst.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Central cells occupy lamina II throughout its depth in all 31 segments, densest in the cervical and lumbosacral enlargements, and the class recurs in the spinal trigeminal nucleus.
