The transient subgroup sits exactly on the interface between lamina II outer, which receives peptide containing C fibres, and lamina II inner, which receives touch endings, so it hears both conversations.

NUMBER:

A defensible estimate is 1.5 to 3 million in the human cord, 2 million as the working figure, with low confidence since a rodent share of 5 to 20 percent of lamina II neurons has been scaled up.

That is about 1 percent of the roughly 200 million spinal neurons and about 0.002 percent of the roughly 100 billion in the nervous system, split about half excitatory and half inhibitory.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Transient central cells rest unusually depolarised at minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts and answer a steady current with a few spikes in the first ten or twenty milliseconds, then stop, because their sodium channels inactivate without recovering.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

They take direct C fibre input and input from protein kinase C gamma cells driven by A beta touch fibres, and their key output is onto vertical cells, the junction where the touch and pain streams join.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is 0.2 to 0.4 micrometres thick and unmyelinated, running head to tail parallel to the dendrites within 200 to 400 micrometres of the soma, and never leaves the region.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Excitatory central cells use glutamate loaded by VGLUT2 and inhibitory ones GABA with glycine, and both carry AMPA, NMDA, GABA A, glycine and often mu opioid receptors that let morphine silence them.

MYELINATION:

None, because lamina II is almost free of myelin and this axon travels a few hundred micrometres at most, so insulation would cost without buying speed.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low, and lowest for the transient subtype since a cell that bursts once and stops spends little restoring ion gradients; the tonic inhibitory cells are the expensive half.

PLASTICITY:

The transient central to vertical synapse strengthens after inflammation and nerve injury, one mechanism by which touch becomes painful, and excitability also shifts by altering sodium channel availability.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here have an extra duty, because these cells rest close to threshold and small rises in extracellular potassium would tip them into firing, so astrocytic Kir4.1 potassium buffering matters more here than almost anywhere.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove central cells and a pinprick still hurts, but the cross talk between touch and pain is cut, so the layer can no longer turn a bedsheet into a source of pain or raise the alarm around an injury.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Transient firing is itself a computation, because reporting onset and ignoring continuation hands downstream circuits a derivative, a signal proportional to change rather than level.

Sodium channel inactivation acts as a memory lasting hundreds of milliseconds, inhibitory central cells damp incoming terminals by presynaptic inhibition, and both kinds are silenced from the far side by retrograde endocannabinoids.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ C fibre glutamate 0 to 5 hertz per fibre; protein kinase C gamma glutamate carrying A beta touch 0 to 100 hertz; GABA and glycine tonic 1 to 10 hertz.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Excitatory type: glutamate onto vertical cells and neighbouring excitatory interneurons.

◦ Inhibitory type: GABA and glycine onto excitatory interneurons and onto primary afferent terminals.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, sodium channel availability 0 to 1, refractory flag, synaptic weights.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Rest minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, threshold minus 38, capacitance 0.06 to 0.10 nanofarads, leak 0.005 to 0.008 microsiemens, availability threshold 0.15, cost per spike 35 percent.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 10 to 15 milliseconds, availability loss 20 to 30, recovery 200 to 400, burst duration 10 to 20.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ When inhibition fails, the touch driven route through these cells to vertical cells and on to lamina I opens, producing allodynia.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to make a transient central cell burst at the onset of a maintained input and then fall silent.
Create a number variable called potential with initial value -55.
Create a number variable called available with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called spikes with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called crossed with initial value false.
Create a boolean variable called can_spike with initial value false.
Create a boolean variable called fire_now with initial value false.
Repeat the following steps exactly 3000 times.
    Calculate the difference of potential and -55.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.006.
    Calculate the difference of 0.30 and the result.
    Calculate the product of the result and 1.25.
    Calculate the sum of potential and the result.
    Set potential to the result.
    Check if potential is greater than -38.
    Set crossed to the result.
    Check if available is greater than 0.15.
    Set can_spike to the result.
    Check if both crossed and can_spike are true.
    Set fire_now to the result.
    If fire_now is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of spikes and 1.
        Set spikes to the result.
        Set potential to -45.
        Calculate the product of available and 0.65.
        Set available to the result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spikes to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Language chosen: Python, because the transient burst comes from one extra state variable and Python keeps it in plain sight.

import math
DT = 0.1                                  # milliseconds per step
def transient_central(drive_na=0.30, steps=3000):
    v, p = -55.0, 1.0                     # millivolts; sodium channels available
    c, gl, el = 0.08, 0.006, -55.0        # nanofarads, microsiemens, millivolts
    spikes = []
    for i in range(steps):
        p_inf = 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp((v + 52.0) / 3.0))  # availability falls if depolarised
        tau_p = 25.0 if v > -52.0 else 300.0              # slow recovery once repolarised
        p += DT * (p_inf - p) / tau_p
        v += DT * (drive_na - gl * (v - el)) / c
        if v >= -38.0 and p > 0.15:       # a spike needs available sodium channels
            spikes.append(round(i * DT, 1))
            v, p = -45.0, p * 0.65
        elif v >= -38.0:
            v = -38.0                     # depolarised block, the burst is over
    return spikes
s = transient_central()
print("burst of", len(s), "spikes at", s, "then silence")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A short rod lying head to tail in lamina II, a quarter the length of an islet cell, answering a maintained current with a handful of spikes and then nothing.

CHAPTER 187. ANTENNA CELLS (NON-COG)

The antenna cell is a listening post with aerials pointing two ways at once, up into the layer where injury is reported and down into the layers where touch arrives.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An antenna cell is a medium sized dorsal horn neuron with two clearly separated dendritic fields, named by Miklós Réthelyi and János Szentágothai in 1969 for its upward pointing aerials.

Its job is convergence, since it is one of the few superficial dorsal horn cells arranged to sample the pain stream and the touch stream directly rather than through go betweens.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

One to three thick dendrites climb steeply and branch only near their tips in lamina I, while a separate set fans down through laminae III and IV, leaving the middle sparse so the cell looks like a radio mast.

SIZE:

The cell body is 20 to 30 micrometres across, two to three times the width of a vertical, radial or central cell, and the whole cell can span 400 micrometres top to bottom.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Excitatory antenna cells, positive for the vesicular glutamate transporter 2 (VGLUT2) and often the neurokinin 1 receptor, with axons descending into laminae III and IV.

• Inhibitory antenna cells, positive for glutamic acid decarboxylase, the enzyme that makes gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA), with axons ascending into lamina I.

• Lamina III antenna cells, overlapping the lamina III neurokinin 1 cells that project to the brain.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Antenna cells sit in deep lamina II or lamina III, and since their dendrites occupy lamina I above and laminae III and IV below, one cell spans four layers and is best documented in the lumbar enlargement, L3 to L5.

It bridges three interfaces: lamina I to lamina II at the top, lamina II inner to lamina III at the soma, and lamina III to lamina IV below.

NUMBER:

A defensible estimate is 100 thousand to 400 thousand in the human cord, 250 thousand as a working figure, with low confidence since no one has counted them in a large sample of any species.

That is about 0.12 percent of the roughly 200 million spinal neurons and about 0.00025 percent of the roughly 100 billion in the nervous system, with a safe bound of fewer than one million.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Because the two dendritic fields are far apart and joined only by a thin stem, the cell is electrically compartmentalised, so lamina I and lamina IV synapses reach the soma along different cables with different losses.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The dorsal dendrites collect C fibre and A delta input including substance P, the ventral fan collects A beta input from the thick fast fibres of touch and vibration, and inhibition from islet and parvalbumin cells lands on both.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Most axons are local, 0.3 to 0.6 micrometres thick and unmyelinated, travelling 100 to 400 micrometres opposite the main dendritic fan, while a projecting minority ascend in the anterolateral tract.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Excitatory cells release glutamate and inhibitory ones GABA with glycine, and both carry AMPA and N methyl D aspartate (NMDA) receptors plus the neurokinin 1 receptor, which is visibly pulled inside the cell after a strong noxious volley.

MYELINATION:

Mostly none, since a quarter of a millimetre needs no insulation, except in the projecting minority whose myelinated axons carry a signal the whole length of the cord.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Higher than the small interneurons around it, since the membrane area is several times larger and the substance P driven depolarisation keeps sodium flowing in for seconds at a time.

PLASTICITY:

Repeated noxious input internalises the neurokinin 1 receptor for minutes, and after nerve injury the ventral touch driven field grows stronger, biasing the cell toward reporting touch as injury.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

This cell is served by two different astrocyte populations at its two ends, the fine bushy astrocytes of lamina II clearing peptide from the aerials and the larger fibrous astrocytes of laminae III and IV clearing fast touch synapses.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take it away and the dorsal horn loses one of its few single cell comparators, so it can no longer judge instantly that a firm grip is pressure rather than injury, or that the same grip on sunburn is injury after all.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The two dendritic fields behave as semi independent computing units, so the cell performs a genuine comparison rather than a sum, and inhibition on one field leaves the other untouched.

Substance P acting on neurokinin 1 receptors leaves a slow depolarisation lasting many seconds, a chemical memory of a noxious event, and dendritic calcium drives protein synthesis out at the tips.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Dorsal field: C fibre and A delta glutamate plus substance P from lamina I, 0 to 30 hertz per fibre.

◦ Ventral field: A beta mechanoreceptor glutamate from laminae III and IV, 0 to 200 hertz per fibre; GABA and glycine on both fields, tonic 1 to 10 hertz.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Excitatory type: glutamate onto laminae III and IV neurons; inhibitory type: GABA onto lamina I projection neurons; projecting subset: glutamate to brainstem and thalamus.

• STATE.

◦ Dorsal, ventral and somatic potentials; neurokinin 1 slow current; receptor internalisation fraction; two synaptic weight vectors.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Rest minus 60 millivolts, threshold minus 42, somatic capacitance 0.06 to 0.09 nanofarads, dendritic 0.02 to 0.04 each, leak 0.003 to 0.005 microsiemens, coupling 0.015 to 0.025 microsiemens.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds, dendrite to soma equalisation 3 to 8, neurokinin 1 rise 300 to 500 and decay several seconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss of inhibition on the ventral field lets touch alone reach threshold, one cellular route to allodynia, and ablating neurokinin 1 cells in animals abolishes much post injury hypersensitivity.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to make an antenna cell fire only when its dorsal aerials and its ventral fan are driven together.
Create a number variable called dorsal with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called ventral with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called potential with initial value -60.
Create a boolean variable called crossed with initial value false.
Repeat the following steps exactly 4000 times.
    Calculate the difference of 0.17 and dorsal.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.02.
    Calculate the sum of dorsal and the result.
    Set dorsal to the result.
    Calculate the difference of 0.12 and ventral.
    Calculate the product of the result and 0.02.
    Calculate the sum of ventral and the result.
    Set ventral to the result.
    Calculate the sum of dorsal and ventral.
    Calculate the product of the result and 78.
    Calculate the sum of -60 and the result.
    Set potential to the result.
End the loop.
Check if potential is greater than -42.
Set crossed to the result.
Display crossed to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Language chosen: JavaScript, because it runs anywhere with no toolchain and the two branch comparison reads as two lines of arithmetic.

// Three compartment antenna cell: dorsal aerials, ventral fan, soma.
function antennaCell(dorsal, ventral, steps) {
  const dt = 0.1, EL = -60.0;      // milliseconds; resting millivolts
  const gL = 0.004, gC = 0.020;    // leak and coupling, microsiemens
  const Cd = 0.030, Cs = 0.070;    // dendrite and soma capacitance, nanofarads
  let vD = EL, vV = EL, vS = EL, nk1 = 0.0, spikes = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < steps; i++) {
    const t = i * dt, iD = dorsal(t), iV = ventral(t);   // nanoamps
    nk1 += dt * (0.9 * Math.max(iD - 0.15, 0.0) - nk1) / 350.0;  // substance P, slow
    vD += dt * (iD + nk1 - gL * (vD - EL) - gC * (vD - vS)) / Cd;
    vV += dt * (iV - gL * (vV - EL) - gC * (vV - vS)) / Cd;
    vS += dt * (gC * (vD - vS) + gC * (vV - vS) - gL * (vS - EL)) / Cs;
    if (vS >= -42.0) { spikes++; vS = -65.0; }
  }
  return spikes;
}
const pinch = t => (t > 50 && t < 350) ? 0.17 : 0.0;   // dorsal aerials
const brush = t => (t > 50 && t < 350) ? 0.12 : 0.0;   // ventral fan
const none = () => 0.0;
console.log(antennaCell(pinch, none, 4000), antennaCell(none, brush, 4000),
            antennaCell(pinch, brush, 4000));   // only the third fires

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A cell body 20 to 30 micrometres across at the bottom of lamina II or in lamina III, with a small tuft climbing into lamina I, a wide fan spreading through laminae III and IV, and almost nothing between them.

CHAPTER 188. LAMINA V WIDE DYNAMIC RANGE NEURONS (Touch-To-Damage Grading Neuron) (COG)

This is the cell that answers the question every doctor asks, because its firing rate is a genuine dial running from a feather touch to a crushing injury.

Lamina V is an address and wide dynamic range is laboratory shorthand that a newcomer cannot unpack.

Touch-To-Damage names the actual span of the cell's sensitivity, because a single one of these neurons will fire to gentle brushing of the skin, harder to firm pressure, and hardest to frankly injurious heat or pinch, and many of them also take input from muscle and from viscera.

Grading names the work, which is not detection but scaling, since the cell reports how much rather than merely whether.

Neuron marks it as the cell in which repeated noxious stimulation at about one per second escalates output stimulus after stimulus in the effect known as windup, a short-term sensitisation thought to seed chronic pain.

This chapter now carries the whole of the wide dynamic range class, which was previously described a second time at the end of the catalogue, so that one cell type occupies exactly one chapter.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A wide dynamic range neuron is a large cell in the deep dorsal horn that responds across the whole range of stimulus strengths, firing a little to a brush, more to pressure and hard to a pinch or burn.

It is marked cognitive because its output is the closest thing the nervous system has to an internal number for pain intensity, and because it learns and compares rather than merely relaying.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The soma is large and multipolar, and its dendrites climb out of lamina V through laminae IV and III and often to lamina I, which is why one cell can hear both touch fibres and pain fibres.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 20 to 45 micrometres across, they are recorded 500 to 1200 micrometres below the cord surface, and the dendritic tree spans 600 to 1200 micrometres top to bottom.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Interneuronal wide dynamic range cells, the majority, whose axons stay in the cord.

• Projection cells, whose axons cross the midline and ascend in the spinothalamic and spinoreticular tracts.

• Convergent viscerosomatic cells, taking input from skin and organs, the reason a heart attack is felt in the left arm.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The core population sits in Rexed lamina V, the broad neck of the dorsal horn, with a large contingent in lamina VI, at all spinal levels and studied most in the lumbar enlargement, L3 to L5.

Lamina V is itself an interface, where the sensory dorsal horn above meets the motor intermediate zone below, which is why a painful stimulus produces immediate withdrawal.

NUMBER:

A defensible estimate is 1 to 2 million in the human cord, 1.5 million as the working figure, with low to moderate confidence because the class is defined by behaviour rather than by any stain and cannot be counted in fixed tissue.

That is about 0.75 percent of the roughly 200 million spinal neurons and about 0.0015 percent of the roughly 100 billion in the nervous system, of which plausibly one in twenty projects to the brain.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Rest is near minus 62 millivolts with a background discharge of 0.1 to 5 spikes per second, and the defining dynamic is windup, in which C fibre stimulation at about one per second makes each response larger than the last, often two to five times over sixteen stimuli.

Each volley leaves a slow depolarisation that has not decayed before the next arrives, expelling the magnesium that plugs the N methyl D aspartate (NMDA) receptor channel, with L type calcium and calcium activated cation currents adding more.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

A beta, A delta and C fibres all converge directly on one cell, joined by excitatory interneurons of laminae I to IV, with inhibition from local GABA and glycine cells and from descending serotonin, noradrenaline and enkephalin systems.

A brainstem loop called diffuse noxious inhibitory control quiets the cell whenever anything painful happens elsewhere, which is why biting your lip can dull a headache.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Projecting cells have myelinated axons 1 to 4 micrometres thick that cross the midline and ascend up to a metre at a few metres per second to about 40, while the majority keep thin propriospinal axons spanning a few segments.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

They release glutamate packaged by the vesicular glutamate transporter 2 (VGLUT2), and their receptor list reads like a pharmacy: AMPA, NMDA, neurokinin 1, GABA A, glycine, mu opioid, alpha 2 adrenoceptor and the excitatory 5 hydroxytryptamine 3 receptor.

MYELINATION:

Partial and specific, since the ascending axons of the projecting subset are myelinated for a journey of up to a metre while the local axons of the majority are barely insulated.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High by spinal standards, because the cell is large, fires continuously even at rest, and must clear the calcium that floods in through NMDA receptors and L type channels during windup.

PLASTICITY:

Windup lasts seconds, but repeated input converts it into central sensitisation lasting hours to days, with a lower threshold, a wider receptive field and larger responses, backed by transcription of genes such as c fos.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here are larger and more fibrous than those of lamina II and their special job is buffering potassium around a continuously firing cell, and raising their glutamate transporter GLT 1 reduces windup, so astrocytic uptake sets the ceiling on sensitisation.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and lamina I still reports that damage occurred, so a burn registers, but graded intensity vanishes along with summation, referred pain and reflexes scaled to the threat.

The region becomes a smoke alarm instead of a thermometer.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Windup is a genuine short term memory implemented by receptor biophysics rather than synaptic weights, since the recent history of the cell is stored in the position of a single magnesium ion.

The long dorsal dendrites can produce local calcium spikes without a somatic spike, back propagating spikes tell every branch the cell has fired, and released nitric oxide diffuses backwards across synapses and outwards to widen local blood vessels.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ A beta glutamate 0 to 200 hertz per fibre; A delta 0 to 50; C fibre glutamate with substance P and calcitonin gene related peptide 0 to 5; visceral afferents 0 to 20.

◦ Local GABA and glycine tonic 1 to 20 hertz; descending serotonin and noradrenaline as a gain from 0.3 to 2.0.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Glutamate onto laminae VII and VIII motor circuits, onto sympathetic preganglionic neurons, and for the projecting subset onto thalamus and brainstem; firing 0 to about 100 hertz.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, slow depolarisation in millivolts, intracellular calcium, chloride reversal potential, receptive field gain map.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Rest minus 62 millivolts, threshold minus 45, capacitance 0.3 to 0.6 nanofarads, input resistance 30 to 150 megaohms, windup increment 3 to 4 millivolts per volley saturating near 25 to 40.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds, NMDA decay 100 to 250, windup decay 2 to 5 seconds, central sensitisation hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Persistent central sensitisation underlies neuropathic pain, loss of diffuse noxious inhibitory control is measurable in fibromyalgia, and prolonged calcium loading can kill the very cell that was measuring the injury.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to make a wide dynamic range neuron wind up during a train of sixteen noxious volleys.
Create a number variable called slow with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called total with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called responses with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 16 times.
    Calculate the quotient of slow divided by 40.
    Calculate the difference of 1 and the result.
    Calculate the product of the result and 3.5.
    Calculate the sum of slow and the result.
    Set slow to the result.
    Calculate the product of slow and 1.1.
    Calculate the sum of the result and 3.
    Set total to the result.
    Add total to the end of responses.
    Calculate the product of slow and 0.72.
    Set slow to the result.
End the loop.
Display responses to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Language chosen: Python, because windup is bookkeeping across trials and Python makes the accumulation and its decay obvious.

import math
def windup(n_stimuli=16, interval_ms=1000.0):
    """Spikes evoked by each C fibre volley in a 1 hertz train."""
    slow = 0.0             # slow depolarisation from NMDA and neurokinin 1, millivolts
    tau_slow = 3000.0      # decays with a 3 second time constant
    slow_max = 40.0        # saturation ceiling, millivolts
    counts = []
    for k in range(n_stimuli):
        fast = 3                                    # A fibre and early C fibre burst
        slow += 3.5 * (1.0 - slow / slow_max)       # saturating step per volley
        late = int(slow * 1.1)                      # late C window scales with slow
        counts.append(fast + late)
        slow *= math.exp(-interval_ms / tau_slow)   # decay before the next volley
    return counts
c = windup()
print("spikes per volley:", c)
print("windup ratio, last over first:", round(c[-1] / c[0], 2))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A large cell 500 to 1200 micrometres deep that answers brush, pressure and pinch in a smooth graded sequence, then winds up when its C fibre input is stimulated sixteen times at one per second.

CHAPTER 189. CLARKE COLUMN NEURONS, ALSO CALLED THE POSTERIOR THORACIC NUCLEUS (NON-COG)

A slender column of large cells in your lower back is right now forwarding reports from every muscle spindle in your legs to the cerebellum at up to 110 metres per second.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Clarke column neurons form a narrow nucleus running lengthwise through the cord, named after Jacob Lockhart Clarke, who described it in the 1850s.

They relay unconscious proprioception, the continuous sense of limb position and speed that the body computes without telling you, from muscle spindles and Golgi tendon organs to the cerebellum.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell bodies are oval or pear shaped with the nucleus set off centre and coarse clumps of Nissl substance, the stacked protein making machinery, and their long dendrites run head to tail parallel to the incoming sensory fibres.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 25 to 60 micrometres across, the column is 0.5 to 1.5 millimetres wide and 20 to 30 centimetres long in an adult, and the axons are 10 to 20 micrometres thick including myelin.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Muscle spindle dominated cells, driven by group Ia and group II afferents reporting length and velocity.

• Golgi tendon organ dominated cells, driven by group Ib afferents reporting force.

• Cells with direct excitatory corticospinal input and cells receiving it only through inhibitory interneurons.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The nucleus occupies Rexed lamina VII, the intermediate zone, at the medial base of the dorsal horn against the dorsal columns, running from segment C8 to L2 or L3 and largest around T12.

It sits at a true interface, sensory territory above and behind and motor territory below and in front, and its work is done below L3 by the lumbar border cells and above C8 by the accessory cuneate nucleus.

NUMBER:

A defensible estimate is 50 thousand to 150 thousand in the human cord counting both sides, 100 thousand as a working figure, with low confidence since no reliable modern count of the human nucleus exists.

That is about 0.05 percent of the roughly 200 million spinal neurons and about 0.0001 percent of the roughly 100 billion in the nervous system, bounded below twenty thousand and above one million.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

They fire tonically even when the limb is still, so both stretch and shortening register as changes from a baseline, and they follow input faithfully into the hundreds of hertz because the cerebellum needs a stream rather than a summary.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are group Ia, Ib and II afferents from the same side, arriving in unusually large boutons using the vesicular glutamate transporter 1 (VGLUT1) that make transmission almost one to one, plus corticospinal fibres direct and indirect.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon ascends without crossing, in the dorsolateral funiculus, at about 30 to 110 metres per second, entering the cerebellum through the inferior cerebellar peduncle and ending as mossy fibres in the anterior lobe vermis and paravermis.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

They are glutamatergic with strongly VGLUT1 positive mossy terminals, carry fast AMPA receptors suited to high frequency transmission alongside NMDA, GABA A and glycine receptors, and express glial cell line derived neurotrophic factor (Gdnf) as their identity badge.

MYELINATION:

Extensive and essential, since myelin lets the signal jump between gaps called nodes of Ranvier and so reach the cerebellum from the foot in about a hundredth of a second.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high, because a large tonically firing cell must maintain gradients along a thick axon up to half a metre long, with mitochondria packed at the nodes and in the giant mossy terminals.

PLASTICITY:

Deliberately among the least plastic cells in the nervous system, because a measuring instrument that drifts is worse than useless, so gain is set from outside by cortical input and by gamma motor neurons resetting the spindles.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The oligodendrocytes here build some of the thickest sheaths in the cord, with many wraps and long internodes matched to an axon conducting at up to 110 metres per second, while astrocytes must clear glutamate within a millisecond to keep successive volleys distinct.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and reflexes survive and conscious position sense survives, but the automatic feedback that lets the cerebellum predict and correct movement is gone, producing the wide based stamping walk of sensory ataxia.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The nucleus performs a comparison rather than a relay, since it receives both the sensory report and a copy of the descending motor command, an arrangement engineers call corollary discharge and the raw material of motor learning.

The giant VGLUT1 boutons make transmission close to deterministic so the cell's job is fidelity rather than integration, thick axons instruct their own oligodendrocytes, and the energy load is met by lactate handed over directly from astrocytes.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Group Ia spindle afferents 0 to 200 hertz encoding length and velocity; group II 0 to 100 hertz encoding length; group Ib 0 to 100 hertz encoding force, all ipsilateral.

◦ Corticospinal glutamate, direct excitatory and indirect inhibitory, as gain and offset; local GABA and glycine 0 to 50 hertz.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Glutamate onto cerebellar granule cells as mossy fibres via the inferior cerebellar peduncle; collaterals onto local interneurons; firing 5 to 300 hertz.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, adaptation current, previous muscle length for velocity estimation, corticospinal gain term.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Rest minus 63 millivolts, threshold minus 45, capacitance 0.15 to 0.30 nanofarads, length gain about 6 nanoamps per unit fractional length, velocity gain about 0.3, baseline drive about 0.42 nanoamps.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 8 to 12 milliseconds, synaptic 1 to 2, axonal conduction 5 to 15 milliseconds from lumbar cord to cerebellum.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In Friedreich ataxia the sensory neurons feeding this nucleus degenerate and the Clarke cells atrophy and die, a change so consistent it is treated as defining the disease.

◦ Demyelination of the tract in multiple sclerosis or vitamin B12 deficiency slows the report and produces sensory ataxia on the same side, because the tract never crosses.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn muscle length and stretch velocity into a Clarke column firing rate for the cerebellum.
Create a number variable called previous with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called velocity with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called lengths with initial value empty list.
Add 1.00 to the end of lengths.
Add 1.03 to the end of lengths.
Add 1.05 to the end of lengths.
Add 1.02 to the end of lengths.
For each sample in lengths, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the difference of sample and previous.
    Calculate the product of the result and 10.
    Set velocity to the result.
    Set previous to sample.
    Calculate the difference of sample and 1.
    Calculate the product of the result and 6.
    Set drive to the result.
    Calculate the product of velocity and 0.3.
    Calculate the sum of drive and the result.
    Calculate the sum of the result and 0.32.
    Calculate the product of the result and 150.
    Display the result to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Language chosen: C, because a fixed step loop like this drops straight into a real time cerebellar model.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>
#define PI 3.14159265358979
#define DT 0.0001                 /* seconds, that is 0.1 millisecond steps */
int main(void) {
    double v = -63.0;             /* membrane potential, millivolts */
    double len, vel, prev = 1.0, drive, cortical, t;
    int i, spikes = 0;
    for (i = 0; i < 10000; i++) {                    /* one second of movement */
        t = i * DT;
        len = 1.0 + 0.05 * sin(2.0 * PI * 2.0 * t);  /* muscle stretched at 2 hertz */
        vel = (len - prev) / DT;                     /* lengths per second */
        prev = len;
        cortical = (t > 0.5) ? -0.25 : 0.0;          /* corollary discharge damps the relay */
        drive = 6.0 * (len - 1.0) + 0.30 * vel + 0.42 + cortical;   /* nanoamps */
        v += DT * 1000.0 * (drive - 0.020 * (v + 63.0)) / 0.20;     /* microsiemens, nanofarads */
        if (v >= -45.0) { v = -60.0; spikes++; }
    }
    printf("dorsal spinocerebellar spikes in 1 second: %d\n", spikes);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A large pear shaped cell with an off centre nucleus, packed into a narrow column at the medial base of the dorsal horn only between C8 and L3, smothered in giant VGLUT1 boutons and firing all the time.

CHAPTER 190. LAMINA X NEURONS (Deep-Core Crossroads Neuron) (NON-COG)

Ringing the hollow core of your spinal cord is a sleeve of grey matter thinner than a pencil lead, and some of its cells dip a hair-tipped finger into the fluid inside and taste it.

Lamina X is purely a coordinate, the ring of grey matter around the central canal, and the number tells a reader nothing.

Deep-Core names the position that distinguishes it, the innermost ring of the cord wrapped around the central canal.

Crossroads names the defining job, because this is the one place where signals from the skin and signals from the gut, bladder and heart land on the same cells, making it the cord's chief site of somatic and visceral convergence.

Neuron marks it as a cell of a region regarded as critical for visceral pain, the poorly localised ache of a diseased organ, and among the least studied regions of the cord, so the name deliberately claims convergence and no more.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Lamina X is the tenth and innermost layer of spinal grey matter, mapped by the Swedish anatomist Bror Rexed in 1952.

Most of its cells are interneurons, local cells serving neighbours not the brain, merging gut and skin signals to steer organ control and movement.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Most are small multipolar cells whose dendrites, the input branches, cross the midline into the other half of the cord.

A minority push one dendrite into the central canal, capped by a bulb of hairs that taste the cerebrospinal fluid.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are small, roughly 10 to 25 micrometres wide, matching a measured input resistance of 1.0 gigaohm.

The sensory bulb is 3.0 to 5.0 micrometres in rodents and 5.2 to 8.8 in macaques.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Fluid contacting neurons, marked by the polycystic kidney disease 2-like 1 (PKD2L1) channel and releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brake chemical.

• Central canal cluster cells releasing acetylcholine and GABA together.

• Dorsal grey commissure cells fed by the internal organs.

• Projection neurons reaching thalamus and parabrachial area.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The grey sleeve around the central canal at every segment, first cervical to coccygeal, and the only spinal tissue touching the ependyma that lines it.

Laterally it joins lamina VII, it inhibits the thoracic intermediolateral cell column, and at S2 to S4 it abuts the sacral parasympathetic nucleus.

NUMBER:

Best estimate 2 to 6 million cells, 1 to 3 percent of the cord's 200 million neurons, with low confidence since no human count exists.

The fluid contacting subset runs about 8 cells per 40 micrometre section in all species measured, near 100 thousand per cord.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Measured resting voltage is minus 56 millivolts and input resistance 1.0 gigaohm.

Among 21 rat cells firing split as tonic 9, transient 6, delayed 3, single spiking 2 and bursting 1, with slow 3.9 millisecond upstrokes in fluid contacting cells.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

All 20 cells tested answered the fifth lumbar dorsal root, and inhibition appeared in all 9 tested for it.

None answered the ventral root, and the slowest inputs conducted under 0.5 metres per second.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Most axons are short, crossing the midline through the grey commissure to the mirror-image cell opposite.

A minority climb the ventrolateral funiculus to brainstem and thalamus.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA and glycine dominate the fluid contacting cells, acetylcholine the central canal cluster, glutamate the excitatory and projecting cells, with somatostatin and enkephalin acting slowly.

MYELINATION:

Local axons are bare or thinly wrapped, since the fatty insulation called myelin is largely absent from grey matter.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest, since these cells are small and slow firing, but the region depends on the thin sulcal branches of the anterior spinal artery.

PLASTICITY:

Inflammation of an organ sensitises these cells for hours to days.

After injury the neighbouring ependymal cells proliferate and fluid contacting neurons in mice carry stem cell markers, the cord's only credible repair reserve.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The specialised glia are ependymal cells, which beat hairs to stir the fluid and double as a dormant stem cell pool, unlike glia elsewhere.

In adults it mostly vanishes, the canal being shut in 88 percent of young adults and everyone over 65.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete these cells and the two halves of the cord stop talking across the midline here.

Visceral pain would lose its diffuse, nauseating, hard to locate character.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

These are sensors, not relays, reading acidity through PKD2L1 and acid-sensing ion channel 3 (ASIC3), and reading flow through the bending of their hairs.

They also broadcast peptides into the fluid, reaching cells they never touch.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: canal pH 7.20 to 7.40; wall bending 0 to 1; dorsal root drive to 200 hertz.

• OUTPUTS: GABA and glycine across the midline; acetylcholine to motor neurons; peptides into the fluid.

• STATE: membrane voltage; sensor current in picoamps; recent spikes.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 56 millivolts; input resistance 1000 megaohm; threshold minus 40.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 25 milliseconds; spike upstroke 3.9 milliseconds; peptides seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: syringomyelia hollows this sleeve, giving cape-like loss of pain and temperature.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to fire a fluid contacting lamina X neuron from the acidity and motion of the fluid in the central canal.
Create a number variable called lift with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 20000 times.
    Calculate the difference of 7.32 and the newest canal acidity reading.
    Set drive to the result.
    Calculate the product of drive and 120.
    Set drive to the result.
    Calculate the sum of drive and the canal bending current.
    Set drive to the result.
    Calculate the difference of drive and lift.
    Set drive to the result.
    Calculate the quotient of drive divided by 250.
    Set drive to the result.
    Calculate the sum of lift and drive.
    Set lift to the result.
    Check if lift is greater than or equal to 16.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set lift to -4.
        Display "spike" to the console.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: a few scalars stepped every 0.1 ms, so clarity beats speed.
import math
DT, TAU, RIN = 0.1, 25.0, 1000.0            # ms, ms, megaohm
VREST, VTHR, VRESET = -56.0, -40.0, -60.0   # measured lamina X values, mV
PH0, G_PH, G_BEND = 7.32, 120.0, 30.0       # pH, pA per pH, pA per bend
def step(v, ph, bend):
    i = G_PH * (PH0 - ph) + G_BEND * bend              # pA
    v += (-(v - VREST) + RIN * i / 1000.0) / TAU * DT  # mV
    return (VRESET, True) if v >= VTHR else (v, False)
v, spikes = VREST, 0
for n in range(20000):                      # 2 seconds
    t = n * DT
    ph = PH0 - (0.10 if 500.0 < t < 1500.0 else 0.0)
    v, fired = step(v, ph, 0.5 * math.sin(2 * math.pi * t / 400.0))
    spikes += fired
print(spikes, "spikes in 2 s")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other neuron puts a hair-tipped finger into the cerebrospinal fluid and reads its acidity and motion.

A small GABA-making cell carrying PKD2L1 against the ependymal lining settles it.

CHAPTER 191. LISSAUER TRACT INTERNEURONS (Neighbouring-Segment Spreading Neuron) (NON-COG)

Pain does not enter the spinal cord and stop; it first runs a short lane up and down the cord's back corner, and the neurons inside decide how far it spreads.

The old label names a white matter bundle rather than a cell, so it is an address rather than a description, and the eponym belongs to the tract and not to the neuron.

Neighbouring-Segment records the measured fact that these propriospinal axons, arising from cells in the substantia gelatinosa, travel only about one to two spinal segments up or down before diving back into the grey matter.

Spreading names the consequence, that a painful input arriving at one segment is shared sideways along the cord to the dorsal horns above and below it, which is why stimulating this tract changes the excitability of dorsal horn cells for prolonged periods.

Neuron marks it as a local propriospinal cell rather than a long tract cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Lissauer's tract is the thin cap of white matter over the tip of the dorsal horn, described in 1885 by Heinrich Lissauer.

Its interneurons sit inside that cap, passing pain signals one or two segments away and damping arriving fibres at their own terminals.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

They are small spindle-shaped or star-shaped cells whose dendrites, the input branches, run lengthwise beside the tract fibres.

Each therefore samples a long thin column of segments, not a ball of nearby tissue.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are roughly 8 to 20 micrometres wide, with dendritic fields several hundred micrometres long but narrow.

The tract is roughly one percent of the cord's cross-section.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Interstitial neurons whose cell bodies sit inside the tract, ringed by passing axons.

• Marginal cells on the lamina I boundary, straddling grey and white matter.

• Medial cells near the dorsal columns, and lateral cells distinct from the neighbouring lateral spinal nucleus.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The crescent between the dorsolateral sulcus and the apex of the dorsal horn, at every segment.

Its defining interface is the dorsal root entry zone, where Schwann cell myelin yields to oligodendrocyte myelin at the Obersteiner-Redlich line.

Medially it borders the dorsal columns, ventrally lamina I, laterally the dorsolateral funiculus.

NUMBER:

Best estimate 100 thousand to 400 thousand interstitial cells, under 0.2 percent of the cord's 200 million neurons, with low confidence.

Fibre counts are firmer: over two thirds of the tract's axons in rat are sensory, and 13 percent of the rest are myelinated.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

No human recordings exist, so the bound comes from the tract itself, whose fibres need pulses of 155 to 380 microseconds.

After each impulse a supernormal period up to 20 milliseconds long speeds conduction by 25 percent and raises excitability by 40 percent.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from the slow pain fibres in the tract, plus returning input from lamina II.

Outputs land on lamina I and II cells and on the arriving fibres' own terminals.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons run one to two segments before diving into grey matter, and few from the seventh cervical segment pass beyond the sixth or eighth.

Most are unmyelinated, 0.05 to 0.6 micrometres wide, so conduction stays under one metre per second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Gamma-aminobutyric acid and glycine for the inhibitory cells, glutamate for the excitatory ones, with enkephalin and somatostatin as slower modulators.

MYELINATION:

Overwhelmingly absent: bare axons in a peripheral nerve sit in Schwann cell pockets, while here they lie naked and packed, parted only by astrocyte processes.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low per cell but high per millimetre of axon, since an unmyelinated fibre must pump sodium out along its whole surface, not leap between gaps.

PLASTICITY:

Repeated slow fibre input produces wind-up, a growing response to an unchanging stimulus.

After nerve injury that spread lets pain occupy skin beyond the injured dermatome, and burning this tract in the dorsal root entry zone operation relieves 65 to 77 percent of avulsion cases.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here build an unusually dense wall stopping peripheral Schwann cells and their axons from invading the cord.

Microglia here activate among the first anywhere after a nerve is cut, while oligodendrocytes do little.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and a pinprick would light up exactly one dermatome and stop dead at its edge.

The blurred halo around a sore spot is theirs.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

They make axoaxonic synapses, contacts onto another cell's output terminal rather than its inputs, the machinery of presynaptic inhibition.

Strong enough, it flips into a dorsal root reflex firing backwards into the skin, and bare packed axons let one fibre nudge another electrically.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: slow volleys to 20 hertz; thinly myelinated volleys to 100 hertz; descending serotonin.

• OUTPUTS: excitation and inhibition of lamina I and II, here and up to two segments away.

• STATE: membrane voltage; relayed drive per segment; inhibition level; time since last impulse.

• PARAMETERS: reach 2 segments; relay fraction 0.45; maximum inhibition 0.6; conduction 0.3 to 1.0 metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: supernormal 20 milliseconds; dorsal root potential 100 to 300 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: the painful field widens beyond the injured dermatome, as in root avulsion.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to spread a pain volley from one spinal segment into the one or two segments above and below it.
Create a number variable called entrySegment with initial value 15.
Create a number variable called relay with initial value 0.45.
Create a number variable called distance with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called spread with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called inhibition with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 2 times.
    Calculate the sum of distance and 1.
    Set spread to the result.
    Calculate the quotient of relay divided by spread.
    Set spread to the result.
    Calculate the product of spread and 0.6.
    Set inhibition to the result.
    Calculate the sum of entrySegment and distance.
    Display the result to the console.
    Calculate the difference of entrySegment and distance.
    Display the result to the console.
    Display inhibition to the console.
    Calculate the sum of distance and 1.
    Set distance to the result.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C: a cord model runs this kernel millions of times a second. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#define NSEG 31   /* segments on one side */
#define REACH 2   /* 1 to 2 segments */
int main(void) {
    double drive[NSEG] = {0.0}, pad;
    const int entry = 15;       /* entry segment */
    const double relay = 0.45;  /* tract share */
    for (int s = 0; s < NSEG; s++) {
        int d = abs(s - entry);
        if (d <= REACH) drive[s] = relay / (1.0 + d);
    }
    drive[entry] += 1.0 - relay;
    for (int s = 0; s < NSEG; s++) {
        if (drive[s] <= 0.0) continue;
        pad = 0.6 * drive[s] / (0.25 + drive[s]);   /* saturating gate */
        printf("seg %2d drive %.3f inhib %.3f\n", s + 1, drive[s], pad);
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron whose cell body sits inside a bundle of naked pain fibres, dendrites stretched lengthwise along them.

Stimulate the tract and inhibition runs backwards along the fibres that brought the pain in.


PART ELEVEN: RETINA - THE EYE'S NEURAL SHEET

CHAPTER 192. ROD PHOTORECEPTORS (NON-COG)

A single rod can announce the arrival of one particle of light, which makes it the most sensitive detector your body owns.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A rod is a slender nerve cell at the back of the eye that turns light into an electrical signal, and it is the cell of night vision.

Unlike almost every other neuron it is busiest in darkness and falls quiet in light.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A rod has four parts in a line: an outer segment holding roughly 700 to 1,000 flattened membrane discs, an inner segment full of mitochondria, a cell body, and a tiny output ending called a spherule.

SIZE:

The outer segment is about 2 micrometres across and 25 micrometres long, thinner than spider silk, and the whole cell runs about 100 to 120 micrometres end to end.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Rods are famously uniform, and single-cell gene sequencing finds one main rod population in the human retina, with central rods merely longer and thinner than peripheral ones.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Rods fill the outer nuclear layer of the retina, the light-sensitive sheet lining the back of the eyeball, with their outer segments pushed into the retinal pigment epithelium, a layer of dark support cells.

NUMBER:

About 92 million rods per eye, so roughly 184 million across both eyes, from Curcio and colleagues who counted eight human retinas.

Confidence is high for the average and moderate for any individual, because the measured range was 77.9 to 107.3 million per eye.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

In darkness a rod sits at about minus 40 millivolts on an inward dark current of 20 to 50 picoamperes, and light closes those channels to hyperpolarise it toward minus 70 millivolts.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Each rod ends in one spherule carrying a single synaptic ribbon, a bar-shaped conveyor that queues vesicles for continuous release, sitting above two horizontal cell processes and usually two rod bipolar dendrites.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The rod axon is a short unbranched spike-free fibre, tens of micrometres long in the periphery and several hundred near the fovea where it runs sideways in the fibre layer of Henle.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Rods release glutamate, the main excitatory messenger of the nervous system, continuously in the dark at hundreds of vesicles per second, and light reduces that release, so a rod speaks by falling silent.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable.

Myelin would scatter light before it reached the photoreceptors, so the retina contains no myelin-forming oligodendrocytes at all until the optic nerve head.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Photoreceptors consume more oxygen per gram than any cell in the body, their inner segments hold 55 to 65 percent of all retinal mitochondria, and most of that energy bails out the dark current, so a rod burns more fuel in darkness than in light.

PLASTICITY:

Rods do not rewire like cortical neurons but retune their gain continuously through calcium feedback onto the light cascade, and in disease they retract their spherules and sprout processes into the inner retina.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The retinal pigment epithelium does something no other support cell does: it eats the shed disc tips each dawn and rebuilds the bent form of vitamin A that rhodopsin needs, using the enzyme RPE65.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove rods and the retina keeps colour and daylight but loses the entire night, and the periphery becomes a useless grey field, because outside the central few degrees rods outnumber cones more than twenty to one.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

A rod amplifies before it integrates, since one activated rhodopsin switches on around 100 transducin molecules and each activated phosphodiesterase destroys roughly a thousand cyclic guanosine monophosphate molecules.

Falling calcium acts as a backward-travelling messenger that speeds recovery and resets sensitivity, which is dark adaptation at the molecular level.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: photons from 380 to 700 nanometres, peak 498 nanometres, as photoisomerisations per rod per second from 0.001 in starlight to above 10,000 where the rod saturates.

• OUTPUTS: graded glutamate from one ribbon onto two rod bipolar dendrites and two horizontal cell processes, plus gap junction current into neighbouring cones.

• STATE: activated rhodopsin, transducin and phosphodiesterase pools, cyclic guanosine monophosphate level, internal calcium, open channel fraction, membrane voltage.

• PARAMETERS: dark current 20 to 50 picoamperes; single photon response about 1 picoampere; quantum efficiency 0.67; about 100 million rhodopsin copies; 700 to 1,300 discs.

• TIME CONSTANTS: single photon response peaks near 150 to 200 milliseconds; disc renewal 10 days; full dark adaptation 20 to 40 minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: rhodopsin mutations cause about a quarter of dominant retinitis pigmentosa, affecting roughly 1 in 3,000 to 1 in 4,000 people, killing rods first and then starving cones.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn photons caught by a rod outer segment into the rod's graded membrane voltage.
Create a number variable called dark current with initial value 30.
Create a number variable called single photon amplitude with initial value 0.7.
Create a number variable called half saturating suppression with initial value 12.
Create a number variable called decay factor with initial value 0.994.
Create a number variable called suppression with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called working value with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called voltage trace with initial value empty list.
For each photon count in the list of photons caught this millisecond, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the product of photon count and single photon amplitude.
    Set working value to that product.
    Calculate the sum of suppression and working value.
    Set suppression to that sum.
    Calculate the product of suppression and decay factor.
    Set suppression to that product.
    Calculate the sum of suppression and half saturating suppression.
    Set working value to that sum.
    Calculate the quotient of suppression divided by working value.
    Set working value to that quotient.
    Calculate the product of working value and negative 30.
    Set working value to that product.
    Calculate the difference of working value and 40.
    Add that difference to the end of voltage trace.
    If suppression is greater than 300, then perform the following actions.
        Display "the rod is saturated and can report nothing further" to the console.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Return voltage trace to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because a rod's job is stochastic and the standard library
# alone supplies the random draws, so this sample runs anywhere unchanged.
import math, random

DARK_PA, SPR_PA, HALF_PA = 30.0, 0.7, 12.0   # dark current, one photon, half saturation
TAU_R, TAU_F = 80.0, 300.0                   # rise and fall constants, milliseconds
QE, RATE = 0.67, 0.01                        # quantum efficiency, photons per millisecond

kernel = [math.exp(-i / TAU_F) - math.exp(-i / TAU_R) for i in range(1500)]
peak = max(kernel)
kernel = [SPR_PA * v / peak for v in kernel]  # single photon response in picoamperes

random.seed(3)
linear = [0.0] * 2500
for ms in range(1000):
    if random.random() < RATE * QE:           # so rare that photons arrive singly
        for j, k in enumerate(kernel):
            linear[ms + j] += k

for ms in range(0, 1000, 200):
    s = DARK_PA * linear[ms] / (linear[ms] + HALF_PA)      # saturating suppression
    print("t=%4d ms  current=%5.2f pA  Vm=%6.2f mV"
          % (ms, DARK_PA - s, -40.0 - 30.0 * s / DARK_PA))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

If a cell is depolarised in the dark, releases glutamate all night, saturates in daylight, reports single photons by going quiet, and hands its worn tip to a pigmented epithelium each dawn, it is a rod.

CHAPTER 193. CONE PHOTORECEPTORS (NON-COG)

Every word you have ever read arrived through cones, and almost all of them through a patch of retina no wider than the head of a pin.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A cone is a photoreceptor built for daylight, speed and colour rather than sensitivity.

It gives you sharp detail, fast motion and all of colour vision, and it never saturates however bright the day becomes.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A cone follows the rod's four-part plan, but its outer segment is a tapering cone of membrane folds that stay attached to the surface instead of sealing into free discs, and its output ending is a broad flat pedicle rather than a small spherule.

SIZE:

A foveal cone inner segment is only about 2.0 to 2.5 micrometres wide with an outer segment 30 to 50 micrometres long, while peripheral cones swell to 5 to 8 micrometres wide with short squat outer segments.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

By shape, cones form a smooth gradient from slim foveal to stout peripheral rather than two clean classes; by pigment they split into the short, middle and long wavelength types, which are the next three entries here and are subdivisions of this same population, not extra cells.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cones occupy the outer nuclear layer across the whole retina, with outer segments embedded in the retinal pigment epithelium at the same neural-to-epithelial interface the rods use.

NUMBER:

About 4.6 million cones per eye on the Curcio count, so roughly 9.2 million across both eyes, inside the sectional budget of about 5 million per eye.

Confidence is high for the mean and moderate for individuals, since the range was 4.08 to 5.29 million per eye and peak foveal density ran from about 100,000 to 324,000 per square millimetre around a mean of 199,000.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

A cone rests near minus 40 millivolts in darkness on a dark current of 20 to 40 picoamperes and hyperpolarises only to about minus 55 or minus 60 millivolts in bright light.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

One cone pedicle holds around 20 ribbons in the fovea and 40 to 50 in the periphery, each ribbon over a triad of two horizontal cell processes and one or more invaginating ON bipolar dendrites, with flat basal contacts feeding OFF bipolar cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The cone axon is a thick, unmyelinated, spike-free fibre that conducts passively, so it acts as a low-pass filter, gently smoothing the fastest flicker before it reaches the pedicle.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Cones release glutamate steadily in darkness and reduce it in light, and the same molecule reaches ON bipolar cells through the sign-inverting mGluR6 receptor and OFF bipolar cells through sign-conserving AMPA and kainate receptors, so one chemical carries two opposite messages.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable.

Cones lie directly in the light path and any myelin would scatter the image, so no myelin-forming cells exist in the retina until the optic nerve head.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Cones pack dense mitochondria into an ellipsoid beneath the outer segment that doubles as a microlens, they draw oxygen almost entirely from the choroid behind the retina, and foveal cones are especially exposed because the fovea has no retinal capillaries of its own.

PLASTICITY:

Cone gain control is fast and continuous, following Weber's law so the response tracks relative rather than absolute change in light, and adaptive optics imaging shows individual cones shifting position over weeks even in healthy eyes.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Cones have a private second visual cycle that rods lack, in which Muller cells regenerate the bent form of vitamin A locally, which is why cones recover from bleaching in seconds while rods need many minutes.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete cones and the retina keeps night vision but loses colour, reading, faces and any detail finer than a coarse blur, and acuity collapses from a normal one minute of arc to near legal blindness.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Cone inner segments act as optical waveguides, which is why light entering the edge of the pupil looks dimmer, the effect named after Stiles and Crawford.

Cones run calcium-dependent gain control within milliseconds, receive horizontal cell feedback that sharpens their output before it leaves the pedicle, and couple to each other and to rods through connexin 36 gap junctions.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: photons from 380 to 700 nanometres at about 100 to more than 1,000,000 photoisomerisations per cone per second, plus horizontal cell feedback of a few millivolts.

• OUTPUTS: graded glutamate onto roughly ten bipolar types and two horizontal cell types, and gap junction current to neighbouring cones and rods.

• STATE: bleached pigment fraction, transducin and phosphodiesterase pools, cyclic guanosine monophosphate level, internal calcium, membrane voltage, adaptation gain.

• PARAMETERS: dark current 20 to 40 picoamperes; 20 to 50 ribbons per pedicle; peak foveal density about 199,000 per square millimetre; sensitivity about one hundredth of a rod.

• TIME CONSTANTS: response peak 20 to 50 milliseconds; bleach recovery about 100 milliseconds; cone dark adaptation 5 to 10 minutes; outer segment renewal about 10 days.

• FAILURE MODE: achromatopsia, a total loss of cone function affecting roughly 1 in 30,000 people and usually caused by CNGA3 or CNGB3 mutations, leaves night vision intact but removes colour and acuity.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute a cone's fast flash response after its gain has been set by the background light.
Create a number variable called dark current with initial value 25.
Create a number variable called gain with initial value 0.692.
Create a number variable called first stage with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called second stage with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called half saturating signal with initial value 2.5.
Create a number variable called working value with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called current trace with initial value empty list.
For each flash strength in the list of photoisomerisations per time step, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the product of flash strength and gain.
    Set working value to that product.
    Calculate the sum of first stage and working value.
    Set first stage to that sum.
    Calculate the sum of second stage and half saturating signal.
    Set working value to that sum.
    Calculate the quotient of second stage divided by working value.
    Set working value to that quotient.
    Calculate the product of working value and dark current.
    Set working value to that product.
    Calculate the difference of dark current and working value.
    Add that difference to the end of current trace.
    Calculate the product of first stage and 0.92.
    Set second stage to that product.
    Calculate the product of first stage and 0.08.
    Set first stage to that product.
End the loop.
Return current trace to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C is chosen because a whole-retina simulation steps millions of cones per
   video frame. Build with: cc -std=c99 cone.c -o cone                        */
#include <stdio.h>
#define DT        0.001    /* time step in seconds                            */
#define TAU_R     0.012    /* first cascade stage constant, seconds           */
#define TAU_D     0.022    /* second cascade stage constant, seconds          */
#define DARK_CUR  25.0     /* dark current in picoamperes                     */
#define I_HALF    4500.0   /* background giving half gain, isomerisations/s   */
#define SAT       2.5      /* half saturating internal signal, model units    */

int main(void) {
    double x = 0.0, y = 0.0;                     /* two stages of the cascade */
    double gain = 1.0 / (1.0 + 2000.0 / I_HALF); /* steady background of 2000 */
    int t;
    for (t = 0; t < 120; t++) {
        if (t == 20) x += 600.0 * gain;          /* one brief flash           */
        double suppressed = DARK_CUR * y / (y + SAT);
        if (t % 10 == 0)
            printf("t=%3.0f ms  current=%6.2f pA  Vm=%7.2f mV\n", t * DT * 1000.0,
                   DARK_CUR - suppressed, -40.0 - 20.0 * suppressed / DARK_CUR);
        double dx = -x / TAU_R, dy = (x - y) / TAU_D;
        x += dx * DT;
        y += dy * DT;
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A cone is the only photoreceptor with a wide flat pedicle carrying dozens of ribbons, a mitochondrial lens in its neck, and a response that is over before a rod's has properly begun.

CHAPTER 194. S-CONES, OR SHORT-WAVELENGTH CONES (Short-Wave Blue-Catching Cell) (NON-COG)

There is a tiny blue blind spot at the exact centre of your gaze, and it exists because this cell refuses to live there.

The letter S is a one-character barcode that teaches nothing on its own, so the wavelength dimension is written into the name instead.

Short-Wave places this cell at the blue end of the visible spectrum, with a pigment peaking near 420 to 440 nanometres.

Blue-Catching names the perceptual work it starts.

Cell marks it as a photoreceptor, and the name warns that this is a rare and coarse channel, since these cells are under ten percent of all cones and are absent from the very centre of the fovea, which is why the sharpest point of vision is blue-blind.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An S-cone is the rarest of the three colour cones, tuned to short-wavelength light that we call violet and blue.

It supplies one half of the blue-versus-yellow channel, the oldest colour comparison in the primate brain, and contributes almost nothing to brightness or fine detail.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

An S-cone looks much like any other cone but tends to have a slightly taller, more slender inner segment and a pedicle that sits a little deeper in the outer plexiform layer.

SIZE:

Inner segment about 2 to 3 micrometres wide near the fovea and 5 to 8 micrometres in the periphery, with an outer segment of 10 to 40 micrometres, matching neighbouring cones.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

None are recognised, and single-cell sequencing of human retina finds one S-cone transcriptional class, although the S-cones inside the central one degree differ from peripheral ones in packing and connection.

WHERE IT LIVES:

S-cones sit in the outer nuclear layer everywhere except the very centre, where a roughly 0.34 degree S-cone-free zone leaves the foveola with no blue sensitivity at all.

NUMBER:

Roughly 5 to 10 percent of cones are S-cones, so taking 7 percent gives about 320,000 per eye and about 640,000 across both eyes.

Confidence is moderate, resting on adaptive optics imaging and immunostaining rather than a single large count, and the percentage rises modestly from centre to periphery.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Electrically an S-cone behaves like any cone, resting near minus 40 millivolts in darkness and hyperpolarising in light, but its peak sensitivity lies near 420 nanometres rather than in the yellow-green.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The S-cone pedicle drives a dedicated blue-ON bipolar cell that reaches past the L and M pedicles to find it, and that bipolar drives the small bistratified ganglion cell, which is excited by blue and inhibited by yellow.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A short, unmyelinated, passively conducting axon identical in kind to other cone axons, displaced sideways into the fibre layer of Henle near the fovea.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, released tonically in darkness and reduced by light, with no transmitter unique to S-cones.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable.

Like every retinal neuron the S-cone is unmyelinated, because myelin in the light path would blur the image before it was ever captured.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The same very high oxidative demand as other cones, supplied by the choroid, with the added burden that short-wavelength light is the most energetic in the visible range and does the most photochemical damage.

PLASTICITY:

S-cone sensitivity declines measurably with age faster than L and M sensitivity, partly through yellowing of the lens and partly through genuine loss of S-cone signal.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The macular pigments lutein and zeaxanthin, concentrated in Muller cell fibres over the central retina, absorb exactly the blue light S-cones use, so the support tissue around them acts as a permanent blue filter that no other cone class has to see through.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove S-cones and the world does not turn grey; it loses blues and violets, so skies, veins and bruises turn dull, and the retina keeps acuity and brightness untouched.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

S-cones are laid down first in fetal development and lay out a quasi-regular lattice by mutual repulsion, an example of a cell type patterning its own mosaic.

They are largely excluded from the connexin 36 gap junction network that links L and M cones, which keeps the blue signal from being smeared into the luminance signal.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: photons 380 to 540 nanometres, peak near 420 nanometres, plus feedback from H2 horizontal cells.

• OUTPUTS: graded glutamate to blue-ON bipolar cells, to some OFF midget bipolar cells and to H2 horizontal cells.

• STATE: bleached pigment fraction, cascade activation, cyclic guanosine monophosphate level, internal calcium, membrane voltage.

• PARAMETERS: peak wavelength 419 to 426 nanometres; 5 to 10 percent of cones; S-free zone radius about 0.17 degrees; gene OPN1SW on chromosome 7.

• TIME CONSTANTS: flash response peak 20 to 50 milliseconds; the blue-yellow channel is the slowest colour channel, resolving flicker only to about 10 hertz.

• FAILURE MODE: tritan colour deficiency, from OPN1SW mutations with incomplete penetrance and an incidence near 1 in 500, removes blue-yellow discrimination while leaving red-green intact.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to report how many S-cones sit at a given distance from the centre of the fovea.
Create a number variable called free zone radius with initial value 0.17.
Create a number variable called far fraction with initial value 0.07.
Create a number variable called s fraction with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called cone density with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called working value with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called s cone counts with initial value empty list.
For each eccentricity in the list of test eccentricities in degrees, perform the following operations.
    Set cone density to the smooth cone density fit evaluated at eccentricity.
    If eccentricity is less than free zone radius, then perform the following actions.
        Set s fraction to 0.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of eccentricity and free zone radius.
        Set working value to that difference.
        Set working value to one minus the exponential decay of working value over 0.30.
        Calculate the product of far fraction and working value.
        Set s fraction to that product.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of cone density and s fraction.
    Add that product to the end of s cone counts.
    Display that product to the console.
End the loop.
Return s cone counts to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because this is a short numeric table where clarity beats speed.
import math

def sensitivity(nm, lmax, width=0.032):
    """Log-Gaussian stand-in for a cone absorption spectrum."""
    x = math.log10(nm) - math.log10(lmax)
    return math.exp(-(x * x) / (2.0 * width * width))

def cone_density(ecc):
    """Smooth fit to human cone density, cones per square millimetre."""
    return 183000.0 * math.exp(-ecc / 0.6) + 12000.0 * math.exp(-ecc / 8.0) + 4000.0

S_FREE_DEG, S_FAR = 0.17, 0.07     # S-free radius, and S share far from centre

def s_fraction(ecc):
    if ecc < S_FREE_DEG:
        return 0.0
    return S_FAR * (1.0 - math.exp(-(ecc - S_FREE_DEG) / 0.30))

print("ecc(deg)   cones/mm2   S-cones/mm2")
for ecc in (0.0, 0.1, 0.5, 1.0, 2.0, 5.0, 10.0, 20.0):
    d = cone_density(ecc)
    print("%8.1f %11.0f %13.0f" % (ecc, d, d * s_fraction(ecc)))

print("S sensitivity at 420 nm: %.3f" % sensitivity(420.0, 420.0))
print("S sensitivity at 560 nm: %.5f" % sensitivity(560.0, 420.0))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only cone missing from the centre of the fovea, the only one with its own private bipolar cell that reaches over its neighbours, and the only one whose pigment gene sits on an ordinary chromosome rather than the X.

CHAPTER 195. M-CONES, OR MIDDLE-WAVELENGTH CONES (Middle-Wave Green-Catching Cell) (NON-COG)

Call it the green cone if you like, but it peaks in yellow-green and its real job is to disagree, very slightly, with the cone next door.

M alone is an alphabetical address, so the name states the position on the spectrum that the letter was standing in for.

Middle-Wave marks a pigment peaking around 530 to 545 nanometres, in the centre of the visible band.

Green-Catching names the sensation this channel feeds.

Cell marks it as a photoreceptor, and together with its long-wave partner it carries the bulk of daylight vision, the two types making up more than ninety percent of all cones.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An M-cone is a colour cone tuned to middle-wavelength light, peaking near 530 nanometres.

Together with the L-cone it builds both the red-green colour channel and the brightness channel, and red-green vision works only because the tiny difference between their outputs is amplified.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

An M-cone is structurally indistinguishable from an L-cone under a microscope, sharing the same tapering outer segment, mitochondrial ellipsoid and flat pedicle.

SIZE:

Identical to cones generally: about 2.0 to 2.5 micrometres wide in the fovea, 5 to 8 micrometres in the periphery, with outer segments of 10 to 50 micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There are no shape subtypes, but there is genetic variety, since people carry one to several OPN1MW gene copies in a tandem array and hybrid genes formed by unequal crossing over produce pigments with shifted peaks.

WHERE IT LIVES:

M-cones are spread through the outer nuclear layer from the very centre of the foveola to the ora serrata at the retinal edge, mixed randomly with L-cones rather than arranged in a pattern.

NUMBER:

Taking about 93 percent of cones as L or M and an average L to M ratio near two to one gives roughly 1.4 million M-cones per eye and about 2.9 million across both eyes.

Confidence is low for any individual, because measured L to M ratios in colour-normal men run from 1.1 to 1 up to 16.5 to 1, a fortyfold spread, with no effect on colour discrimination.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Electrically an M-cone is an ordinary cone, resting near minus 40 millivolts in darkness with fast responses peaking in 20 to 50 milliseconds, differing from an L-cone only in which photons it absorbs.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

In the central retina one M-cone drives one ON midget bipolar cell and one OFF midget bipolar cell privately, a one-to-one wiring found nowhere else in the nervous system.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A short, thick, unmyelinated, spike-free cone axon that conducts passively, displaced sideways into the fibre layer of Henle in the central retina.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, released tonically in darkness, with the sign inverted at ON bipolar cells by mGluR6 and preserved at OFF bipolar cells by AMPA and kainate receptors.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable.

No retinal neuron is myelinated, because a fatty sheath in the light path would scatter the image before capture.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The same extreme oxidative demand as other cones, met by the choroid, and no measurable difference from L-cones has been reported.

PLASTICITY:

The M-cone signal is normalised by the visual system rather than by the cell, which is why people with wildly different L to M ratios still call the same wavelength yellow.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

M-cones use the standard cone support arrangement, the retinal pigment epithelium plus the Muller cell visual cycle, with nothing special to report; the only asymmetry is that macular pigment absorbs blue light and so barely filters M-cone input.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove M-cones and the retina keeps brightness, acuity and blue-yellow vision but loses red-green entirely, which is deuteranopia; reds, greens, oranges and browns collapse into one confusable band.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The M-cone's most important property is not electrical at all: its pigment sits only about 28 nanometres from the L pigment, so the retina must extract a tiny difference from two nearly identical signals, and the private midget wiring exists to preserve that difference.

Because M and L cones are randomly interleaved, every local patch has its own L to M ratio, and the visual system must calibrate itself to the mosaic it happens to have been given.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:
