• INPUTS: photons 450 to 640 nanometres, peak near 530 nanometres, plus H1 horizontal cell feedback and gap junction current from neighbours.

• OUTPUTS: graded glutamate to one ON and one OFF midget bipolar cell centrally, to diffuse bipolar cells, and to H1 horizontal cells.

• STATE: bleached pigment fraction, cascade activation, cyclic guanosine monophosphate level, internal calcium, membrane voltage, adaptation gain.

• PARAMETERS: peak wavelength 530 to 534 nanometres; gene OPN1MW at Xq28; about 31 percent of cones on average, range 6 to 45 percent.

• TIME CONSTANTS: flash response peak 20 to 50 milliseconds; the red-green channel resolves flicker to roughly 10 to 15 hertz, far slower than the luminance channel.

• FAILURE MODE: deuteranomaly and deuteranopia together affect about 6 percent of men of northern European ancestry, caused by unequal recombination in the X-linked opsin array.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to build a patch of the L and M cone mosaic and measure the local middle-wavelength share.
Create a number variable called probability of L with initial value 0.667.
Create a number variable called patch size with initial value 400.
Create a number variable called m count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called local share with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called mosaic with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 400 times.
    If a fresh random number between zero and one is less than probability of L, then perform the following actions.
        Add "L" to the end of mosaic.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Add "M" to the end of mosaic.
        Calculate the sum of m count and 1.
        Set m count to that sum.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Calculate the quotient of m count divided by patch size.
Set local share to that quotient.
Display local share to the console.
If local share is less than 0.1, then perform the following actions.
    Display "this patch is nearly red-green blind by mosaic alone" to the console.
End the conditional block.
Return mosaic to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

// JavaScript is chosen because a cone mosaic demo belongs in a browser next to
// a picture of the mosaic itself, with no build step at all. Run with: node m.js
const W = 0.032;                                   // log-Gaussian spectral width
function sensitivity(nm, lmax) {
  const x = Math.log10(nm) - Math.log10(lmax);
  return Math.exp(-(x * x) / (2 * W * W));
}
const L_MAX = 558, M_MAX = 530;

// A test light made of three narrow bands: [wavelength nm, radiance].
const light = [[450, 1.0], [530, 1.0], [610, 1.0]];
const excite = (lmax) => light.reduce((s, [nm, p]) => s + p * sensitivity(nm, lmax), 0);
const L = excite(L_MAX), M = excite(M_MAX);
console.log("L = " + L.toFixed(4) + "   M = " + M.toFixed(4));
console.log("midget red-green signal L minus M = " + (L - M).toFixed(4));

// A 20 by 20 patch at a 2 to 1 L to M ratio, then count isolated M-cones.
const N = 20, mosaic = [];
let mCount = 0;
for (let i = 0; i < N * N; i++) {
  const isL = Math.random() < 2 / 3;
  mosaic.push(isL ? "L" : "M");
  if (!isL) mCount++;
}
let isolated = 0;
for (let r = 1; r < N - 1; r++)
  for (let c = 1; c < N - 1; c++)
    if (mosaic[r * N + c] === "M" &&
        ![mosaic[(r-1)*N+c], mosaic[(r+1)*N+c], mosaic[r*N+c-1], mosaic[r*N+c+1]].includes("M"))
      isolated++;
console.log("M-cones: " + mCount + " of " + N * N + ", isolated: " + isolated);

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

An M-cone cannot be recognised by shape at all, only by the fact that it absorbs about 28 nanometres shorter than its identical-looking neighbour, and that single small difference is the whole of red-green vision.

CHAPTER 196. L-CONES, OR LONG-WAVELENGTH CONES (Long-Wave Red-Catching Cell) (NON-COG)

The cone we call red actually peaks in yellow-green, and it is the most numerous colour detector in your eye by a wide margin.

L is a letter, not a lesson, so the name carries the spectral fact directly.

Long-Wave marks a pigment peaking near 560 to 565 nanometres, the far end of the visible range.

Red-Catching names the percept it serves.

Cell marks it as a photoreceptor, and the name sits deliberately alongside its middle-wave and short-wave siblings so the single dimension separating the three, the wavelength each is tuned to, is visible in all three names at once, this type being the most numerous and outnumbering the middle-wave type roughly two to one in many eyes.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An L-cone is a colour cone tuned to long-wavelength light, peaking near 558 nanometres, which is yellow-green rather than red.

With the M-cone it carries the red-green colour channel and most of the brightness signal that drives acuity and motion.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

An L-cone is structurally identical to an M-cone, with the same tapering outer segment, mitochondrial ellipsoid and broad flat pedicle, and no microscope can separate the two.

SIZE:

The standard cone dimensions: about 2.0 to 2.5 micrometres wide in the fovea, 5 to 8 micrometres in the periphery, with outer segments of 10 to 50 micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two genuine spectral variants exist, because position 180 of the OPN1LW protein carries either serine or alanine, and the alanine version peaks about 4 nanometres shorter.

WHERE IT LIVES:

L-cones are present from the exact centre of the foveola, where they and M-cones alone are packed at up to about 199,000 cones per square millimetre, out to the ora serrata at the retinal edge.

NUMBER:

Taking about 93 percent of cones as L or M and an average L to M ratio near two to one gives roughly 2.9 million L-cones per eye and about 5.7 million across both eyes.

Confidence is low for any individual, because measured ratios in colour-normal men span 1.1 to 1 up to 16.5 to 1, so some people carry over four million L-cones in one eye.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

An L-cone behaves like any cone, resting near minus 40 millivolts in darkness with a flash response peaking in 20 to 50 milliseconds, and differs only in which photons it absorbs.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

In the central retina one L-cone drives a private pair of midget bipolar cells, one ON and one OFF, which in turn drive single midget ganglion cells, giving a labelled line from one cone to the brain.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A short, thick, unmyelinated, passively conducting cone axon, swept sideways into the fibre layer of Henle wherever the fovea has displaced the inner retina.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, released tonically in darkness, inverted at ON bipolar cells by the mGluR6 receptor and preserved at OFF bipolar cells by AMPA and kainate receptors.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable.

Myelin in the light path would scatter the image, so the retina contains no myelin-forming cells until the optic nerve head.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The same extreme oxidative demand as other cones, supplied by the choroid, with the foveal L-cones especially vulnerable because the foveal avascular zone has no capillaries of its own.

PLASTICITY:

L-cone signals are normalised centrally rather than locally, so people with very different L to M ratios agree on which wavelength looks pure yellow, a striking case of the brain calibrating to its own sensor array.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Standard cone support applies, the retinal pigment epithelium plus the Muller cell cone visual cycle, and nothing about L-cone glia is special; the honest statement is that no L-specific glial specialisation has been demonstrated.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove L-cones and the retina keeps acuity and blue-yellow vision but loses red-green and dims the long-wavelength end of the spectrum, which is protanopia, in which deep reds look almost black.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Spectral tuning in an L-cone is set by three amino acid positions in the opsin protein, sites 180, 277 and 285, whose substitutions shift the peak by roughly 4, 9 and 15 nanometres respectively, a molecular dial for colour.

Only one of the two X-linked opsin genes is expressed per cone, chosen stochastically by a locus control region, so each cone effectively flips a coin during development to decide whether it will be L or M.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: photons 490 to 700 nanometres, peak near 558 nanometres, plus H1 horizontal cell feedback and gap junction current from neighbours.

• OUTPUTS: graded glutamate to private ON and OFF midget bipolar cells centrally, to diffuse bipolar cells, and to H1 horizontal cells.

• STATE: bleached pigment fraction, cascade activation, cyclic guanosine monophosphate level, internal calcium, membrane voltage, adaptation gain.

• PARAMETERS: peak wavelength 555 to 560 nanometres, shifted about 4 nanometres by the serine or alanine at site 180; gene OPN1LW at Xq28; about 62 percent of cones on average.

• TIME CONSTANTS: flash response peak 20 to 50 milliseconds; luminance flicker resolved to about 60 hertz, far faster than the red-green channel it also serves.

• FAILURE MODE: protanomaly and protanopia together affect roughly 2 percent of men, and blue-cone monochromacy, in which the whole L and M array is silenced, affects about 1 in 100,000 and leaves acuity near 20/100 with nystagmus.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to predict a long-wavelength pigment's peak sensitivity from its three spectral tuning sites.
Create a number variable called base peak with initial value 530.
Create a number variable called site 180 shift with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called site 277 shift with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called site 285 shift with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called peak wavelength with initial value 0.
If the residue at site 180 is equal to "Ser", then perform the following actions.
    Set site 180 shift to 4.
End the conditional block.
If the residue at site 277 is equal to "Tyr", then perform the following actions.
    Set site 277 shift to 9.
End the conditional block.
If the residue at site 285 is equal to "Thr", then perform the following actions.
    Set site 285 shift to 15.
End the conditional block.
Calculate the sum of base peak and site 180 shift.
Set peak wavelength to that sum.
Calculate the sum of peak wavelength and site 277 shift.
Set peak wavelength to that sum.
Calculate the sum of peak wavelength and site 285 shift.
Set peak wavelength to that sum.
Display peak wavelength to the console.
Calculate the difference of peak wavelength and 530.
If that difference is less than 12, then perform the following actions.
    Display "red-green discrimination will be anomalous" to the console.
End the conditional block.
Return peak wavelength to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the model is a small lookup and a sum, where a reader
# checking the biology matters far more than execution speed.
BASE = 530.0                       # the middle-wavelength backbone, nanometres
SHIFTS = {("180", "Ser"): 4.0, ("180", "Ala"): 0.0,
          ("277", "Tyr"): 9.0, ("277", "Phe"): 0.0,
          ("285", "Thr"): 15.0, ("285", "Ala"): 0.0}

def lambda_max(s180, s277, s285):
    """Peak sensitivity from the three tuning sites of the opsin protein."""
    total = BASE
    for site, residue in (("180", s180), ("277", s277), ("285", s285)):
        total += SHIFTS[(site, residue)]
    return total

PIGMENTS = {"normal L, serine 180": ("Ser", "Tyr", "Thr"),
            "normal L, alanine 180": ("Ala", "Tyr", "Thr"),
            "normal M": ("Ala", "Phe", "Ala"),
            "anomalous L-M hybrid": ("Ser", "Tyr", "Ala")}

for name, residues in PIGMENTS.items():
    print("%-22s peak = %5.1f nm" % (name, lambda_max(*residues)))

normal_gap = lambda_max("Ser", "Tyr", "Thr") - lambda_max("Ala", "Phe", "Ala")
anom_gap = lambda_max("Ser", "Tyr", "Ala") - lambda_max("Ala", "Phe", "Ala")
print("normal L minus M separation: %.1f nm" % normal_gap)
print("anomalous separation:        %.1f nm" % anom_gap)
print("red-green sensitivity retained: %.0f%%" % (100.0 * anom_gap / normal_gap))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the most common cone, invisible to anatomy, defined entirely by three amino acids on an X-linked gene, and it is the reason a ripe strawberry looks different from its leaves.

CHAPTER 197. ROD BIPOLAR CELLS (COG)

This small cell throws away most of what it hears, and that act of deliberate deafness is what lets you see by starlight.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A rod bipolar cell is the single relay that carries the rod signal into the inner retina.

It performs two computations: it flips the sign of the rod message, so that light becomes excitation, and it applies a threshold that discards the constant chemical noise of the rods while keeping the rare genuine single-photon events.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is a small, neat cell with a bushy dendritic tuft that pushes up into the invaginations of many rod spherules, a compact cell body at the outer edge of the inner nuclear layer, and a long axon ending in swollen terminals at the very bottom of the inner plexiform layer.

SIZE:

Cell body about 7 to 10 micrometres across, dendritic field 10 to 30 micrometres wide, and an axon 40 to 80 micrometres long crossing the whole inner plexiform layer.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Classically there is exactly one rod bipolar type, which is unusual in a retina with a dozen cone bipolar types; recent single-cell sequencing hints at internal heterogeneity, but the evidence for true human subtypes is weak and should be treated as unresolved.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The cell body sits in the outermost tier of the inner nuclear layer, the dendrites reach up into the outer plexiform layer to meet rod spherules, and the axon terminals occupy sublamina 5, the innermost stratum of the inner plexiform layer.

NUMBER:

Rod to rod bipolar convergence runs from about 10 to 1 near the fovea to about 30 to 1 far out, and peak density in primate retina is 15,000 to 20,000 cells per square millimetre at 1 to 3 millimetres from the centre.

Dividing 92 million rods by an average convergence near 20 gives roughly 4 to 5 million rod bipolar cells per eye, so 8 to 10 million across both eyes, which is about 13 percent of the bipolar budget.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The rod bipolar cell does not fire action potentials in the usual sense; it holds a graded membrane potential near minus 45 to minus 55 millivolts and depolarises by 10 to 25 millivolts in response to light.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Its dendrites invaginate 15 to 45 rod spherules, and its axon terminals form ribbon synapses onto two amacrine cells: the glycine-releasing AII cell and the GABA-releasing A17 cell, which feeds inhibition straight back onto the same terminal.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A single thin unmyelinated axon of 40 to 80 micrometres that conducts passively and ends in two to four bulbous terminals, each holding several ribbons.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The cell releases glutamate from its terminals, and receives glutamate at its dendrites through mGluR6, a metabotropic receptor that inverts the signal, plus GABA at its terminals through GABA-A and GABA-C receptors from A17 cells.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable.

Its entire axon lies inside the retina, which contains no myelin-forming oligodendrocytes, and at 40 to 80 micrometres there would be nothing to gain from insulating it.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate by retinal standards and far below a photoreceptor's, though continuous graded transmitter release at ribbon synapses makes the axon terminal the cell's main energy sink, with mitochondria clustered there.

PLASTICITY:

The threshold at the rod-to-bipolar synapse is not fixed; it shifts with background light so that the cell stops discarding signals when photons become plentiful, an adaptive change in the computation itself.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Muller cell processes wrap the rod bipolar axon terminals tightly and clear released glutamate through the EAAT1 transporter, which matters more here than elsewhere because these terminals release continuously rather than in bursts.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove rod bipolar cells and the rods still work perfectly but nothing downstream ever hears them, so the inner retina becomes purely a daylight machine and night vision vanishes completely.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its defining trick is a nonlinear threshold applied to each rod input separately before summation, which is essential because 20 noisy rods pooled linearly would drown the one rod that actually caught a photon.

The signal passes through a G protein cascade rather than a direct ion channel, which is why the rod pathway is slow, and reciprocal inhibition from A17 amacrine cells shapes each terminal locally without involving the cell body.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from 15 to 45 rod spherules at 0 to 100 micromolar in the cleft, and GABA feedback from A17 amacrine cells.

• OUTPUTS: graded glutamate from two to four ribbon terminals onto AII and A17 amacrine cells in sublamina 5.

• STATE: mGluR6 occupancy, G protein alpha-o activation, TRPM1 open fraction, membrane voltage, terminal calcium, releasable vesicle pool.

• PARAMETERS: convergence 15 to 45 rods; resting potential minus 45 to minus 55 millivolts; light response 10 to 25 millivolts; threshold near 1 single-photon response; density 15,000 to 20,000 per square millimetre at peak.

• TIME CONSTANTS: response onset 20 to 60 milliseconds and time to peak 100 to 200 milliseconds, set by the G protein cascade; threshold readjusts over seconds to minutes with background light.

• FAILURE MODE: complete congenital stationary night blindness from mutations in NYX, TRPM1, GRM6, GPR179 or LRIT3, with NYX and TRPM1 together accounting for over three quarters of cases, and the same signature appears in melanoma-associated retinopathy when antibodies attack TRPM1.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert many noisy rod inputs into one rod bipolar cell current using a threshold before summation.
Create a number variable called threshold with initial value 1.0.
Create a number variable called steepness with initial value 0.18.
Create a number variable called maximum conductance with initial value 60.
Create a number variable called half opening drive with initial value 8.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 6.
Create a number variable called working value with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called conductance with initial value 0.
For each rod signal in the list of rod inputs, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the difference of rod signal and threshold.
    Set working value to that difference.
    Calculate the quotient of working value divided by steepness.
    Set working value to that quotient.
    Set working value to the logistic function of working value.
    Calculate the sum of drive and working value.
    Set drive to that sum.
End the loop.
Calculate the sum of drive and half opening drive.
Set working value to that sum.
Calculate the quotient of drive divided by working value.
Set working value to that quotient.
Calculate the product of working value and maximum conductance.
Set conductance to that product.
Display conductance to the console.
Return conductance to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the claim here is statistical, and the standard library
# gives the random draws and arithmetic needed to prove it in a few lines.
import math, random

N_RODS = 25        # rods converging on one rod bipolar cell in mid periphery
NOISE_SD = 0.30    # continuous dark noise of one rod, in single-photon units
THETA = 1.00       # half point of the synaptic threshold, same units
SLOPE = 0.18       # steepness of that threshold
OFFSET = 6.0       # tonic drive so the model never runs below zero
G_MAX = 60.0       # maximum TRPM1 conductance, picosiemens
K_HALF = 8.0       # drive that opens half the TRPM1 channels
FLASH = 2.0        # mean photoisomerisations shared across the 25 rods

def synapse(x, thresholded):
    if not thresholded:
        return x
    return 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp(-(x - THETA) / SLOPE))

def trial(mean_photons, thresholded):
    drive = OFFSET
    for _ in range(N_RODS):
        hit = 1 if random.random() < mean_photons / N_RODS else 0
        drive += synapse(hit + random.gauss(0.0, NOISE_SD), thresholded)
    return G_MAX * drive / (drive + K_HALF)

def stats(v):
    m = sum(v) / len(v)
    return m, sum((x - m) ** 2 for x in v) / (len(v) - 1)

def d_prime(thresholded, n=4000):
    md, vd = stats([trial(0.0, thresholded) for _ in range(n)])
    ml, vl = stats([trial(FLASH, thresholded) for _ in range(n)])
    return (ml - md) / math.sqrt(0.5 * (vd + vl))

random.seed(7)
print("linear synapse      d-prime = %.2f" % d_prime(False))
print("thresholded synapse d-prime = %.2f" % d_prime(True))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only bipolar cell that touches rods and nothing else, the only one stained strongly by protein kinase C alpha, and the only one whose axon reaches the innermost stripe of the inner plexiform layer to hand its signal to an amacrine cell rather than to a ganglion cell.

CHAPTER 198. CONE BIPOLAR CELLS (COG)

Every cone in your eye hands its signal to a committee of about ten different relay cells, and each member hears the same whisper and files a different report.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A cone bipolar cell is a second-order neuron, meaning the first cell in the chain after the light-catching photoreceptor, and its job is not to relay but to split, fanning one cone's voltage into parallel channels that separately carry brightening, darkening, fine detail, coarse motion and colour.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body sits in the inner nuclear layer, the middle band of the retina, with a dendritic bush above that ends either inside a cone's terminal, called an invaginating contact, or flat against its base, called a basal contact, a difference that predicts the cell's whole physiology.

SIZE:

The cell body is 5 to 10 micrometres across, about one tenth the width of a human hair, the whole cell stands 30 to 70 micrometres tall, dendritic fields run from about 5 to 100 micrometres, and each cell carries 15 to 100 output ribbon synapses.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Primate anatomy defines flat midget bipolar, invaginating midget bipolar, diffuse types DB1, DB2, DB3a, DB3b, DB4, DB5 and DB6, the blue-cone bipolar and the giant bipolar, about eleven to twelve cone-driven types, and human gene-expression atlases recover a comparable set of roughly fourteen bipolar clusters once the rod bipolar cell is included.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The soma occupies the outer half of the inner nuclear layer from the foveal slope to the ora serrata at the retina's edge, with dendrites in the outer plexiform layer and terminals in the inner plexiform layer, so this is the only neuron holding a synapse in each of the retina's two wiring layers at once.

NUMBER:

About 15 to 18 million cone bipolar cells per eye, roughly 30 to 36 million across both eyes, which fits inside the retinal budget of about 35 million bipolar cells per eye once rod bipolar cells are added, and amounts to about 0.04 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the human nervous system.

Confidence is moderate, because the count is inferred from cone density and convergence ratios rather than from a direct census of human tissue.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

It fires no action potentials at all, signalling instead with graded voltage over a range of 10 to 20 millivolts around a resting level near minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, with input resistance in the gigaohm range and a membrane time constant of 10 to 30 milliseconds.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from cone ribbon synapses and from horizontal cells delivering an inhibitory surround with gamma-aminobutyric acid, the brain's main inhibitory chemical, while outputs are dyads, meaning each release site faces two partners at once, usually one ganglion cell and one amacrine cell.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is short, 20 to 40 micrometres, thinner than 1 micrometre, and so brief that voltage spreads down it passively with no action potential required.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Every cone bipolar cell releases glutamate, the main excitatory messenger of the nervous system, continuously rather than in bursts, packaged by vesicular glutamate transporter type 1 and triggered by L-type calcium channels of the Cav1.3 and Cav1.4 families clustered at the ribbon.

MYELINATION:

None, because there are no oligodendrocytes, the myelin-making cells, anywhere inside the retina, and myelin begins only behind the lamina cribrosa where the optic nerve leaves the eye.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high, since the retina consumes more oxygen per gram than any other human tissue, about ninety percent of its adenosine triphosphate, the cell's energy currency, goes to pumping ions, and these cells can burn extracellular lactate when glucose is short.

PLASTICITY:

Gain is reset continuously across the ten-billion-fold range of natural light levels, mainly at the ribbon and by dopamine acting as a daylight signal, while in retinitis pigmentosa the dendrites retract and sprout into the wrong layer, a slow structural rewriting called retinal remodelling.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

There are no astrocytes here at all, since retinal astrocytes stay in the nerve fibre layer, so the whole job falls to the Muller cell, a radial glial cell that mops up glutamate with the transporter GLAST, converts it to glutamine, hands it straight back for reuse, buffers potassium through Kir4.1 channels and delivers lactate.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this class and the retina still catches light and still has output neurons but has no channels, so there is no brightening and no darkening signal, no fine and coarse streams, no colour opponency, and the electroretinogram loses its b-wave, the upward deflection reporting inner retinal function.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The dendritic tip is a computer in its own right, inverting the sign of the message through a metabotropic receptor, and horizontal cells feed back partly by ephaptic action, an electrical field effect through the extracellular space with no transmitter at all.

ON terminals are electrically fused to AII amacrine cells by connexin36 gap junctions, terminals carry the transporter EAAT5 whose chloride current gives feedback within a millisecond, and calcium spikes occur in terminals even though the soma never fires.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: cone glutamate in the cleft, normalised 0 to 1, from 1 to 14 named pedicles; horizontal cell feedback in millivolts; amacrine inhibitory conductance.

• OUTPUTS: graded glutamate release, 0 to 1 of maximum, onto named ganglion and amacrine dyad partners at a specified inner plexiform layer stratum.

• STATE: membrane voltage; receptor or cascade activation; slow adaptation average; terminal calcium; releasable vesicle pool.

• PARAMETERS: convergence 1 to 14 cones; polarity sign minus 1 for ON and plus 1 for OFF; gain 1 to 2; ribbon count 15 to 100.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 30 milliseconds; synaptic transfer 1 to 20 milliseconds; adaptation 100 to 1000 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of transmission from cones gives a negative electroretinogram with no b-wave, and dendritic retraction in retinitis pigmentosa disconnects the surviving inner retina from any prosthesis placed in front of it.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn the voltages of the cones above a cone bipolar cell into one graded output voltage.
Create a number variable called centre_total with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called cone_count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called gap with initial value 0.
For each cone_voltage in contacted_cones, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the sum of centre_total and cone_voltage.
    Set centre_total to the result.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in contacted_cones.
Set cone_count to the result.
Calculate the quotient of centre_total divided by cone_count.
Set drive to the result.
Calculate the difference of drive and surround_voltage.
Set drive to the result.
Calculate the product of drive and signed_gain.
Set drive to the result.
Calculate the sum of resting_voltage and drive.
Set drive to the result.
Calculate the difference of drive and membrane_voltage.
Set gap to the result.
Calculate the product of gap and smoothing_fraction.
Set gap to the result.
Calculate the sum of membrane_voltage and gap.
Set membrane_voltage to the result.
Display membrane_voltage to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the whole cell is one low-pass filter plus one release curve.

import math

def cone_bipolar(cone_mv, surround_mv, sign=-1.0, gain=1.5, tau=20.0, dt=1.0):
    """Graded relay: no spikes, one membrane filter, one release curve."""
    v_rest, v, out = -55.0, -55.0, []
    for c, s in zip(cone_mv, surround_mv):
        # sign = -1 for ON types, +1 for OFF types
        v_target = v_rest + sign * gain * (c - s)
        v += (v_target - v) * (dt / tau)
        out.append((round(v, 2), round(1 / (1 + math.exp(-(v + 45) / 4)), 4)))
    return out

if __name__ == "__main__":
    cone = [-40.0] * 50 + [-55.0] * 150   # darkness, then light
    surround = [-40.0] * 200              # horizontal cell local average
    trace = cone_bipolar(cone, surround)
    print("dark:", trace[40], "light:", trace[-1])

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A small cell in the middle band of the retina with a dendritic tuft above and a varicose knob below, no myelin, no action potential, a ribbon at its output, and a glial partner that recycles its transmitter and hands it back.

CHAPTER 199. ON BIPOLAR CELLS (Brightening-Signalling Neuron) (COG)

This cell turns a message upside down, because the cone shouts loudest in the dark, and the ON bipolar cell answers by falling silent, then wakes the instant light arrives.

ON is a two-letter laboratory shorthand that has to be explained every time it is used, so the name says the event out loud.

Brightening names the moment the cell owns, the instant light on its patch of retina increases.

Signalling names the work of passing that increase inward.

Neuron marks it as a bipolar relay cell, and the name quietly records the trick behind it, because these cells carry a receptor called metabotropic glutamate receptor six that flips the sign of the photoreceptor's message, so a fall in transmitter from the cone becomes a rise in this cell's response.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An ON bipolar cell depolarises, meaning its voltage rises towards zero, when light increases, which is remarkable because cones do the opposite and release less glutamate in light, so this cell must invert the sign of its input and in doing so creates the brightening channel of vision.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Its dendrites end as invaginating contacts, pushing deep inside the cone terminal to sit beside the release ribbon, and its axon always ends in the inner half of the inner plexiform layer, the wiring band nearest the output neurons, a zone called sublamina b.

SIZE:

Soma 5 to 10 micrometres and cell height 30 to 70 micrometres, with terminals in macaque retina stratifying about 11.1 micrometres from the ganglion cell layer for DB4, 11.4 for DB5, 6.0 for the invaginating midget bipolar and only 2.3 for DB6, carrying about 68 to 98 ribbons each.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The cone-driven ON types are the invaginating midget bipolar, DB4, DB5, DB6, the blue-cone bipolar and the giant bipolar, and the rod bipolar cell is also an ON cell using the same molecular machinery, which is why one genetic fault can darken both day and night vision.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Soma in the inner nuclear layer, dendrites inside cone pedicles in the outer plexiform layer, and terminals restricted to sublamina b, so the interface that matters is the boundary between sublamina a and sublamina b, an invisible line separating the darkening half of the retina from the brightening half.

NUMBER:

Roughly 7 to 9 million cone-driven ON bipolar cells per eye, about 14 to 18 million across both eyes, rising to perhaps 11 to 13 million per eye if rod bipolar cells are counted as ON cells, which they are, and amounting to about 0.02 percent of the human nervous system.

Confidence is moderate, because the figure comes from convergence ratios rather than a direct human count.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Graded with no somatic action potentials, resting near minus 55 to minus 60 millivolts in darkness, depolarising by 10 to 20 millivolts in light, and lagging the OFF cell by roughly 5 to 15 milliseconds because a chain of proteins must switch off before the channel can open.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input comes from cone ribbons at invaginating positions, output goes to ON parasol and ON midget ganglion cells with DB4 supplying roughly half the bipolar input to ON parasol cells and DB6 sending about 70 percent of its ribbon contacts to ON midget cells, and terminals are coupled to AII amacrine cells by connexin36 gap junctions.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Short, thin, unmyelinated, and defined entirely by which sublayer it stops in.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases glutamate, but the interesting chemistry is on the receiving side, where metabotropic glutamate receptor 6, a receptor working through an internal messenger rather than opening a pore itself, drives the G protein Gao with beta3 and gamma13 subunits, which closes the channel TRPM1.

MYELINATION:

None, since no oligodendrocytes exist inside the retina and no retinal neuron of any kind is myelinated.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High, with the unusual burden that the cascade proteins mGluR6, TRPM1, nyctalopin, GPR179, LRIT3 and the regulators RGS7 and RGS11 must be continuously trafficked to and maintained at the dendritic tips.

PLASTICITY:

The cascade is this cell's main site of light adaptation, with regulator of G protein signalling proteins speeding it in bright light so the cell does not saturate.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Muller cells wrap the dendritic tips and clear glutamate fast enough that cleft concentration can fall within milliseconds, which is exactly what this cell needs, since its signal is the fall in glutamate rather than its rise, so if Muller clearance fails the ON cell is blinded even though every cone is healthy.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove ON bipolar cells and the retina still sees shadows but never sees light arrive, so white text on a black screen becomes unreadable, contrast sensitivity halves, and the electroretinogram loses its b-wave entirely, leaving the negative waveform clinicians recognise instantly.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell is a biochemical amplifier rather than a summing junction, and TRPM1 is thought to be open by default and actively held shut by G protein activity, so the ON cell in darkness strains against its own machinery.

It couples electrically through connexin36, receives ephaptic feedback from horizontal cells, and depends on a scaffold protein, nyctalopin, that physically anchors TRPM1 in place.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: cleft glutamate from 1 to 14 cones, normalised 0 to 1; horizontal cell feedback in millivolts; AII amacrine junctional current in picoamperes.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto ON ganglion cells and amacrine cells in sublamina b, 0 to 1 of maximum.

• STATE: fraction of active Gao; TRPM1 open probability; membrane voltage; terminal calcium.

• PARAMETERS: half-activation glutamate constant near 0.2 normalised units; voltage swing 15 to 20 millivolts; convergence 1 to 14 cones; ribbon count 59 to 98.

• TIME CONSTANTS: cascade 20 to 50 milliseconds; membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; extra latency versus the OFF pathway 5 to 15 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: mutations in GRM6, TRPM1, NYX, GPR179 or LRIT3 cause complete congenital stationary night blindness, and in melanoma-associated retinopathy antibodies against TRPM1 enter these cells and flatten the electroretinogram b-wave.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute one time step of the sign-inverting cascade that makes an ON bipolar cell depolarise when light reduces the glutamate arriving from its cone.
Create a number variable called gap with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called occupancy with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called open_probability with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called target_voltage with initial value 0.
Calculate the sum of cleft_glutamate and half_activation_constant.
Set gap to the result.
Calculate the quotient of cleft_glutamate divided by gap.
Set occupancy to the result.
Calculate the difference of occupancy and active_g_protein.
Set gap to the result.
Calculate the product of gap and cascade_fraction.
Set gap to the result.
Calculate the sum of active_g_protein and gap.
Set active_g_protein to the result.
Calculate the difference of 1 and active_g_protein.
Set open_probability to the result.
Calculate the product of open_probability and voltage_swing.
Set target_voltage to the result.
Calculate the sum of dark_voltage and target_voltage.
Set target_voltage to the result.
Display target_voltage to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because the cascade is a small fixed-size state machine, and C makes the per-time-step cost of simulating ten million of them explicit.

#include <stdio.h>

int main(void) {
    double glut = 1.0, g_on = 0.9, v = -60.0;  /* glutamate, Gao, millivolts */
    const double dt = 0.5, tau_g = 30.0, tau_m = 15.0, g_leak = 1.0;
    int t;
    for (t = 0; t < 400; t++) {
        if (t == 100) glut = 0.1;              /* light: cone stops releasing */
        double occ = glut / (glut + 0.2);      /* mGluR6 occupancy */
        g_on += (occ - g_on) * (dt / tau_g);   /* cascade lags behind */
        double p_open = 1.0 - g_on;            /* active Gao shuts TRPM1 */
        double vt = (p_open * 0.0 + g_leak * -70.0) / (p_open + g_leak);
        v += (vt - v) * (dt / tau_m);
        if (t % 100 == 0)
            printf("t=%6.1f ms  open=%.3f  V=%.1f mV\n", t * dt, p_open, v);
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only cell in the visual pathway that inverts the sign of its input with a metabotropic receptor, its dendrites go inside the cone terminal rather than resting against it, and its failure gives a person normal eyes, normal cones and no b-wave.

CHAPTER 200. OFF BIPOLAR CELLS (Darkening-Signalling Neuron) (COG)

This is the cell that reports the arrival of darkness, and it is the faster twin, because it does not have to switch off a chain of proteins before it can answer.

OFF is a barcode label, and worse, it sounds like an absence of activity when in fact this cell is firing hardest when the world dims.

Darkening names the event it reports, a fall in light on its patch of retina.

Signalling matches its brightening partner so the one dimension separating the pair, the direction of the light change, is the only thing that differs in the two names.

Neuron marks it as a bipolar relay cell whose biology differs in one step, since it uses ordinary sign-conserving ionotropic glutamate receptors of the kainate and AMPA families and so simply passes the photoreceptor's message on unflipped.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An OFF bipolar cell hyperpolarises, meaning its voltage falls further from zero, when light increases, conserving the sign of the cone's message because it reads glutamate through an ionotropic receptor, a receptor that is itself a pore and opens the instant glutamate binds.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Its dendrites end as flat or basal contacts on the underside of the cone terminal, outside the invagination and further from the release site, and its axon terminates in the outer half of the inner plexiform layer, called sublamina a, farthest from the ganglion cells.

SIZE:

Soma 5 to 10 micrometres, with dendritic fields from about 5 micrometres for the flat midget bipolar on one cone to 30 to 60 micrometres for DB1 and DB2 in the periphery, and the DB1 terminal sitting roughly 20 to 25 micrometres from the ganglion cell layer in central retina.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Five types are recognised in macaque and human retina, the flat midget bipolar, DB1, DB2, DB3a and DB3b, and they split by speed, since DB2 and DB3b express the kainate receptor subunit GluK1 and respond transiently while flat midget bipolar, DB1 and DB3a lack GluK1 and respond in a sustained way.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Soma in the inner nuclear layer and terminals confined to sublamina a, with the critical interface at the base of the cone pedicle, where triad-associated contacts sit near the ribbon and non-triad-associated contacts sit further away, and roughly three quarters of the basal synapses on DB2 and DB3b dendrites are of the distant, non-triad kind.

NUMBER:

Roughly 7 to 9 million per eye, about 14 to 18 million across both eyes, or about 0.02 percent of the human nervous system, with the ON and OFF populations close to equal.

Confidence is moderate, because the figure is inferred from convergence ratios rather than counted in human tissue.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Graded with no somatic spikes in most types, resting near minus 45 to minus 50 millivolts in darkness because the ionotropic channels are already partly open, and answering a step of light roughly 5 to 15 milliseconds sooner than the ON partner because there is no cascade to unwind.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input comes from cones through kainate receptors, and notably all five macaque OFF types use kainate rather than AMPA receptors, while output goes to OFF parasol and OFF midget ganglion cells and to AII amacrine cells, which send glycine, an inhibitory transmitter, back onto them at night.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Short and unmyelinated, ending high in the inner plexiform layer, with stratification depth the single best predictor of which ganglion cell it will talk to.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases glutamate and receives glutamate through kainate receptors built from GluK1, GluK2 and GluK3 subunits, with GluK1 at the distant non-triad contacts and GluK2 or GluK3 at the triad-associated ones, which is how one cone hands out fast and slow copies of the same message.

MYELINATION:

None, since myelin-forming oligodendrocytes are absent from the retina entirely.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High, with the added cost that kainate receptors desensitise heavily and must be continuously recycled and resensitised.

PLASTICITY:

Desensitisation and recovery is itself a fast gain control working over tens to hundreds of milliseconds, and in photoreceptor degeneration OFF dendrites retract earlier and more severely than ON dendrites in several animal models.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Muller glia matter here in a specific way, because these receptors sit far from the ribbon at non-triad contacts, so the glutamate reaching them has already diffused through extracellular space and been partly captured by Muller transporters, making the glial cell a spatial filter that sets how much of the cone's signal they ever see.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete OFF bipolar cells and the retina can see a light switched on but not switched off, so dark text on white paper vanishes, spotting a dark shape against a bright sky becomes impossible, and the retina loses the faster of its two answers.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Receptor desensitisation converts a step into a pulse, which is genuine temporal computation happening inside a protein rather than in a circuit, and receptor placement at triad or non-triad positions means the cell computes with diffusion distance, a purely geometric variable.

Some OFF types generate tetrodotoxin-sensitive sodium spikes in the axon terminal, so a subset uses both graded voltage and all-or-nothing events.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: cleft glutamate from 1 to 14 cones, normalised 0 to 1, sampled at the receptor's diffusion distance; horizontal cell feedback; AII glycinergic conductance.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto OFF ganglion cells and amacrine cells in sublamina a, 0 to 1 of maximum.

• STATE: open receptor conductance; fraction of receptors not desensitised; membrane voltage; terminal calcium.

• PARAMETERS: desensitisation strength 0.3 to 0.6; opening time constant about 1 millisecond; closing 10 to 20 milliseconds; recovery 40 to 100 milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: opening about 1 millisecond; membrane about 10 milliseconds; desensitisation recovery 40 to 100 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: no isolated human disease removes only OFF bipolar cells, but mutations in CACNA1F, which cause incomplete congenital stationary night blindness, degrade transmission from cones to both ON and OFF cells.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

Create a number variable called gap with initial value 0.

Create a number variable called target_voltage with initial value 0.
Calculate the product of cleft_glutamate and available_receptors.
Set target_conductance to the result.
Calculate the difference of target_conductance and open_conductance.
Set gap to the result.
Calculate the product of gap and opening_fraction.
Set gap to the result.
Calculate the sum of open_conductance and gap.
Set open_conductance to the result.
Calculate the product of cleft_glutamate and desensitisation_strength.
Set available_target to the result.
Calculate the difference of 1 and available_target.
Set available_target to the result.
Calculate the difference of available_target and available_receptors.
Set gap to the result.
Calculate the product of gap and recovery_fraction.
Set gap to the result.
Calculate the sum of available_receptors and gap.
Set available_receptors to the result.
Calculate the product of open_conductance and voltage_swing.
Set target_voltage to the result.
Calculate the sum of hyperpolarised_voltage and target_voltage.
Set target_voltage to the result.
Display target_voltage to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the whole point is a two-state receptor, and the desensitisation term fits on one readable line.

def off_bipolar(glut, dt=0.5, t_open=1.0, t_close=15.0, t_rec=60.0, desens=0.5):
    """Kainate desensitisation turns a step of darkness into a pulse."""
    avail, g, v, out = 1.0, 0.0, -50.0, []
    for x in glut:
        target = avail * x
        g += (target - g) * (dt / (t_open if target > g else t_close))
        avail += ((1.0 - desens * x) - avail) * (dt / t_rec)
        # single leak conductance of 1.0 with a reversal of -70 mV
        v += (((g * 0.0) + (1.0 * -70.0)) / (g + 1.0) - v) * (dt / 10.0)
        out.append((round(g, 3), round(v, 1)))
    return out

if __name__ == "__main__":
    tr = off_bipolar([1.0] * 200 + [0.1] * 200 + [1.0] * 200)  # dark, light, dark
    print("dark:", tr[199], "light:", tr[399], "off-peak:", tr[410])

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Flat contacts on the outside of the cone terminal, kainate receptors rather than AMPA receptors, an axon stopping in the outer half of the inner plexiform layer, and a response that beats its ON twin by about ten milliseconds.

CHAPTER 201. MIDGET BIPOLAR CELLS (COG)

In the very centre of your eye one cone gets one private wire to the brain, and this is that wire, the smallest and most exclusive neuron in the human visual system.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A midget bipolar cell contacts a single cone and nothing else, a one-to-one arrangement found nowhere else in the nervous system at this scale, which gives humans acuity of about one sixtieth of a degree and, because its inhibitory surround is built from mixed cones, also gives them red-green colour vision.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A tiny compact dendritic tuft rises to exactly one cone pedicle, and there are two per cone, an invaginating midget bipolar whose dendrites push inside the cone terminal and a flat midget bipolar whose dendrites rest against its base.

SIZE:

The dendritic field is about 5 to 10 micrometres wide, the width of the single pedicle it serves, and the axon arbor is the smallest in the retina at about 68 square micrometres in top view in macaque, roughly 9 micrometres across, yet it carries about 85 output ribbon synapses.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two, the invaginating midget bipolar, an ON cell immunoreactive for cholecystokinin, and the flat midget bipolar, an OFF cell immunoreactive for recoverin.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Soma in the inner nuclear layer, densest on the foveal slope and through the macula, with the invaginating type ending about 6.0 micrometres from the ganglion cell layer in sublamina b and the flat type in sublamina a, so the two halves of one cone's private line sit in different halves of the inner plexiform layer.

NUMBER:

About 7 million per eye and roughly 14 million across both eyes, because in central retina each of the roughly 5 million cones supports two midget bipolar cells, which is about 0.016 percent of the human nervous system.

Density matches cone density out to about 10 millimetres eccentricity then falls to about 60 percent of it, so a far peripheral midget bipolar may contact three cones, and confidence is good because this has been measured directly with recoverin labelling in macaque.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Graded and spikeless, resting near minus 50 millivolts with a light response of about 10 millivolts, and because it is so small its input resistance is several gigaohms, so a handful of channels opening produces a large voltage change and a single cone's vesicle can be heard clearly.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input comes from one cone plus H1 horizontal cells, which build a broad indiscriminate surround from surrounding long-wavelength and middle-wavelength cones, and output goes to a single midget ganglion cell that projects to a single cell in the parvocellular layers of the lateral geniculate nucleus, the visual relay station of the thalamus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Very short, very thin, unmyelinated, and terminating in the tightest arbor in the retina.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, with the invaginating type using metabotropic glutamate receptor 6 and TRPM1 on its input side and the flat type using kainate receptors lacking GluK1, which is why it responds in a sustained rather than transient way.

MYELINATION:

None, since no retinal neuron is myelinated.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high per unit volume, because about 85 release sites are crammed into the smallest terminal in the retina and all release continuously, while the central fovea is avascular, so these cells depend on diffusion from the choroid behind the retina and on Muller cell lactate.

PLASTICITY:

Least plastic in wiring terms, since the one-to-one connection is set during development and defended, but strongly adaptive in gain, and in macular degeneration this population is lost first with no rewiring restoring acuity.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

In the fovea the Muller cells form a cone-shaped plug, the Muller cell cone, whose elongated vertically aligned processes keep light scatter low and shepherd each private line without letting neighbouring signals mix, so here glia serve an optical purpose as well as a metabolic one.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove midget bipolar cells and the fovea stops being a fovea, acuity collapses to something closer to peripheral vision, reading becomes impossible, and red-green colour vision disappears even though every cone and every opsin remains perfectly intact.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Colour opponency is created here rather than in the brain, arising from wiring geometry rather than any colour-selective molecule, because a single cone centre is compared against a mixed-cone surround, and evidence indicates this opponent signal is already present before the midget ganglion cell and carried entirely by modulation of an excitatory conductance.

The cell also depends on horizontal cell feedback that is partly ephaptic, an electrical field effect requiring no transmitter, and on local protein synthesis in a terminal too far from the nucleus to wait for delivery.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: cleft glutamate from exactly one cone centrally, or 1 to 3 cones beyond 10 millimetres eccentricity, normalised 0 to 1; H1 horizontal cell surround voltage.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto one midget ganglion cell and accompanying amacrine cells, 0 to 1 of maximum, at about 85 ribbon sites.

• STATE: membrane voltage; cascade or receptor activation; terminal calcium; vesicle pool.

• PARAMETERS: cone convergence 1; surround weight 0.6 to 0.8; polarity sign minus 1 for invaginating and plus 1 for flat; arbor area about 68 square micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; sustained response with no fast desensitisation; surround feedback 5 to 20 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: loss in age-related macular degeneration and macular dystrophies destroys central acuity permanently, and in retinal remodelling the private line breaks so no downstream prosthesis can reconstruct it.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the colour-opponent output of a midget bipolar cell that listens to exactly one cone in the centre and to a pool of mixed cones in the surround.

    Calculate the sum of surround_total and neighbour_voltage.
    Set surround_total to the result.
End the loop.

Set surround_mean to the result.

Display output_voltage to the console.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because colour opponency here falls out of three lines of arithmetic with no colour term anywhere in the code.

def midget_bipolar(centre_mv, surround_mv, sign=-1.0, w=0.75, gain=1.4):
    """One cone in the centre, a mixed pool of cones in the surround."""
    surround = sum(surround_mv) / len(surround_mv)
    return round(-52.0 + sign * gain * (centre_mv - w * surround), 2)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Light hyperpolarises a cone, so a strongly driven cone sits more
    # negative in millivolts than its less strongly driven neighbours.
    # Long-wavelength light: this M cone centre is barely driven.
    red = midget_bipolar(-40.0, [-52.0, -53.0, -51.0, -52.0, -50.0])
    # Middle-wavelength light: this M cone centre is driven hard.
    green = midget_bipolar(-56.0, [-46.0, -45.0, -47.0, -46.0, -44.0])
    print("ON midget, red light  :", red)
    print("ON midget, green light:", green)
    print("opponent swing, mV    :", round(green - red, 2))
    # No wavelength appears anywhere: opponency comes from wiring alone.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

One cone, one bipolar cell, one ganglion cell, one thalamic cell, all the way to the cortex, with the smallest axon arbor in the retina and an ON and OFF pair sitting on every central cone.

CHAPTER 202. DIFFUSE BIPOLAR CELLS (COG)

If the midget bipolar cell is a soloist, this is the choir, five to fourteen cones pooled into one voice, trading the last of the detail for speed, sensitivity and the ability to see something move.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A diffuse bipolar cell spreads its dendrites across many neighbouring cones and adds their signals, which costs spatial detail but buys signal-to-noise ratio, meaning the true signal stands further above the random flicker of photon capture, and it feeds the fast, colourless, motion-sensitive stream of vision.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A broad flat dendritic canopy spans several cone terminals, contacts are flat for the OFF subtypes and invaginating for the ON subtypes, and the axon terminal sits at a depth in the inner plexiform layer that varies systematically by subtype.

SIZE:

Dendritic fields are about 20 to 40 micrometres across centrally and 70 to 100 micrometres in the far periphery, and in macaque retina mean cone convergence is 8.7 cones for DB4, 7.3 for DB5 and 6.2 for DB6, with total contacts of 39.7, 26.7 and 14.6 and ribbon counts of about 97, 68 and 98.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Seven, since DB1, DB2, DB3a and DB3b are OFF cells in sublamina a while DB4, DB5 and DB6 are ON cells in sublamina b, with DB4 and DB5 both stratifying at about 58 percent of inner plexiform layer depth and DB6 at about 93.6 percent, almost touching the ganglion cell layer.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Soma in the inner nuclear layer across the whole retina, present even at the foveal slope where axons are displaced sideways by the pit, with dendrites in the outer plexiform layer and terminals spread across the full depth of the inner plexiform layer, so the seven types together tile that depth from top to bottom.

NUMBER:

About 6 to 8 million per eye, roughly 12 to 16 million across both eyes, or about 0.017 percent of the human nervous system, derived from cone density divided by convergence and multiplied by seven types, so confidence is moderate.

Because each type independently tiles the retina, every cone is read by all seven.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Graded and mostly spikeless, resting near minus 50 millivolts with responses of 10 to 20 millivolts, though several types are strongly transient, DB2 and DB3b most of all because they express the kainate subunit GluK1, and some types carry voltage-gated sodium channels producing all-or-nothing spikes in the axon terminal.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input comes from 5 to 14 cones weighted by distance, and output goes principally to parasol ganglion cells, the large motion-and-contrast cells feeding the magnocellular layers of the thalamus, with DB4 alone supplying roughly half their bipolar input while DB6 sends about 70 percent of its ribbon contacts to ON midget ganglion cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Unmyelinated and short, with a wide varicose terminal arbor whose stratification depth is the cell's functional identity.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate on output, with ON types using metabotropic glutamate receptor 6 and TRPM1 on input and OFF types using kainate receptors, GluK1 present in DB2 and DB3b and absent in DB1 and DB3a.

MYELINATION:

None, for the standard retinal reason that no myelinating glia exist inside the eye.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High, with the largest terminals in the retina and up to about 98 ribbons apiece releasing continuously, though in vascularised retina these cells can draw glucose and lactate straight from the deep capillary plexus rather than only from Muller cells.

PLASTICITY:

Strong adaptation, since these cells must work from starlight to noon, and their pooled receptive field size changes with light level as horizontal and amacrine cell coupling shifts under dopamine control.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Because their terminals are large and lie deep in the inner plexiform layer beside the deep capillary bed, these cells sit where Muller endfeet, pericytes and capillaries meet and contribute to neurovascular coupling, the local rise in blood flow that follows local activity, while Muller potassium siphoning stops a widely pooled, continuously active terminal from poisoning its neighbourhood.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove diffuse bipolar cells and the retina keeps its acuity and its colour but loses its speed and its sensitivity, so motion detection collapses, dim-light contrast fades, flicker fusion frequency falls, and the magnocellular stream that warns you of a car in your peripheral vision goes quiet.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Pooling is itself a computation, a spatial low-pass filter built from dendritic geometry with distance-dependent weights, layered with a temporal high-pass filter produced by receptor desensitisation and vesicle depletion at the ribbon.

ON diffuse types are electrically coupled to AII amacrine cells by connexin36 gap junctions, making them the entry point for rod signals into daylight circuitry, and their terminals show calcium spikes and are targets of dopaminergic volume transmission, in which a transmitter diffuses broadly rather than crossing a single synapse.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: cleft glutamate from 5 to 14 cones with distance-dependent weights, normalised 0 to 1; horizontal cell surround; amacrine inhibition; AII gap junction current for ON types.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto parasol and midget ganglion cells and amacrine cells at a defined inner plexiform layer depth, 0 to 1 of maximum.

• STATE: pooled cone average; slow adaptation average; membrane voltage; terminal calcium; vesicle pool.

• PARAMETERS: convergence 6.2 to 12.5 cones by type; stratification depth 0 to 100 percent, DB4 and DB5 near 58 percent and DB6 near 93.6 percent; ribbon count 15 to 98.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 15 milliseconds; transient high-pass 50 to 200 milliseconds; adaptation 100 to 1000 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: degeneration of these cells or their parasol targets, as in glaucoma, causes early loss of motion and contrast sensitivity while letter acuity on a chart still reads normal, which is one reason glaucoma is found late.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to pool the weighted signals of several cones into one diffuse bipolar cell and to report only the change in that pooled signal.

    Calculate the sum of weighted_total and weighted_cone_voltage.
    Set weighted_total to the result.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in weighted_cone_voltages.
Set cone_count to the result.
Calculate the quotient of weighted_total divided by cone_count.
Set pooled to the result.
Calculate the difference of pooled and slow_average.
Set gap to the result.
Calculate the product of gap and adaptation_fraction.
Set gap to the result.
Calculate the sum of slow_average and gap.
Set slow_average to the result.
Calculate the difference of pooled and slow_average.
Set transient to the result.
Calculate the product of transient and signed_gain.
Set output_voltage to the result.
Calculate the sum of resting_voltage and output_voltage.
Set output_voltage to the result.
Display output_voltage to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

JavaScript is chosen because a weighted pool followed by an adaptation stage is the per-frame arithmetic a browser-based retina simulator would run, needing no toolchain.

// Diffuse bipolar cell: weighted pooling, then a transient high-pass stage.
function diffuseBipolar(coneVoltages, weights, state, dt = 1.0) {
  let total = 0.0, wsum = 0.0;
  for (let i = 0; i < coneVoltages.length; i++) {
    total += weights[i] * coneVoltages[i];   // distance-weighted pooling
    wsum += weights[i];
  }
  const pooled = total / wsum;                          // mean cone mV
  state.slow += (pooled - state.slow) * (dt / 120.0);   // slow adaptation
  const transient = -1.6 * (pooled - state.slow);       // ON sign inversion
  state.v += (-52.0 + transient - state.v) * (dt / 12.0);
  return state.v;
}

const weights = [1.0, 0.9, 0.9, 0.6, 0.6, 0.4, 0.4];   // seven cones, DB4-like
const state = { slow: -45.0, v: -52.0 };
for (let t = 0; t < 400; t++) {
  const cones = new Array(7).fill(t < 200 ? -45.0 : -58.0);  // light step
  const v = diffuseBipolar(cones, weights, state);
  if (t === 199 || t === 205 || t === 399) console.log(t, v.toFixed(1));
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A wide dendritic canopy over many cones, a terminal parked at a precise depth that names the subtype, a mosaic tiling the retina independently of the other six diffuse types, and a response that fades if the light simply stays on.

CHAPTER 203. BLUE-CONE BIPOLAR CELLS (COG)

Only about one cone in ten is blue-sensitive, they are scattered thinly and absent from the very centre of vision, and this rare neuron reaches past every red and green cone in its way to find them.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The blue-cone bipolar cell contacts short-wavelength-sensitive cones, called S-cones, ignores the long-wavelength and middle-wavelength cones between them, depolarises when blue light increases, and forms the first stage of the blue-yellow colour axis, the oldest colour system in vertebrate evolution.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is sometimes called the wide-axoned S-cone bipolar, because its dendrites travel long distances sideways while skipping neighbours, and its tips make invaginating ribbon junctions deep inside the S-cone terminal before its axon plunges to the very bottom of the inner plexiform layer.

SIZE:

Dendritic field roughly 30 to 80 micrometres wide, far wider than its cone count suggests, and in macaque retina it contacts on average 1.7 cones with a standard deviation of 0.8, makes about 24.1 contacts, carries about 59 output ribbons, and ends about 3.3 micrometres from the ganglion cell layer.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Essentially one ON type in primate retina, since the S-cone OFF pathway is carried mainly by S-cone contacts onto OFF midget bipolar cells and by DB6 contacts rather than by a second dedicated blue-cone bipolar type.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Soma in the inner nuclear layer but absent or vanishingly rare in the central foveola, because S-cones are missing from roughly the central 100 micrometres, about 0.35 degrees of visual angle, and its axon terminates in stratum 5, the innermost stratum of the inner plexiform layer, right at the interface with the ganglion cell layer.

NUMBER:

About 0.4 to 0.5 million per eye and roughly 0.8 to 1.0 million across both eyes, matching S-cone numbers since S-cones are 8 to 10 percent of the roughly 5 million cones per eye, and about 0.001 percent of the human nervous system.

Confidence is good, because an independent count also reports blue-cone bipolar cells as only 1 to 2 percent of the whole bipolar population.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Graded, spikeless, of ON polarity, resting near minus 55 millivolts in darkness, with relatively slow and sustained responses that fit the known sluggishness of human blue-yellow colour perception compared with red-green.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input comes from 1 to 3 S-cones through metabotropic glutamate receptor 6 and TRPM1 plus feed-forward inhibition from HII horizontal cells using gamma-aminobutyric acid, the main inhibitory chemical, and output goes almost exclusively to the inner dendritic tier of the small bistratified ganglion cell, the blue-ON yellow-OFF cell feeding the koniocellular thalamus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Long relative to the cell's size, unmyelinated, and terminating deeper than nearly any other bipolar axon.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate on output, metabotropic glutamate receptor 6 and TRPM1 on input, with gamma-aminobutyric acid arriving from HII horizontal cells.

MYELINATION:

None, as for every neuron inside the retina.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate, lower per cell than the diffuse types because it carries only about 59 ribbons, but sustained because release never stops.

PLASTICITY:

The wiring itself is the plastic marvel, since during development these dendrites must recognise S-cones by molecular identity and refuse every neighbouring cone, a selection process with few parallels in the nervous system.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The Muller cell processes around S-cone pedicles form part of a specialised microenvironment in which the machinery for HII horizontal cell feedback is concentrated, so the glial sheath here helps confine a chemical conversation to a single cone type, and no comparable cone-type-specific glial specialisation is described elsewhere in the outer plexiform layer.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this one cell and the retina loses blue, not blue detection, since S-cones would still absorb short-wavelength light, but the entire blue-yellow opponent comparison, leaving a person who sees a bright world in reds, greens and greys with no way to tell a violet from a grey of the same brightness.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell performs molecular recognition as computation, selecting partners by cone identity rather than proximity, a decision made in development and frozen into the anatomy, and it receives feed-forward inhibition rather than the usual feedback arrangement.

Its output onto the small bistratified ganglion cell is spatially coextensive with an OFF input from diffuse cells, so the blue and yellow receptive fields overlap exactly instead of forming a centre and a surround.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: cleft glutamate from 1 to 3 S-cones within a 30 to 80 micrometre reach, normalised 0 to 1; HII horizontal cell inhibitory conductance.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto small bistratified ganglion cells in stratum 5 of the inner plexiform layer, 0 to 1 of maximum, at about 59 ribbons.

• STATE: fraction of active Gao; TRPM1 open probability; membrane voltage; terminal calcium.

• PARAMETERS: dendritic reach 30 to 80 micrometres; S-cone convergence 1.7 plus or minus 0.8; polarity sign minus 1; total cone contacts about 24.

• TIME CONSTANTS: cascade 20 to 50 milliseconds; membrane 15 to 25 milliseconds; sustained response decaying over hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: this pathway is among the first to weaken in glaucoma and diabetic retinopathy, which is why short-wavelength automated perimetry, a blue-on-yellow visual field test, can detect damage before standard testing does.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to build the input of a blue-cone bipolar cell by contacting only short-wavelength cones and ignoring every other cone within its dendritic reach.
Create a number variable called s_total with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called s_count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called s_mean with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called output_voltage with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called is_short_wavelength with initial value false.
Create a text variable called cone_opsin with initial value unknown.
Create a number variable called cone_voltage with initial value 0.
For each cone_record in nearby_cones, perform the following operations.
    Set cone_opsin to the opsin type of cone_record.
    Set cone_voltage to the voltage of cone_record.
    Check if cone_opsin is equal to short.
    Set is_short_wavelength to the result.
    If is_short_wavelength is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of s_total and cone_voltage.
        Set s_total to the result.
        Calculate the sum of s_count and 1.
        Set s_count to the result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Calculate the quotient of s_total divided by s_count.
Set s_mean to the result.
Calculate the difference of s_mean and dark_reference_voltage.
Set output_voltage to the result.
Calculate the product of output_voltage and signed_gain.
Set output_voltage to the result.
Display output_voltage to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the essential operation is a filter over a list of typed neighbours, and dictionaries make cone identity readable at a glance.

def blue_cone_bipolar(cones, reach=60.0, sign=-1.0, gain=1.3, dark_ref=-45.0):
    """Skip every L and M cone within reach and wire only to S cones."""
    chosen = [c for c in cones if c["type"] == "S" and c["um"] <= reach]
    if not chosen:
        return None    # no S cone in range, so no such cell forms here
    centre = sum(c["mv"] for c in chosen) / len(chosen)
    return round(-54.0 + sign * gain * (centre - dark_ref), 2), len(chosen)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    # One dendritic tree's neighbourhood under blue light. Light
    # hyperpolarises a cone, so driven S cones sit more negative.
    patch = [
        {"type": "L", "um": 6.0,  "mv": -45.0},
        {"type": "M", "um": 9.0,  "mv": -45.0},
        {"type": "S", "um": 22.0, "mv": -60.0},
        {"type": "S", "um": 48.0, "mv": -58.0},
        {"type": "M", "um": 70.0, "mv": -45.0},
    ]
    print(blue_cone_bipolar(patch))   # depolarised output, 2 S cones used

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Long dendrites that visibly bypass their nearest neighbours, an axon diving to the innermost stratum of the inner plexiform layer, a partner list of exactly one ganglion cell type, and a population of only 1 to 2 percent of all bipolar cells.

CHAPTER 204. GIANT BIPOLAR CELLS (Largest-Spread Two-Ended Neuron) (COG)

There is a cell in the middle of your retina wide enough to reach a hundred of the light detectors above it, and it deliberately ignores about half of the ones it can touch.

Giant is a size word that ranks the cell without saying what the size buys, so the honest fix keeps the one solid measured fact and states it plainly.

Largest-Spread names the established finding, that among primate cone bipolar cells this type has the widest dendritic and axonal fields of all, exceeded by nothing except unusually large examples of the diffuse bipolar type that feeds melanopsin ganglion cells.

Two-Ended keeps the bipolar relay architecture visible in plain words.

Neuron marks it as a cell whose downstream job is genuinely not settled, since it is suspected to feed the giant melanopsin ganglion cells but that contact has not been demonstrated, so the name deliberately claims nothing beyond reach.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A bipolar cell is a small relay neuron in the middle layer of the retina, the thin sheet of nerve tissue lining the back of the eye.

It takes the electrical signal from the light detectors, called photoreceptors, and passes it inward toward the cells that send messages to the brain.

The giant bipolar cell is the widest member of that relay family in monkey and human retina.

It was first drawn by Anthony Mariani in 1983 in the rhesus monkey, where he called it the giant bistratified bipolar cell because its output branch ends in two separate depths.

A second and closely related giant form was described in 2011 by Joo, Peterson, Dacey, Buck and Haverkamp as an eleventh bipolar type with a very large, sparsely branched input tree.

Its striking habit is choosiness.

Instead of collecting from every cone in reach, it contacts on average about 52 percent of them, with a measured range from 27 to 82 percent in six reconstructed cells.

Functionally it is therefore a wide, sparse, coarse sampler: a channel that reports the average brightness of a patch of retina rather than fine detail.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body sits in the outer half of the inner nuclear layer, the middle cell layer of the retina.

From the top of the cell body a few long, thin, barely branched dendrites, meaning the input fibres, run sideways through the outer plexiform layer, the upper wiring layer where photoreceptors hand over their signal.

The dendrites end in flat, wide-cleft basal contacts pressed against the flattened base of a cone, a structure called the cone pedicle.

From the bottom of the cell body a single short axon, meaning the output fibre, dives into the inner plexiform layer, the lower wiring layer.

In the bistratified form the axon ends in two flat tiers, in strata that Polyak numbered 7a and 7d, near the top and the bottom of that layer.

In the monostratified giant form the axon ends in one broad tier near the middle of the inner plexiform layer, at the same depth as the diffuse bipolar type DB4.

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly 7 to 9 micrometres across, about a tenth the width of a human hair.

The dendritic field exceeds 50 micrometres in central retina and reaches 70 to 100 micrometres in the periphery, which is the widest dendritic spread of any primate bipolar cell.

Across that field it contacts about 15 to 20 cones.

The axon is short, roughly 20 to 50 micrometres from cell body to terminal, because the whole retina is under half a millimetre thick.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two forms are described.

The giant bistratified bipolar cell splits its output between an upper and a lower tier, and because those tiers match the branching depths of the small bistratified blue-yellow ganglion cell it has long been suspected of feeding the blue-yellow colour circuit.

The giant monostratified large-field bipolar cell keeps its output in one middle tier and selectively contacts long-wavelength and middle-wavelength cones rather than short-wavelength cones.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It is found across the whole neural retina of humans and other primates, in the inner nuclear layer, with dendrites in the outer plexiform layer and terminals in the inner plexiform layer.

It is absent from the very centre of the fovea, the pinhead-sized pit of sharpest vision, because there the inner cells are pushed sideways into the sloping Henle fibre layer.

The cell physically straddles the interface between the outer plexiform layer, where photoreceptors talk, and the inner plexiform layer, where the retina decides.

NUMBER:

There is no published count.

From a dendritic field of 70 to 100 micrometres, a coverage factor near one, and a retinal area of about 1,000 square millimetres, a defensible estimate is 100,000 to 250,000 per eye, with a best guess near 150,000.

Confidence is low, and this is an inference from geometry rather than a measurement.

That is under one half of one percent of the roughly 35 million bipolar cells in one eye, and about 0.0003 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the whole nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Like almost all bipolar cells it does not fire the sharp all-or-nothing electrical pulses called action potentials.

It works by graded potential, meaning its voltage slides smoothly up and down in proportion to the light.

Resting membrane voltage in darkness sits near minus 45 to minus 35 millivolts, and a bright flash moves it by only a few millivolts.

Input resistance is high, roughly 1 to 3 gigaohms, and whole-cell capacitance is small, roughly 3 to 8 picofarads, so the cell is electrically tiny and responds quickly.

Calcium entry at the output terminal runs through L-type calcium channels built from the Cav1.4 protein, which do not inactivate, allowing release to continue for hours.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input comes from cone pedicles at flat basal contacts, which in primate bipolar cells use fast ionotropic glutamate receptors of the AMPA and kainate families and preserve the sign of the signal.

Horizontal cells, the sideways-spreading cells of the outer layer, subtract a wide average from the same cone pedicles, giving the giant bipolar an antagonistic surround.
