Output is made at ribbon synapses in the inner plexiform layer, usually onto a dyad, meaning a pair of postsynaptic partners, typically one amacrine cell process and one ganglion cell dendrite.

Amacrine cells feed inhibition back onto the terminal itself, so the terminal is both a transmitter and a target.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is short, unmyelinated, and roughly 0.5 to 1 micrometre thick.

It conducts passively, meaning the voltage simply spreads down it without regeneration, which is possible only because the distance is tens of micrometres.

Conduction is effectively instantaneous on the scale of vision, under one millisecond.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The transmitter is glutamate, the main excitatory chemical messenger of the nervous system.

Glutamate is loaded into vesicles by the transporter VGLUT1 and released continuously in darkness from a ribbon, a bar-shaped protein scaffold built mainly of the protein RIBEYE that holds dozens of vesicles ready at once.

Release is graded and tonic rather than pulsed, which is how a non-spiking cell transmits a continuous variable.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable in the usual sense, and for a definite anatomical reason.

Myelin is the fatty insulating wrap made by cells called oligodendrocytes, and there are no oligodendrocytes anywhere inside the neural retina, so no retinal neuron is myelinated until axons leave the eye at the optic nerve head.

The giant bipolar cell never leaves the retina, so it is unmyelinated for life.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Its energy bill is dominated by continuous vesicle release and continuous calcium pumping in darkness, not by spiking.

Mitochondria, the cell's power plants, cluster in the axon terminal beside the ribbon.

Fuel arrives as glucose and as lactate handed over by Müller cells, the retina's main glial cells, and the retina as a whole consumes more oxygen per gram of tissue than the brain does.

PLASTICITY:

Ribbon length and the size of the ready pool of vesicles change with background light over minutes, which is part of how the eye adapts.

Dopamine released by a specialised amacrine cell in daylight adjusts gain across the whole outer retina.

In photoreceptor degeneration, bipolar dendrites retract and then sprout aberrantly, a structural remodelling that limits the usefulness of late-stage retinal implants.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Müller cells wrap this cell almost completely, and they do a job glia elsewhere do not: they act as living optical fibres that guide light through the retina to the photoreceptors.

They also mop up released glutamate with the transporter GLAST, convert it to glutamine, and hand it back, so the giant bipolar cell's transmitter is recycled rather than resynthesised.

They buffer potassium through Kir4.1 channels, which matters here because sustained graded release leaks potassium continuously rather than in bursts.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this one cell type and the outer retina loses its widest and sparsest sampler.

Every other bipolar cell reports on a small, dense, contiguous patch of cones, whereas the giant reports on a broad, deliberately thinned-out sample.

The region would keep its detail and lose its cheapest estimate of the average light level over a large patch, the signal that helps the inner retina judge contrast against local background.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell never integrates to a threshold and never fires, so any simulation that models it as a spiking unit is simply wrong.

Its computation is analogue: a weighted sum over a chosen subset of cones, minus a horizontal cell average, passed through the saturating nonlinearity of calcium-driven ribbon release.

Its wiring rule is itself a computation, because choosing about half the cones in reach, apparently by cone type, is a decision made in development and then frozen into anatomy.

Its terminal is under presynaptic inhibition from amacrine cells, so the output gain is set from below as well as from above.

It participates in volume signalling too, since glutamate that spills out of the cleft reaches receptors on neighbouring processes and on Müller cells.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: graded voltage from 15 to 20 cone pedicles, range minus 60 to minus 35 millivolts, of which only about 52 percent are actually contacted.

• INPUTS: a wide inhibitory average from H1 and H3 horizontal cells at the same pedicles, expressed as a shift of a few millivolts.

• INPUTS: presynaptic inhibition at the axon terminal from GABA-releasing and glycine-releasing amacrine cells, 0 to 1 normalised.

• OUTPUTS: graded glutamate release rate, 0 to 1 normalised, onto amacrine cell processes and ganglion cell dendrites at dyad ribbon synapses.

• STATE: membrane voltage, ribbon ready-pool occupancy, terminal calcium concentration, adaptation gain.

• PARAMETERS: dendritic field diameter 70 to 100 micrometres, cones in reach 15 to 20, contact probability 0.27 to 0.82, capacitance 3 to 8 picofarads, input resistance 1 to 3 gigaohms.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 15 to 25 milliseconds, ribbon pool refill 100 to 300 milliseconds, light adaptation tens of seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: in photoreceptor degeneration such as retinitis pigmentosa the dendrites lose their cone partners, retract and sprout, so the wide coarse channel first falls silent and is then corrupted by spontaneous inner retinal activity.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the graded glutamate output of one giant bipolar cell from a sparse selected subset of the cones above it.
Create a list variable called contacted_voltages with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called voltage_total with initial value 0.
For each cone in cones_in_dendritic_field, perform the following operations.
    If the contact flag of cone is equal to true, then perform the following actions.
        Add the membrane voltage of cone to the end of contacted_voltages.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in contacted_voltages.
If the total number of items in contacted_voltages is equal to 0, then perform the following actions.
    Return 0 to the calling function.
End the conditional block.
For each voltage in contacted_voltages, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the sum of voltage_total and voltage.
    Set voltage_total to that sum.
End the loop.
Calculate the quotient of voltage_total divided by the total number of items in contacted_voltages.
Create a number variable called centre_signal with initial value that quotient.
Calculate the difference of centre_signal and horizontal_cell_average.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value that difference.
Calculate the product of drive and minus 0.8.
Create a number variable called target_voltage with initial value the sum of minus 45 and that product.
Calculate the difference of target_voltage and membrane_voltage.
Calculate the product of that difference and 0.05.
Set membrane_voltage to the sum of membrane_voltage and that product.
Create a number variable called release with initial value the saturating function of membrane_voltage.
Display release to the console.
Return release to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because retinal models are almost always prototyped in it and the
# whole computation is a handful of floating point operations with no timing pressure.
import math

def giant_bipolar_step(cone_mv, contacted, hc_mv, st, dt=0.001):
    """One 1 ms step. Returns glutamate release rate, 0 to 1."""
    total, n = 0.0, 0
    for v, c in zip(cone_mv, contacted):
        if c:                       # only about half the cones in reach are wired in
            total += v
            n += 1
    if n == 0:
        return 0.0
    centre = total / n              # mean voltage of the contacted cones, mV
    drive = centre - hc_mv          # horizontal cell surround subtracted
    target = -45.0 - 0.8 * drive    # sign inverting: cones hyperpolarise, cell depolarises
    st["v"] += (target - st["v"]) * dt / 0.020      # 20 ms membrane filter
    return 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp(-(st["v"] + 45.0) / 4.0))   # saturating ribbon release

st = {"v": -45.0}
contacted = [True, False] * 6       # 12 cones in reach, 6 actually contacted
for _ in range(200):                # 200 ms in darkness
    dark = giant_bipolar_step([-40.0] * 12, contacted, -40.0, st)
for _ in range(200):                # 200 ms of light: cones and H cells hyperpolarise
    light = giant_bipolar_step([-55.0] * 12, contacted, -48.0, st)
print("release in darkness", round(dark, 3), "in light", round(light, 3))
# prints: release in darkness 0.5 in light 0.802

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other primate bipolar cell reaches 100 micrometres across the cone mosaic, and no other one refuses half the cones it touches.

If you see a bipolar cell with a huge, spindly, barely branched dendritic tree whose terminals skip cones in an apparently deliberate pattern, and whose axon ends either in two flat tiers or in one broad middle tier of the inner plexiform layer, you are looking at a giant bipolar cell.

CHAPTER 205. H1 CELLS (Red-Green Backdrop Neuron) (COG)

This is the cell that lets you see a black cat as black in bright sunshine and still black in a dim room, because it never reports light, only difference.

H1 is an alphanumeric series label that tells a reader nothing except that someone counted first.

Red-Green names the spectral diet, because long and middle wavelength cones supply about ninety-nine percent of this cell's input while short-wavelength cones are almost entirely ignored.

Backdrop names the work, since horizontal cells feed a wide averaged signal back onto the cones, which is the raw material of the antagonistic surround that lets a bipolar cell report local contrast rather than absolute light.

Neuron marks it as a horizontal cell that also sends a long axon whose terminal touches rod spherules exclusively, so the same backdrop service is offered to the night receptors.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A horizontal cell is a sideways-running interneuron, meaning a local connector neuron, in the upper wiring layer of the retina.

The H1 cell, also written HI, is the classic and most numerous horizontal cell of the human and monkey retina.

It listens to a patch of cones, averages them, and pushes that average back onto the very cones it listened to, as negative feedback.

That single trick builds the antagonistic surround, the ring of opposite sign around the centre of every receptive field, which is what turns raw brightness into contrast.

Because H1 cells pool long-wavelength cones and middle-wavelength cones without preference, they report luminance rather than colour, and their voltage response was historically called the luminosity or L-type S-potential after its discoverer Gunnar Svaetichin.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body lies at the very top of the inner nuclear layer, just beneath the photoreceptor terminals.

Stout dendrites radiate outward and end in dense clusters of round or doughnut-shaped terminals, each cluster plugged into the base of one cone pedicle as a lateral element of the ribbon synapse.

A single thick axon leaves the cell body, runs more than a millimetre sideways through the outer plexiform layer, and swells into a separate structure called the axon terminal, which carries a fan of hundreds of lollipop-shaped endings.

Those axon terminal endings contact rod spherules, the tiny output knobs of rods, and only rod spherules.

The axon is so thin and so long that the cell body end and the axon terminal end behave as two nearly independent compartments, one cone-driven and one rod-driven.

SIZE:

The dendritic tree is about 15 micrometres across in the fovea and 80 to 100 micrometres across in the periphery.

In the fovea it gathers from about 6 or 7 cones, and in the periphery from as many as 18.

Measured receptive fields, which include the widening effect of coupling to neighbours, run from about 122 micrometres mean diameter at 4 millimetres from the fovea to about 309 micrometres at 11 millimetres.

The axon exceeds 1,000 micrometres, making it one of the longest processes contained entirely within the retina.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There are no accepted subtypes of the H1 cell itself.

The cell is functionally two-part, however, and simulations should treat it that way: a cone-contacting cell body and dendritic compartment, and a rod-contacting axon terminal compartment, joined only weakly through a high-resistance axon.

WHERE IT LIVES:

H1 cells occupy the outermost row of the inner nuclear layer across the entire human retina, from the foveal slope to the far periphery at the ora serrata, the scalloped edge where the retina stops.

Their dendrites and their axon terminals both lie within the outer plexiform layer.

The cell sits exactly at the interface between the photoreceptor layer and the inner retina, which is why it can act on the very first synapse in vision.

NUMBER:

Horizontal cells number roughly 1 million per eye in total.

H1 cells are the majority, and a defensible estimate is about 600,000 per eye, with a range of 500,000 to 700,000; confidence is moderate, because the split among horizontal cell types is inferred from density measurements rather than counted whole.

Their density spans a factor of 25, from about 25,000 cells per square millimetre at 1 millimetre from the foveal centre down to about 1,000 cells per square millimetre in the far periphery.

That is roughly 0.37 percent of the neurons in one retina, and about 0.0014 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the whole nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

H1 cells never fire action potentials; they hold a smooth graded voltage.

In darkness they sit depolarised near minus 40 to minus 30 millivolts, because cones are releasing glutamate onto them continuously.

Light hyperpolarises them by up to 20 or 30 millivolts, slowly, and roughly in proportion to the logarithm of intensity.

Their glutamate receptors are fast ionotropic channels of the AMPA and kainate families, giving a sign-conserving response.

They are electrically coupled to their neighbours through gap junctions, which are direct pores between cells, so the recorded receptive field is far wider than the anatomical dendritic tree.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input at the dendrites comes from long-wavelength and middle-wavelength cone pedicles, contacted without spectral preference, plus at most sparse contact with short-wavelength cones.

Input at the axon terminal comes from rod spherules, exclusively.

Output is negative feedback onto the same cone pedicles, plus gap junction coupling to other H1 cells.

Feed-forward inhibition onto bipolar cell dendrites in the same synaptic cleft also occurs, which sharpens the surround one synapse further along.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is over 1 millimetre long, unmyelinated, and unusually thin, roughly 0.2 to 0.5 micrometres.

Its high internal resistance means voltage signals attenuate steeply along it, so it does not so much transmit as isolate.

The result is a single cell running two separate feedback loops at once, one for daylight cone vision and one for night-time rod vision.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

H1 cells contain gamma-aminobutyric acid, abbreviated GABA, the main inhibitory chemical messenger of the nervous system, together with its synthesising enzyme glutamic acid decarboxylase.

The feedback they exert on cones is now understood as a hybrid of three mechanisms: ephaptic inhibition, meaning a direct electrical field effect through connexin hemichannels in the horizontal cell tip; modulation of the cone's calcium channels by protons, that is by acidity, within the narrow cleft; and slower GABA action.

The ephaptic component is essentially instantaneous, the proton component takes tens to hundreds of milliseconds, and GABA sets the overall efficacy of both.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable, for the same anatomical reason that applies to every neuron inside the retina.

There are no oligodendrocytes within the neural retina, so even this exceptionally long axon is bare.

That bareness is functional rather than a defect, because the signal attenuation it causes is precisely what keeps the rod and cone compartments separate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Energy goes into holding a depolarised membrane in darkness and into running connexin hemichannels, which leak current continuously by design.

Mitochondria are spread through the dendrites and concentrated in the axon terminal.

Demand falls in bright light, when the cell hyperpolarises and its channels close, so this is one of the few neurons that costs less to run in daylight.

PLASTICITY:

Gap junction coupling between H1 cells is regulated by dopamine acting through D1 receptors, so the receptive field physically shrinks in bright light and widens in dim light, over minutes.

The strength of feedback also adapts to the background level over seconds.

After photoreceptor loss, H1 cells sprout long aberrant neurites into the inner retina, one of the earliest signs of retinal remodelling.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Müller cell processes form the outer limiting membrane, a belt of junctions just above the H1 cell body that seals the outer retina, and no glial structure quite like it exists elsewhere in the nervous system.

Those same Müller processes clear glutamate and, critically for this cell, buffer protons in the synaptic cleft, which makes the glia direct participants in the pH feedback mechanism rather than bystanders.

Astrocytes are absent from the outer retina entirely, so Müller cells do the whole job alone.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete H1 cells and the outer retina keeps its light meter and loses its edge detector.

Every downstream cell would report absolute brightness instead of local contrast, so a page of text would become a uniform grey wash that brightens and dims with the room lights.

Colour would survive but constancy would not, and the retina would saturate at almost every sunlit surface.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell is the standard example of an ephaptic synapse, meaning one that works by an electric field rather than by a chemical messenger, and any faithful model must include a non-chemical pathway.

It is a syncytium as much as a neuron, because gap junctions merge dozens of H1 cells into one electrical sheet whose extent is under dopaminergic control.

Its two compartments compute different things from different photoreceptors at the same time.

Its output changes the acidity of a synaptic cleft, which is chemistry rather than signalling in the ordinary sense.

It never spikes, never integrates to a threshold, and has no refractory period.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate-driven graded voltage from 6 to 18 long-wavelength and middle-wavelength cone pedicles at the dendrites, cone voltage range minus 60 to minus 30 millivolts.

• INPUTS: glutamate from several hundred rod spherules at the axon terminal, active only in dim light below about 0.01 candela per square metre.

• INPUTS: gap junction current from neighbouring H1 cells, coupling conductance roughly 0.1 to 1 nanosiemens per neighbour and modulated by dopamine.

• OUTPUTS: a feedback shift of the cone calcium channel activation curve, minus 5 to 0 millivolts, delivered to every contacted cone pedicle.

• OUTPUTS: cleft pH change and GABA release at the same pedicles, plus feed-forward inhibition onto bipolar dendrites.

• STATE: cell body membrane voltage, axon terminal membrane voltage, cleft proton concentration, coupling conductance, adaptation gain.

• PARAMETERS: dendritic diameter 15 to 100 micrometres, cones contacted 6 to 18, axon length above 1,000 micrometres, dark resting voltage minus 40 to minus 30 millivolts.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 5 to 15 milliseconds, ephaptic feedback under 1 millisecond, proton feedback 100 to 300 milliseconds, dopaminergic coupling change 1 to 10 minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: when horizontal cell feedback fails, as when connexin hemichannels are lost, the surround collapses, contrast sensitivity falls and the electroretinogram b-wave loses its shape; after photoreceptor death the cells sprout and wire wrongly.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute one time step of H1 horizontal cell feedback onto the cones it contacts.
Create a number variable called cone_total with initial value 0.
For each cone in contacted_cones, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the sum of cone_total and the membrane voltage of cone.
    Set cone_total to that sum.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in contacted_cones.
Calculate the quotient of cone_total divided by the total number of items in contacted_cones.
Create a number variable called cone_average with initial value that quotient.
Calculate the sum of cone_average and 40.
Calculate the product of that sum and 0.6.
Create a number variable called target_voltage with initial value the difference of that product and 40.
Calculate the difference of target_voltage and membrane_voltage.
Calculate the product of that difference and 0.1.
Set membrane_voltage to the sum of membrane_voltage and that product.
Calculate the difference of neighbour_voltage and membrane_voltage.
Calculate the product of that difference and 0.03.
Set membrane_voltage to the sum of membrane_voltage and that product.
Calculate the sum of membrane_voltage and 40.
Calculate the product of that sum and 0.12.
Create a number variable called ephaptic_shift with initial value that product.
Calculate the sum of membrane_voltage and 40.
Calculate the product of that sum and 0.05.
Calculate the difference of that product and proton_shift.
Calculate the product of that difference and 0.005.
Set proton_shift to the sum of proton_shift and that product.
Calculate the sum of ephaptic_shift and proton_shift.
Return that sum to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python again, because the fast ephaptic term and the slow proton term are just two
# first order filters, and clarity matters more than speed for a single cell.
def h1_step(cone_mv, st, dt=0.001):
    """One 1 ms step. Returns the feedback shift applied to each contacted cone, in mV.
    A negative value means the cone's calcium current is disinhibited."""
    mean_cone = sum(cone_mv) / len(cone_mv)                  # pool L and M cones equally
    target = -40.0 + 0.60 * (mean_cone + 40.0)               # graded, sign conserving
    st["v"] += (target - st["v"]) * dt / 0.010               # 10 ms membrane filter
    st["v"] += 0.30 * (st["v_neighbour"] - st["v"]) * dt / 0.010   # gap junction pull
    ephaptic = 0.12 * (st["v"] + 40.0)                       # instantaneous field effect
    st["ph"] += (0.05 * (st["v"] + 40.0) - st["ph"]) * dt / 0.200  # 200 ms proton term
    return ephaptic + st["ph"]

st = {"v": -40.0, "ph": 0.0, "v_neighbour": -40.0}
for _ in range(1000):                       # 1 second of steady light on 9 cones
    fb = h1_step([-55.0] * 9, st)
print("H1 voltage", round(st["v"], 2), "mV, feedback", round(fb, 3), "mV")
# prints: H1 voltage -46.87 mV, feedback -1.166 mV

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Look for a cell in the top row of the inner nuclear layer with a compact bush of doughnut-shaped cone contacts and, a full millimetre away, a completely separate fan of hundreds of lollipop endings sitting inside rod terminals.

Nothing else in the human body is a single neuron whose two ends serve day vision and night vision independently, and no other horizontal cell contacts rods exclusively at its axon terminal.

CHAPTER 206. H2 CELLS (Blue-Sampling Backdrop Neuron) (COG)

Only about one cone in twenty in your eye is sensitive to blue light, and this spidery cell goes hunting for them.

H2 is the second entry in a numbered list and teaches nothing.

Blue-Sampling names the one property that separates this cell from its neighbour in the set, since about forty-two percent of its cone contacts are on short-wavelength cones and fifty-eight percent on long and middle wavelength cones, a far heavier blue diet than any other horizontal cell.

Backdrop is the shared family word for the averaged feedback signal all horizontal cells return to the photoreceptors to build the surround.

Neuron marks it as a horizontal cell, and the name deliberately does not promise colour opponency, because primate horizontal cells were shown to be cone-specific without spectral opponency.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The H2 cell, also written HII, is the second horizontal cell type of the human and monkey retina, and the only one that seeks out short-wavelength cones on purpose.

A horizontal cell is a sideways-running connector neuron in the outer plexiform layer, the upper wiring layer of the retina, where photoreceptors hand their signal on.

Short-wavelength cones, usually called S-cones or blue cones, are the rarest of the three colour detectors, making up only about 5 to 10 percent of all cones and absent altogether from the very centre of the fovea, the pit of sharpest vision.

The H2 cell directs its main dendrites, meaning its input fibres, straight at those rare cones wherever they occur in its territory, while also picking up weaker contacts from nearly every other cone within reach.

Its job is to provide feedback and feed-forward inhibition specialised for the blue-yellow colour circuit, and, remarkably, it does this while remaining a plain luminosity cell itself.

Dacey and colleagues showed in 1996 that primate H2 cells have cone specificity without spectral opponency, meaning that even though they are wired preferentially to S-cones, they hyperpolarise to light of every wavelength and never invert their sign.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Where the H1 cell is stout and bushy, the H2 cell is spidery and intricate.

Its dendrites are fine, wandering and irregularly branched, and they end in small wispy terminals rather than in tidy clusters of doughnuts.

Major dendrites bend out of their way toward S-cone pedicles, the flattened output bases of blue cones, and cluster densely there.

Its axon is quite unlike the H1 axon: it is short, only about 100 to 200 micrometres, and it curls rather than running straight.

That short curled axon ends in wispy terminals that contact cone pedicles, with a further preference for blue cone pedicles, so both ends of the cell serve cones and neither end touches rods.

SIZE:

At any given eccentricity the H2 dendritic field is larger than the H1 field and the cells are sparser on the ground.

In central retina the dendritic tree spans roughly 20 to 40 micrometres and gathers from about 8 to 12 cones, including one to three S-cones.

In peripheral retina it spans roughly 100 to 150 micrometres and gathers from 20 or more cones.

The cell body is about 8 to 12 micrometres across and sits in the outermost row of the inner nuclear layer.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No subtypes of H2 are recognised.

The nearest thing to internal variation is regional: in the far periphery, where S-cones are sparse and scattered, individual H2 cells become correspondingly more asymmetric, reaching further in the direction of the nearest blue cones.

WHERE IT LIVES:

H2 cells lie in the outermost row of the inner nuclear layer, with dendrites and axon terminals in the outer plexiform layer, throughout the human retina.

They are absent from the very centre of the foveola, the innermost few hundred micrometres, because S-cones themselves are absent there and inner retinal cells are displaced sideways into the Henle fibre layer.

Like all horizontal cells they occupy the interface between the photoreceptor layer above and the bipolar cell layer below, and their whole existence is spent modifying the first synapse of vision.

NUMBER:

Within the roughly 1 million horizontal cells per eye, H2 cells are a clear minority.

A defensible estimate is about 230,000 per eye, with a range of 150,000 to 300,000, which is roughly a quarter of all horizontal cells; confidence is moderate to low, because published work reports H2 density relative to H1 rather than an absolute human whole-retina count.

That is about 0.14 percent of the neurons in one retina, and roughly 0.0005 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the whole nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

H2 cells are non-spiking and graded, sitting depolarised near minus 40 millivolts in darkness and hyperpolarising by up to about 20 millivolts in light.

Their response is sustained, meaning it lasts as long as the light lasts, with no adaptation to zero.

Because their S-cone input is strong and their long-wavelength and middle-wavelength input is weaker but far more numerous, the summed response is still monophasic, that is, always the same sign.

They are electrically coupled through gap junctions into their own network, separate from the H1 network, so H2 cells talk to H2 cells and not to H1 cells.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input: dense dendritic contacts at S-cone pedicles, plus contacts with nearly all long-wavelength and middle-wavelength cones in the dendritic field, all as lateral elements at cone ribbon synapses.

Input: further cone contacts made by the short curled axon terminal, again with a preference for blue cones.

Output: negative feedback onto those same cone pedicles, and feed-forward inhibition onto the dendrites of the blue cone bipolar cell.

The machinery for that feed-forward step, meaning the vesicle release proteins and the receiving GABA receptors, is physically enriched in the small patch of neuropil directly beneath each S-cone pedicle, which is a striking case of a synapse type being concentrated at one address.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is short at 100 to 200 micrometres, thin, curled and unmyelinated.

Because it is short, voltage passes along it with far less loss than in the H1 axon, so the H2 cell behaves much more like a single electrical compartment.

That single-compartment behaviour means the same signal is delivered at the dendrites and at the axon terminal, which suits a cell that serves cones at both ends.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The transmitter is gamma-aminobutyric acid, abbreviated GABA, the main inhibitory chemical messenger of the nervous system.

H2 cells are the strongest primate candidate for genuine vesicular GABA release in the outer retina, because the vesicular GABA transporter and the receiving GABA-A receptor subunits are found concentrated beneath S-cone pedicles precisely where H2 dendrites cluster.

Alongside GABA, H2 cells use the same two non-classical feedback routes as H1 cells: an almost instantaneous ephaptic effect through connexin hemichannels, meaning a direct electrical field acting across the cleft, and a slower change in cleft acidity that shifts the cone's calcium channels.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable, because no neuron inside the neural retina is myelinated.

Myelin is made by oligodendrocytes, and oligodendrocytes are excluded from the retina, appearing only behind the sieve-like lamina cribrosa where the optic nerve leaves the eye.

For a cell whose entire axon is 200 micrometres long, insulation would in any case buy nothing.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Demand is modest and steady: it is dominated by maintaining a depolarised membrane in darkness, by tonic GABA loading into vesicles, and by continuously leaky hemichannels.

Mitochondria are distributed along the fine dendrites, close to the S-cone contact clusters where release is concentrated.

Like other outer retinal neurons, H2 cells draw glucose and lactate from Müller cells and sit in the highest oxygen-consuming tissue in the body by weight.

PLASTICITY:

Coupling within the H2 network is modulated by dopamine and by nitric oxide, so the effective size of the S-cone-weighted pool changes with the ambient light level over minutes.

Feedback gain adapts to background over seconds, keeping blue-yellow signalling usable across a very wide range of illumination.

In photoreceptor degeneration H2 cells, like H1 cells, retract their fine terminals and later sprout aberrant processes.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Müller cells provide the same glutamate recycling, potassium buffering and proton buffering here as they do for H1 cells, but the proton buffering matters more for H2 because its most important contacts are packed into the small clefts under rare S-cone pedicles where local acidity swings fastest.

Müller processes also physically wall off the neuropil beneath each cone pedicle, which is what allows the feed-forward GABA machinery beneath an S-cone to act as an isolated micro-compartment.

There are no astrocytes and no oligodendrocytes in this layer at all.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete H2 cells and the outer retina keeps its brightness and its red-green comparisons and loses the specialised inhibitory scaffolding that supports blue-yellow vision.

Blue would not vanish, because the blue cone bipolar cell would still receive its direct S-cone input, but that input would lose its matched surround and its feed-forward normalisation.

Practically, the region would lose the ability to keep blue-yellow judgements stable as the overall colour of the illumination changes, so a white wall under a blue sky and under a tungsten lamp would no longer look the same colour.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell demonstrates that wiring specificity and response specificity are separate things: it is selectively wired to one cone type yet computes a non-selective average, which is a warning to any modeller who infers function from connectivity alone.

It works partly by ephaptic field effect and partly by changing the acidity of a synaptic cleft, neither of which is a conventional synapse.

It is embedded in a private gap-junction network, so the correct simulation unit is a coupled sheet, not an isolated cell.

Its feed-forward output is spatially compartmentalised under individual cone pedicles, meaning the cell computes different things at different points on its own dendritic tree.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: strong graded drive from 1 to 5 short-wavelength cone pedicles, voltage range minus 60 to minus 30 millivolts, weight about 0.7 of the total.

• INPUTS: weaker graded drive from 8 to 25 long-wavelength and middle-wavelength cone pedicles, weight about 0.3 of the total.

• INPUTS: gap junction current from neighbouring H2 cells only, 0.1 to 1 nanosiemens per neighbour, dopamine modulated.

• OUTPUTS: feedback shift of the calcium channel activation curve at all contacted cone pedicles, minus 5 to 0 millivolts.

• OUTPUTS: feed-forward GABA release onto blue cone bipolar dendrites beneath S-cone pedicles, 0 to 1 normalised, maximal in darkness.

• STATE: membrane voltage, cleft proton level, vesicular GABA pool, network coupling conductance.

• PARAMETERS: dendritic field 20 to 150 micrometres, axon length 100 to 200 micrometres, S-cone weight 0.6 to 0.8, cells per eye about 230,000.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 8 to 15 milliseconds, ephaptic feedback under 1 millisecond, proton feedback 100 to 300 milliseconds, coupling change 1 to 10 minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: when S-cone circuits degrade, as they do early in glaucoma and in diabetic retinopathy, blue-yellow discrimination fails first, producing the acquired tritan defect used clinically as an early warning sign.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute one time step of an H2 horizontal cell, weighting blue cones heavily and all other cones lightly.
Create a number variable called s_total with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called lm_total with initial value 0.
For each cone in contacted_cones, perform the following operations.
    If the short wavelength flag of cone is equal to true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of s_total and the membrane voltage of cone.
        Set s_total to that sum.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of lm_total and the membrane voltage of cone.
        Set lm_total to that sum.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Calculate the quotient of s_total divided by s_cone_count.
Create a number variable called s_pool with initial value that quotient.
Calculate the quotient of lm_total divided by lm_cone_count.
Create a number variable called lm_pool with initial value that quotient.
Calculate the product of the sum of s_pool and 40 and 0.7.
Create a number variable called s_term with initial value that product.
Calculate the product of the sum of lm_pool and 40 and 0.3.
Create a number variable called lm_term with initial value that product.
Calculate the sum of s_term and lm_term.
Create a number variable called target_voltage with initial value the difference of that sum and 40.
Calculate the difference of target_voltage and membrane_voltage.
Calculate the product of that difference and 0.083.
Set membrane_voltage to the sum of membrane_voltage and that product.
Calculate the product of the sum of membrane_voltage and 40 and 0.06.
Create a number variable called gaba_release with initial value the sum of 1 and that product.
If gaba_release is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
    Set gaba_release to 0.
End the conditional block.
Display gaba_release to the console.
Return gaba_release to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python, because the point of this cell is a weighted pool and a sign convention,
# and Python makes both readable at a glance with no library dependencies.
def h2_step(s_cone_mv, lm_cone_mv, st, dt=0.001):
    """One 1 ms step. Returns tonic GABA release (0 to 1) and the ephaptic shift in mV."""
    s_pool = sum(s_cone_mv) / len(s_cone_mv)        # rare blue cones, heavy weight
    lm_pool = sum(lm_cone_mv) / len(lm_cone_mv)     # common red and green cones, light weight
    target = -40.0 + 0.70 * (s_pool + 40.0) + 0.30 * (lm_pool + 40.0)
    st["v"] += (target - st["v"]) * dt / 0.012      # 12 ms membrane filter
    # GABA release is tonic in darkness and falls as the cell hyperpolarises in light
    gaba = max(0.0, 1.0 + 0.060 * (st["v"] + 40.0))
    ephaptic = 0.10 * (st["v"] + 40.0)              # instantaneous feedback onto cones
    return gaba, ephaptic

st = {"v": -40.0}
for _ in range(500):                 # half a second of blue light: S-cone drops hard
    gaba, ephaptic = h2_step([-58.0], [-42.0] * 11, st)
print("H2", round(st["v"], 2), "mV, GABA", round(gaba, 3), "shift", round(ephaptic, 3), "mV")
# prints: H2 -53.2 mV, GABA 0.208 shift -1.32 mV

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Find the horizontal cell whose thin, spidery dendrites visibly detour toward the scattered blue cones, and whose axon is short and curled instead of long and straight, and which never touches a rod at either end.

If the axon runs a millimetre and ends on rods, it is an H1 cell; if it curls back after 150 micrometres and ends on cones, especially blue ones, it is an H2 cell.

CHAPTER 207. H3 CELLS (Broad-Sweep Backdrop Neuron) (COG)

This is the quietly controversial cell of the human eye: a horizontal cell that looks almost exactly like the commonest one, except that everywhere in the retina it is a third bigger, and lopsided.

H3 is the third label in a numbered series and carries no meaning.

Broad-Sweep names the measured distinction, since in human retina this type has a dendritic field about forty percent larger in area than the first type and bears about thirty percent more dendritic terminal clusters, so it averages light over a wider patch of the sheet.

Backdrop again names the shared duty of returning an averaged signal to the cones.

Neuron marks it as the cell completing the set, since it avoids short-wavelength cones altogether, so the three names now vary along one visible dimension, which cones are pooled and over how wide an area.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The H3 cell, also written HIII, was proposed as a third human horizontal cell type by Helga Kolb, Eduardo Fernandez, Elke Schütte and Peter Ahnelt in 1994, in a paper whose title was itself a question: are there three types of horizontal cell in the human retina.

Like other horizontal cells it is a sideways-running connector neuron in the outer plexiform layer, the upper wiring layer, that averages a patch of cones and pushes that average back onto them as negative feedback.

What distinguishes it is scale and shape rather than chemistry: at every eccentricity its dendritic tree is about one third larger than that of an H1 cell nearby, and in the periphery it is conspicuously asymmetric, reaching much further in one direction than another.

Like the H1 cell it makes large dendritic terminals in long-wavelength and middle-wavelength cones and appears to avoid short-wavelength cones altogether.

Functionally it should therefore deliver a wider, coarser, direction-biased surround: the same operation as H1 performed over a larger and deliberately lopsided patch.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

In outline it resembles an H1 cell: a cell body in the top row of the inner nuclear layer, stout radiating dendrites, and clusters of large terminals plugged into cone pedicles as lateral elements of the cone ribbon synapse.

The differences are that the whole tree is larger, the branch pattern is looser, and the outline of the tree is elliptical or irregular rather than round, especially away from the centre of the retina.

The cell has an axon, but in the original human material that axon was never followed all the way to a terminal, so the identity of the photoreceptors it contacts remains genuinely unknown; the working suspicion is a mixture of rods and cones.

SIZE:

The rule of thumb is one third larger than a neighbouring H1 cell.

That gives roughly 20 micrometres across in the fovea and 110 to 130 micrometres across in the far periphery.

Cone contacts follow: about 9 to 12 cone pedicles in foveal retina, rising to about 20 to 25 in peripheral retina.

The cell body is 8 to 12 micrometres across, indistinguishable by size alone from an H1 cell body.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

None are described, and the honest position is that H3 may itself be a subtype rather than a type.

Golgi staining and electron microscopy of human tissue support three morphological classes, but transcriptomic surveys, which sort cells by which genes they switch on, generally resolve only two horizontal cell clusters in human and monkey retina.

The unresolved question is whether H3 cells are a genuinely separate population or the large, asymmetric tail of the H1 population, and a good simulation should carry the type as a parameterised variant of H1 rather than as an independent module.

WHERE IT LIVES:

H3 cells lie in the outermost row of the inner nuclear layer, with dendrites in the outer plexiform layer, and were described across the human retina from central to peripheral regions.

They have been reported clearly in human tissue and are much less obvious in macaque monkey tissue, which is one reason the type remains debated.

Like every horizontal cell, H3 occupies the interface between the photoreceptor layer and the bipolar cell layer, and it acts on the first synapse in the visual pathway.

NUMBER:

If H3 cells are real and distinct, they make up the remainder of the roughly 1 million horizontal cells per eye after H1 and H2 are counted.

A defensible estimate is about 170,000 per eye, with a range of 100,000 to 250,000, and confidence is low; the type's very existence is contested, so a simulation should expose this count as an adjustable parameter that can be set to zero.

That is roughly 0.10 percent of the neurons in one retina and about 0.0004 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the whole nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

No one has recorded from a confirmed human H3 cell, so its physiology is inferred rather than measured, and that must be stated plainly.

By anatomy it should behave as a luminosity cell: non-spiking, graded, sitting near minus 40 millivolts in darkness and hyperpolarising in light, with a sustained response and no colour opponency, since it pools long-wavelength and middle-wavelength cones and skips short-wavelength cones.

Because the dendritic tree is larger, the expected receptive field and integration area are correspondingly larger and the response should be slightly slower and smoother.

Gap junction coupling with like neighbours is assumed by analogy with H1 and H2, but has not been demonstrated for H3 specifically.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input: 9 to 25 long-wavelength and middle-wavelength cone pedicles, contacted by large dendritic terminals, with short-wavelength cones apparently skipped.

Output: negative feedback onto those same cone pedicles, and presumed feed-forward inhibition onto bipolar dendrites in the same cleft.

Axonal connections are unknown, which is a real gap in the human connectome and should be represented in software as an explicit unknown rather than filled in by guesswork.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

An axon exists and leaves the cell body, but its length, its calibre and its destination have not been established in human tissue.

It is certainly unmyelinated, because nothing inside the retina is myelinated.

If it resembles the H1 axon it will be long, thin and highly attenuating, creating a second, largely independent compartment; if it resembles the H2 axon it will be short and the cell will behave as one compartment.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

By analogy with the other horizontal cells the transmitter is gamma-aminobutyric acid, abbreviated GABA, the main inhibitory chemical messenger of the nervous system, supported by ephaptic feedback through connexin hemichannels and by proton, that is acidity, modulation of the cone's calcium channels.

No transmitter study has been performed on identified H3 cells specifically.

The safest modelling assumption is that H3 uses the identical feedback machinery to H1 and differs only in the geometry over which it applies it.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable, because the retina contains no oligodendrocytes, the cells that manufacture the fatty insulating myelin sheath.

Every process of every retinal neuron is bare until axons pass through the lamina cribrosa at the optic disc.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Expected to match H1: a steady cost dominated by holding a depolarised membrane in darkness, by continuously conducting hemichannels, and by tonic transmitter loading, with the bill falling in bright light.

The larger dendritic tree means somewhat more membrane to maintain, so a modest upward adjustment of about a third over H1 is a reasonable first estimate.

No direct measurement exists.

PLASTICITY:

Presumed dopaminergic control of gap junction coupling, as in H1 and H2, changing effective receptive field size over minutes with ambient light.

Presumed sprouting after photoreceptor loss, since all horizontal cells studied do this.

The asymmetry of the dendritic tree is itself a developmental outcome, most likely shaped by competition with neighbouring horizontal cells for cone contacts, so the shape encodes a history.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing about the glia around an H3 cell is known to differ from the glia around an H1 cell, and that should be said plainly rather than dressed up.

Müller cells provide the outer limiting membrane just above the cell body, recycle glutamate through the GLAST transporter, buffer potassium through Kir4.1 channels and buffer protons in the cleft where feedback happens.

There are no astrocytes and no oligodendrocytes in this layer.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

If H3 cells are real, removing them would leave the outer retina with a surround that is uniformly sized and uniformly round.

What it would lose is a second, wider, direction-biased layer of lateral inhibition running in parallel with the H1 sheet.

Concretely, the region would lose an oriented bias in contrast processing: elongated edges in one direction would no longer be treated any differently from edges in another, and the coarse, large-scale component of brightness normalisation would be weakened.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The most important point for a simulation is epistemic rather than biophysical: this is a type whose existence is uncertain, and the model should treat it as a hypothesis with a switch, not as an established fact.

Beyond that, everything that makes H1 unconventional applies here: ephaptic field feedback, proton signalling in the cleft, gap junction fusion into a sheet, tonic graded release, no spikes and no threshold.

The elliptical, asymmetric dendritic field means the cell computes an oriented spatial filter, so a rotation parameter belongs in the type definition.

Because it deliberately skips short-wavelength cones, its wiring embodies a cone-type recognition rule that operates during development.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: graded voltage from 9 to 25 long-wavelength and middle-wavelength cone pedicles, minus 60 to minus 30 millivolts, with short-wavelength cones excluded by a wiring rule.

• INPUTS: presumed gap junction current from like neighbours, 0.1 to 1 nanosiemens per neighbour, unverified for this type.

• OUTPUTS: feedback shift of the cone calcium channel activation curve at all contacted pedicles, minus 5 to 0 millivolts.

• OUTPUTS: presumed feed-forward inhibition onto bipolar dendrites; axonal output target unknown and must be flagged as unknown.

• STATE: membrane voltage, cleft proton level, coupling conductance, and a boolean existence flag for this contested type.

• PARAMETERS: dendritic long axis 20 to 130 micrometres, axis ratio about 1.5 to 2 in the periphery, size relative to local H1 cell 1.33, short-wavelength cone weight 0, cells per eye 0 to 250,000.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds, ephaptic feedback under 1 millisecond, proton feedback 100 to 300 milliseconds, coupling change 1 to 10 minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: if the type is real, its loss would broaden and isotropise the outer retinal surround and blunt large-scale contrast normalisation; in photoreceptor degeneration it would retract and sprout like every other horizontal cell.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute one time step of an H3 horizontal cell using an elliptical dendritic field that skips short wavelength cones.
Create a number variable called weight_total with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called weighted_voltage with initial value 0.
For each cone in cones_near_cell, perform the following operations.
    If the short wavelength flag of cone is equal to true, then perform the following actions.
        Skip to the next iteration of the loop.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the quotient of the horizontal offset of cone divided by long_axis.
    Create a number variable called x_term with initial value the product of that quotient and itself.
    Calculate the quotient of the vertical offset of cone divided by short_axis.
    Create a number variable called y_term with initial value the product of that quotient and itself.
    Calculate the sum of x_term and y_term.
    Create a number variable called radius_squared with initial value that sum.
    If radius_squared is greater than 4, then perform the following actions.
        Skip to the next iteration of the loop.
    End the conditional block.
    Create a number variable called weight with initial value the decaying function of radius_squared.
    Calculate the sum of weight_total and weight.
    Set weight_total to that sum.
    Calculate the product of weight and the membrane voltage of cone.
    Set weighted_voltage to the sum of weighted_voltage and that product.
End the loop.
If weight_total is equal to 0, then perform the following actions.
    Return 0 to the calling function.
End the conditional block.
Calculate the quotient of weighted_voltage divided by weight_total.
Create a number variable called pooled_voltage with initial value that quotient.
Calculate the product of the sum of pooled_voltage and 40 and 0.55.
Create a number variable called target_voltage with initial value the difference of that product and 38.
Calculate the difference of target_voltage and membrane_voltage.
Calculate the product of that difference and 0.083.
Set membrane_voltage to the sum of membrane_voltage and that product.
Calculate the product of the sum of membrane_voltage and 38 and 0.1.
Return that product to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python, because the defining feature here is a geometric wiring rule over a cone
# mosaic, and list comprehension over coordinates keeps that rule visible.
import math

def h3_step(cones, st, dt=0.001, long_axis=95.0, short_axis=60.0):
    """cones: list of (dx_um, dy_um, voltage_mV, is_s_cone). Returns feedback shift in mV."""
    wsum = vsum = 0.0
    for dx, dy, v, is_s in cones:
        if is_s:
            continue                                  # H3 dendrites skip blue cones
        r2 = (dx / long_axis) ** 2 + (dy / short_axis) ** 2   # elliptical, oriented field
        if r2 > 4.0:
            continue                                  # outside the dendritic tree
        w = math.exp(-r2)                             # taper toward the edge
        wsum += w
        vsum += w * v
    if wsum == 0.0:
        return 0.0
    pooled = vsum / wsum
    target = -38.0 + 0.55 * (pooled + 40.0)
    st["v"] += (target - st["v"]) * dt / 0.012        # 12 ms membrane filter
    return 0.10 * (st["v"] + 38.0)                    # feedback onto every contacted cone

field = [(x * 20.0, y * 20.0, -55.0, (x + y) % 7 == 0)
         for x in range(-4, 5) for y in range(-3, 4)]     # 63 cones on a 20 um grid
st = {"v": -38.0}
for _ in range(500):
    fb = h3_step(field, st)
print("H3", round(st["v"], 2), "mV, feedback", round(fb, 3), "mV")
# prints: H3 -46.25 mV, feedback -0.825 mV

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Put an H3 cell beside an H1 cell from the same patch of retina and the H3 tree is visibly a third wider and clearly lopsided, while its terminals sit only in red and green cones and never in blue ones.

It is the only named human horizontal cell whose axon has never been traced to its ending, and the only one whose existence as a separate type is still an open question.

CHAPTER 208. AII AMACRINE CELLS (Night-Vision Splitting Neuron) (COG)

On a moonless night, almost every photon your eye manages to catch is funnelled through this one small cell, which makes it the narrowest bottleneck in human vision.

AII is a catalogue code from a morphological survey and teaches nothing about the cell.

Night-Vision names the regime it owns, because in dim light the rod signal reaches the rest of the retina almost entirely through this cell, and its pathway sets the scotopic threshold at which vision begins.

Splitting names the actual operation, since the cell takes one rod signal and divides it two ways, passing it unchanged through electrical gap junctions to the ON cone bipolar cells and sign-inverted through glycine release to the OFF cone bipolar cells, so both halves of vision are served from a single input.

Neuron marks it as a cell that connectome work found contacts at least twenty-eight different cell classes, the most complex interaction repertoire of any known vertebrate neuron.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An amacrine cell is an interneuron of the inner retina, meaning a local connector cell that talks to its neighbours rather than sending a fibre out of the eye.

The AII amacrine cell is the smallest and most numerous of them, and it is the hub of the night vision circuit.

It receives the amplified rod signal from rod bipolar cells and then does something no other retinal neuron does: it splits that single signal into two opposite streams.

To the ON stream, the cells that report light getting brighter, it passes the signal unchanged through electrical junctions.

To the OFF stream, the cells that report light getting dimmer, it passes the same signal through inhibitory chemical synapses that flip its sign.

In daylight it keeps working, supplying what is called crossover inhibition, a correction that stops OFF cone bipolar cells from saturating at high light levels.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body sits right on the border between the inner nuclear layer, the middle sheet of cell bodies, and the inner plexiform layer, the lower wiring carpet.

A single thick primary dendrite drops straight down from the cell body like a plumb line.

In the outer half of the wiring carpet, called sublamina a, that dendrite grows stubby knobs called lobular appendages.

In the inner half, called sublamina b, it ends in a fine bushy spray called the arboreal dendrites.

The two halves do opposite jobs: the knobs release inhibitory chemical transmitter, and the bushy spray makes electrical junctions.

SIZE:

The cell body is about 7 to 10 micrometres across, one of the smallest neurons in the human body.

The dendritic field is roughly 15 to 30 micrometres wide near the centre of vision and 30 to 70 micrometres wide in the far periphery.

Total dendritic length is only a few hundred micrometres, so the whole cell would fit inside a full stop on this page.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

No separate named subtypes are recognised, but the central and peripheral forms differ measurably.

Foveal AII cells are smaller, their inner dendrites are finer, and the two-tiered shape is partly hidden because the retinal layers are stretched sideways at the centre.

Foveal cells carry about 19 gap junctions per 1,000 square micrometres of membrane against about 30 in peripheral cells, and about 58 ribbon inputs per 1,000 square micrometres in the inner tier against about 71 peripherally.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lies at the inner nuclear layer to inner plexiform layer border across the whole neural retina, from the foveal centre to the ora serrata, the scalloped rim where the retina stops.

In macaque it is present as close as 140 micrometres from the foveal centre, even though the rod bipolar cells that normally feed it do not appear until about 600 micrometres out.

It straddles three interfaces at once: the boundary between the cell body layer and the wiring layer, the boundary between sublamina a and sublamina b, and the functional boundary between the rod system and the cone system.

NUMBER:

The AII is the most numerous amacrine type, making up roughly 11 percent of all amacrine cells.

Its density runs from about 5,000 cells per square millimetre in central retina down to about 1,000 cells per square millimetre in the periphery of the macaque eye.

A defensible estimate for a human eye is about 3 million cells, with a range of 1.5 to 4 million, so roughly 6 million across both eyes.

That is about 2.3 percent of the roughly 260 million neurons in the two retinas, and about 0.007 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the whole nervous system.

Confidence is moderate: the density figures are solid monkey measurements, but no whole human retina has been counted cell by cell.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Unusually for an amacrine cell, the AII fires small fast electrical pulses called spikelets, roughly 10 to 30 millivolts tall and only a millisecond or two wide.

They are produced by a cluster of voltage-gated sodium channels on the thick proximal dendrite and are abolished by tetrodotoxin, the pufferfish poison that blocks those channels.

They are not full action potentials, because they do not travel far and do not reach the overshoot of a spiking neuron.

The cell rests near minus 60 millivolts, has a very high input resistance of around 1 gigaohm, and can respond to the absorption of a single photon somewhere in its rod pool with a blip of one or two millivolts.

The same sodium channels give the cell a resonance, a preferred frequency of oscillation, in the 10 to 100 hertz range.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

About 25 to 30 rod bipolar cells converge onto one peripheral AII cell through ribbon synapses, releasing glutamate onto AMPA-type receptors on the inner dendrites.

Output leaves by two completely different routes from the two tiers.

The lobular appendages in the outer tier make conventional glycinergic synapses onto the axon terminals of OFF cone bipolar cells and onto the dendrites of OFF ganglion cells.

The arboreal dendrites in the inner tier make gap junctions, direct pores between cells, with the terminals of ON cone bipolar cells and with three to five neighbouring AII cells.

Those junctions are built from connexin 36, the neuronal gap junction protein, and the cell assembles different junctions depending on whether the partner is another AII or a bipolar cell.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Not Applicable, and the reason is in the name.

Amacrine means without a long fibre, and the AII cell has no axon at all: the same short dendrites that receive its inputs also carry its outputs, so there is no separate output cable to describe.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The AII releases glycine, the smallest amino acid and one of the two main inhibitory messengers in the nervous system, packaged by the vesicular inhibitory amino acid transporter and recovered by the glycine transporter GlyT1.

Glycine is small enough to pass straight through connexin 36 pores, so it leaks into the coupled ON cone bipolar cells, which is why some bipolar cells stain for a transmitter they never make.

Dopamine acting on D1 receptors and nitric oxide acting through cyclic GMP both modulate the cell, mainly by changing how strongly its gap junctions conduct.

MYELINATION:

None.

Myelin, the fatty insulation that speeds nerve conduction, is absent from the entire retina inside the eye, because a coat of fat would scatter light before it reached the photoreceptors.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High for such a small cell, because it is active continuously in both darkness and daylight and never gets an off shift.

It is packed with mitochondria and with calretinin, a calcium-buffering protein used as its identifying marker in monkey and human tissue.

It sits in the inner retina, the region most vulnerable to oxygen shortage, so it fails early when retinal blood flow is compromised.

PLASTICITY:

Its most important adjustable property is the strength of its electrical coupling, and that follows an inverted U curve against background light.

Coupling is weak in complete darkness, which keeps each cell private so a single photon is not diluted; it peaks at mid-twilight light levels, where averaging across many cells cancels noise; and it falls again in bright light.

Dopamine and nitric oxide carry out this adjustment by changing the phosphorylation state of connexin 36, and the whole cycle is also under circadian control.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Müller cells, the radial glia that span the retina like scaffolding poles, wrap the inner plexiform layer around the AII and buffer potassium through Kir4.1 channels.

What is unusual here is the glycine housekeeping: unlike in the spinal cord, where glial transporters clear glycine, retinal Müller cells carry little GlyT1, so the AII cell recovers most of its own transmitter.

Microglia, the resident immune cells, wrap closely around AII cells during retinal degeneration and are implicated in the rewiring that follows.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this one cell type and starlight vision ends.

Rods would still catch photons and rod bipolar cells would still amplify them, but the signal would have nowhere to go, because the AII is the only exit from the primary rod pathway into the cone circuitry that reaches the brain.

In daylight the region would lose crossover inhibition, so OFF ganglion cells would clip and flatten their responses to bright edges.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The AII is a hybrid device, chemical on one side and electrical on the other, and it inverts the sign of the signal on the chemical side while preserving it on the electrical side.

Its gap junction network turns thousands of separate cells into one adjustable electrical sheet, so the effective receptive field of a single AII can be tuned by a chemical messenger rather than by rewiring.

Its dendritic spikelets act as a local booster, regenerating small signals before they are handed on.

Glycine crossing the gap junctions is a chemical message travelling through an electrical synapse, a genuinely unusual piece of biology.

When photoreceptors die, the AII becomes tonically hyperpolarised, its intrinsic resonance is unmasked as an oscillation near 10 hertz, and that rhythm spreads through the coupled AII and ON bipolar cell network to produce the rhythmic bursting that plagues blind retinas.

Connexin 36 phosphorylation is measurably raised in both fast and slow degeneration models, which increases the coupling that carries the oscillation.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from 25 to 30 rod bipolar ribbon synapses onto AMPA receptors, 0 to 1 normalised release rate.

• INPUTS: junctional current from 3 to 5 neighbouring AII cells and from coupled ON cone bipolar terminals, coupling conductance roughly 0.1 to 1 nanosiemens per partner.

• INPUTS: GABA from wide-field amacrine cells, dopamine 0 to 100 nanomolar, and nitric oxide as gain signals.

• OUTPUTS: glycine release from lobular appendages onto OFF cone bipolar terminals and OFF ganglion cell dendrites, sign-inverting.

• OUTPUTS: bidirectional electrical current into ON cone bipolar terminals, sign-preserving.

• STATE: membrane voltage, sodium channel activation and inactivation, gap junction conductance, connexin 36 phosphorylation level, vesicle pool at the lobules.

• PARAMETERS: resting voltage minus 60 millivolts, spikelet threshold about minus 48 millivolts, spikelet amplitude 10 to 30 millivolts, input resistance about 1 gigaohm, dendritic field 15 to 70 micrometres, density 1,000 to 5,000 cells per square millimetre.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 15 milliseconds, spikelet 1 to 2 milliseconds, glycine synapse 3 to 10 milliseconds, coupling change under dopamine 1 to 10 minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: after photoreceptor loss in retinitis pigmentosa the cell hyperpolarises, oscillates near 10 hertz and drives ganglion cell hyperactivity that masks any residual or restored light response, which is why gap junction blockers improve optogenetic vision restoration in animal models; in inner retinal ischaemia it degenerates early, and loss of its crossover inhibition distorts daylight OFF responses.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to split the rod signal arriving at an AII amacrine cell into an electrical ON output and a chemical OFF output.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called membrane_potential with initial value -60.
Create a number variable called spikelet with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called swing with initial value 0.
For each partner in rod_bipolar_inputs, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the sum of drive and the release_rate of the partner.
    Set drive to the result.
    Calculate the sum of count and 1.
    Set count to the result.
End the loop.
Calculate the quotient of drive divided by count.
Calculate the product of the result and 30.
Calculate the sum of -60 and the result.
Set membrane_potential to the result.
Check if membrane_potential is greater than -48.
If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set spikelet to 15.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Set spikelet to 0.
End the conditional block.
Calculate the sum of membrane_potential and spikelet.
Calculate the difference of the result and -60.
Set swing to the result.
Calculate the product of swing and 0.3.
Display the result to the console.
Calculate the product of swing and -0.9.
Display the result to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the AII is a two-output device and a returned dictionary makes both outputs explicit side by side.

# AII amacrine cell: rod bipolar input split into an electrical ON arm
# and a sign-inverting glycinergic OFF arm.
import math

TAU_MS = 12.0                  # membrane time constant
REST_MV = -60.0                # resting potential
SPIKELET_THRESHOLD_MV = -48.0  # dendritic sodium channel threshold
SPIKELET_MV = 15.0             # small tetrodotoxin-sensitive spikelet
GAP_GAIN = 0.30                # connexin 36 coupling to ON cone bipolar cells
GLYCINE_GAIN = 0.90            # sign-inverting output to OFF cone bipolar cells
N_ROD_BIPOLARS = 28            # convergence onto one peripheral AII cell

def aii_step(vm, release, dt_ms):
    drive = sum(release) / float(len(release))
    target = REST_MV + 30.0 * drive
    vm = vm + (target - vm) * (dt_ms / TAU_MS)
    spikelet = 0.0
    if vm > SPIKELET_THRESHOLD_MV:
        spikelet = SPIKELET_MV * math.tanh((vm - SPIKELET_THRESHOLD_MV) / 4.0)
    return vm, vm + spikelet

def outputs(v_eff):
    swing = v_eff - REST_MV
    return {"to_ON_cone_bipolar_mV": round(GAP_GAIN * swing, 2),
            "to_OFF_cone_bipolar_mV": round(-GLYCINE_GAIN * max(0.0, swing), 2)}

vm = REST_MV
for t in range(120):
    photons = 1.0 if 40 <= t < 90 else 0.05      # a dim flash in darkness
    vm, v_eff = aii_step(vm, [photons] * N_ROD_BIPOLARS, 1.0)
    if t in (39, 60, 89, 119):
        print("t=%3d ms  Vm=%6.2f  effective=%6.2f  %s" % (t, vm, v_eff, outputs(v_eff)))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other neuron in the body wears knobs on one tier and a bush on the other, uses chemistry to invert a signal on one side while using raw electricity to preserve it on the other, and leaks its own inhibitory transmitter into its partners through the very junctions that couple them.

CHAPTER 209. STARBURST AMACRINE CELLS (COG)

It looks like a firework caught at the instant of bursting, and every one of its radiating spokes is a separate little computer that can tell which way the world is moving.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An amacrine cell is a local connector neuron of the inner retina, one that talks sideways to its neighbours instead of sending a fibre to the brain.

The starburst amacrine cell is the one that makes motion direction visible.

Each of its dozens of straight radiating dendrites responds more strongly to movement travelling outward from the cell body than to movement travelling inward, and this is the earliest point anywhere in the visual system where direction is computed.

It hands that answer to direction-selective ganglion cells, the output neurons that tell the brain which way something moved, by inhibiting them selectively when motion runs the wrong way.

It is also unusual in releasing two transmitters at once, one excitatory and one inhibitory.

Before birth it has a second career: it generates the slow travelling waves of activity that sweep across the immature retina and wire up the visual brain before the eyes have ever opened.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell is radially symmetric, like a dandelion clock, with slender dendrites leaving the cell body in every direction and branching only a few times.

There is no axon and no obvious main trunk, and the dendrites all stay within a single thin sheet of the inner plexiform layer, the lower wiring carpet.

Output release sites, seen as small swellings called varicosities, are confined to the outer third of each dendrite, while excitatory inputs arrive all along its length.

The population comes in two mirror-image sheets, one for light onset and one for light offset, and the two sheets sit at fixed depths that connectomics laboratories use as the retina's built-in depth ruler.

SIZE:

The cell body is about 7 to 10 micrometres across.

The dendritic field is roughly 150 micrometres wide in central retina and up to about 600 micrometres in far peripheral retina, so a single cell can span a patch of visual field about the width of your thumbnail at arm's length in the periphery.

Each dendrite is well under a micrometre thick, which is precisely what makes it electrically semi-isolated from its siblings.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There are exactly two, and they are mirror images.

The ON starburst cell keeps its cell body in the inner nuclear layer, the middle sheet of cell bodies, and spreads its dendrites in the inner half of the wiring carpet.

The OFF starburst cell has its cell body displaced downward into the ganglion cell layer and spreads its dendrites in the outer half.

WHERE IT LIVES:

ON starburst cell bodies sit in the innermost row of the inner nuclear layer; OFF starburst cell bodies sit in the ganglion cell layer, where they are called displaced amacrine cells.

Their dendrites occupy two narrow strata of the inner plexiform layer, one on each side of its midline, which are the two bands that stain for the acetylcholine-making enzyme.

They are present across the whole retina of humans and macaques, becoming sparser and wider toward the periphery.

Their dendritic sheets form the interface between the bipolar cell input layer and the direction-selective ganglion cell output layer, and the OFF cell body sits at the interface between the wiring carpet and the ganglion cell layer.

NUMBER:

Cholinergic starburst cells are 3 to 5 percent of all amacrine cells.

Measured densities in human and macaque retina run from 300 to 1,000 cells per square millimetre for the ON type and 100 to 500 cells per square millimetre for the OFF type.

A defensible estimate is about 1.2 million per eye counting both types, with a range of 0.9 to 1.5 million, so roughly 2.4 million across both eyes.

That is about 0.9 percent of the roughly 260 million neurons in the two retinas, and about 0.003 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the whole nervous system.

Confidence is moderate: the density measurements are direct and include human tissue, but the conversion to a whole-eye total assumes an even coverage that has not been verified end to end.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The starburst cell does not normally fire action potentials; it works with graded voltages and, crucially, with graded calcium signals in each dendritic tip.

It rests near minus 60 millivolts and depolarises by 5 to 20 millivolts to light in its centre.

The thin proximal dendrites have a high axial resistance, so voltage spreads poorly from one spoke to another, and each spoke therefore integrates its own inputs almost independently.

Calcium-sensitive imaging shows that the tip of a spoke responds two to three times more strongly to motion running outward along it than to motion running inward.

In macaque the cell also has a strong antagonistic surround that is not made of inhibition at all but comes from the excitatory bipolar cells feeding it, and that surround is essential for its radial motion sensitivity.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory input arrives as glutamate from ON or OFF cone bipolar cells all along each dendrite, with the important detail that bipolar cells contacting the proximal part of the dendrite respond more slowly than those contacting the distal part.

Inhibitory input arrives as GABA, gamma aminobutyric acid, from neighbouring starburst cells, forming a dense reciprocal network.

Output goes from the distal varicosities onto direction-selective ganglion cells, onto other starburst cells, and onto a spiking polyaxonal amacrine cell that is itself direction selective.

The wiring is asymmetric in a very specific way: a direction-selective ganglion cell receives acetylcholine roughly evenly from all the starburst cells around it, but receives GABA only from the starburst cells lying on its null side, the side motion comes from when it is being silenced.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Not Applicable in the ordinary sense.

The word amacrine means without a long fibre, and this cell has no axon whatsoever: each dendrite is both an input cable and an output terminal, which is exactly why the computation can be done separately in every spoke.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases acetylcholine, the transmitter of the neuromuscular junction, made by choline acetyltransferase and packaged by the vesicular acetylcholine transporter, acting on nicotinic receptors on its targets.

It simultaneously releases GABA, packaged by the vesicular inhibitory amino acid transporter and made by the enzymes GAD65 and GAD67, acting mainly on GABA-A receptors containing the alpha 2 subunit.

The two transmitters leave from the same distal region but behave differently: the acetylcholine signal is broad and largely symmetric, while the GABA signal is the directional, asymmetrically wired one.

MYELINATION:

None.

No neuron inside the human retina is myelinated, since the fatty insulation that speeds conduction elsewhere would scatter light before it reached the photoreceptors.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate in adulthood and high in development.

Acetylcholine synthesis requires continuous choline uptake through the high-affinity transporter CHT1, and the developmental waves the cell generates are sustained bursts of activity that consume energy across the entire immature retina.

PLASTICITY:

Before eye opening, starburst cells drive stage two retinal waves, slow patches of activity that crawl across the retina roughly once a minute and refine the maps that connect eye to brain.

Direction preference develops largely without visual experience, but wave activity sculpts the dendritic arbour itself.

The gene FRMD7 is required to build the asymmetric wiring, and starburst cells are among the few retinal neurons whose adult wiring depends on a single identified developmental gene.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Müller cells, the radial glia spanning the retina, and a thin layer of astrocytes at the inner surface provide the usual potassium buffering and metabolic support.

What is special here is the enzyme acetylcholinesterase, which breaks acetylcholine down and is concentrated in exactly the two thin bands where starburst dendrites run, sharpening in space and time a signal that would otherwise linger and spread.

Those same two bands, easily stained, are used as fixed depth landmarks by every laboratory reconstructing the inner retina, so this cell type doubles as the anatomical survey marker for its own neighbourhood.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove starburst cells and the retina stops reporting direction.
