Laser ablation experiments show that direction selectivity in ganglion cells disappears when these cells are killed, and the optokinetic reflex, the automatic eye movement that keeps a moving scene stable on the retina, is lost.

The region would still see edges, brightness and colour perfectly well; it would simply become blind to which way anything was going.

In development, its removal would leave the visual brain without the waves that pre-wire its maps.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This is the textbook example of dendritic computation, where the real work happens in a branch rather than in the cell body.

Each spoke combines three ingredients: a space and time gradient in the bipolar cell input, with slow inputs near the soma and fast inputs near the tip; a chloride gradient inside the dendrite that changes how inhibition acts along its length; and mutual GABAergic inhibition from overlapping neighbours.

Calcium enters only in microdomains at the distal varicosities, so transmitter release is controlled locally rather than by the whole-cell voltage.

Two transmitters are co-released from the same region but with different thresholds and different wiring rules, which is a rare form of dual signalling.

Acetylcholine also acts partly by volume transmission, diffusing a short distance to reach receptors that are not directly opposite a release site.

In the immature retina the same cell is a rhythm generator, using a slow after-hyperpolarisation and recurrent cholinergic excitation among starburst cells to launch and terminate waves.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from ON or OFF cone bipolar cells at 20 to 40 sites distributed along each dendrite, with a proximal-to-distal latency gradient of roughly 10 to 30 milliseconds.

• INPUTS: GABA from 10 to 30 overlapping neighbouring starburst cells, 0 to 1 normalised.

• INPUTS: a strong non-GABAergic antagonistic surround carried by the bipolar cell input itself.

• OUTPUTS: acetylcholine onto nicotinic receptors of direction-selective ganglion cells, broadly and near symmetrically.

• OUTPUTS: GABA onto GABA-A alpha 2 receptors of direction-selective ganglion cells, wired only from the null side, and onto neighbouring starburst cells.

• STATE: per-dendrite membrane voltage, per-varicosity calcium concentration, internal chloride concentration, vesicle pools for acetylcholine and for GABA, and in development an after-hyperpolarisation variable.

• PARAMETERS: dendritic field 150 to 600 micrometres, about 8 to 12 primary dendrites, release sites in the distal third only, resting voltage minus 60 millivolts, direction selectivity index 0.3 to 0.8.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 15 to 30 milliseconds, proximal-to-distal delay 10 to 30 milliseconds, calcium microdomain 50 to 200 milliseconds, acetylcholine clearance a few milliseconds, developmental waves once every 30 to 120 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: mutation of FRMD7 leaves the GABAergic wiring symmetric instead of asymmetric, horizontal direction selectivity is lost, and the result in people is X-linked idiopathic infantile nystagmus, in which the eyes oscillate because the horizontal optokinetic reflex has no directional signal to work with.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the direction preference of one starburst amacrine cell dendrite by testing whether its inputs arrive together at the tip.
Create a number variable called site_count with initial value 8.
Create a number variable called spacing with initial value 15.
Create a number variable called speed with initial value 0.5.
Create a number variable called travel with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called outward_arrivals with initial value empty list.
Create a list variable called inward_arrivals with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called index with initial value 0.
While index is less than site_count, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the difference of site_count and 1.
    Calculate the difference of the result and index.
    Calculate the product of the result and spacing.
    Calculate the quotient of the result divided by speed.
    Set travel to the result.
    Calculate the product of index and spacing.
    Calculate the quotient of the result divided by speed.
    Calculate the sum of the result and travel.
    Add the result to the end of outward_arrivals.
    Calculate the sum of travel and travel.
    Add the result to the end of inward_arrivals.
    Calculate the sum of index and 1.
    Set index to the result.
End the loop.
Call the function named count_coincidences.
Calculate the difference of outward_peak and inward_peak.
Calculate the sum of outward_peak and inward_peak.
Calculate the quotient of the difference divided by the sum.
Display the result to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the whole computation is a small set of delayed arrival times, and plain lists show the coincidence at the dendritic tip without hiding it in a solver.

# Starburst amacrine dendrite: why motion outward from the soma wins.
# Inputs are picked up along the dendrite and must travel to the tip,
# where the release sites are. Outward motion makes them arrive together.
N_SITES = 8                    # bipolar cell inputs along one dendrite
SITE_SPACING_UM = 15.0         # spacing between input sites
PROPAGATION_UM_PER_MS = 0.5    # spread of depolarisation toward the tip
WINDOW_MS = 8.0                # coincidence window at the release sites

def arrival_times(stimulus_speed, outward):
    """When each input's contribution reaches the distal tip."""
    times = []
    for i in range(N_SITES):
        if outward:
            reached = i * SITE_SPACING_UM / stimulus_speed
        else:
            reached = (N_SITES - 1 - i) * SITE_SPACING_UM / stimulus_speed
        travel = (N_SITES - 1 - i) * SITE_SPACING_UM / PROPAGATION_UM_PER_MS
        times.append(reached + travel)
    return times

def peak_coincidence(times):
    """Largest number of inputs overlapping within the window."""
    best = 0
    for t in range(0, 500):
        count = 0
        for a in times:
            if abs(t - a) <= WINDOW_MS:
                count += 1
        best = max(best, count)
    return best

speed = 0.5                                            # image speed in um per ms
out = peak_coincidence(arrival_times(speed, True))     # centrifugal, soma to tip
inn = peak_coincidence(arrival_times(speed, False))    # centripetal, tip to soma
print("centrifugal: %d of %d inputs coincide" % (out, N_SITES))
print("centripetal: %d of %d inputs coincide" % (inn, N_SITES))
print("direction selectivity index: %.2f" % ((out - inn) / float(out + inn)))
# prints 8, then 1, then 0.78

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Nothing else in the nervous system looks like a perfect radial firework with its output swellings confined to the outer third of every spoke, releases an excitatory and an inhibitory transmitter from the same varicosities, and computes a different answer in each of its own dendrites while the cell body sits there knowing nothing about any of it.

CHAPTER 210. A17 AMACRINE CELLS (Rod-Signal Curbing Neuron) (COG)

On a moonless night, the A17 cell is what stops your retina from shouting.

A17 is a serial number from a classification table and says nothing.

Rod-Signal names the traffic it polices, because its varicosities are driven by glutamate released from rod bipolar cell terminals and by nothing else of consequence.

Curbing names the work, since each varicosity releases the inhibitory transmitter gamma-aminobutyric acid straight back onto the same rod bipolar terminal that just excited it, cutting the depolarisation short, which sharpens the timing of dim-light signals and improves the signal-to-noise ratio of the rod pathway.

Neuron marks it as a cell gathering from around one hundred rod bipolar cells, yet keeping each contact electrically private to its own varicosity, so the restraint is applied terminal by terminal rather than across the whole cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The A17 is a wide-field inhibitory interneuron, a nerve cell that talks only to other cells inside the retina, and it returns the calming chemical gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) straight back to the rod bipolar terminal that just excited it.

That loop, called reciprocal feedback, gives night vision automatic gain control, so weak signals pass and the rod channel never saturates.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Picture a spider whose legs are threads of glass strung with a thousand beads.

Fifteen to thirty fine unbranched processes radiate from the cell body, and every few micrometres each swells into a bead, called a varicosity, that takes glutamate in and pushes GABA out at the same spot; there is no axon.

SIZE:

The cell body is 8 to 10 micrometres across and the dendritic field approaches one millimetre in the cat, with human peripheral cells of similar order.

Each bead is about 1 micrometre wide and holds roughly one cubic micrometre, small enough that a trickle of calcium raises the local concentration fast.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The cell is A17 in cat, rat and monkey and S1 and S2 in rabbit, the rabbit names coming from its habit of soaking up indoleamines such as serotonin.

Human gene-expression atlases split the wide-field GABA amacrines into several clusters, so there are probably two or three near-identical human variants, though the count is unsettled.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The cell body sits in the innermost row of the inner nuclear layer, at its interface with the inner plexiform layer, the carpet where bipolar cells meet ganglion cells.

Its processes occupy stratum 5 of sublamina b, exactly where rod bipolar terminals end, and it is densest 3 to 6 millimetres from the centre of gaze and absent from the rod-free foveal centre.

NUMBER:

Roughly 300,000 to 800,000 per eye, centred near 600,000, at low confidence because it is scaled from cat, rat and rabbit densities onto a human retina of about 1000 square millimetres.

That is 1 to 3 percent of the eye's 30 million amacrine cells and about 0.001 percent of the 86 billion neurons in the human nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

A17 cells are graded, not spiking, signalling by smooth swings of 10 to 40 millivolts from a resting level near minus 60 millivolts.

Each bead is isolated from its neighbours by the thin process between them, and large-conductance calcium-activated potassium channels, called BK channels, open as calcium rises and keep each response local.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

One cell collects glutamate from several thousand rod bipolar terminals through calcium-permeable AMPA receptors, fast glutamate receptors that admit calcium as well as sodium.

It returns GABA onto fast GABA type A and slow GABA type C receptors, and it carries nicotinic acetylcholine receptors and gap junctions, direct pores, to neighbouring A17 cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Not Applicable, for a structural reason and not an omission: the A17 has no axon, every bead is both input and output, and no fibre carries signal away from the cell.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA is the working transmitter, and the stored serotonin behind the old name indoleamine-accumulating amacrine is a marker, not the message.

Release is triggered by calcium arriving through the glutamate receptor itself, skipping the voltage-gated calcium channels almost every other synapse needs.

MYELINATION:

None, as for every retinal neuron, because myelin is opaque and would sit directly in the light path.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low, since the cell never spikes; the recurring cost is clearing calcium from a thousand beads within tens of milliseconds each.

PLASTICITY:

Feedback weakens over minutes as the retina moves into daylight, dopamine from neighbouring dopaminergic amacrine cells uncouples A17 cells from each other, and the synapses depress during long bright stimuli.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Muller cells, the tall radial glia spanning the retina, clear GABA from the tiny reciprocal cleft within milliseconds using the GAT-3 transporter and supply the glutamine from which it is rebuilt.

Without that, one bead's private feedback would leak into its neighbours' and the cell's thousandfold independence would collapse.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete the A17 and rod bipolar output saturates, so starlight and moonlight collapse into a single reading of bright.

Responses grow noisier and longer, and a faint moving shape in a dark field becomes undetectable.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This is a neuron that is not one computer but a thousand, each bead running a complete input-to-output loop in parallel.

Release bypasses voltage-gated calcium channels, BK channels form a brake that switches itself on, gap junctions fuse many private loops into one for large stimuli, and acetylcholine and dopamine set behaviour from outside the cell's own input stream.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate conductance per varicosity from one rod bipolar terminal, 0 to about 1 nanosiemens; acetylcholine and dopamine, 0 to 1 normalised; gap-junction current from neighbours, in picoamperes.

• OUTPUTS: GABA release probability per varicosity, 0 to 1, onto the terminal that supplied the input.

• STATE: per-varicosity voltage, free calcium, BK activation, vesicle pool.

• PARAMETERS: about 1000 varicosities, 2 picofarads each, calcium fraction of receptor current about 0.06, release half-point near 1 micromolar with cooperativity about 4.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 4 milliseconds, calcium clearance 20 milliseconds, GABA type A decay 10 milliseconds, GABA type C decay 100 milliseconds, adaptation minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: when rods die in retinitis pigmentosa the loop remodels and breaks, and blocking A17 inhibition experimentally gives oversized sluggish rod responses and poor detection of dim moving targets.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn glutamate arriving at one A17 varicosity into a pulse of inhibitory feedback sent straight back to the rod bipolar terminal that released it.
Create a number variable called calcium with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called calcium squared with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called release with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called feedback trace with initial value empty list.
For each glutamate sample in the input stream, perform the following operations.
    Set calcium to the sum of calcium and the product of glutamate sample and 0.06.
    Set calcium to the difference of calcium and the product of calcium and 0.45.
    Check if calcium is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set calcium to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set calcium squared to the product of calcium and calcium.
    Set release to the quotient of calcium squared divided by the sum of 1 and calcium squared.
    Add release to the end of feedback trace.
End the loop.
Display feedback trace to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: one varicosity is plain scalar arithmetic and the units stay visible.
DT, C_M, G_LEAK = 1e-4, 2e-12, 1e-10         # s, farads, siemens
E_LEAK, E_AMPA, E_K = -0.060, 0.0, -0.085    # volts
G_BK_MAX, K_BK = 5e-10, 2e-6                 # siemens, molar
CA_FRACTION, CA_REST, TAU_CA = 0.06, 5e-8, 0.020
FARADAY, VOL, BUFFER = 96485.0, 1e-15, 0.01  # C/mol, litres, free fraction
KD_REL, HILL = 1.0e-6, 4.0
def a17_varicosity(g_trace):
    v, ca, out = E_LEAK, CA_REST, []
    for g in g_trace:                        # glutamate conductance, siemens
        g_bk = G_BK_MAX * ca / (ca + K_BK)   # calcium switches the brake on
        i_ampa = g * (v - E_AMPA)            # negative is inward
        i_tot = G_LEAK * (v - E_LEAK) + i_ampa + g_bk * (v - E_K)
        v += (-i_tot / C_M) * DT
        ca += ((-CA_FRACTION * i_ampa * BUFFER) / (2 * FARADAY * VOL)
               - (ca - CA_REST) / TAU_CA) * DT
        ca = max(ca, CA_REST)
        out.append(ca ** HILL / (ca ** HILL + KD_REL ** HILL))
    return out
print("peak release: %.4f" % max(a17_varicosity([0.0]*50 + [4e-10]*300 + [0.0]*400)))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other neuron in the body is a thousand independent computers strung along threads of glass.

An axonless cell whose beads take glutamate in and push GABA out at the same spot, driven by calcium coming through the glutamate receptor itself, is an A17.

CHAPTER 211. DOPAMINERGIC AMACRINE CELLS (Day-Mode Switching Neuron) (COG)

Perhaps twenty thousand of these cells, in an eye holding well over a hundred million neurons, decide whether the retina runs in daytime mode or night-time mode.

Dopaminergic is a transmitter barcode, naming the chemical in the cell's vesicles rather than the job the chemical does.

Day-Mode names the state the cell installs, because its output uncouples the electrical junctions of the night circuitry and tunes horizontal and bipolar cells for bright, high-acuity, colour-rich vision.

Switching names the act of changing the retina between two whole operating regimes rather than carrying a picture, and the switch is thrown both by ambient light and by an internal circadian clock that this cell helps reset.

Neuron marks it as the sole source of dopamine in the entire retina, driven in part by the melanopsin ganglion cells of the innermost layer, so the signal travels backwards through the sheet.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is the retina's own dopamine factory, dopamine being the signalling chemical the midbrain loses in Parkinson's disease.

It behaves less like a wire and more like a gland, spreading dopamine to thousands of cells it never touches, after which gap junctions close, receptive fields shrink, the rod pathway switches off, the cone pathway switches on, and the eye's growth is restrained.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Imagine a fishing net cast over the whole retina, woven from only a few thousand cells.

Two or three thick branches leave a large cell body into a dense plexus hugging stratum 1, the outermost stratum of the inner plexiform layer, and the signature is the ring: processes wrap in necklaces around AII amacrine cell bodies.

SIZE:

The cell body is 10 to 15 micrometres across, at the large end for an amacrine cell.

The dendritic field spans 400 to 1500 micrometres with total process length in millimetres, so few cells cover enormous ground and overlap heavily, giving a coverage factor in the tens.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Cells staining strongly for tyrosine hydroxylase, the enzyme that makes dopamine, are called TH1 and form the classic stratum 1 plexus, while weakly staining TH2 cells are sparser and poorly understood.

A second split is positional, between cells in the inner nuclear layer and those displaced into the ganglion cell layer, and a third is functional, since some also reach the outer plexiform layer and count as interplexiform cells.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies lie at the inner border of the inner nuclear layer, at its interface with the inner plexiform layer, with a minority displaced into the ganglion cell layer.

The plexus sits in stratum 1 of the inner plexiform layer, ascending processes cross to the outer plexiform layer where photoreceptors meet bipolar and horizontal cells, and density is highest near the centre of gaze and parafovea.

NUMBER:

Human whole-mount counts give 10 to 30 cells per square millimetre, about 18 in healthy controls per Ortuno-Lizaran and colleagues in 2020, so 10,000 to 30,000 per eye at moderate confidence from few samples.

That is 0.03 to 0.1 percent of the eye's amacrine cells and about 0.00005 percent of the 86 billion neurons in the nervous system, one of the rarest named neuron types in the body.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Unusually for an amacrine cell this one fires true action potentials, the sharp all-or-nothing pulses used by long-range neurons.

It is a pacemaker, ticking at 3 to 10 spikes per second in complete darkness with no input at all and rising several-fold in light, with a slow calcium-activated potassium current after each spike as the metronome.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Glutamate arrives from ON bipolar cells and from melanopsin-containing intrinsically photosensitive retinal ganglion cells, a rare case of a ganglion cell signalling backwards into the retina.

Most output is not synaptic: dopamine diffuses to D1 receptors on horizontal cells, cone terminals and AII cells, and to D2 and D4 receptors on photoreceptors and on itself.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Strictly there is no axon, but long beaded processes leave the plexus, travel a millimetre or more, carry action potentials and release transmitter along the way, unmyelinated and conducting well under one metre per second, because slow wide spread is the point.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Dopamine, built from tyrosine by tyrosine hydroxylase, the same rate-limiting enzyme used by the midbrain neurons that die in Parkinson's disease.

Most of these cells also release GABA, and dopamine output follows a daily rhythm peaking by day and opposing melatonin at night.

MYELINATION:

None, as for every retinal neuron, because myelin is opaque and the retina must stay clear.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High and distinctive: the cell spikes day and night and runs a synthesis pathway needing oxygen, iron and the cofactor tetrahydrobiopterin, while dopamine oxidises into damaging by-products that demand constant antioxidant defence.

PLASTICITY:

Dopamine synthesis rises over hours in bright light and falls in darkness, and tyrosine hydroxylase expression is under circadian control.

Bright outdoor light raises retinal dopamine in children, the leading explanation for why time outdoors slows short-sightedness.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Muller glia here carry the transporters and enzymes that destroy dopamine, so they set how far each molecule travels and are the physical medium of the signal rather than bystanders.

Microglia, the resident immune cells, cluster around these processes early in Parkinson's disease and their activation tracks the loss of the plexus.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the retina loses its day and night gearbox, leaving horizontal and AII amacrine cells permanently coupled so receptive fields stay blurry in sunlight.

Contrast sensitivity drops, light adaptation turns sluggish, photoreceptor renewal drifts, and in a growing child the eye elongates into myopia.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its main output is volume transmission, chemical broadcast into tissue rather than point-to-point synapses, so a synapse-only model misses almost everything it does.

It is intrinsically rhythmic, receives a signal from a ganglion cell against the usual direction of flow, gates gap junctions across three other cell classes within minutes, runs on a circadian clock, spans two plexiform layers, and takes part in emmetropisation, the loop matching the eye's length to its optics.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from ON bipolar and melanopsin ganglion cells, 0 to 1 normalised with irradiance logarithmic; GABA from amacrine cells; circadian phase, 0 to 24 hours.

• OUTPUTS: spikes, 0 to about 30 hertz, and a dopamine field of nanomolar to low micromolar reaching horizontal cells, cone terminals, AII and A8 amacrine cells, outer segments and the retinal pigment epithelium.

• STATE: membrane voltage, afterhyperpolarisation, calcium, vesicular dopamine store, tyrosine hydroxylase activity.

• PARAMETERS: dark rate 3 to 10 hertz, light rate 10 to 30 hertz, dendritic field 400 to 1500 micrometres, density 10 to 30 cells per square millimetre.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 30 milliseconds, afterhyperpolarisation 300 milliseconds, dopamine clearance tens of seconds, uncoupling minutes, growth effects weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: in Parkinson's disease these cells fall by 26 to 58 percent by region and contacts onto AII cells drop about 60 percent, and patients lose contrast sensitivity and colour discrimination and sleep badly.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to run the dopaminergic amacrine cell as a self-ticking pacemaker whose rate rises in light and whose dopamine builds slowly in the surrounding tissue.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value negative 0.055.
Create a number variable called afterhyperpolarisation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called dopamine with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called push with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
For each light sample in the irradiance stream, perform the following operations.
    Set push to the difference of negative 0.055 and voltage.
    Set push to the sum of push and 0.030.
    Set push to the sum of push and the product of light sample and 0.010.
    Set push to the difference of push and afterhyperpolarisation.
    Set voltage to the sum of voltage and the product of push and 0.0333.
    Set afterhyperpolarisation to the difference of afterhyperpolarisation and the product of afterhyperpolarisation and 0.0033.
    Check if voltage is greater than or equal to negative 0.040.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set voltage to negative 0.060.
        Set afterhyperpolarisation to the sum of afterhyperpolarisation and 0.006.
        Set spike count to the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set dopamine to the sum of dopamine and 0.01.
    End the conditional block.
    Set dopamine to the difference of dopamine and the product of dopamine and 0.001.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C: a whole-retina model runs this pacemaker for thousands of cells every millisecond. */
#include <stdio.h>
#define DT          0.001    /* seconds per step */
#define V_REST     -0.055
#define V_TH       -0.040    /* volts, threshold */
#define V_RESET    -0.060
#define TAU_M       0.030    /* seconds */
#define TAU_AHP     0.300    /* seconds, the metronome */
#define AHP_STEP    0.006    /* volts of brake per spike */
#define DRIVE       0.030    /* volts of intrinsic drive */
#define LIGHT_GAIN  0.010    /* volts per unit melanopsin drive */
static int run(double light, double seconds)
{
    double v = V_REST, ahp = 0.0, drive = DRIVE + LIGHT_GAIN * light;
    int spikes = 0, k, steps = (int)(seconds / DT);
    for (k = 0; k < steps; k++) {
        v += (((V_REST - v) + drive - ahp) / TAU_M) * DT;
        ahp -= (ahp / TAU_AHP) * DT;
        if (v >= V_TH) { v = V_RESET; ahp += AHP_STEP; spikes++; }
    }
    return spikes;
}
int main(void)
{
    printf("darkness: %.1f Hz\n", run(0.0, 20.0) / 20.0);
    printf("daylight: %.1f Hz\n", run(1.0, 20.0) / 20.0);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Stain a whole human retina for tyrosine hydroxylase and almost nothing lights up, because there are only tens of thousands of these cells in a sheet of well over a hundred million.

What appears is a delicate net wearing rings around other cells' bodies, and a large sparse spontaneously spiking amacrine cell that makes dopamine and follows a daily rhythm can be nothing else.

CHAPTER 212. POLYAXONAL AMACRINE CELLS (Self-Motion Cancelling Neuron) (COG)

Almost every neuron inside the retina is a whisperer, but this one fires real nerve impulses that ripple outward for millimetres like a stone dropped in a pond.

Polyaxonal describes anatomy in Greek, meaning many axons, and leaves the purpose invisible.

Self-Motion names the thing the cell detects, motion sweeping across the whole visual field at once, which is overwhelmingly caused by the observer's own eye and head movements rather than by anything in the world.

Cancelling names the service, because these wide-field spiking cells pool rectified input from bipolar terminals across the surround and then suppress object-motion-sensitive ganglion cells whenever background and object move together, so the retina reports only genuine differential motion.

Neuron marks it as the cell that keeps a moving object visible while the smear produced by your own eyes is subtracted away before the signal ever leaves the eye.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A polyaxonal amacrine cell is an inhibitory retinal interneuron, a cell that stays inside the eye, which breaks the amacrine rule by growing four to ten axons, an axon being the long output fibre a neuron uses to send signals over distance.

It spots a sudden change in one small patch of the image and delivers a wave of the calming chemical gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) to distant patches milliseconds later, which is how the retina tells object motion apart from motion caused by your own eye.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A compact bushy dendritic tree gathers input from bipolar cells within a small circle, while long fine beaded axons leave that circle and run outward across the retina.

Signals travel inward along the dendrites but not along the axons, so the axons are true one-way cables.

SIZE:

The cell body is 10 to 15 micrometres across and the dendritic field of the classic A1 type in monkey is about 400 micrometres, roughly the width of a full stop on this page.

The axonal field spreads up to 4 to 5 millimetres, so the output territory is about a hundred times the area of the input territory.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The best characterised is A1, whose dendrites sit in stratum 3, the middle of the inner plexiform layer, and which answers both brightening and dimming; rabbit work adds a family labelled PA1 to PA5.

Greschner and colleagues in 2014 recorded polyaxonal cells from macaque retina on multielectrode arrays and found several populations each tiling the retina, so treat the human count as at least three and probably five or more.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies sit at the inner border of the inner nuclear layer, at the interface with the inner plexiform layer, with a minority displaced into the ganglion cell layer.

A1 dendrites occupy stratum 3 at the ON and OFF boundary, the axons run in strata 2 to 4 crossing under other cells' territories without synapsing on them, and the type thins near the centre of gaze.

NUMBER:

A 400 micrometre dendritic field at a coverage factor of one to two implies 10 to 30 cells per square millimetre, so 10,000 to 30,000 cells of one polyaxonal type per eye and 50,000 to 200,000 across the class, at low confidence because this is inferred from field size rather than counted in human tissue.

The class is 0.2 to 0.7 percent of the eye's 30 million amacrine cells and around 0.0002 percent of the 86 billion neurons in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

This cell fires full sodium action potentials, rare among amacrine cells, starting them near the cell body and propagating outward along every axon at once, slowly, because the axons are thin and bare.

Light responses are ON and OFF, strongly transient, sparse, and strikingly coordinated across neighbours when the whole image changes.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input is glutamate from both ON and OFF bipolar cells onto the dendritic arbor, plus inhibition from other amacrine cells that builds an antagonistic surround.

Output is GABA from thousands of axonal varicosities onto distant bipolar terminals, amacrine cells and ganglion cell dendrites, and same-type neighbours are joined by gap junctions, direct pores between cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Four to ten unmyelinated beaded axons per cell, each 0.2 to 0.5 micrometres thick, conducting at a fraction of a metre per second so that distance becomes delay.

That delay is the point: it lets the retina compare what is happening here now with what happened there a few milliseconds ago.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA in the large wide-field types such as A1, and glycine, the other main inhibitory chemical of the inner retina, in some smaller polyaxonal cells.

MYELINATION:

None, because myelin is white and opaque and would sit in the light path, so the retina buys transparency at the cost of slow conduction.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate to high for an interneuron, since spiking costs sodium pumping and the cell must maintain more than 20 millimetres of thin bare membrane, with mitochondria concentrated in the axonal varicosities.

PLASTICITY:

Gap-junction coupling between neighbours is modulated by dopamine and nitric oxide, so the synchronised group changes size between day and night, and transient response strength adapts to recent contrast over hundreds of milliseconds.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Human retinal astrocytes live almost entirely in the nerve fibre layer and hardly touch this cell, so its partner is the Muller cell, the tall radial glia spanning the retina.

Two Muller jobs matter here: clearing potassium along an axon that dumps it into a very narrow space over millimetres, and taking up GABA at the distant varicosities so inhibition stays where it was aimed.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take this cell away and the retina loses its sense of elsewhere, because every other inner retinal circuit compares a point with its immediate neighbourhood and only this one compares a point with somewhere millimetres away.

An object moving against a still background would then look identical to the whole world sliding because your eye moved.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The signature computation is a travelling wave, so a point-neuron model cannot represent it and the cell must be simulated as a cable with delay.

Input and output are spatially separated, gap junctions synchronise neighbours into a sheet-wide alarm, fine patterns averaging to zero still drive it because summation is nonlinear, and it enforces one-way conduction without myelin.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from ON and OFF bipolar cells within the dendritic field, 0 to 1 normalised; GABA and glycine from surrounding amacrine cells; gap-junction current from neighbours, in picoamperes.

• OUTPUTS: sparse transient spikes, 0 to 60 hertz in a burst, and GABA at thousands of varicosities up to 2.5 millimetres away.

• STATE: somatic voltage, refractory timer, position of every spike travelling on every axon, calcium per varicosity.

• PARAMETERS: dendritic field 400 micrometres, axonal field up to 5 millimetres, 4 to 10 axons, segment length 100 micrometres, conduction about 0.5 metres per second, refractory period 10 milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 milliseconds, axonal delay 0.2 milliseconds per 100 micrometres, contrast adaptation 100 to 300 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: if long-range inhibition fails the retina reports self-generated motion as world motion, and in advanced glaucoma and post-degeneration remodelling these cells fire spontaneous synchronised bursts, a likely source of flickering phantom images.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to fire a spike when local contrast changes and then carry it outward along several axons, releasing inhibition at each varicosity as the wave passes.
Create a number variable called steps since spike with initial value 1000.
Create a number variable called release count with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called travelling spikes with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called spike position with initial value 0.
For each drive sample in the bipolar input stream, perform the following operations.
    Set steps since spike to the sum of steps since spike and 1.
    Check if both drive sample is greater than 0.5 and steps since spike is greater than 100 are true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set steps since spike to 0.
        Repeat the following steps exactly 4 times.
            Add a new spike record with segment 0 and countdown 2 to the end of travelling spikes.
        End the loop.
    End the conditional block.
    Set spike position to 0.
    For each spike record in travelling spikes, perform the following operations.
        Set the countdown of spike record to the difference of the countdown of spike record and 1.
        Check if the countdown of spike record is greater than 0.
        If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
            Set spike position to the sum of spike position and 1.
            Skip to the next iteration of the loop.
        End the conditional block.
        Set release count to the sum of release count and 1.
        Set the segment of spike record to the sum of the segment of spike record and 1.
        Set the countdown of spike record to 2.
        Check if the segment of spike record is greater than or equal to 20.
        If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
            Remove the item at position spike position from travelling spikes.
        Otherwise, perform the following actions.
            Set spike position to the sum of spike position and 1.
        End the conditional block.
    End the loop.
End the loop.
Display release count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: the propagating spike is naturally a small list of moving markers.
DT, SEG_LENGTH, VELOCITY = 1e-4, 100e-6, 0.5   # s, metres, metres per second
N_SEG, N_AXONS = 20, 4                         # 2 mm of reach on 4 axons
THRESHOLD, REFRACTORY = 0.5, 0.010             # drive to fire, seconds
DELAY = int(round(SEG_LENGTH / VELOCITY / DT)) # steps to cross one segment
def polyaxonal(drive_trace):
    last, pending = -1.0, []                   # (arrival step, axon, segment)
    release = [[0.0] * N_SEG for _ in range(N_AXONS)]
    for step, drive in enumerate(drive_trace):
        if drive > THRESHOLD and step * DT - last > REFRACTORY:
            last = step * DT
            for axon in range(N_AXONS):
                pending.append((step + DELAY, axon, 0))   # launch on all axons
        moving = []
        for arrival, axon, seg in pending:
            if arrival == step:
                release[axon][seg] += 1.0      # wave passes, GABA goes out
                if seg + 1 < N_SEG:
                    moving.append((step + DELAY, axon, seg + 1))
            else:
                moving.append((arrival, axon, seg))
        pending = moving
    return release
out = polyaxonal([0.0] * 100 + [1.0] * 5 + [0.0] * 400)
print("varicosities released:", sum(1 for a in out for x in a if x > 0))
print("time to the rim: %.1f ms" % (N_SEG * DELAY * DT * 1e3))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Lay retina on a grid of electrodes and watch: most cells light one electrode, but this one lights an expanding ring over several milliseconds.

An amacrine cell with a small bushy input tree, several long thin output axons, real sodium spikes and a receptive field far smaller than its projective field is a polyaxonal amacrine cell.

CHAPTER 213. INTERPLEXIFORM CELLS (Inner-To-Outer Return Neuron) (COG)

Information in the retina is supposed to flow one way, from light to brain, and the interplexiform cell is the smuggler that carries messages back up the river.

Interplexiform is an address, saying only that the cell sits between the two synaptic layers.

Inner-To-Outer names the direction of travel, which is the cell's whole defining oddity, since retinal traffic otherwise runs outward-to-inward from photoreceptor to ganglion cell and this is the one class that carries information the other way.

Return names the act of sending a report back to the first synaptic layer, where the signal modulates horizontal cell receptive field size and light responsiveness, adjusting how the retina samples its own input.

Neuron marks it as a cell whose direction and destination are both firmly established, the name deliberately not overclaiming a single computation because the transmitter and the exact role differ between species.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An interplexiform cell is defined purely by address: it has branches in both of the retina's wiring layers, the outer plexiform layer where photoreceptors hand over to bipolar and horizontal cells, and the inner plexiform layer where bipolar cells hand over to ganglion cells.

Every other retinal interneuron works in one layer, so this is the retina's only internal feedback line from the deep retina back to the first synapse in vision, and it carries a state signal rather than an image.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body sits among the amacrine cells at the inner edge of the inner nuclear layer, so at first glance it looks ordinary.

The giveaway is one to three thin ascending processes that climb across the whole thickness of that layer and spread in the outer plexiform layer, and both arbors are beaded and both release transmitter.

SIZE:

The cell body is about 8 to 12 micrometres across, and the ascending processes cross a human inner nuclear layer roughly 20 to 40 micrometres thick.

Each arbor is wide-field, several hundred micrometres to more than a millimetre across.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Macaque retina, the best guide to human, holds at least two chemically distinct populations, one staining for tyrosine hydroxylase, the enzyme that makes dopamine, the other for gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the main inhibitory chemical.

Other vertebrates add glycine-releasing and somatostatin-releasing forms, so the human total is probably two to four subtypes and is unsettled; in fish the dopaminergic form is huge and dominant, which is why textbook pictures overstate its prominence in humans.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell body in the inner nuclear layer at its interface with the inner plexiform layer, descending arbor usually in stratum 1 alongside the dopaminergic plexus.

Ascending processes cross the entire inner nuclear layer and end in the outer plexiform layer among horizontal cell dendrites and cone pedicles, so the cell occupies the interface between outer and inner retina and is the only human retinal neuron defined by spanning it.

NUMBER:

There is no published direct count in human retina, and that must be said plainly rather than papered over.

The dopaminergic subset is a fraction of the 10,000 to 30,000 dopaminergic amacrine cells per eye and the GABAergic form is called sparse in every primate study, so a defensible upper bound is 50,000 per eye with a central estimate of 5,000 to 20,000, under 0.1 percent of the eye's amacrine cells and about 0.00003 percent of the 86 billion neurons in the nervous system, at low confidence.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The dopaminergic form fires slow sustained action potentials at a few hertz in darkness and faster in light, while the GABAergic form is thought to respond in a graded way with smooth voltage changes and no spikes.

Both are sustained rather than transient, fitting a cell that reports the average state of the retina over seconds to minutes.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Almost all input arrives in the inner plexiform layer from amacrine and bipolar cells and, in some species, from centrifugal fibres entering the eye from the brain.

Outer-layer output goes chiefly to horizontal cells, switching on D1-type dopamine receptors that raise cyclic adenosine monophosphate and close their gap junctions, shrinking a wide electrical sheet into something local and sharpening every nearby bipolar surround.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Not Applicable in the strict sense, because these cells have no classical axon with a myelinated trunk and a distant terminal field.

The ascending process is functionally axon-like, being thin, directional and ending in a releasing arbor, but it lacks an axon initial segment and is only tens of micrometres long.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Dopamine in the tyrosine hydroxylase positive subset, acting mostly by volume transmission, meaning diffusion to receptors on cells the process never touches, and GABA in the other subset at conventional synapses onto horizontal cells and bipolar dendrites.

MYELINATION:

None, in common with every retinal neuron, because the retina must stay optically clear and myelin is opaque.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate, though the dopaminergic subset carries the same expensive synthesis and antioxidant burden as dopaminergic amacrine cells, and the long thin ascending processes depend on transport from the cell body, so they fail first under energy stress.

PLASTICITY:

This cell is a plasticity device in its own right, since its purpose is to change other cells' properties, and its output rises in sustained bright light and falls after prolonged darkness over hours.

In fish the number of its outer-layer synapses measurably changes within hours of a light and dark switch, with evidence for slower turnover in mammals.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Muller cells form the sleeves through which the ascending processes travel, so the glia physically define the route between the two plexiform layers.

They also clear dopamine and GABA from the outer plexiform layer, and because that layer has a small extracellular volume the glial clearance rate sets how far and how long the signal spreads.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove interplexiform cells and the outer retina goes deaf to the inner retina, so the first synapse in vision is tuned only by its own local light level.

Horizontal cells stay broadly coupled, bipolar surrounds stay wide, edges look softer, and the retina adapts to a sudden change in ambient light more slowly and less completely.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The defining feature is retrograde information flow inside a sensory sheet, which no integrate-and-fire model captures because such models have no notion of direction reversal.

It uses volume transmission, its main effect is to change a target's gap-junction conductance rather than to signal through it, it works through the second messenger cyclic adenosine monophosphate so effects outlast the stimulus, and in some species it is the target of centrifugal fibres from the brain.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate and GABA from amacrine and bipolar cells in the inner plexiform layer, 0 to 1 normalised; ambient light integrated over seconds as log illuminance.

• OUTPUTS: dopamine or GABA into the outer plexiform layer onto horizontal cells, cone pedicles and bipolar dendrites, and into stratum 1 of the inner plexiform layer onto AII and other amacrine cells.

• STATE: membrane voltage, transmitter store in each of two arbors, cyclic adenosine monophosphate level in target horizontal cells.

• PARAMETERS: arbor diameter 300 to 1200 micrometres per layer, 1 to 3 ascending processes, ascending path 20 to 40 micrometres, half-maximal uncoupling dopamine in the high nanomolar range.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane tens of milliseconds, outer-layer clearance seconds, gap-junction uncoupling 30 seconds to several minutes, light and dark cycle effects hours.

• FAILURE MODE: losing this line removes state-dependent control of the first visual synapse, and in Parkinson's disease, where the dopaminergic population shrinks by a quarter to a half, patients show the predicted loss of contrast sensitivity and poor adaptation to changing light.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to read the overall light level in the inner retina, release dopamine into the outer plexiform layer, and use it to close the gap junctions between horizontal cells.
Create a number variable called dopamine with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called coupling with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called target coupling with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called half point with initial value 0.5.
Create a list variable called coupling trace with initial value empty list.
For each luminance sample in the ambient light stream, perform the following operations.
    Set dopamine to the sum of dopamine and the product of 0.0017 and the difference of luminance sample and dopamine.
    Set target coupling to the quotient of half point divided by the sum of half point and dopamine.
    Set coupling to the sum of coupling and the product of 0.01 and the difference of target coupling and coupling.
    Check if coupling is less than 0.05.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set coupling to 0.05.
    End the conditional block.
    Add coupling to the end of coupling trace.
End the loop.
Display coupling to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

// JavaScript: this slow feedback loop is small, self-contained and often demonstrated live in a browser.
const DT = 0.05;              // seconds per step, the loop is deliberately slow
const TAU_DA = 30.0;          // seconds, dopamine rise and fall
const TAU_GJ = 5.0;           // seconds, junctions follow dopamine with a lag
const G_MAX = 1.0;            // maximum horizontal cell coupling
const KD = 0.5;               // dopamine giving half maximal uncoupling
function interplexiform(luminanceTrace) {
  let dopamine = 0.0, coupling = G_MAX;
  const out = [];
  for (const lum of luminanceTrace) {              // 0 at night, 1 at noon
    dopamine += ((lum - dopamine) / TAU_DA) * DT;
    const target = G_MAX * (KD / (KD + dopamine)); // D1 receptors close junctions
    coupling += ((target - coupling) / TAU_GJ) * DT;
    out.push(coupling);
  }
  return out;
}
const night = new Array(2000).fill(0.0);           // 100 seconds of darkness
const day = new Array(2000).fill(1.0);             // then 100 seconds of daylight
const trace = interplexiform(night.concat(day));
console.log("coupling at night:", trace[1999].toFixed(3));
console.log("coupling at noon :", trace[3999].toFixed(3));

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Fill one retinal cell with dye: if the dye reaches both plexiform layers from a single cell body, you are looking at an interplexiform cell, and nothing else in the human retina does that.

It is the only neuron here defined by an address rather than a shape, a transmitter or a response, and the only one that carries information deliberately backwards.

CHAPTER 214. MIDGET GANGLION CELLS, ALSO CALLED P CELLS OR PARVOCELLULAR CELLS (Fine-Detail Red-Green Neuron) (COG)

Read this sentence and you are using midget cells: eight of every ten fibres in your optic nerve belong to this one type, and at the centre of your gaze each listens to exactly one cone.

The old label is part size description and part address, because P and parvocellular simply name the small-celled layers of a relay station in the thalamus where these cells happen to terminate, which teaches a destination rather than a job.

Fine-Detail names the achievement, since in and near the fovea each of these cells is fed by a single cone through a single midget bipolar cell, a non-diverging chain often called the private line, so the grain of the image is carried to the brain without blurring.

Red-Green names the second thing the same wiring buys, because drawing the centre from one long-wavelength or medium-wavelength cone against a mixed surround makes the cell report the red-green difference that carries most everyday colour discrimination.

Neuron rather than cell keeps it in step with the family, and the name is worth the space because this one type makes up roughly eighty percent of all human retinal ganglion cells.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A midget ganglion cell is an output neuron of the retina, one of the few retinal cells whose fibre leaves the eye for the brain, and it is the smallest and commonest of them.

At the centre it runs a private line, one cone to one midget bipolar cell to one midget ganglion cell, so nothing is pooled and nothing is blurred, and the same wiring gives colour opponency, meaning the cell reports the difference between two kinds of cone rather than the amount of light.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Imagine a tiny bush no wider than a few cells, sitting on a long thin cable that runs out of the back of the eye.

The cell body lies in the ganglion cell layer and a single stout dendrite rises into the inner plexiform layer into one small dense tuft whose depth sets polarity, inner making an ON cell that fires to brightening and outer an OFF cell that fires to dimming.

SIZE:

The cell body is 10 to 15 micrometres across and the axon is 0.5 to 1 micrometre thick, while the dendritic tuft is only about 5 to 10 micrometres wide at the centre of gaze and grows to 100 to 300 micrometres in the far periphery.

Dacey and Petersen showed in human retina in 1992 that a parasol tuft is about ten times wider at three degrees out and about three times wider in the far periphery.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The primary division is ON-centre and OFF-centre, two mosaics that each tile the retina completely, with human ON fields 30 to 50 percent wider than OFF fields.

A second division is by cone input, long-wavelength centre against medium-wavelength surround and the reverse, and a third is positional, between foveal cells with one-to-one cone input and peripheral cells that pool a dozen or more cones and lose most colour selectivity.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies occupy the ganglion cell layer across the whole retina, stacked up to six deep in the ring around the foveal pit and scattered singly in the periphery.

Dendrites lie in the inner plexiform layer, sublamina b for ON and sublamina a for OFF, axons run in the nerve fibre layer through the lamina cribrosa, the sieve-like collagen plate in the sclera, and end in parvocellular layers 3 to 6 of the lateral geniculate nucleus, the visual relay in the thalamus.

NUMBER:

Human retinas hold roughly 0.7 to 1.5 million ganglion cells, about 1.2 million as a working figure from Curcio and Allen in 1990, and midget cells are about 80 percent overall, near 90 percent centrally and 40 to 45 percent in the far periphery.

That gives about 950,000 per eye and 1.9 million across both eyes at high confidence, roughly 0.002 percent of the 86 billion neurons in the nervous system, so almost everything you consciously see arrives on fewer than two million wires.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Midget cells fire true action potentials and their response is sustained, so a steady stimulus gives a steady change in rate on a maintained discharge of 10 to 20 spikes per second.

Contrast gain is low and keeps growing with contrast instead of saturating early, and temporal resolution falls away above roughly 10 to 20 flickers per second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitation is glutamate from midget bipolar cells, exactly one in the fovea and a handful in the periphery, and inhibition is GABA and glycine from amacrine cells.

The antagonistic surround originates upstream in horizontal cells feeding back onto cones and is inherited through the bipolar cell, and output goes to relay neurons in the parvocellular layers with a small projection to the pretectum.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A single unbranched axon of 0.5 to 1 micrometre travels up to 40 millimetres inside the eye completely unmyelinated, a bare stretch that is metabolically brutal because sodium pumps must run along its whole length.

Magnocellular signals reach the lateral geniculate nucleus about 3 milliseconds before parvocellular ones and the cortex about 5 milliseconds earlier.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, the nervous system's main excitatory chemical, with no significant co-transmitter established in the human midget cell.

MYELINATION:

Unmyelinated inside the eye and myelinated outside it, with an abrupt transition at the lamina cribrosa, because oligodendrocytes, the cells that make myelin, are absent from the retina and first appear just behind that plate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high per unit volume in the unmyelinated intraretinal axon, which is packed with mitochondria to pay for continuous ion pumping.

The papillomacular bundle, the midget axons from the macula, has the thinnest fibres and least reserve, which is why in Leber hereditary optic neuropathy and dominant optic atrophy, both mitochondrial diseases, these fibres fail first and produce a central blind spot.

PLASTICITY:

Adult midget cells hold their partners and the one-to-one foveal wiring is set in development, with short-term plasticity appearing as contrast adaptation over hundreds of milliseconds.

In human glaucoma the dendrites shrink and their mitochondria fragment before the cell dies.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes are unusual in the retina in living almost exclusively in the nerve fibre layer, where their whole job is to scaffold these unmyelinated axons, buffer potassium and shuttle lactate as fuel, a service they perform for no other retinal neuron.

At the optic disc the astrocytes of the lamina cribrosa form a load-bearing mesh whose failure under pressure is thought to strangle the axons in glaucoma.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete midget cells and acuity collapses from reading the bottom line of an eye chart to something near legal blindness, and red and green become indistinguishable because the whole red-green channel is carried here.

What remains is motion, flicker, coarse shape and blue-yellow colour, so the world would look like fast grey weather.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The defining computation is a subtraction performed by wiring rather than arithmetic: one cone in the centre, different cones in the surround, and the answer is a colour difference.

Dendritic geometry sets acuity so morphology must be modelled explicitly, the unmyelinated axon is an energy-hungry liability that must be modelled because it is where the cell dies, mitochondrial trafficking there runs over hours, and the cell releases nitric oxide and helps match local blood flow to local activity.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from 1 midget bipolar cell centrally rising to 5 to 30 in the far periphery, 0 to 1 normalised; GABA and glycine from amacrine cells; an inherited horizontal cell surround of opposite sign.

• OUTPUTS: spikes to parvocellular layers 3 to 6 of the lateral geniculate nucleus, 0 to about 150 hertz, resting near 10 to 20 hertz.

• STATE: membrane voltage, centre filter state, surround filter state, contrast adaptation level, spike threshold.

• PARAMETERS: dendritic field 5 micrometres centrally to 300 peripherally, soma 10 to 15 micrometres, axon 0.5 to 1 micrometre, surround weight 0.6 to 0.8 of centre.

• TIME CONSTANTS: centre 20 milliseconds, surround 60 milliseconds, contrast adaptation hundreds of milliseconds, dendritic remodelling in disease weeks to months.

• FAILURE MODE: in glaucoma the dendritic tree shrinks before death and central sensitivity falls, in mitochondrial optic neuropathies the thin papillomacular axons fail first and give a dense central blind spot, and in age-related macular degeneration these cells survive but lose their cones.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the sustained colour-opponent firing rate of one foveal midget ganglion cell that sees one cone in its centre and different cones in its surround.
Create a number variable called centre with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called surround with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called opponent with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called rate with initial value 0.
For each time step in the stimulus stream, perform the following operations.
    Set centre to the sum of centre and the product of 0.05 and the difference of the long wavelength cone signal and centre.
    Set surround to the sum of surround and the product of 0.0167 and the difference of the medium wavelength cone signal and surround.
    Set opponent to the difference of centre and the product of surround and 0.75.
    Set rate to the sum of 12 and the product of opponent and 90.
    Check if rate is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if rate is greater than 150.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set rate to 150.
    End the conditional block.
    Display rate to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: the midget computation is two filters and one subtraction, and it reads like the biology.
DT = 0.001                                # seconds per step
TAU_CENTRE, TAU_SURROUND = 0.020, 0.060   # seconds, cone centre and slower surround
W_CENTRE, W_SURROUND = 1.0, 0.75          # surround never quite cancels the centre
GAIN, BASE_RATE, MAX_RATE = 90.0, 12.0, 150.0     # spikes per second
def midget(l_cone, m_cone):
    centre, surround, rates = 0.0, 0.0, []
    for l_signal, m_signal in zip(l_cone, m_cone):
        centre += ((l_signal - centre) / TAU_CENTRE) * DT
        surround += ((m_signal - surround) / TAU_SURROUND) * DT
        opponent = W_CENTRE * centre - W_SURROUND * surround
        rate = BASE_RATE + GAIN * opponent
        rates.append(min(MAX_RATE, max(0.0, rate)))   # rectify and saturate
    return rates
steps = 1000                              # one second, long enough to settle
red, dark = [0.3] * steps, [0.0] * steps  # red drives L cones, spares M cones
white = [0.3] * steps                     # white drives both cone types equally
print("rate to red   : %.1f Hz" % midget(red, dark)[-1])
print("rate to white : %.1f Hz" % midget(white, white)[-1])

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Nothing else in the human body is a neuron with a dendritic tree five micrometres wide receiving a synapse from exactly one sensory cell.

A small ganglion cell with a single primary dendrite, one compact monostratified tuft, a sustained response and red-green opponency is a midget cell.

CHAPTER 215. PARASOL GANGLION CELLS, ALSO CALLED M CELLS OR MAGNOCELLULAR CELLS (Rapid-Motion Contrast Neuron) (COG)

Something moves at the edge of your vision and you turn before you know why: a parasol cell just fired, and its message reached your brain about five milliseconds before anything the midget cells had to say.

Parasol describes the umbrella spread of the dendrites, and M or magnocellular again names only the large-celled thalamic layers these axons reach, so neither tells a student what the cell is for.

Rapid-Motion names the speed the cell owns, since it answers transiently and follows high temporal frequencies, and destroying the magnocellular layers in macaques severely damages flicker-fusion thresholds and the seeing of fast-moving stimuli.

Contrast names its sensitivity, since it fires to small differences in luminance against the background rather than to colour.

Neuron marks it as a cell carrying a scale lesson, because these are only about ten percent of ganglion cells, with the on-answering and off-answering kinds each near six percent in central retina, so a tenth of the optic nerve carries most of the sense of movement.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A parasol ganglion cell is a large output neuron of the retina, the alarm channel that reports change, movement and faint differences in brightness, and does it fast.

It is colour-blind by design, adding cone signals together instead of subtracting them, which buys the contrast gain that lets it answer brightness differences of only one or two percent, several times more sensitive than a midget cell, as Kaplan and Shapley first quantified in the early 1980s.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The name comes from the shape, a broad flat radiating canopy of dendrites on a thick stalk, like a parasol seen from above.

The large cell body sits in the ganglion cell layer, three to six thick primary dendrites branch into a wide sparse arbor confined to one narrow depth of the inner plexiform layer, inner for ON and outer for OFF, and the axon is noticeably thick.

SIZE:

The cell body is 20 to 30 micrometres across, twice the diameter and up to eight times the volume of a midget cell body, and the axon is 1 to 2 micrometres thick.

The canopy runs from roughly 50 micrometres centrally to 400 micrometres or more peripherally, and human parasol fields exceed macaque ones by about 20 percent in the periphery and as much as 90 percent at five degrees, which matters to anyone porting monkey data into a human model.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

ON-centre and OFF-centre parasol cells form two independent mosaics, and the OFF mosaic is denser with smaller cells, matching the fact that natural scenes hold more dark patches than bright ones.

Human ON fields are 30 to 50 percent wider than OFF fields, and there is no accepted chromatic subdivision since the type is achromatic, though a weak blue-cone input to the surround has been reported.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies lie in the ganglion cell layer across the whole retina and, unlike midget cells, stay at reasonable density even in the far periphery.

Dendrites occupy the inner plexiform layer, sublamina b for ON and sublamina a for OFF, axons run in the nerve fibre layer through the lamina cribrosa, the sieve-like plate in the sclera, and terminate in magnocellular layers 1 and 2 of the lateral geniculate nucleus and in the superior colliculus, the midbrain structure driving reflexive eye movements.

NUMBER:

Parasol cells are roughly 8 to 10 percent of retinal ganglion cells, about 5 percent centrally and about 20 percent in the far periphery.

Against a total of about 1.2 million that gives 100,000 to 130,000 per eye and 200,000 to 260,000 across both eyes at moderate to high confidence, about 0.0003 percent of the 86 billion neurons in the nervous system, so the entire fast-motion alarm system of human vision runs on about a quarter of a million cells.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Parasol cells fire fast, above 100 spikes per second in a burst, and the response is transient, decaying within 50 to 100 milliseconds even while the stimulus continues, following flicker up to roughly 50 to 60 cycles per second.

They answer at twice the frequency of a fine contrast-reversing grating whose average brightness never changes, which reveals that their input is rectified in small subunits before pooling.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitation is glutamate from dozens of diffuse bipolar cells, chiefly type DB3 in the OFF pathway and DB4 or DB5 in the ON pathway, each terminal behaving as a small rectifying subunit.

Inhibition is GABA and glycine from many amacrine cells including the polyaxonal cells that supply long-range inhibition, and output lands on magnocellular relay neurons and on neurons of the superior colliculus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A single thick axon of 1 to 2 micrometres, two to four times the cross-sectional area of a midget axon, unmyelinated inside the retina and myelinated immediately past the lamina cribrosa.

Because of that calibre and heavier myelin, parasol signals reach the lateral geniculate nucleus about 3 milliseconds ahead of midget signals and the cortex about 5 milliseconds ahead.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate at all output synapses, with no significant co-transmitter established in the human parasol cell.

MYELINATION:

Unmyelinated within the retina and heavily myelinated beyond the lamina cribrosa, with thicker sheaths and longer internodes than midget axons carry, since the retina excludes oligodendrocytes entirely so nothing opaque sits in the light path.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Among the highest of any retinal neuron, because a large cell body, wide canopy, thick unmyelinated intraretinal axon and high firing rates add up, so these axons are dense in mitochondria.

Their large energy requirement at the optic nerve head is the leading explanation for why they are hit early when perfusion or pressure goes wrong.

PLASTICITY:

Contrast gain control is the signature adaptive process: within about 50 milliseconds of a rise in contrast the cell divides down its own gain to stay in range.

In experimental glaucoma the canopy shrinks and the axon narrows before death, which is now thought to explain much of the historic claim that large cells are lost first.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The astrocytes of the nerve fibre layer wrap these thick unmyelinated axons and supply them with lactate, a job unique in the retina to ganglion cell axons.

At the optic nerve head the specialised astrocytes of the lamina cribrosa bear the mechanical load of the pressure difference across the eye wall, and because parasol axons are the thickest fibres passing the largest pores they sit where strain is greatest.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove parasol cells and vision becomes a series of still photographs: you keep acuity and colour and can still read, but you lose the sense of things moving, the ability to see a faint shape in poor light, and the reflexive glance towards something appearing at the edge of vision.

Flicker above about twenty cycles per second vanishes, and the superior colliculus loses most of its retinal drive, so the eye movements that point the fovea at whatever just moved become slow and inaccurate.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This is the standard example in vision science of nonlinear subunit pooling: each bipolar input is rectified separately and then summed, so a pattern averaging to nothing still drives the cell.

It performs divisive contrast gain control on a fifty millisecond timescale, back-propagating action potentials help synchronise the arbor, neighbours are weakly coupled through amacrine cells, it helps widen nearby capillaries when busy, and its fate is decided mechanically at the lamina cribrosa by pressure, matrix stiffness and axonal transport.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from roughly 20 to 100 diffuse bipolar cells, each a separately rectified subunit, 0 to 1 normalised; GABA and glycine from local and polyaxonal amacrine cells; ambient luminance as log illuminance.

• OUTPUTS: spikes to magnocellular layers 1 and 2 of the lateral geniculate nucleus and to the superior colliculus, 0 to about 200 hertz in a burst, resting near 5 to 20 hertz.

• STATE: membrane voltage, per-subunit filter states, pooled drive, contrast gain state, refractory timer.

• PARAMETERS: dendritic field 50 micrometres centrally to over 400 peripherally, soma 20 to 30 micrometres, axon 1 to 2 micrometres, contrast sensitivity several times that of a midget cell.

• TIME CONSTANTS: subunit filter 15 milliseconds, contrast gain control 50 milliseconds, transient decay 50 to 100 milliseconds, luminance adaptation seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: in glaucoma these cells shrink and are lost early and patients first lose low-contrast flicker and motion detection in the mid-peripheral field, which is why frequency-doubling and flicker tests catch the disease before standard charts do.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to pool many separately rectified bipolar subunits into one transient parasol response and then divide that response by a slow measure of recent contrast.
Create a number variable called pooled with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called gain state with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called gain with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called rate with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called subunit values with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 12 times.
    Add 0 to the end of subunit values.
End the loop.
For each time step in the stimulus stream, perform the following operations.
    Set pooled to 0.
    For each subunit in subunit values, perform the following operations.
        Set subunit to the sum of subunit and the product of 0.0667 and the difference of the local contrast for that subunit and subunit.
        Check if subunit is less than 0.
        If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
            Set pooled to the difference of pooled and subunit.
        Otherwise, perform the following actions.
            Set pooled to the sum of pooled and subunit.
        End the conditional block.
    End the loop.
    Set pooled to the quotient of pooled divided by 12.
    Set gain state to the sum of gain state and the product of 0.02 and the difference of pooled and gain state.
    Set gain to the quotient of 1 divided by the sum of 1 and the product of 4 and gain state.
    Set rate to the sum of 8 and the product of 250 and the product of gain and pooled.
    Display rate to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C: every parasol cell pools dozens of subunits every millisecond, so this loop must be cheap. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>
#define N_SUB      12       /* rectified bipolar subunits in one canopy */
#define DT         0.001    /* seconds per step */
#define TAU_SUB    0.015    /* seconds, transient subunit filter */
#define TAU_GAIN   0.050    /* seconds, contrast gain control */
#define GAIN_K     4.0      /* strength of the divisive gain control */
#define OUT_GAIN   250.0    /* spikes per second per unit pooled drive */
#define BASE       8.0      /* maintained spikes per second */
static double parasol_step(const double *contrast, double *sub, double *gainstate)
{
    int i; double pooled = 0.0, g;
    for (i = 0; i < N_SUB; i++) {
        sub[i] += ((contrast[i] - sub[i]) / TAU_SUB) * DT;
        pooled += fabs(sub[i]);                /* rectify before pooling */
    }
    pooled /= N_SUB;
    *gainstate += ((pooled - *gainstate) / TAU_GAIN) * DT;
    g = 1.0 / (1.0 + GAIN_K * (*gainstate));   /* strong contrast divides gain down */
    return BASE + OUT_GAIN * g * pooled;
}
int main(void)
{
    double sub[N_SUB] = {0.0}, gainstate = 0.0, c[N_SUB], rate = 0.0;
    int i, k;
    for (i = 0; i < N_SUB; i++) c[i] = (i % 2 == 0) ? 0.2 : -0.2;  /* mean zero grating */
    for (k = 0; k < 500; k++) rate = parasol_step(c, sub, &gainstate);
    printf("rate to a mean zero grating: %.1f Hz\n", rate);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Show the retina a fine striped pattern whose average brightness never changes and then reverse it: a midget cell shrugs, and a parasol cell fires twice per reversal.

A large ganglion cell with a wide radiating canopy, a thick axon, a transient achromatic response and a terminal in the magnocellular layers of the thalamus is a parasol cell and nothing else in the human eye.

CHAPTER 216. SMALL BISTRATIFIED GANGLION CELLS, ALSO CALLED K CELLS OR KONIOCELLULAR CELLS (Blue-Yellow Opponent Neuron) (COG)

This is the cell that lets the sky look blue rather than merely bright.

Small bistratified describes only that the dendrites sit in two layers, and koniocellular, meaning dust-like cells, is a stain-based address in the thalamus.

Blue-Yellow names the comparison the cell exists to make, since its inner dendritic tier is excited by short-wavelength blue-sensitive cones through the dedicated blue-cone bipolar cell while the outer tier is inhibited by the medium and long wavelength cones that the eye reads as yellow.

Opponent is the technical word made plain, since the cell reports the difference between two colour signals rather than the amount of either.

Neuron marks it as a ganglion cell whose wiring shows the effort of balance, because bipolar input to the inner tier is about fourfold greater than to the outer tier, compensating for the sparseness of blue cones, which are only around five to ten percent of all cones.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It is a retinal ganglion cell, an output neuron of the eye whose long fibre reaches the brain, and its job is subtraction.

It adds the signal from short-wavelength cones, which prefer blue-violet light near 440 nanometres, and subtracts the combined long-wavelength and middle-wavelength cone signal, giving blue-ON, yellow-OFF colour opponency.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Bistratified means its dendrites, the branching antennae that collect signals, form two separate flat sheets instead of one.

The larger inner sheet lies at stratum 5 of the inner plexiform layer, the wiring band where light-increase circuits run, and the smaller outer sheet at stratum 1, the light-decrease half, so one cell owns a physical plus and a physical minus.

SIZE:

The cell body is 12 to 20 micrometres across and the axon about 1 micrometre thick.

The inner sheet spans 20 to 30 micrometres near the fovea, the pinhead-sized pit of sharpest vision, and 300 to 600 micrometres in the far periphery, about 10 percent wider than a parasol cell beside it.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

A large bistratified cell shares the blue-ON polarity with a wider spread, a further distinct blue-ON type was reported in primate retina in 2020, and blue-OFF cells exist but are midget-like rather than bistratified.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies sit in the ganglion cell layer from the foveal slope outwards, scarce in the central 100 micrometres because that patch holds almost no short-wavelength cones, with dendrites straddling strata 1 and 5 at the interface between the two halves of the inner plexiform layer.

The axon leaves the retina at the lamina cribrosa, a sieve-like collagen plate, and runs through the optic chiasm to koniocellular layers K1 to K6 of the dorsal lateral geniculate nucleus, named from the Greek for dust, with minor branches to superior colliculus and pretectum.

NUMBER:

They are about 1 percent of ganglion cells in the fovea and 6 to 10 percent in the periphery, so 5 to 8 percent overall, giving 60,000 to 90,000 per eye and 120,000 to 180,000 across both eyes.

Confidence is moderate because the counts come mainly from macaque, and this is about two ten-thousandths of one percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the human nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

It idles at a few spikes per second, reaches 50 to 100 for strong blue light, is sluggish in time and coarse in space, and receives its short-wavelength input 10 to 20 milliseconds later than its long and middle wavelength input.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The inner sheet takes glutamate, the main excitatory messenger, from the blue cone bipolar cell; the outer sheet takes glutamate from diffuse OFF bipolar cells; both take gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) and glycine from amacrine cells, the retina's local interneurons.

Its targets are koniocellular relay cells, which feed the cytochrome oxidase blobs of layer 3 and the top of layer 1 of primary visual cortex.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

At about 1 micrometre it conducts faster than a midget cell and slower than a parasol cell over a path of roughly 50 millimetres.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Output is glutamate throughout, and inputs are glutamate, GABA, glycine and diffusely released dopamine that sets day-night gain.

MYELINATION:

Inside the retina the axon is bare, because myelin, the fatty insulating wrap that speeds conduction, would scatter light before it reached the photoreceptors; oligodendrocytes begin wrapping immediately behind the lamina cribrosa.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:
