The bare intraretinal segment pumps ions without myelin's savings, so it is dense with mitochondria, and the retina burns more oxygen per gram than almost any tissue in the body.

PLASTICITY:

Learning-related strengthening is absent; what changes is gain, rescaled across orders of magnitude of background light and retuned by dopamine between night and day.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Müller cells, giant radial glia spanning the whole retina, clear spent glutamate, buffer potassium through Kir4.1 channels and supply lactate, while astrocytes appear only in the nerve fibre layer.

One detail is special: the inner sheet sits in the deepest stratum among rod bipolar terminals, so a single Müller sheath serves a colour circuit and a night-vision circuit micrometres apart.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete it and the retina still reports edges, motion and red-green difference, but the whole blue-yellow dimension of colour disappears, and blue lips would look like ordinary pallor, as though the world were printed with the cyan ink removed.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Two dendritic sheets in two wiring layers give one cell an internal plus and minus, so the subtraction is done by geometry rather than chemistry, and a built-in delay makes it compare blue now against yellow a moment ago.

Dendritic sodium channels carry spikes backwards into both sheets, gap junctions couple it weakly to amacrine cells, and dopamine released into the fluid rather than at a synapse, called volume transmission, resets its gain over minutes.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Blue cone bipolar glutamate on the inner sheet, normalised contrast minus one to plus one.

◦ Diffuse OFF bipolar glutamate on the outer sheet, same range.

◦ Amacrine GABA and glycine, 0 to 10 nanosiemens, and dopamine as a gain scalar of 0.5 to 1.5.

• OUTPUTS: 0 to 100 spikes per second to koniocellular layers K1 to K6, plus superior colliculus and pretectum.

• STATE: membrane potential, twelve-step delay buffer of recent blue contrast, slow adaptation term, refractory countdown.

• PARAMETERS: opponent gain about 140 spikes per second per unit contrast, baseline 2 to 5, ceiling 90 to 100, inner sheet 20 to 600 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds, synaptic smoothing 30 milliseconds, blue delay 10 to 20 milliseconds, adaptation seconds to minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: among the first casualties of glaucoma, giving blue-yellow (tritan) loss that short-wavelength automated perimetry detects before standard fields change, with further decline in diabetes and ageing.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the firing rate of a small bistratified ganglion cell from blue cone contrast and yellow cone contrast.
Create a number variable called delayed_blue with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called smoothed with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called blue_history with initial value empty list.
Create a boolean variable called buffer_is_full with initial value false.
For each frame in the cone_input_stream, perform the following operations.
    Add the blue cone contrast of the frame to the end of blue_history.
    Check if the total number of items in blue_history is greater than 12.
    Set buffer_is_full to the result of that check.
    If buffer_is_full is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set delayed_blue to the item at position 1 in blue_history.
        Remove the item at position 1 from blue_history.
    End the conditional block.
    Set smoothed to the sum of smoothed and the product of 0.033 and the difference of the difference of delayed_blue and the yellow cone contrast of the frame and smoothed.
    Set firing_rate to the sum of 4 and the product of 140 and smoothed.
    Display firing_rate to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python was chosen because a colour-opponent filter needs no libraries and must stay readable to anyone auditing the colour science.

# Small bistratified cell: blue-ON, yellow-OFF, with a delayed blue path.
DT, TAU, DELAY = 0.001, 0.030, 12    # step s, smoothing s, blue lag in steps
GAIN, BASE, CEIL = 140.0, 4.0, 95.0  # spikes/s per unit contrast, floor, ceiling
buffer = [0.0] * DELAY               # delay line for the blue path
v = 0.0                              # smoothed opponent signal

def step(blue, yellow):
    global v
    buffer.append(blue)
    delayed = buffer.pop(0)          # ON drive, inner dendritic sheet
    v += ((delayed - yellow) - v) * (DT / TAU)   # subtract, then smooth
    return max(0.0, min(CEIL, BASE + GAIN * v))

for ms in range(300):
    b = 0.6 if 100 <= ms < 200 else 0.0    # blue-isolating step of contrast
    if ms % 50 == 0:
        print("t=%3d ms  rate=%5.1f Hz" % (ms, step(b, 0.0)))
    else:
        step(b, 0.0)

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other ganglion cell puts a large sheet in the deepest ON stratum and a small sparse one in the outermost OFF stratum, and no other cell in the eye both fires for blue and falls silent for yellow.

CHAPTER 217. INTRINSICALLY PHOTOSENSITIVE RETINAL GANGLION CELLS (IPRGCS) (Daylight-Sensing Clock-Setting Neuron) (NON-COG)

A few thousand cells in each eye see without any help from the rods and cones, and they are why some blind people still keep a twenty-four hour day.

The existing label is accurate but is a committee phrase reduced to an acronym, and intrinsically photosensitive tells the reader a mechanism, that the cell holds its own pigment melanopsin and needs no rod or cone, without telling them why that matters.

Daylight-Sensing names what the pigment is tuned for, a slow, sustained measure of how much blue-rich ambient light is falling on the eye, which is the honest signal of day versus night rather than of shape or edge.

Clock-Setting names the destination and the purpose, since these axons drive the suprachiasmatic nucleus that sets the twenty-four hour rhythm, and also the olivary pretectal nucleus that narrows the pupil.

Neuron marks it as a tiny minority, roughly nought point four to one point five percent of human ganglion cells, yet blind people lacking image vision can still entrain their sleep cycle through them.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An intrinsically photosensitive retinal ganglion cell (ipRGC) is an output neuron of the retina that is itself a light detector, carrying its own light-absorbing protein, called melanopsin.

Its business is not shape but total light averaged over minutes, which sets the body clock, holds the pupil small, suppresses the sleep hormone melatonin, raises alertness and pushes mood.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is large and the dendritic tree is enormous, flat and sparsely branched, which is why one variant is called the giant sparse cell.

Melanopsin sits along the dendrites themselves, so the whole net is the light-catching surface, and in displaced forms the cell body slips out into the inner nuclear layer while the dendrites reach back inwards.

SIZE:

Dendritic fields span 400 to 1200 micrometres, the giant sparse variant reaching about 1 millimetre, with some of the largest cell bodies in the retina.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Mouse work defines six types, M1 to M6, with M1 in the OFF stratum carrying the most pigment, M2 in the ON stratum, M3 bistratified, and M4 to M6 carrying less and behaving more like ordinary vision cells.

Human retina holds M1, displaced M1, gigantic M1, displaced gigantic M1 and M2 cells, and unlike the mouse the displaced form is the majority of human M1 cells.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies occupy the ganglion cell layer, and in displaced forms the inner edge of the inner nuclear layer, so the type straddles that interface, with dendrites in stratum 1 for M1 and stratum 5 for M2.

Density peaks just outside the fovea at 15 to 40 cells per square millimetre, falls below 5 at 10 millimetres out, and is zero in the fovea itself.

Axons run in the retinohypothalamic tract to the suprachiasmatic nucleus of the hypothalamus, the master body clock, and also to olivary pretectal nucleus, intergeniculate leaflet, ventrolateral preoptic nucleus, habenula, superior colliculus and koniocellular lateral geniculate nucleus.

NUMBER:

Human counts run from about 4,000 to over 7,000 per eye, which is 0.4 to 1.5 percent of the roughly 1.07 to 1.2 million ganglion cells in an eye, so call it 3,000 to 8,000 per eye and 6,000 to 16,000 across both.

Confidence is fair for the order of magnitude and poor for the exact figure, because faint subtypes are easily missed in post-mortem tissue, and this is roughly one neuron in seven million in the human nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The intrinsic response is famously slow, taking seconds to begin at threshold, holding steady while the light lasts, and continuing many seconds after it stops.

Firing rates are low, a few to a few tens of spikes per second, and melanopsin is far less sensitive than rod or cone pigment, so these cells stay quiet below daylight levels.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

They also take glutamate from ON bipolar cells carrying the rod and cone estimate of brightness plus GABA and glycine from amacrine cells, summing that fast estimate with their own slow one.

They release glutamate, M1 cells additionally release the peptide pituitary adenylate cyclase activating polypeptide (PACAP) onto clock neurons, and some keep branches inside the retina contacting dopamine-releasing amacrine cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Medium calibre and slow conducting, which does not matter for a message about the hour of the day, and often branching so one cell reaches clock and pupil nuclei at once.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate is primary, with PACAP co-released by M1 cells, and incoming chemistry is glutamate, GABA, glycine and dopamine.

MYELINATION:

Bare inside the retina like every ganglion cell, then myelinated by oligodendrocytes from the lamina cribrosa onwards.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest, because firing rates are low, but unusual in being continuous, since the cell runs day and night with its light-detecting cascade burning energy whenever light is present.

PLASTICITY:

Sensitivity adapts to light history over hours and days, melanopsin is bistable so long-wavelength light resets a spent pigment molecule without rebuilding it, and about 31 percent of these cells are lost in healthy people over 70.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Müller glia do the usual glutamate and potassium housekeeping and no specialised glial partner is known, so nothing is special on the support side.

What is special is durability: these cells outlast every other ganglion cell type after optic nerve injury and are relatively spared in Leber hereditary optic neuropathy and dominant optic atrophy.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and vision is untouched, which is what makes the loss so strange: the person reads and drives normally while sleep wanders around the day, melatonin arrives at the wrong hours, the pupil fails to hold small in bright light, and winter light therapy stops working.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell is photoreceptor, computer and transmitter in one: light activates melanopsin, then the G protein Gq, then the enzyme phospholipase C beta 4, which opens transient receptor potential canonical channels, mainly TRPC6 and TRPC7, with hyperpolarisation-activated cyclic nucleotide-gated channels recruited in non-M1 cells.

Because the pigment lives in the dendrites, a photon caught hundreds of micrometres away still makes current at the cell body, intraretinal branches form a backwards loop onto dopamine-releasing amacrine cells, and peptide release makes its message to the hypothalamus outlast its spikes by hours.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Melanopic irradiance near 480 nanometres, as log10 photons per square centimetre per second, useful range 10 to 16.

◦ Rod and cone driven ON bipolar glutamate, 0 to 1 normalised, dominant below daylight.

◦ Amacrine GABA and glycine, 0 to 5 nanosiemens.

• OUTPUTS: a sustained 0 to 20 spikes per second plus slow PACAP release to suprachiasmatic nucleus, olivary pretectal nucleus, intergeniculate leaflet, habenula, superior colliculus and koniocellular lateral geniculate nucleus.

• STATE: activated cascade level, available melanopsin fraction, long-term light history average, membrane potential.

• PARAMETERS: activation threshold near log10 irradiance 11, peak sustained rate 10 to 20 spikes per second, spectral peak about 480 nanometres, dendritic field 400 to 1200 micrometres.

• TIME CONSTANTS: rise 1 to 5 seconds, decay after light offset 10 to 30 seconds, pupil after-effect measurable from 1.8 seconds after offset, light history adaptation hours to days.

• FAILURE MODE: Alzheimer and Parkinson patients have 25 to 30 percent fewer of these cells than age-matched controls with matching circadian breakdown, advanced glaucoma and diabetic retinopathy destroy them, oversensitivity produces the light aversion of migraine, and dysfunction is implicated in seasonal affective disorder.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert steady light at the eye into the slow sustained signal an intrinsically photosensitive retinal ganglion cell sends to the body clock.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called cascade_state with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called drive_is_negative with initial value false.
Create a boolean variable called light_is_rising with initial value false.
For each sample in the light_recording, perform the following operations.
    Set drive to the quotient of the difference of sample and 11 divided by 3.
    Check if drive is less than 0.
    Set drive_is_negative to the result of that check.
    If drive_is_negative is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set drive to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if drive is greater than cascade_state.
    Set light_is_rising to the result of that check.
    If light_is_rising is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set cascade_state to the sum of cascade_state and the product of 0.0005 and the difference of drive and cascade_state.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set cascade_state to the sum of cascade_state and the product of 0.00005 and the difference of drive and cascade_state.
    End the conditional block.
    Set firing_rate to the product of cascade_state and 12.
    Display firing_rate to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C was chosen because a whole-body simulation must integrate this cascade at millisecond resolution across simulated days, hundreds of millions of steps, with no interpreter overhead.

#include <stdio.h>
/* ipRGC melanopsin cascade: quick to charge, very slow to discharge. */
/* Compile: cc iprgc.c -o iprgc */
int main(void) {
    const double dt = 0.001, tau_rise = 2.0, tau_fall = 20.0;
    const double thresh = 11.0, span = 3.0, rate_max = 12.0;
    double g = 0.0;                              /* cascade state, 0 to 1 */
    int step;
    for (step = 0; step < 600000; step++) {      /* 600 s simulated */
        double t = step * dt;
        double logI = (t >= 100.0 && t < 400.0) ? 14.0 : 8.0;  /* 300 s pulse */
        double drive = (logI - thresh) / span;
        if (drive < 0.0) drive = 0.0;
        if (drive > 1.0) drive = 1.0;
        g += (drive - g) * (dt / (drive > g ? tau_rise : tau_fall));
        if (step % 100000 == 0)
            printf("t=%6.1f s  cascade=%.3f  rate=%5.2f Hz\n", t, g, rate_max * g);
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Kill every rod and cone, block all synaptic transmission with drugs, then shine blue light: the few cells that depolarise slowly and stay depolarised long after the light has gone are these.

CHAPTER 218. DIRECTION-SELECTIVE GANGLION CELLS (COG)

This cell fires hard when an object slides one way across the eye and falls almost silent when the same object slides back, and it decides that before any signal leaves the eyeball.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A direction-selective ganglion cell is a retinal output neuron that reports which way something moved, with a preferred direction that produces a burst and an opposite null direction that produces almost nothing.

The retina therefore sends the brain a ready-made motion vector rather than a movie, and in humans the main use is holding the image of the world still while head and body move.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

In primate retina the ON-OFF type is the recursive bistratified cell, with one flat dendritic sheet in the OFF half and one in the ON half of the inner plexiform layer, and the ON type is the recursive monostratified cell, with a single sheet in the ON half.

Recursive means some dendrites curl back towards the cell body, and in both types the dendrites run bundled along the narrow tracks used by starburst amacrine cells.

SIZE:

The recursive bistratified cell averages 388 micrometres across, standard deviation 82, enclosing about 119,892 square micrometres, and the recursive monostratified cell is comparable, lying at about 64 percent of the depth of the inner plexiform layer.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

ON-OFF direction-selective cells answer both brightening and darkening edges and form four populations tuned to four roughly cardinal directions, each tiling the retina separately.

ON direction-selective cells answer only brightening, prefer much slower motion, and in mouse form three populations whose preferred directions line up with the planes of the three semicircular canals of the inner ear, so the eye's motion detectors are pre-aligned with the balance organ.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies sit in the ganglion cell layer across the whole retina, denser along the horizontal midline in primates, and dendrites lie exactly in the two bands of the inner plexiform layer rich in choline acetyltransferase, at roughly 28 and 64 percent depth, so this type is defined by living at the interface with the starburst amacrine plexus.

Axons reach the nucleus of the optic tract and dorsal terminal nucleus of the accessory optic system, the hub that turns image slip into an eye-muscle command, plus pretectum, superior colliculus and in part the lateral geniculate nucleus.

NUMBER:

In macaque the recursive bistratified population is about 1.5 percent of peripheral ganglion cells and the recursive monostratified population 2.5 percent, standard deviation 0.54, so together about 4 percent.

That is roughly 48,000 per eye out of 1.2 million and about 96,000 across both eyes, one ten-thousandth of one percent of the human nervous system, with moderate confidence for macaque and low for human, where these cells are matched by molecule and shape rather than recorded directly.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Preferred-direction motion produces 30 to 100 spikes per second and null-direction motion a small fraction of that, measured as the direction selectivity index, preferred minus null divided by their sum, typically 0.3 to 0.8.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitation is glutamate from bipolar cells plus untuned acetylcholine from starburst amacrine cells, while inhibition is GABA from those same starburst cells and is tuned, because the ganglion cell accepts GABA synapses only from starburst dendrites pointing along its null direction.

The starburst amacrine cell is a radial interneuron, unique in the nervous system for releasing one excitatory and one inhibitory transmitter, and each of its dendrites answers best to motion running outward from its own cell body.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Medium diameter and moderate conduction speed, with branches that let one cell address several midbrain targets at once.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The cell releases glutamate and receives glutamate, acetylcholine on nicotinic receptors, and GABA on type A receptors containing the alpha 2 subunit.

MYELINATION:

Unmyelinated inside the retina, myelinated by oligodendrocytes beyond the lamina cribrosa.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High during motion, because the cell is driven hard by acetylcholine while sustaining large chloride currents from GABA, and pumping that chloride back out costs energy continuously.

PLASTICITY:

The wiring asymmetry behind direction selectivity is built during development and is robust once set, although in young animals prolonged motion in one direction can reverse the preference, and the gene FRMD7 is required to build the horizontal asymmetry.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Müller cells do the usual housekeeping, but one point is specific: null-direction suppression stays sharp only if starburst GABA is cleared fast, and Müller cells carry the GABA transporter GAT-3 that does it, so here glial uptake protects a computation rather than merely tidying up.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and the retina still reports every edge, colour and flicker, but the eye can no longer lock on to a moving world, so scenery seen from a train would smear instead of stepping cleanly past, the reflex that pins each patch and flicks back having no error signal.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Direction selectivity is computed in the dendrites, not the cell body: each patch of dendrite is separately vetoed by a starburst synapse timed to arrive with the passing edge, so the cell is a field of small detectors whose votes are summed.

That inhibition is largely shunting, opening a leak that drains excitation rather than pushing voltage down, which is division rather than subtraction, and recursive dendrites contact neighbours of the same type through gap junctions while back-propagating spikes sweep outward from the cell body.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Bipolar glutamate spread across the dendritic field, 0 to 1 normalised contrast.

◦ Starburst acetylcholine, untuned, 0 to 1, arriving with the leading edge.

◦ Starburst GABA from null-side dendrites only, 0 to 20 nanosiemens, timed to the edge.

• OUTPUTS: 0 to 100 spikes per second to nucleus of the optic tract and dorsal terminal nucleus, pretectum, superior colliculus and lateral geniculate nucleus.

• STATE: per-subunit excitation and inhibition, membrane potential, recent motion history for adaptation.

• PARAMETERS: preferred direction in radians, direction selectivity index 0.3 to 0.8, dendritic field about 388 micrometres, inhibition to excitation ratio about 3 to 1, eight to sixteen starburst subunits.

• TIME CONSTANTS: inhibition rise 5 to 20 milliseconds, cholinergic excitation 2 to 10 milliseconds, dendritic integration about 10 milliseconds, best image slip 1 to 10 degrees per second for ON types.

• FAILURE MODE: mutation of FRMD7 removes the asymmetric inhibition onto horizontally tuned cells, abolishes the horizontal optokinetic reflex and produces idiopathic infantile nystagmus, a lifelong involuntary side-to-side oscillation of the eyes.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the response of a direction-selective ganglion cell by subtracting starburst inhibition wired only to its null side.
Create a list variable called starburst_activation with initial value empty list.
Create a list variable called wiring_weight with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called inhibition with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called index with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called inhibition_saturated with initial value false.
Set starburst_activation to the eight measured starburst dendrite responses for this frame.
Set wiring_weight to the fixed list 0, 0, 0, 0.5, 1, 0.5, 0, 0 that is strongest on the null side.
While index is less than 8 remains true, repeat the following steps.
    Set inhibition to the sum of inhibition and the product of the item at position index in starburst_activation and the item at position index in wiring_weight.
    Set index to the sum of index and 1.
End the loop.
Set inhibition to the product of 3 and the quotient of inhibition divided by 8.
Check if inhibition is greater than 1.
Set inhibition_saturated to the result of that check.
If inhibition_saturated is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set inhibition to 1.
End the conditional block.
Set firing_rate to the product of 24 and the difference of 1 and inhibition.
Display firing_rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python was chosen because direction selectivity is vector geometry, which Python expresses in a form a reviewer can check by hand.

import math
# Direction-selective ganglion cell driven by asymmetric starburst inhibition.
PREFERRED, N_SAC = 0.0, 8   # preferred direction in radians; starburst inputs
BASE_EXC, ACH = 12.0, 1.0   # bipolar glutamate drive; untuned cholinergic boost
GABA = 3.0                  # null-side GABA, stronger than what it opposes

def response(motion_dir):
    exc = BASE_EXC * (1.0 + ACH)                # glutamate plus acetylcholine
    inh = 0.0
    for k in range(N_SAC):
        a = 2.0 * math.pi * k / N_SAC           # this starburst dendrite's heading
        # A starburst dendrite fires hardest for motion running outward along it.
        act = max(0.0, math.cos(motion_dir - a))
        # Only null-pointing starbursts synapse onto this ganglion cell.
        wiring = max(0.0, -math.cos(a - PREFERRED))
        inh += GABA * act * wiring
    inh = min(1.0, inh / N_SAC)                 # shunting inhibition divides drive
    return max(0.0, exc * (1.0 - inh))

pref, null = response(PREFERRED), response(PREFERRED + math.pi)
print("preferred %.1f Hz, null %.1f Hz" % (pref, null))
print("direction selectivity index %.2f" % ((pref - null) / (pref + null)))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Show it a bar moving right and it shouts; show it the same bar moving left and it barely whispers, and its dendrites hug the two acetylcholine-rich bands of the inner plexiform layer so tightly they look fused with them.

CHAPTER 219. SMOOTH MONOSTRATIFIED, LARGE SPARSE, BROAD THORNY, AND RECURSIVE BISTRATIFIED GANGLION CELLS (Wide-Field Rare-Type Output Neuron) (COG)

Nine tenths of the eye's output cables belong to two famous cell types; this entry is the strange, rare, wide-armed minority that carries the rest.

The existing heading is four morphological addresses strung together, each describing how the dendrites look under a microscope rather than what the cells do, and honesty requires admitting that the settled function of most of them is not yet known.

Wide-Field records the one firm shared anatomical fact, that all four spread far wider dendritic fields than the midget and parasol types and so pool over large patches of retina.

Rare-Type records the second firm fact, that together these types form only a few percent of the optic nerve, with the thorny group alone at about one percent, yet they carry their own separate messages to the superior colliculus and thalamus.

Output Neuron marks them as ganglion cells, and where evidence exists it is suggestive rather than settled, for example broad thorny cells answering bright or dark objects entering or leaving their field regardless of direction, which is why they have been proposed as a pursuit error signal.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are four of the roughly seventeen wide-field ganglion cell types of the primate retina, grouped because they all watch large patches of space and report what the two dominant types cannot.

The smooth monostratified cell reports fine texture inside a large area, the large sparse cell samples very wide regions thinly, the broad thorny cell reports that something is slipping across the middle of your gaze whichever way it slips, and the recursive bistratified cell reports direction of motion.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The smooth monostratified cell has one flat sheet of unusually smooth branches and the large sparse cell a very wide, thinly branched sheet.

The broad thorny cell has dendrites covered in fine spikes filling a thick slab through the middle of the wiring layer rather than one thin plane, while the recursive bistratified cell has two sheets with branches looping back towards the cell body.

SIZE:

The smooth monostratified cell averages 419 micrometres across in mid-peripheral retina, standard deviation 110, with a cell body of 16 plus or minus 2 micrometres and an axon of 1.06 plus or minus 0.22 micrometres, roughly half a parasol axon.

The large sparse cell in human retina averages 737 to 791 micrometres, the broad thorny cell 369 plus or minus 63 with a cell body of 18 plus or minus 2, and the recursive bistratified cell 388 plus or minus 82.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The smooth monostratified cell has an inner ON form at 61 plus or minus 5 percent depth in the inner plexiform layer and an outer OFF form at 37 plus or minus 5 percent, each sharing the parasol plane to within one or two micrometres.

The large sparse cell also has inner and outer forms, and in macaque and human more than 80 percent of cells carrying the transcription factor Satb2 are large sparse cells, while broad thorny and recursive bistratified cells each form a single tiling population.

WHERE IT LIVES:

All four have cell bodies in the ganglion cell layer from central retina to far periphery, commoner peripherally, with broad thorny dendrites filling the slab between the ON and OFF starburst bands, so that cell lives at the interface between the layer's two halves rather than in either, and recursive bistratified dendrites lying on the starburst bands themselves.

Smooth monostratified cells were labelled from both lateral geniculate nucleus and superior colliculus in the same retinas, so one branching axon reaching both is likely; broad thorny cells project to koniocellular geniculate layers and superior colliculus; large sparse cells feed superior colliculus, pretectum and medial inferior pulvinar.

NUMBER:

Smooth monostratified cells are about 3 percent of peripheral ganglion cells, at roughly 9 per square millimetre at 7 to 8 millimetres eccentricity with a coverage factor of 1.7, large sparse cells 1.5 to 2 percent, broad thorny about 1 percent and recursive bistratified about 1.5 percent.

Against 1.2 million per eye that is roughly 36,000, 18,000 to 24,000, 12,000 and 18,000 respectively, so about 84,000 per eye and 168,000 across both eyes, of which the recursive bistratified cells are the same cells already listed as direction-selective and are counted once for the section total.

Confidence is fair for macaque and lower for human, and together they are about two ten-thousandths of one percent of the human nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The smooth monostratified cell peaks at 50 plus or minus 35 spikes per second to drifting gratings, prefers 13 plus or minus 5 hertz, and responds at 52 plus or minus 3 milliseconds when ON and 38 milliseconds when OFF.

The broad thorny cell is much slower, at 90 plus or minus 9 milliseconds ON and 69 plus or minus 6 OFF, and strongly transient, meaning it fires at a change then stops.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

All four take glutamate from bipolar cells and GABA and glycine from amacrine cells, and all release glutamate onto their brain targets.

The broad thorny cell is defined by its inhibition, with a surround suppression index of 0.86 plus or minus 0.04 for the ON phase and 0.62 plus or minus 0.09 for the OFF phase, so motion in the surround almost completely silences it.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The smooth monostratified axon at 1.06 micrometres is about half the parasol axon's 1.94 plus or minus 0.33 micrometres, so it conducts considerably more slowly, and the others are of medium calibre.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate for output in every case, with glutamate, GABA, glycine and acetylcholine arriving, and diffusely released dopamine as a shared gain control.

MYELINATION:

Bare inside the retina, myelinated by oligodendrocytes from the lamina cribrosa onwards, with speed set mainly by axon diameter.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Wide sheets mean a large membrane area to keep polarised, expensive even at rest, and the smooth monostratified cell's high firing rates add to that.

PLASTICITY:

None show classical learning-related plasticity; they adapt gain to background light and recent contrast over seconds, and their mosaics are refined by activity during development.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Müller glia handle glutamate clearance and potassium buffering as everywhere, but one point is specific: these receptive fields are built from many small independent subunits, which works only if glutamate at one bipolar terminal does not spill onto the next, so tight Müller ensheathment of individual terminals is what keeps the subunits separate and the cell nonlinear.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these four and the retina keeps sharp vision, colour and simple motion but loses its coarse alarm system: the shimmer of leaves would stop registering at all, and the eye would lose its automatic notice that a tracked object has begun to slip, so gaze would drift off moving targets without the brain being told.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The smooth monostratified cell rectifies before it sums, each patch of dendrite listening to one bipolar cell, discarding the negative half of that signal and only then passing it on, so a fine grating averaging to zero across the whole field still produces a large response.

The evidence is quantitative, since it answers at twice the stimulus frequency out to 5 plus or minus 1 cycles per degree while its linear response dies at 1 plus or minus 0.2, a four to one ratio, and the broad thorny cell instead answers edges crossing its centre at 4 to 12 degrees per second while staying silent for a full-field drifting plaid, exactly the arithmetic for saying the target slipped rather than the world moved.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Bipolar glutamate at 8 to 32 spatial subunits per receptive field, each 0 to 1 normalised contrast.

◦ Wide-field amacrine GABA carrying the surround, 0 to 20 nanosiemens, dominant in broad thorny cells.

◦ Starburst acetylcholine and GABA, for the recursive bistratified cell only.

• OUTPUTS: 0 to 100 spikes per second to magnocellular and koniocellular geniculate layers, superior colliculus, pretectum and medial inferior pulvinar.

• STATE: per-subunit rectified drive, membrane potential, transient adaptation variable, surround suppression level.

• PARAMETERS: receptive field centre 280 to 600 micrometres, subunit count 8 to 32, surround suppression index 0.6 to 0.9, preferred temporal frequency 13 plus or minus 5 hertz, latency 38 to 90 milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: subunit rectification effectively instantaneous, membrane about 10 milliseconds, transient adaptation 50 to 200 milliseconds, surround delay 10 to 20 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: these large, wide-field cells are disproportionately vulnerable in glaucoma, where big cells die first, so the earliest losses are of coarse peripheral motion and texture rather than reading acuity, which is why patients notice nothing until much of the field is gone.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the response of a smooth monostratified ganglion cell whose receptive field is built from many small rectified subunits.
Create a list variable called subunit_signal with initial value empty list.
Create a list variable called subunit_weight with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called linear_total with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called rectified_total with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called this_signal with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called index with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called count with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called signal_is_positive with initial value false.
Set subunit_signal to the contrast in each bipolar subunit of the receptive field.
Set subunit_weight to the weight of each subunit measured from the field centre.
Set count to the total number of items in subunit_signal.
While index is less than count remains true, repeat the following steps.
    Set this_signal to the item at position index in subunit_signal.
    Set linear_total to the sum of linear_total and the product of this_signal and the item at position index in subunit_weight.
    Check if this_signal is greater than 0.
    Set signal_is_positive to the result of that check.
    If signal_is_positive is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set rectified_total to the sum of rectified_total and the product of this_signal and the item at position index in subunit_weight.
    End the conditional block.
    Set index to the sum of index and 1.
End the loop.
Display linear_total to the console.
Display the product of 90 and the quotient of rectified_total divided by count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C was chosen because a full retinal simulation evaluates tens of subunits for each of tens of thousands of these cells on every video frame, and that inner loop is the hot path.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>
/* Smooth monostratified cell: rectify each subunit first, then sum. */
/* Compile: cc smooth.c -o smooth -lm */
#define NSUB 16
int main(void) {
    const double rf = 300.0, period = 120.0, contrast = 0.5, gain = 90.0;
    const double sigma = rf / 2.0, spacing = 2.0 * rf / NSUB;
    double x[NSUB], w[NSUB];
    int i, step;
    for (i = 0; i < NSUB; i++) {                  /* lay subunits across the field */
        x[i] = -rf + (i + 0.5) * spacing;
        w[i] = exp(-(x[i] * x[i]) / (2.0 * sigma * sigma));
    }
    for (step = 0; step < 8; step++) {
        double phase = 2.0 * M_PI * step / 8.0, lin = 0.0, rect = 0.0, s;
        for (i = 0; i < NSUB; i++) {
            s = contrast * sin(2.0 * M_PI * x[i] / period + phase);
            lin  += w[i] * s;                     /* a linear cell would see this */
            rect += w[i] * (s > 0.0 ? s : 0.0);   /* this cell rectifies first */
        }
        printf("phase %d: linear %7.3f rectified %7.3f rate %6.2f Hz\n",
               step, lin, rect, gain * rect / NSUB);
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Show a drifting grating too fine for the cell to resolve and a linear cell says nothing while the smooth monostratified cell answers steadily at twice the drift frequency.

No other ganglion cell has thorn-covered dendrites filling a thick slab through the middle of the wiring layer, the large sparse cell is the widest thin net in the human retina, and the recursive bistratified cell is the one whose branches turn back on themselves.


PART TWELVE: OLFACTORY SYSTEM - SMELL

CHAPTER 220. OLFACTORY SENSORY NEURONS (NON-COG)

This is the only nerve cell in your body that touches open air, at the roof of your nose.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An olfactory sensory neuron catches an airborne molecule and turns it into an electrical pulse.

It is detector and messenger at once, using one of about 400 working human odorant receptor genes.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is bipolar, meaning two processes leave opposite ends of the cell body.

One ends in a knob bearing ten to thirty cilia, hair-like filaments in mucus that hold every receptor.

SIZE:

The cell body is 5 to 8 micrometres wide, a micrometre being a thousandth of a millimetre.

Cilia run 30 to 200 micrometres, and the axon, 0.1 to 0.7 micrometres thick, is among the body's thinnest.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

About 400 receptor-defined classes exist, one per intact gene, beside some 400 dead pseudogene versions.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The olfactory epithelium of the olfactory cleft, at the roof of each nasal cavity, on superior turbinate and upper septum.

Axons form 15 to 20 fila olfactoria and pierce the cribriform plate of the ethmoid bone.

That plate is the interface, air on one side and brain on the other, and the axons end in the bulb's glomerular layer.

NUMBER:

Six to twelve million in total, with 6 to 10 million per nasal cavity often quoted.

Confidence is moderate for the order of magnitude and poor for the exact value.

Ten million is about 0.01 percent of the body's 86 billion neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Input resistance reaches several gigaohms, so a whisper of current swings the voltage hard.

Rest is near minus 60 millivolts, and firing rarely passes 30 spikes per second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It receives no synapses at all, because its input is chemistry in the air.

Its terminals excite mitral tufts, tufted tufts, external tufted cells and periglomerular cells inside one glomerulus.

About 5,568 human glomeruli serve some 400 receptors, so roughly 14 to 16 belong to each.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Unmyelinated, meaning no fatty insulating sleeve, and slow at 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second.

It regrows from nose into brain throughout adult life, which almost no other axon can do.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, the brain's main excitatory messenger, acting on AMPA and NMDA receptors.

MYELINATION:

None at all, which is the price of packing millions of hair-thin wires through a bony sieve.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High for its size, since the cilia run a constant cascade and ion pumps never rest.

Replacement is the hidden cost, because a 30 to 60 day lifespan means rebuilding the population yearly.

PLASTICITY:

There is no learning synapse here, so plasticity means survival and rewiring.

Activity keeps cells alive, the chosen receptor steers each new axon home, and basal cells regenerate the sheet.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Sustentacular cells are this neuron's private liver, using cytochrome P450 enzymes to destroy inhaled toxins.

They, not the neurons, carry the ACE2 protein used by SARS-CoV-2, which is why COVID-19 steals smell.

Olfactory ensheathing cells, a glia found nowhere else, escort the bare axons into the brain.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the nose becomes a humidifier.

Flavour collapses to sweet, salt, sour, bitter and umami, and gas leaks go unnoticed.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The real computation is in the cilia, where the receptor activates the G protein Golf and adenylyl cyclase type 3.

That enzyme makes cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cyclic AMP), which opens a cyclic-nucleotide-gated channel.

Calcium entering opens ANO2, a calcium-activated chloride channel, and hoarded chloride pours out, supplying up to 90 percent of the current.

Calmodulin then desensitises the channel while the NCKX4 exchanger clears calcium, which is why you stop smelling your own house.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: odorant concentration at the cilia, 1 nanomolar to 100 micromolar; sniff gain 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate at 0 to 30 hertz into one glomerulus, onto mitral, tufted, external tufted and periglomerular cells.

• STATE: bound receptor fraction, cyclic AMP, ciliary calcium, adaptation, membrane voltage, cell age.

• PARAMETERS: receptor identity 1 to 400; half-saturation 1 to 50 micromolar; conduction 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: transduction 150 to 500 milliseconds; adaptation 0.5 to 5 seconds; replacement 30 to 60 days.

• FAILURE MODE: viral loss of supporting cells causes anosmia, total loss of smell; injury shears axons at the cribriform plate.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert odour concentration into an olfactory sensory neuron firing rate.
Create a number variable called ciliary_calcium with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called adaptation with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called rate_record with initial value empty list.
For each concentration in odour_time_series, perform the following operations.
    Set bound_fraction to the quotient of concentration divided by the sum of concentration and half_saturation.
    Set cyclic_amp to the difference of bound_fraction and adaptation.
    If cyclic_amp is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set cyclic_amp to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set cng_current to the product of cyclic_amp and cng_conductance.
    Set ciliary_calcium to the sum of the product of ciliary_calcium and calcium_decay and cng_current.
    Set total_current to the sum of cng_current and the product of ciliary_calcium and chloride_gain.
    Set firing_rate to the product of total_current and spike_gain.
    Add firing_rate to the end of rate_record.
    Set adaptation to the product of the sum of adaptation and the product of ciliary_calcium and adaptation_rate and adaptation_decay.
End the loop.
Return rate_record to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the model is a short loop over a numeric time series, where clarity beats speed.

def olfactory_sensory_neuron(concentrations):
    # Cyclic AMP cascade, ANO2 chloride amplifier, calcium adaptation.
    half_sat, cng_cond, cl_gain, spike_gain, max_rate = 5.0, 0.6, 4.0, 12.0, 30.0
    ca_decay, adapt_rate, adapt_decay = 0.90, 0.05, 0.98
    calcium, adaptation, rates = 0.0, 0.0, []
    for conc in concentrations:
        bound = conc / (conc + half_sat)     # receptor occupancy, 0 to 1
        camp = max(0.0, bound - adaptation)
        cng = camp * cng_cond                # cation current
        calcium = calcium * ca_decay + cng
        total = cng + calcium * cl_gain      # chloride amplification
        rates.append(min(max_rate, total * spike_gain))
        adaptation = (adaptation + calcium * adapt_rate) * adapt_decay
    return rates

if __name__ == "__main__":
    trace = [0.0] * 20 + [20.0] * 100 + [0.0] * 80   # odour on, then clean air
    out = olfactory_sensory_neuron(trace)
    print("peak", round(max(out), 2), "adapted", round(out[119], 2))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron exposed to open air, replaced every month or two, wired straight into the brain.

A bipolar cell with cilia in mucus, one receptor gene, and six weeks to live can be nothing else.

CHAPTER 221. MITRAL CELLS (Late-Reporting Odor Output Neuron) (COG)

Named for the bishop's mitre their triangular bodies resemble, these carry every smell you will ever have out of the bulb.

Mitral describes a bishop's hat, a shape word that says nothing about the message.

Late-Reporting names the dimension that separates it from its partner cell, because it has a higher spike threshold, a longer latency after the start of a sniff and a lower firing rate than the tufted cell, and it carries a strong inhibitory surround so that structurally different odorants activating neighbouring clusters actively suppress it, which sharpens contrast at the cost of speed.

Odor names the content of the message, one glomerulus and therefore in effect one receptor channel per cell.

Output Neuron names the job, because this is the cell that carries smell out of the olfactory bulb along the lateral olfactory tract to the whole of olfactory cortex, so nothing about a smell reaches the rest of the brain except through cells of this kind and their tufted partners.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A mitral cell is a large excitatory projection neuron, meaning it excites its targets and wires out of the region.

It listens to exactly one glomerulus, and so to one receptor type, then broadcasts that channel to olfactory cortex.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A plump triangular body sits in a sheet one to a few cells thick, with one thick apical dendrite.

That dendrite ends in a tuft filling a single glomerulus, and long lateral dendrites spread sideways and release transmitter themselves.

SIZE:

The cell body is large by brain standards, more than 20 micrometres across and often 20 to 30 in humans.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Type I cells run lateral dendrites deep in the external plexiform layer, while displaced type II cells sit higher.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The mitral cell layer of the olfactory bulb, between the external plexiform layer above and the granule cell layer below.

Its tuft sits at the interface with the glomerular layer, where bulb tissue meets incoming nose axons.

Axons run in the lateral olfactory tract to anterior olfactory nucleus, olfactory tubercle, piriform cortex, cortical amygdala and lateral entorhinal cortex.

NUMBER:

Human counts give about 51,000 per bulb at age 25, 33,000 at 60 and 15,000 at 95.

Across both bulbs, 60,000 to 100,000 is a fair adult figure, with moderate confidence from few specimens.

That is under 0.001 percent of all neurons, so ten million nose cells funnel into under a hundred thousand outputs.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Rest is near minus 60 millivolts, and the cell flips between a quiet downstate and an active upstate.

In the upstate it rings with intrinsic subthreshold oscillations at 10 to 50 hertz, from persistent sodium current opposed by potassium.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Glomerular drive comes partly from nose terminals but largely through external tufted cells in a multistep path.

Granule cells inhibit it at reciprocal dendrodendritic synapses, where two cells face each other and each releases onto the other.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

A single myelinated axon of 1 to 3 micrometres, conducting at several metres per second and branching widely.

Olfactory cortex is reached in one synaptic step with no thalamic relay, which is unique among the senses.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, at both the axon terminals and the lateral dendrites.

MYELINATION:

Yes, by oligodendrocytes once the axon joins the lateral olfactory tract.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high, because the cell keeps a huge dendritic tree charged and pumps ions from axon and dendrites alike.

PLASTICITY:

Long-term potentiation and depression at the granule cell synapse make lateral inhibition learnable.

Adults never replace these cells, so all plasticity here is synaptic rather than cellular.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes wrap the reciprocal synapses so tightly that their glutamate clearance sets how far spillover travels.

That fixes the width of lateral inhibition, and bulb microglia stay unusually active because this is a front line for inhaled pathogens.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove mitral cells and the bulb becomes a beautifully organised machine with no exit.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The apical tuft fires local spikes that travel to the soma and back, making it a semi-independent computer.

Back-propagating action potentials run along lateral dendrites and trigger glutamate release, turning an output spike into a sideways broadcast.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glomerular glutamate at 0 to 100 hertz; GABA from periglomerular and granule cells; cortical feedback; acetylcholine and noradrenaline.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate at 0 to 80 hertz to anterior olfactory nucleus, olfactory tubercle, piriform cortex, cortical amygdala, entorhinal cortex.

• STATE: somatic voltage, tuft voltage, oscillation phase, lateral dendrite calcium, adaptation current, sniff phase.

• PARAMETERS: soma 20 to 30 micrometres; one glomerulus per cell; oscillation 10 to 50 hertz; threshold about minus 45 millivolts.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 30 milliseconds; oscillation 20 to 100 milliseconds; dendrodendritic inhibition 50 to several hundred milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: alpha-synuclein reaches bulb and anterior olfactory nucleus at Braak stage 1 of Parkinson's disease, and smell fails years before tremor.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide when a mitral cell fires by combining glomerular drive with an intrinsic oscillation and granule cell inhibition.
Create a number variable called membrane_voltage with initial value -60.
Create a number variable called oscillation_phase with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called inhibition with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called spike_times with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called step_index with initial value 0.
While step_index is less than total_steps, repeat the following steps.
    Set drive to the item at position step_index in glomerular_input.
    Set oscillation_phase to the sum of oscillation_phase and phase_increment.
    Set oscillation to the product of oscillation_amplitude and the sine of oscillation_phase.
    Set leak to the product of leak_rate and the difference of membrane_voltage and resting_voltage.
    Set membrane_voltage to the difference of the sum of membrane_voltage and the sum of drive and oscillation and the sum of leak and inhibition.
    If membrane_voltage is greater than spike_threshold, then perform the following actions.
        Add step_index to the end of spike_times.
        Set membrane_voltage to reset_voltage.
        Set inhibition to the sum of inhibition and granule_feedback_strength.
    End the conditional block.
    Set inhibition to the product of inhibition and inhibition_decay.
    Set step_index to the sum of step_index and 1.
End the loop.
Return spike_times to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python with the standard maths library is chosen because the model needs only a sine oscillator and a loop.

import math

def mitral_cell(drive_series, dt=0.001):
    # A 40 Hz intrinsic subthreshold oscillation gates spike timing.
    v, v_rest, v_thresh, v_reset = -60.0, -60.0, -45.0, -65.0
    leak_rate, osc_hz, osc_amp = 0.05, 40.0, 2.0
    fb_strength, fb_decay = 3.0, 0.97   # granule cell feedback inhibition
    inhibition, phase, spikes = 0.0, 0.0, []
    for i, drive in enumerate(drive_series):
        phase += 2.0 * math.pi * osc_hz * dt
        v += drive + osc_amp * math.sin(phase) - leak_rate * (v - v_rest) - inhibition
        if v > v_thresh:
            spikes.append(i * dt)
            v = v_reset
            inhibition += fb_strength
        inhibition *= fb_decay
    return spikes

if __name__ == "__main__":
    out = mitral_cell([0.6] * 500)   # 500 ms of steady glomerular drive
    print(len(out), "spikes")
    if out:
        print("first spike at", round(out[0], 3), "seconds")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Look for a big triangular soma in a sheet one cell thick and one thick dendrite rising to exactly one glomerulus.

No other human neuron combines a private sensory channel, a self-made 40 hertz metronome, and a one-synapse line to cortex.

CHAPTER 222. TUFTED CELLS (Early-Reporting Odor Output Neuron) (COG)

If the mitral cell files a considered report, the tufted cell is the wire-service reporter who gets the headline out first.

Tufted is again a shape word, describing the tuft of dendrites rather than the timing that makes this cell distinct.

Early-Reporting names the one dimension along which the pair differ, because tufted cells are more excitable, respond to olfactory sensory neuron stimulation at shorter latency and with higher firing rates, and are driven at odour concentrations too low to move mitral cells.

Odor and Output place this cell in the same job as its partner, since tufted cells are the second class of projection neuron leaving the olfactory bulb, though they aim mainly at nearby targets such as the anterior olfactory nucleus and the olfactory tubercle rather than the full extent of olfactory cortex.

Neuron marks it as the cell that says a smell is present while the other says what it is, since its responses adapt whereas mitral cell responses are sustained.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A tufted cell is a smaller excitatory projection neuron that also listens to one glomerulus and sends glutamate onward.

Its business is speed and sensitivity, firing at fainter odours and earlier in the sniff than a mitral cell.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A smaller, rounder body carries one apical dendrite ending in a tuft inside a single glomerulus.

Lateral dendrites are short and stay high in the external plexiform layer, and external tufted cells may have none.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 10 to 20 micrometres across, clearly smaller than the mitral cell's more than 20.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

External tufted cells sit at the rim of the glomerular layer, burst rhythmically and drive the glomerulus.

Middle tufted cells do most long-range projecting, internal tufted cells grade into mitral cells, and a superficial vasopressin group does not burst.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Throughout the external plexiform layer, with external tufted cells exactly at the interface between glomerular layer and external plexiform layer.

Axons run mainly to the anterior olfactory nucleus and olfactory tubercle, and some never leave the bulb at all.

NUMBER:

No reliable human count exists, and that gap is worth stating plainly.

In rodents tufted cells outnumber mitral cells two or three to one once external tufted cells are included.

Scaling gives roughly 200,000 to 400,000 across both bulbs, low confidence, a few percent of the bulb's ten million neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Lower threshold and higher input resistance than mitral cells mean the same current yields more spikes.

External tufted cells burst at 1 to 8 hertz with no input, driven by persistent sodium and hyperpolarisation-activated currents.

Tufted cells fire tens of milliseconds earlier in the sniff than mitral cells, and that phase lead encodes odour strength.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Strong direct input from olfactory sensory neurons arrives at the tuft, more direct than mitral cells receive.

Output onto mitral cells of the same glomerulus makes the external tufted cell an obligatory relay in their excitation.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thinner and shorter in range than a mitral axon, myelinated in the lateral olfactory tract.

External tufted axons often stay inside the bulb, linking sister glomeruli on the opposite face.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, with the same dendritic release ability as mitral cells.

MYELINATION:

Yes for projecting axons, though with thinner sheaths than mitral axons.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High and unusually steady, because a bursting cell never rests.

PLASTICITY:

Nose synapses onto tufted cells show short-term depression, giving automatic gain control across successive sniffs.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Glomerular astrocytes wrap external tufted dendrites and soak up the potassium thrown out by their constant bursting.

That keeps the glomerulus from running away into seizure-like synchrony, and otherwise the glial support resembles a mitral cell's.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove tufted cells and the bulb loses its early warning system.

Faint odours go unreported, mitral cells lose most of their drive, and the sniff-locked rhythm collapses.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

External tufted cells are genuine intrinsic oscillators, so they compute time rather than merely relaying it.

One of them can trigger a population burst across every mitral cell in its glomerulus, seizing a whole odour channel.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: strong direct nose glutamate at 0 to 100 hertz; GABA and dopamine from periglomerular and short-axon cells.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate to same-glomerulus mitral cells, the sister glomerulus, anterior olfactory nucleus and olfactory tubercle, in bursts.

• STATE: membrane voltage, burst phase, persistent sodium activation, hyperpolarisation-activated current, short-term depression level.

• PARAMETERS: soma 10 to 20 micrometres; burst rate 1 to 8 hertz; three to ten spikes per burst.

• TIME CONSTANTS: burst period 125 to 1000 milliseconds; within-burst interval 5 to 20 milliseconds; depression recovery 200 to 1000 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: losing this channel raises the detection threshold, so a strong smell is named while a faint one goes unnoticed.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to generate the rhythmic bursting of an external tufted cell and pass each burst to its glomerulus.
Create a number variable called membrane_voltage with initial value -60.
Create a number variable called burst_countdown with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called depression with initial value 1.
Create a list variable called output_spikes with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called step_index with initial value 0.
While step_index is less than total_steps, repeat the following steps.
    Set sensory_drive to the product of the item at position step_index in nose_input and depression.
    Set leak to the product of leak_rate and the difference of membrane_voltage and resting_voltage.
    Set membrane_voltage to the difference of the sum of membrane_voltage and the sum of sensory_drive and pacemaker_current and leak.
    If burst_countdown is greater than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Add step_index to the end of output_spikes.
        Set burst_countdown to the difference of burst_countdown and 1.
        Set membrane_voltage to reset_voltage.
    Else if membrane_voltage is greater than burst_threshold, then perform the following actions.
        Set burst_countdown to spikes_per_burst.
        Set depression to the product of depression and depression_factor.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set depression to the sum of depression and the product of recovery_rate and the difference of 1 and depression.
    End the conditional block.
    Set step_index to the sum of step_index and 1.
End the loop.
Return output_spikes to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python again, for continuity with the mitral model, so both cells can run in one loop and be compared.

def external_tufted_cell(nose_input, dt=0.001):
    # Intrinsic burster: pacemaker drive plus a depressing sensory synapse.
    v, v_rest, v_reset, v_burst = -60.0, -60.0, -62.0, -45.0
    leak, pace = 0.02, 0.30          # pace = persistent sodium plus Ih
    spikes_per_burst = 5
    dep_factor, recovery = 0.75, 0.01
    countdown, depression, spike_times = 0, 1.0, []
    for i, drive in enumerate(nose_input):
        v += drive * depression + pace - leak * (v - v_rest)
        if countdown > 0:
            spike_times.append(i * dt)
            countdown -= 1
            v = v_reset
        elif v > v_burst:
            countdown = spikes_per_burst   # start a new burst
            depression *= dep_factor
        else:
            depression += recovery * (1.0 - depression)
    return spike_times

if __name__ == "__main__":
    out = external_tufted_cell([0.05] * 1000)   # one second of weak odour
    print(len(out), "spikes in one second, delivered in bursts of 5")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Look for a 10 to 20 micrometre soma in the external plexiform layer with one tuft in one glomerulus.

If it bursts rhythmically with no input and can single-handedly ignite every mitral cell in its glomerulus, it is an external tufted cell.

CHAPTER 223. PERIGLOMERULAR CELLS (One-Channel Volume-Setting Neuron) (COG)

Around every glomerulus stands a ring of the smallest neurons in the bulb, and their whole job is to say no.

The old name is an address, meaning only around the glomerulus, and teaches nothing about what the cell does.

One-Channel records the decisive anatomical fact turned into function, because these are largely uniglomerular cells, wiring into a single glomerulus and therefore into the single odorant receptor type whose axons converge there, so their inhibition never spreads to a neighbouring smell channel.

Volume-Setting names the work, which is gain control, exerted both presynaptically on the incoming sensory terminals and postsynaptically on the output cells, so that the same odour at a hundredfold higher concentration does not simply saturate the channel.

Neuron marks it as a juxtaglomerular interneuron rather than an output cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A periglomerular cell is a tiny inhibitory interneuron, a local cell that quietens neighbours and never wires away.

It guards the bulb's doorway, turning loud signals down and sharpening the contrast between neighbouring glomeruli.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small round body sits between glomeruli and sends one short bushy dendrite into a single glomerulus.

That dendrite is both ear and mouth, receiving glutamate and releasing GABA, and the axon is very short or absent.

SIZE:

The cell body is 5 to 10 micrometres across, the smallest neuron in the olfactory bulb.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

In rodents calretinin marks about 44 percent, tyrosine hydroxylase about 16 percent, calbindin about 14 percent, and the GABA-A alpha5 subunit about 13 percent.

By wiring there are type 1 cells touched directly by nose axons, and type 2 cells driven by external tufted cells.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The glomerular layer of the olfactory bulb, packed into the shell of tissue around each glomerulus.

That shell is the interface between the olfactory nerve layer, which is pure incoming axon, and the external plexiform layer of bulb circuitry.

NUMBER:

No human count exists, so the figure is inferred from rodent density and the 5,568 glomeruli measured per human bulb.

A defensible estimate is 1 to 2 million across both bulbs, at low confidence.

That is perhaps 10 to 20 percent of bulb neurons and about 0.002 percent of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Tiny surface area gives input resistance near one gigaohm, so a single synapse can fire the cell.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inside the glomerulus it forms reciprocal dendrodendritic synapses with mitral and tufted tufts, taking glutamate and returning GABA at the same contact.

It also clamps the nose terminals themselves through GABA-B and dopamine D2 receptors.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Absent in many cells and extremely short where present, unmyelinated, confined to the glomerular layer.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA, the brain's main inhibitory messenger, in essentially all of them.

The tyrosine-hydroxylase-positive subset also releases dopamine, and in the bulb dopamine is made only here.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable in any real sense, because these cells have no long axon to insulate.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low in absolute terms, because the cells are minute.

The dopamine-making subset costs more, since tyrosine hydroxylase is expensive and its production tracks how much the nostril is used.

PLASTICITY:

Tyrosine hydroxylase switches off within days when a nostril is blocked, and returns when airflow resumes.

Rodents replace these cells for life, but carbon-14 birth dating shows human bulb neurons carry the carbon of their owner's birth year.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here build a near-complete shell around each glomerulus, a chemical fence they build nowhere else.

That fence stops glutamate leaking between glomeruli, protecting the one-receptor-one-glomerulus code on which smell depends.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the glomerular layer loses its volume control and its edges.

Strong smells would saturate every channel at once, so a rose and a bin would look alike to cortex.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

It can inhibit without firing a spike, because calcium in the dendritic tuft alone releases GABA.

It performs presynaptic inhibition, doing its computation on someone else's axon terminal before that terminal has spoken.

Its dopamine drifts through the shell by volume transmission, and gap junctions fuse the glomerulus into a partly single unit.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: nose glutamate for type 1 cells and external tufted glutamate for type 2, 0 to 100 hertz; basal forebrain GABA and acetylcholine.

• OUTPUTS: GABA onto mitral and tufted tufts; GABA and dopamine onto nose terminals, cutting their release.

• STATE: membrane voltage, dendritic calcium, GABA pool, dopamine pool, tyrosine hydroxylase expression level.

• PARAMETERS: soma 5 to 10 micrometres; input resistance about 1 gigaohm; presynaptic gain 0.1 to 0.9 as a multiplier.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; GABA-A 10 to 30 milliseconds; GABA-B and D2 presynaptic inhibition 100 to 1000 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: in Parkinson's disease the dopamine-making bulb cells roughly double, over-suppressing input, a proposed cause of the hyposmia found in over 90 percent of patients.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to apply periglomerular gain control, dividing the nose signal entering a glomerulus by the local activity level.
Create a number variable called local_activity with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called presynaptic_gain with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called dendritic_calcium with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called normalised_output with initial value empty list.
For each nose_drive in glomerular_input_series, perform the following operations.
    Set gated_drive to the product of nose_drive and presynaptic_gain.
    Set dendritic_calcium to the sum of the product of dendritic_calcium and calcium_decay and gated_drive.
    Set local_activity to the sum of the product of local_activity and activity_decay and gated_drive.
    Set presynaptic_gain to the quotient of 1 divided by the sum of 1 and the product of local_activity and inhibition_strength.
    If presynaptic_gain is less than minimum_gain, then perform the following actions.
        Set presynaptic_gain to minimum_gain.
    End the conditional block.
    Set tuft_output to the difference of gated_drive and the product of dendritic_calcium and release_gain.
    If tuft_output is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set tuft_output to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Add tuft_output to the end of normalised_output.
End the loop.
Return normalised_output to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because divisive normalisation is simple arithmetic, and the point is to show the feedback loop plainly.

def periglomerular_gain_control(nose_drive_series):
    # The glomerulus divides its own input by its own recent activity.
    ca_decay, act_decay = 0.85, 0.95
    inhib_strength = 2.0     # how hard GABA and dopamine clamp the terminal
    min_gain = 0.10          # presynaptic inhibition never shuts the door fully
    release_gain = 0.05      # dendritic calcium converted to GABA release
    calcium, activity, gain, out = 0.0, 0.0, 1.0, []
    for drive in nose_drive_series:
        gated = drive * gain
        calcium = calcium * ca_decay + gated
        activity = activity * act_decay + gated
        gain = max(min_gain, 1.0 / (1.0 + activity * inhib_strength))
        out.append(max(0.0, gated - calcium * release_gain))
    return out

if __name__ == "__main__":
    weak = periglomerular_gain_control([0.05] * 200)[-1]
    strong = periglomerular_gain_control([5.00] * 200)[-1]
    print(round(weak, 4), round(strong, 4))   # a 100-fold input gap is compressed

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the smallest neuron in the bulb, with one bushy dendrite in one glomerulus and often no axon at all.

If a cell inhibits an incoming sensory terminal from a dendrite rather than an axon, it is periglomerular.

CHAPTER 224. SHORT-AXON CELLS, INCLUDING SUPERFICIAL SHORT-AXON CELLS (Neighbour-Suppressing Contrast Neuron) (COG)

Their name is one of the great misnomers of neuroscience, because their wires run further than any other local cell in the bulb.

The existing label is an anatomical description that is close to false, because the superficial short-axon cell branches for up to one millimetre across the glomerular layer, which is long, not short, in a structure of that size.

Neighbour-Suppressing names what that reach is for, since these cells form the interglomerular circuit and release gamma-aminobutyric acid, known as GABA, together with dopamine onto external tufted cells and output cells in other glomeruli, so an active smell channel turns down the channels around it.

Contrast names the result, a global centre-surround inhibition that scales with the intensity of sensory input and decorrelates odour representations, so that similar smells become easier to tell apart.

Neuron marks it as an interneuron of the glomerular layer.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A short-axon cell is a local interneuron whose axon is short only beside a projection neuron's.

Superficial ones knit distant glomeruli into an interglomerular network, letting one odour channel rescale another.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

An oval body sits between glomeruli, and its dendrites pointedly stay outside glomeruli, which is the clearest way to tell it from a periglomerular cell.

Its axon runs sideways for a long way, dropping branches into many glomeruli on the route.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 8 to 12 micrometres across, slightly larger than a periglomerular cell.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Superficial short-axon cells come in an oligoglomerular form contacting 5 to 12 glomeruli and a polyglomerular form contacting tens to hundreds.

Deep short-axon cells include Golgi, Van Gehuchten, horizontal and Blanes cells, the last firing persistently for minutes after a brief stimulus.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The interglomerular spaces of the glomerular layer, itself an interface, being the connective tissue between odour channels rather than inside any.

Deep types sit in the internal plexiform and granule cell layers, at the border just below the mitral cell layer.

NUMBER:

No human count exists, and in rodents these are a clear minority of glomerular layer neurons.

Scaling gives roughly 100,000 to 300,000 across both bulbs, at low confidence.

That is on the order of 1 to 3 percent of bulb neurons, well under 0.001 percent of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Superficial cells fire tonically at a few hertz and follow odour input with sustained discharge.

Blanes cells flip into self-sustaining firing that outlasts a brief input by minutes, driven by a calcium-activated cation current.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Superficial cells are driven mainly by external tufted cells rather than by the nose directly.

Their output onto distant external tufted cells is biphasic, a fast GABA-A inhibition followed by slower dopamine D1-like excitation.

Blanes cells inhibit granule cells, and since granule cells inhibit mitral cells, the result is disinhibition, releasing an output cell by silencing its silencer.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Unmyelinated and thin, yet long by local standards at 0.5 to 2 millimetres.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA and dopamine released together from one cell onto one target, which is called co-transmission.

Because the two receptors differ in speed, a single presynaptic burst delivers two opposite instructions separated in time.

MYELINATION:

None, since these axons are thin and local.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Higher than most interneurons, because a millimetre of bare axon is costly to keep polarised.

PLASTICITY:

Dopamine synthesis rises and falls with how much the nostril is used.

The persistent state of a Blanes cell is itself a memory, since a brief input changes behaviour for minutes.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes in the interglomerular spaces are the main sink for dopamine here, because the bulb has few dopamine reuptake transporters.

Glial uptake therefore sets how far dopamine spreads, and so how many glomeruli one cell reaches chemically.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and every glomerulus becomes an island.

A strong smell would flood the output with no way to rescale, and background odours could never be subtracted.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Co-transmission with opposite signs is a real computation, writing a fast negative and a slow positive onto one wire.

Persistent firing in Blanes cells is a memory built from an intrinsic current rather than from any synapse.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from external tufted cells and mitral tufts, 0 to 100 hertz; basal forebrain acetylcholine and GABA; cortical glutamate for deep types.

• OUTPUTS: GABA and dopamine onto external tufted, periglomerular and mitral targets in 5 to several hundred distant glomeruli; GABA onto granule cells for Blanes cells.

• STATE: membrane voltage, calcium-activated cation current activation, GABA pool, dopamine pool, tonic rate.

• PARAMETERS: soma 8 to 12 micrometres; axon span 0.5 to 2 millimetres; glomeruli contacted 5 to several hundred; tonic rate 1 to 10 hertz.

• TIME CONSTANTS: GABA-A 10 to 30 milliseconds; dopamine excitation onset 200 to 500 milliseconds; Blanes persistent firing tens of seconds to minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: without interglomerular normalisation a target odour cannot be found against a strong background, a common complaint in post-viral smell disorder.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the two-part effect of a short-axon cell on a distant external tufted cell.
Create a number variable called gaba_level with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called dopamine_level with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called effect_record with initial value empty list.
For each source_rate in short_axon_firing_series, perform the following operations.
    Set gaba_level to the sum of the product of gaba_level and gaba_decay and the product of source_rate and gaba_release_gain.
    Set dopamine_level to the sum of the product of dopamine_level and dopamine_decay and the product of source_rate and dopamine_release_gain.
    Set inhibitory_effect to the product of gaba_level and gaba_receptor_strength.
    Set excitatory_effect to the product of dopamine_level and dopamine_receptor_strength.
    Set net_effect to the difference of excitatory_effect and inhibitory_effect.
    Add net_effect to the end of effect_record.
    If net_effect is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Display "target inhibited" to the console.
    Else if net_effect is greater than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Display "target excited" to the console.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Display "target unchanged" to the console.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Return effect_record to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the model is two leaky accumulators with different decay rates, clearest as plain arithmetic.

def short_axon_cotransmission(firing_series, dt=0.001):
    # Fast GABA then slow dopamine, released by the same presynaptic cell.
    gaba_decay, dopa_decay = 0.95, 0.9985     # about 20 ms and 700 ms
    gaba_gain, dopa_gain = 1.00, 0.02
