    gaba_strength, dopa_strength = 1.0, 6.0   # GABA-A versus D1-like receptors
    gaba, dopa, net = 0.0, 0.0, []
    for rate in firing_series:
        gaba = gaba * gaba_decay + rate * gaba_gain
        dopa = dopa * dopa_decay + rate * dopa_gain
        net.append(dopa * dopa_strength - gaba * gaba_strength)
    return net

if __name__ == "__main__":
    volley = [1.0] * 20 + [0.0] * 1500        # a 20 ms burst, then silence
    trace = short_axon_cotransmission(volley)
    print("10 ms", round(trace[10], 3))       # inhibited
    print("800 ms", round(trace[800], 3))     # excited

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Look for a glomerular layer cell whose dendrites stay out of glomeruli while its axon crosses half the bulb.

If instead an interneuron deep in the granule layer keeps firing minutes after stimulation stops, that is a Blanes cell.

CHAPTER 225. OLFACTORY BULB GRANULE CELLS (COG)

The most numerous neuron in your olfactory bulb has no axon at all, and speaks entirely through knobs on one branch.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A granule cell is a small inhibitory interneuron supplying almost all the inhibition in the deeper bulb.

It quietens the mitral cell that just excited it and the neighbours around it, which sharpens odours and builds the bulb's gamma rhythm.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

One long apical dendrite climbs into the external plexiform layer studded with large spines called gemmules, and there is no axon whatsoever.

SIZE:

The body is 6 to 10 micrometres across and the apical dendrite climbs 300 to 600 micrometres.

Each gemmule head is one to two micrometres wide, which is large for a spine.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Superficially branching cells contact tufted cells, deeply branching ones contact mitral cells, and intermediates contact both.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The granule cell layer of the olfactory bulb, the deepest and thickest cellular layer, with stragglers in the internal plexiform and mitral layers.

Its gemmules work up in the external plexiform layer, so the cell straddles that interface, with body and business end in different layers.

NUMBER:

Granule cells outnumber mitral cells by 50 to 100 to one, giving roughly 2 to 4 million per bulb.

That is about 5 to 7 million across both bulbs, the large majority of the bulb's ten million neurons.

Confidence is moderate for the ratio and low for the total, since no modern human count exists.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Single gemmules fire local dendritic spikes using low-voltage-activated calcium channels and NMDA receptors, without the cell body doing anything.

A global spike travels up the dendrite and enables release at every gemmule at once, so the cell has local and global modes.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The defining contact is the reciprocal dendrodendritic synapse, where one gemmule presses on a mitral lateral dendrite and the two release onto each other.

Basal dendrites receive an enormous glutamate feedback projection from piriform cortex and anterior olfactory nucleus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Not Applicable, and this is a biological fact rather than missing information.

The cell is genuinely axonless, so every output is a dendritic release event.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA, released from the gemmules on the apical dendrite.

MYELINATION:

None, and it could not be otherwise, since myelin sheathes axons and this cell has none.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low per cell, since each is small and usually silent.

PLASTICITY:

The reciprocal synapse supports long-term potentiation and depression, so lateral inhibition is rewritten by experience.

Rodents add new granule cells for life, but carbon-14 dating shows human bulb neurons carry the carbon of their owner's birth year.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes wrap the reciprocal synapses so closely that they set how long glutamate lingers, and thus how much GABA comes back.

The rostral migratory stream, the glial tube carrying new granule cells in rodents, is vestigial in adult humans.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove granule cells and the bulb loses both contrast and rhythm.

Gamma oscillations vanish, similar odours blur together, and cortical feedback arrives at an empty address.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This is the textbook neuron that computes with dendrites instead of axons, since each gemmule can take glutamate, spike locally and release GABA without the soma knowing.

Presynaptic NMDA receptors inside the gemmule admit the calcium that triggers that release, an arrangement almost unique in the brain.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from mitral and tufted lateral dendrites at the gemmules, 0 to 80 hertz; cortical feedback on basal dendrites; acetylcholine, noradrenaline, serotonin.

• OUTPUTS: GABA from gemmules onto mitral and tufted lateral dendrites, both recurrent and lateral; no axonal output of any kind.

• STATE: somatic voltage, per-gemmule voltage and calcium, NMDA activation per gemmule, asynchronous release pool, global spike flag.

• PARAMETERS: soma 6 to 10 micrometres; 50 to 100 granule cells per mitral cell; hundreds to thousands of gemmules per cell.

• TIME CONSTANTS: synchronous GABA within 10 milliseconds; asynchronous release 100 to 1000 milliseconds; gamma cycle 25 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: weakened granule inhibition widens odour tuning and is proposed to underlie parosmia, the distorted and phantom smells that follow viral injury.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the inhibition one granule cell returns to a row of mitral cells through its reciprocal spines.
Create a list variable called spine_calcium_list with initial value empty list.
Create a list variable called inhibition_out with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called soma_charge with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called global_spike with initial value false.
Create a number variable called spine_index with initial value 0.
For each mitral_rate in mitral_activity_list, perform the following operations.
    Set spine_calcium to the product of mitral_rate and nmda_gain.
    Add spine_calcium to the end of spine_calcium_list.
    Set soma_charge to the sum of soma_charge and the product of spine_calcium and soma_coupling.
End the loop.
If soma_charge is greater than somatic_threshold, then perform the following actions.
    Set global_spike to true.
End the conditional block.
While spine_index is less than the total number of items in spine_calcium_list, repeat the following steps.
    Set local_calcium to the item at position spine_index in spine_calcium_list.
    If global_spike is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set release to the product of the sum of local_calcium and global_boost and release_gain.
    Else if local_calcium is greater than local_spike_threshold, then perform the following actions.
        Set release to the product of local_calcium and release_gain.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set release to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Add release to the end of inhibition_out.
    Set spine_index to the sum of spine_index and 1.
End the loop.
Return inhibition_out to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the computation maps a list of spines onto a list of mitral partners, which Python lists express directly.

def granule_cell(mitral_rates):
    # One gemmule per mitral partner, with local and global release modes.
    nmda_gain, soma_coupling = 0.8, 0.9
    soma_threshold = 12.0     # global spike threshold
    local_threshold = 1.5     # local dendritic spike threshold per gemmule
    release_gain, global_boost = 0.5, 2.0
    spine_calcium = [r * nmda_gain for r in mitral_rates]
    global_spike = sum(spine_calcium) * soma_coupling > soma_threshold
    inhibition = []
    for ca in spine_calcium:
        if global_spike:
            inhibition.append((ca + global_boost) * release_gain)
        elif ca > local_threshold:
            inhibition.append(ca * release_gain)   # purely local release
        else:
            inhibition.append(0.0)
    return inhibition, global_spike

if __name__ == "__main__":
    quiet, g1 = granule_cell([0.2, 0.2, 4.0, 0.2, 0.2])   # one loud partner
    loud, g2 = granule_cell([4.0] * 5)                    # a whole odour
    print([round(x, 2) for x in quiet], g1)
    print([round(x, 2) for x in loud], g2)

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the most numerous neuron in the bulb, it has no axon, and its output synapses sit in giant spines.

If a neuron's inhibitory output is triggered by NMDA receptors on its own release site, it is an olfactory bulb granule cell.

CHAPTER 226. BLANES CELLS (COG)

One brief sniff can leave this cell talking to itself for minutes after the smell has gone.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A Blanes cell is a big inhibitory neuron, meaning one that quietens others, in the deepest layer of the olfactory bulb.

It keeps firing for minutes after a brief input stops, and since everything it silences is itself inhibitory, it works as a switch on the bulb's own volume control.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is a plump star, called stellate, with thick spine-covered dendrites, the branching antennae that gather signals, confined to the granule cell layer, while its single thin axon climbs into the external plexiform layer.

SIZE:

The cell body spans sixteen to twenty-three micrometres, a micrometre being one thousandth of a millimetre, making it the largest of the four described deep short-axon cell types and two to three times wider than neighbouring granule cells.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Deep short-axon cells split three ways by where their axons end, in the glomerular, external plexiform or granule cell layer, and Blanes cells sit mostly in the middle group, with markers such as neuropeptide Y varying between them.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The granule cell layer of the main olfactory bulb, some in the internal plexiform layer, which is the interface between the bulb's inhibitory reservoir and its output layer, with the axon crossing into the external plexiform layer where output cells and granule cells meet.

NUMBER:

Nobody has counted them in a human, so confidence is low.

Each bulb holds about three and a half million neurons in men and six point nine million in women, and scaling the sparse rodent proportion gives twenty thousand to one hundred thousand across both bulbs, under one percent of bulb neurons and seven ten-thousandths of one percent of the body's eighty-six billion.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Rest is sixty-five millivolts negative inside, and a burst of spikes, the brief pulses neurons signal with, admits calcium that opens I-CAN, the calcium-activated non-selective cation current, whose lingering afterdepolarisation sustains firing at a few hertz until a hyperpolarising step switches it off.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitation arrives on axon collaterals of mitral and tufted cells, the bulb's output neurons, and paired recordings show direct inhibition of granule cells, so a firing Blanes cell frees mitral cells from restraint, an arrangement called disinhibition.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin, unmyelinated and short, branching within about a millimetre and never leaving the bulb, so a conduction speed under one metre per second still costs under a millisecond.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Gamma-aminobutyric acid, abbreviated GABA, the brain's main inhibitory messenger, acting on GABA type A receptors, pores that admit chloride and hold the target below threshold, with peptide or nitric oxide co-release likely in some cells.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable in the usual sense, and the reason matters: myelin is fatty insulation that buys speed on long axons, and these are short local cables with no speed problem, so they run bare.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Minutes of firing are expensive, since every spike is repaid by pumping sodium out and potassium back in, I-CAN admits a steady extra trickle, and calcium must be expelled, all burning adenosine triphosphate, abbreviated ATP, the cell's fuel.

PLASTICITY:

Plasticity here is intrinsic rather than synaptic, because leftover calcium from earlier bursts sets how easily the next barrage latches the cell on, and unlike granule cells, replaced for life from the ventricle wall, Blanes cells are born once.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes face an unusual load, because a neuron firing for minutes sheds potassium continuously rather than in pulses and it must be siphoned off, and microglia stay busier here than elsewhere because this layer receives newborn neurons for life.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete it and the bulb loses its short-term memory for smell, inhibition becomes purely reactive, and the region can no longer fade out a steady odour while staying alert to a new one.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its calcium-gated cation current makes the membrane itself bistable, storing one bit with no network loop, while spines give local chemical compartments, sustained firing likely drives local blood flow, and arriving newborn granule cells rebuild its synapses for life.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Glutamate from mitral and tufted collaterals at zero to sixty nanosiemens, acetylcholine and noradrenaline scaling peak I-CAN between zero point five and two, and GABAergic reset steps of five to twenty millivolts.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ GABA onto granule cell dendrites at one to five nanosiemens per contact, in persistent trains of one to fifteen hertz.

• STATE.

◦ Voltage, internal calcium, I-CAN gate from zero to one, adaptation conductance, persistent-mode flag.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Rest minus sixty-five millivolts, threshold minus forty-five, reset minus sixty, peak I-CAN zero point six times leak, half-activation calcium zero point three five.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane twenty to thirty milliseconds, adaptation one hundred to two hundred milliseconds, calcium one to three seconds, persistent epoch up to minutes.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Weak I-CAN costs the bulb its odour memory and adaptation to background smells, plausibly feeding the early smell loss of Parkinson and Alzheimer disease, while over-active I-CAN silences granule cells and lets bulb output run away.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to latch a Blanes cell into persistent firing after a brief odour barrage.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value -65.
Create a number variable called calcium with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 25.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called spikes with initial value empty list.
While [clock is less than 6000] remains true, repeat the following steps.
    If [clock is greater than or equal to 300] is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set drive to the product of 40 and the quotient of calcium divided by the sum of calcium and 0.35.
    End the conditional block.
    Set voltage to the sum of voltage and the quotient of the difference of drive and the sum of voltage and 65 divided by 50.
    Set calcium to the difference of calcium and the quotient of calcium divided by 3000.
    If [voltage is greater than or equal to -45] is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add clock to the end of spikes.
        Set voltage to -60.
        Set calcium to the sum of calcium and 0.12.
    End the conditional block.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 0.5.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in spikes.
Display that count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: no libraries, and the lingua franca of computational neuroscience.
DT, V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET, TAU_M = 0.5, -65.0, -45.0, -60.0, 25.0
E_CAN, E_K, G_CAN, K_CA, TAU_CA = 0.0, -85.0, 0.6, 0.35, 1500.0
CA_STEP, TAU_AHP, AHP_STEP = 0.12, 150.0, 0.08
v, ca, ahp, t, spikes = V_REST, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0, []
while t < 6000.0:
    drive = 25.0 if t < 300.0 else 0.0             # 300 ms odour barrage
    g = G_CAN * ca * ca / (ca * ca + K_CA * K_CA)  # calcium opens I-CAN
    v += (-(v - V_REST) + drive + g * (E_CAN - v) + ahp * (E_K - v)) / TAU_M * DT
    ca -= ca / TAU_CA * DT
    ahp -= ahp / TAU_AHP * DT
    if v >= V_TH:
        v, ca, ahp = V_RESET, ca + CA_STEP, ahp + AHP_STEP   # latch and brake
        spikes.append(t)
    t += DT
print("spikes:", len(spikes), "| last at", round(spikes[-1], 1), "ms")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A fat spiny star sixteen to twenty-three micrometres wide in the deepest bulb layer among tiny granule cells, still firing after a short depolarising pulse has ended, and switching off like a light when hyperpolarised.

CHAPTER 227. MIGRATORY GONADOTROPIN-RELEASING HORMONE NEURONS (Nose-Born Migrating Neuron) (NON-COG)

Puberty begins in the nose, in a few thousand cells born in the lining of the nostril that then crawl into the brain.

The old label is a peptide barcode plus a committee minute, naming a hormone and a movement but no purpose.

Nose-Born records the startling and solid developmental fact, that these neurons arise in the nasal placode alongside the olfactory sensory cells and then crawl along vomeronasal and terminal nerve axons through the cribriform plate into the forebrain, which is why the same failure produces both anosmia and infertility in Kallmann syndrome.

Migrating names the journey itself, the only such crossing made by any neuron destined for the human forebrain.

Neuron marks it as one of the few thousand cells that complete the journey, whose pulsatile secretion into the pituitary portal blood is the switch that starts puberty and paces the reproductive cycle thereafter.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Born outside the brain in the nasal placode, a thickened patch of skin-like tissue in the roof of the embryonic nose, this cell makes gonadotropin-releasing hormone, abbreviated GnRH, a ten amino acid chain called a decapeptide.

It migrates into the hypothalamus and releases GnRH in hourly pulses into a private blood supply feeding the pituitary gland, making these cells the master switch for puberty, the menstrual cycle and fertility.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is a thin spindle with one process from each end, a shape called bipolar, and its long process reaching the median eminence, the release zone under the hypothalamus, carries spines like a dendrite yet conducts and secretes like an axon, earning its own name, the dendron.

SIZE:

Adult human male cell bodies measure about thirty-seven micrometres long by fourteen wide, against twenty-eight by twelve in the rhesus monkey, while the dendron runs up to four millimetres in the mouse, which for a body that narrow is like a person with a two hundred metre arm.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Most cells settle in the preoptic area and infundibular region and project to the median eminence, while a minority stay stranded along the route; a separate one hundred and fifty thousand to two hundred thousand GnRH-producing cells in the human putamen are cholinergic interneurons, not this lineage.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Origin is the nasal placode, and the route follows the vomeronasal and terminal nerves, the latter also called cranial nerve zero, through the nasal septum, across the cribriform plate, the perforated bone dividing nose from brain, then turns at the nasal forebrain junction, the interface where peripheral tissue meets central nervous system.

Adult cell bodies scatter from the septal and preoptic region to the retromammillary region, including the diagonal band of Broca, lamina terminalis, periventricular nucleus and infundibular nucleus, the human name for the arcuate nucleus, with terminals in the median eminence and a few cells in the ganglion terminale on the olfactory bulb.

NUMBER:

About two thousand GnRH neurons run reproduction in an adult human hypothalamus, with good confidence in the order of magnitude and moderate confidence in the exact value.

How many set out from the nose has never been measured, so the honest bound is at least two thousand, which is two ten-millionths of one percent of the body's eighty-six billion neurons and a rounding error against this section's budget of twenty-two million.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The cells fire slowly and irregularly at rest, under one spike per second, and every hour or two the population switches into a synchronised burst lasting minutes that releases a pulse of hormone.

Spikes begin not at the cell body but at one patch ninety micrometres out along the dendron, marked by the anchoring protein ankyrin-G.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The dominant input is kisspeptin from infundibular cells that also make neurokinin B and dynorphin, nicknamed KNDy neurons, whose mutual excitation and muting form the pulse generator itself.

The output is no classical synapse, because the dendron empties GnRH into portal capillaries, so the target is a blood vessel and, through it, the gonadotrophs of the anterior pituitary.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The projection is the dendron, thin and heavily branched near its end, conducting at a fraction of a metre per second, which is irrelevant for an output measured in pulses per hour.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The product is the GnRH decapeptide in dense core vesicles, released by calcium-triggered fusion during bursts, with galanin or glutamate as co-transmitters in some cells.

Pulses come every ninety-five minutes in the early follicular phase of the menstrual cycle, every sixty to seventy around ovulation, then every four to six hours in the luteal phase, and since fast pulses favour luteinising hormone and slow ones follicle-stimulating hormone, timing alone carries two instructions.

MYELINATION:

The dendron is unmyelinated, because myelin buys speed and speed is worthless to a system counted in pulses per hour, and although migrating cells travel with axons ensheathed by olfactory ensheathing cells, the GnRH cell itself is never myelinated.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Baseline demand is low, but migration is costly, since building and dismantling the leading process burns adenosine triphosphate, abbreviated ATP, and each pulse empties and rebuilds vesicles at terminals that sit outside the blood-brain barrier with direct access to glucose.

PLASTICITY:

Plasticity here is hormonal and structural, because across the menstrual cycle glia physically wrap and unwrap the terminals, opening and closing access to portal blood over days, while oestrogen and testosterone remodel synapses on the cell bodies.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Olfactory ensheathing cells escort the migrating neurons out of the nose, and in animals lacking them the neurons never arrive.

Tanycytes, elongated glia lining the third ventricle floor, wrap the terminals and physically retract to expose them when a surge is needed, a mechanical gate with no counterpart in the brain, while local astrocytes release prostaglandin E2 that directly triggers secretion.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and the hypothalamus loses time: temperature, hunger, thirst and sleep would still work perfectly, but the hourly rhythm vanishes, and with it the monthly cycle and the arc from puberty to menopause, leaving a person permanently prepubertal and infertile.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

One of very few neurons not born in the neural tube, so a simulation must model migration before electricity, it has an output process that is neither axon nor dendrite but both, a release site outside the blood-brain barrier, and scattered cell bodies that synchronise across millimetres by volume transmission, meaning signalling by diffusion through tissue rather than through synapses.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Kisspeptin pulses from KNDy neurons at zero point two to one per hour, glutamate, GABA and orexin synapses, circulating oestradiol, progesterone and testosterone, and in development anosmin-1 from the ANOS1 gene plus fibroblast growth factor 8 on receptor FGFR1.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ GnRH decapeptide into hypophyseal portal blood, targeting anterior pituitary gonadotrophs, the interval between pulses carrying the message.

• STATE.

◦ Migration position, then voltage, burst flag, releasable vesicle pool, time since last pulse, steroid feedback level.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Population about two thousand, spike initiation ninety micrometres out, dendron up to four millimetres, baseline under one hertz.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Burst one to five minutes, interpulse interval sixty to three hundred and sixty minutes, migration about ten weeks in the human embryo.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Failed migration gives Kallmann syndrome, anosmia plus congenital hypogonadotropic hypogonadism, in one in thirty thousand males and one in one hundred and twenty-five thousand females, ANOS1 mutations behind roughly eight percent, and a nineteen-week X-linked fetus showed the neurons arrested in a tangle above the cribriform plate.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to walk a newborn gonadotropin releasing hormone neuron from the nose to the hypothalamus and report whether it arrived.
Create a number variable called position with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called anosmin_level with initial value 1.
Create a boolean variable called arrived with initial value false.
Create a number variable called day with initial value 0.
While [day is less than 70] remains true, repeat the following steps.
    Set position to the sum of position and the product of 2 and anosmin_level.
    If [both position is greater than or equal to 45 and anosmin_level is less than 0.5] is true, then perform the following actions.
        Display "stalled above the cribriform plate, the Kallmann pattern" to the console.
        Break out of the current loop immediately.
    End the conditional block.
    If [position is greater than or equal to 100] is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set arrived to true.
        Display "reached the hypothalamus, puberty can begin" to the console.
        Break out of the current loop immediately.
    End the conditional block.
    Set day to the sum of day and 1.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: the random module ships with the language, so this model runs anywhere.
import random
N_START, ROUTE, GATE, DAYS, ATTRITION = 10000, 100.0, 45.0, 70, 0.03

def migrate(anosmin, seed=7):
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    tally = {"arrived": 0, "stuck": 0, "lost": 0}
    for _cell in range(N_START):
        pos, fate = 0.0, "lost"
        for _day in range(DAYS):
            if rng.random() < ATTRITION:
                break                                      # dies or diverts
            pos += 2.0 * anosmin * rng.uniform(0.6, 1.4)   # grip on scaffold
            if pos >= GATE and anosmin < 0.5:
                fate = "stuck"                             # tangle on the bone
                break
            if pos >= ROUTE:
                fate = "arrived"
                break
        tally[fate] += 1
    return tally

for name, level in (("intact anosmin-1", 1.0), ("ANOS1 mutation", 0.4)):
    print(name, migrate(level))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other neuron is born outside the brain, walks in through holes in the skull, then spends eighty years releasing a ten amino acid hormone into a blood vessel once an hour.

Stain a hypothalamus for GnRH and a thin scatter of spindles appears along a line from septum to mammillary bodies, the fossilised path of an embryonic journey.


PART THIRTEEN: AUDITORY AND VESTIBULAR PERIPHERAL NEURONS

CHAPTER 228. TYPE I SPIRAL GANGLION NEURONS (Sound-Detail Reporting Neuron) (NON-COG)

Every word you have ever heard crossed into your brain along a bundle of roughly thirty thousand of these cells per ear, and each one of them listens to a single hair cell through a single synapse.

Type One is an alphanumeric series label that tells a student nothing, so the name has to carry the work instead.

Sound-Detail names the world these cells serve and the precision they serve it with, because every note, word and warning a person consciously hears travels along them and nowhere else, each myelinated cell listening to exactly one inner hair cell through a single ribbon synapse.

Reporting names the job, a faithful one-way dispatch to the brain.

Neuron marks it as the overwhelming bulk of the line, roughly ninety to ninety-five percent of all spiral ganglion cells, graded by spontaneous firing rate from near zero up to about one hundred and twenty spikes per second so that quiet whispers and loud shouts are split across separate channels.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is the first neuron in the hearing pathway, the cell that turns a mechanical event inside the ear into an electrical message the brain can read.

It is a sensory afferent, which means a neuron that carries information inward from a sense organ toward the brain.

Its job is to sit at one inner hair cell, the sensory cell that actually detects sound vibration, and to report every flicker of transmitter that hair cell releases.

Frequency is carried by which fibre fires, because each fibre is wired to one place along the coiled cochlea and each place responds best to one pitch.

Loudness is carried by how fast the fibre fires and by how many neighbouring fibres are recruited.

Timing is carried with astonishing precision, because these cells can lock their spikes to the peaks of a sound wave to within tens of millionths of a second.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is bipolar, meaning the cell body has exactly two processes leaving it on opposite sides, like a bead threaded on a string.

The peripheral process runs outward to the organ of Corti, the strip of sensory tissue inside the cochlea, and ends in one small unbranched swelling called a bouton pressed against one inner hair cell.

The central process runs inward through the internal auditory canal, the bony tunnel connecting ear to skull cavity, and ends in the cochlear nucleus of the brainstem.

The peripheral process is wrapped in myelin, a fatty insulating sheath, until it passes through the habenula perforata, a row of tiny holes in bone, where the myelin stops and the last stretch runs bare.

In humans, and unusually among mammals, the cell body itself is almost always unmyelinated and is instead draped in satellite glial cells, with several cell bodies often sharing one glial envelope.

Detailed human work by Rattay, Glueckert and colleagues describes three glial categories around the human cell body: myelinating Schwann cells, non-myelinating Schwann cells rich in laminin beta 2 and collagen four, and satellite glial cells.

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly fifteen to thirty micrometres across, a micrometre being one thousandth of a millimetre, which makes it a large neuron by peripheral standards.

The peripheral process is about one to three micrometres thick, thinning to well under one micrometre at the bare terminal.

The central axon is about two to four micrometres thick including its myelin.

The peripheral process is short, roughly zero point three to four millimetres depending on where in the spiral it starts.

The central axon is longer, on the order of fifteen to twenty five millimetres from ganglion to brainstem, a figure estimated from anatomy rather than measured fibre by fibre.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Single cell ribonucleic acid sequencing, a method that reads which genes are switched on in one cell at a time, splits these neurons into three molecular families called type one a, type one b and type one c.

Type one a cells are marked by high calretinin, type one b by calbindin and titin, and type one c by the genes Pou4f1 and Lypd1, with Runx1 shared by b and c.

These map roughly onto the older physiological classes of high, medium and low spontaneous rate fibres.

High rate fibres contact the pillar side of the hair cell, the side facing the outside of the spiral, and have low thresholds and small terminals with large synaptic ribbons.

Low rate fibres contact the modiolar side, facing the cochlear core, and have high thresholds and a wide dynamic range that keeps working in loud noise.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The cell bodies sit in Rosenthal canal, a spiral tunnel wound through the modiolus, which is the bony central pillar of the cochlea, inside the densest bone of the human body.

The peripheral terminals lie in the inner spiral plexus of the organ of Corti, directly beneath the inner hair cells, along the whole thirty two to thirty five millimetres of the human cochlear duct.

Two interfaces matter and must be modelled explicitly.

The first is the habenula perforata, where the fibre leaves bone and enters fluid filled sensory tissue and where myelin ends.

The second is the glial and Schwann junction, also called the Obersteiner and Redlich zone, which for the eighth cranial nerve sits unusually far out inside the internal auditory canal and marks the handover from Schwann cell myelin to oligodendrocyte myelin.

The central terminals occupy all three divisions of the cochlear nucleus: anteroventral, posteroventral and dorsal.

NUMBER:

Human temporal bone counts put thirty thousand to thirty five thousand spiral ganglion neurons in each ear, of which ninety to ninety five percent are type one.

That gives roughly twenty seven thousand to thirty three thousand per ear, so about fifty five thousand to sixty thousand across both ears, which fits inside the sixty thousand allotted to the spiral ganglion in this section.

Confidence is high, because the figure comes from repeated serial section counts in post mortem human ears rather than from extrapolation.

Against a whole nervous system of roughly eighty six billion neurons, this is about seven hundredths of a thousandth of one percent, which is to say seven neurons in every ten million.

Counts fall with age at roughly one hundred cells per year of life in human temporal bones, so a healthy ninety year old may have lost a third of them.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The resting membrane voltage sits near minus sixty to minus seventy millivolts.

Spontaneous firing, meaning firing in complete silence, is bimodal across the population: one group whispers below one spike per second, another chatters at forty to eighty spikes per second, with the peak near sixty.

Sound drives an onset burst of several hundred spikes per second that settles within milliseconds to a sustained one hundred to two hundred fifty spikes per second.

The absolute refractory period, the dead time after a spike when no second spike is possible, is about zero point three to zero point seven milliseconds as measured with electrical stimulation in cochlear implant users.

Spikes lock to the phase of a pure tone up to about four to five kilohertz in cat, above which only the envelope is followed.

Modelling work indicates that wave one of the human auditory brainstem response, the first bump in the standard clinical hearing test, is generated largely by the unmyelinated cell body region of these neurons.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input is a single ribbon synapse, a specialised release site built around a protein bar that tethers dozens of transmitter packets ready for instant release.

Roughly eight to ten of these neurons converge on each of the approximately three thousand five hundred human inner hair cells, but each neuron takes input from one hair cell only, with no convergence at all on the receiving side.

The receiving receptors are alpha amino three hydroxy five methyl four isoxazolepropionic acid receptors, known as AMPA receptors, built from GluA2, GluA3 and GluA4 subunits.

The terminal also receives lateral olivocochlear efferent endings from the brainstem, which release dopamine, acetylcholine, gamma aminobutyric acid, calcitonin gene related peptide and enkephalins to trim the fibre gain and protect it.

Output is onto bushy cells of the anteroventral cochlear nucleus through the endbulb of Held, one of the largest synapses in the brain, plus collateral branches to the posteroventral and dorsal divisions.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The central axon is myelinated, tonotopically ordered so that neighbouring fibres carry neighbouring pitches, and it bifurcates on entering the cochlear nucleus into an ascending and a descending branch.

Conduction velocity is roughly ten to twenty metres per second in cat, and has never been measured directly in a living human, so the human value is inferred rather than known.

Spikes are initiated at the first heminode, the last insulated patch just inside the habenula perforata, not at the cell body.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The neuron itself releases glutamate, the standard excitatory transmitter of the brain, at the cochlear nucleus.

It receives glutamate from the inner hair cell, loaded into vesicles by vesicular glutamate transporter three, and mutations in that transporter cause deafness while leaving the hair cell bundle intact.

MYELINATION:

Schwann cells myelinate the peripheral and proximal central segments, and oligodendrocytes take over centrally.

The human cell body is the striking exception, sitting bare inside a satellite glial wrapping, which slows and delays the spike as it crosses the soma and is thought to add a distinct conduction step absent in cats and rodents.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

A fibre resting at sixty spikes per second never stops, so its sodium and potassium pumps run continuously and its terminals are packed with mitochondria.

Blood arrives only through the labyrinthine artery, an end artery with essentially no backup supply, so a few minutes of interruption is enough to silence the ear.

PLASTICITY:

This is not a learning synapse in the usual sense, but it remodels.

After loud noise the ribbon synapses can be destroyed while hair cells survive, a condition called cochlear synaptopathy or hidden hearing loss, and only some of those synapses regrow.

The fibres depend on brain derived neurotrophic factor and neurotrophin three supplied by hair cells and supporting cells, which is why they slowly die after the hair cells do.

They can nonetheless survive for decades after deafness, which is the entire reason cochlear implants work.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here do two jobs that glia elsewhere do not.

Inner phalangeal and border cells, the supporting cells that hug the terminal, clear released glutamate with the transporter GLAST, and when that clearance fails the terminal swells and bursts in the classic picture of cochlear excitotoxicity.

The satellite glial clusters around the bare human cell bodies group several neurons into shared electrical compartments, an arrangement not seen in the cochleas of laboratory animals.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this one cell type and the cochlea becomes a perfect, exquisitely tuned mechanical instrument with nobody listening.

The travelling wave still runs, the hair cells still bend, the outer hair cells still amplify, and not one bit of it reaches the brain.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Coding here is fundamentally stochastic, meaning the timing of each spike depends on the random arrival of transmitter packets at one ribbon, so the fibre is a probability machine rather than a summing machine.

The bare human cell body behaves as a low pass filter and current sink, delaying the spike and, by that delay, generating the far field electrical signature clinicians record as wave one.

Because bare cell bodies are packed side by side in Rosenthal canal, ephaptic interaction, meaning direct electrical eavesdropping between neighbours without any synapse, is anatomically plausible in humans and is a real design question for any implant model.

The terminal signals backwards too, taking up neurotrophins and shipping them to the nucleus to keep survival genes switched on.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate release events from exactly one inner hair cell ribbon synapse, zero to about one thousand vesicles per second.

• INPUTS: efferent modulation from lateral olivocochlear fibres, modelled as a gain scalar between zero point five and one point two.

• OUTPUTS: action potentials onto the endbulb of Held on one anteroventral cochlear nucleus bushy cell, plus collaterals to posteroventral and dorsal divisions.

• STATE: membrane voltage, readily releasable vesicle pool occupancy, time since last spike, short term adaptation level.

• PARAMETERS: characteristic frequency between twenty hertz and twenty kilohertz, spontaneous rate class of zero point five, ten or sixty spikes per second, threshold between zero and forty decibels sound pressure level, saturation rate two hundred to three hundred spikes per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: refractory period zero point three to zero point seven milliseconds, rapid adaptation one to five milliseconds, short term adaptation forty to one hundred milliseconds, vesicle pool refill ten to fifty milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: noise or ageing destroys ribbon synapses, low spontaneous rate fibres go first, the audiogram stays normal, and the patient cannot follow speech in a noisy room.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert transmitter release at one ribbon synapse into a train of nerve impulses with the correct spontaneous rate, saturation and dead time.
Create a number variable called spontaneous rate with initial value 60.
Create a number variable called threshold decibels with initial value 5.
Create a number variable called sound decibels with initial value 40.
Create a number variable called time step with initial value 0.00001.
Create a number variable called vesicle pool with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called release credit with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called time since spike with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called driven rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike total with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 100000 times.
    Set time since spike to the sum of time since spike and time step.
    Set vesicle pool to the sum of vesicle pool and the product of 0.0005 and the difference of 1 and vesicle pool.
    Set driven rate to the product of 6 and the difference of sound decibels and threshold decibels.
    If driven rate is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set driven rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    If time since spike is less than 0.0007, then perform the following actions.
        Skip to the next iteration of the loop.
    End the conditional block.
    Set release credit to the sum of release credit and the product of vesicle pool and the product of time step and the sum of spontaneous rate and driven rate.
    If release credit is greater than or equal to 1, then perform the following actions.
        Set spike total to the sum of spike total and 1.
        Set release credit to the difference of release credit and 1.
        Set vesicle pool to the difference of vesicle pool and 0.15.
        Set time since spike to 0.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spike total to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because its standard library gives clean pseudo random draws, so the genuinely stochastic ribbon synapse can be written the way the biology actually works.
import math, random
DT = 1e-5              # 10 microsecond step
ABS_REF = 0.7e-3       # absolute dead time after a spike, seconds
REL_TAU = 1.0e-3       # relative refractory recovery, seconds
SUBTYPES = {           # name: (spontaneous rate spikes/s, threshold dB SPL)
    "Ia_high": (60.0, 5.0),
    "Ib_med":  (10.0, 20.0),
    "Ic_low":  (0.5, 35.0),
}
def drive(level_db, threshold_db, sat=250.0, spread=30.0):
    """Sigmoid rate versus level curve for one fibre."""
    x = (level_db - threshold_db) / spread
    return sat / (1.0 + math.exp(-4.0 * x))
def run(subtype, level_db, seconds=1.0, seed=1):
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    spont, thr = SUBTYPES[subtype]
    target = spont + drive(level_db, thr)
    pool, refill = 1.0, 0.020        # readily releasable pool, 20 ms refill
    last, t, spikes = -1.0, 0.0, 0
    for _ in range(int(seconds / DT)):
        t += DT
        pool += (1.0 - pool) * DT / refill
        since = t - last
        if since < ABS_REF:
            continue
        recov = 1.0 - math.exp(-(since - ABS_REF) / REL_TAU)
        if rng.random() < target * pool * recov * DT:
            spikes += 1
            last = t
            pool = max(0.0, pool - 0.15)   # one release event empties part of the pool
    return spikes
for name in SUBTYPES:
    print(name, "silence:", run(name, -20.0), "spikes/s   at 40 dB:", run(name, 40.0))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron in the human body whose entire sensory existence hangs on one synapse with one cell, with no second opinion and no convergence to average the noise away.

Add to that a cell body that in humans, almost alone among mammals, refuses to wear myelin, and you have a cell no other type can be mistaken for.

CHAPTER 229. TYPE II SPIRAL GANGLION NEURONS (Ear-Damage Reporting Neuron) (NON-COG)

For a century this thin, silent minority fibre looked like a wiring mistake, and then somebody played a painfully loud sound and discovered the ear has a pain nerve.

Type Two is a serial number, and it hides the fact that this cell is not really part of hearing at all.

Ear-Damage names what it watches, because its thin unmyelinated fibres wander to the outer hair cells and the synapses there are so sparse and weak that ordinary conversational sound barely moves them, whereas outer hair cell injury and very loud sound do.

Reporting names the message, since these cells closely resemble the pain-sensing fibres of the dorsal root ganglion and are the leading candidate for the sensation of auditory pain.

Neuron marks it as a small minority, only about five to ten percent of spiral ganglion cells, which fits an alarm rather than a main line.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is the small, bare, slow second class of hearing nerve cell, making up only about one in twenty of the neurons in the spiral ganglion, the cluster of hearing nerve cell bodies inside the cochlea.

Unlike its type one cousin it ignores the inner hair cells and instead crawls out to the outer hair cells, the three rows of cells that act as the ear's mechanical amplifier.

It is almost deaf to ordinary sound.

Its evidence based job is to report tissue damage, because it fires when the cochlea is injured and hair cells spill their contents, and it is therefore best described as the cochlear nociceptor, a nociceptor being a neuron that reports harm rather than sensation.

A second proposed job, still contested, is to supply the sensory limb of the medial olivocochlear reflex, the brainstem loop that turns the ear's amplifier down in noise.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is small and round and, in humans, unmyelinated in over ninety seven percent of cases, along with both of its processes.

The peripheral process leaves the ganglion, crosses the floor of the tunnel of Corti, a fluid filled triangular gap running the length of the sensory strip, then turns towards the base of the cochlea.

It then spirals along the outer spiral bundle between the supporting Deiters cells for up to about one millimetre before branching.

Each branch makes small bouton endings on many outer hair cells, with reports ranging from about ten to as many as one hundred cells contacted by a single fibre.

The shape is therefore the opposite of the type one cell: one neuron, very many sensory partners, each contributing a tiny amount.

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly ten to fifteen micrometres across, clearly smaller than the type one soma beside it.

The processes are fine, well under one micrometre thick, in the range of unmyelinated pain fibres elsewhere in the body.

The spiralling peripheral segment is long for such a thin fibre, commonly zero point three to one millimetre before it starts branching.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Conductance based modelling combined with genetic labelling in mouse indicates more than one flavour within the type two population, differing in how readily they fire repeatedly.

They are also distinguished by position, since fibres in the base of the cochlea contact fewer outer hair cells than fibres in the apex.

Human subtype structure has not been established, so any simulation should treat human subtypes as an open question rather than a settled list.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The cell bodies sit in Rosenthal canal in the modiolus, scattered among the far more numerous type one cells and usually towards the edge of the ganglion.

The peripheral endings lie in the outer hair cell region of the organ of Corti, in the outer spiral bundles between Deiters cells.

The fibre crosses two interfaces: the tunnel of Corti, where it is exposed to a different fluid from the rest of the organ, and the habenula perforata, the bony pore where fibres enter the sensory epithelium.

Centrally the axon runs in the cochlear nerve and ends mainly in the granule cell domain, a shell of small cells wrapped around the cochlear nucleus.

NUMBER:

The classic estimate is five to ten percent of all spiral ganglion neurons, giving about one thousand five hundred to three thousand per ear.

Human immunostaining for peripherin, the protein filament that marks these cells, found only about two percent of counted human neurons to be positive, which would put the true human figure nearer six hundred to one thousand five hundred per ear.

For the purposes of this section, allow about three thousand across both ears, which together with the type one allocation keeps the spiral ganglion inside its sixty thousand budget.

Confidence is moderate at best, and the honest statement is that the human count is bounded between roughly one thousand and six thousand in total rather than known.

That is around three neurons in every hundred million in the nervous system as a whole.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These cells are electrically reluctant, with high input resistance but a high firing threshold and a strong tendency to fire once and then fall silent.

Because each outer hair cell contributes only a minute current, hundreds of inputs must arrive together before the fibre reaches threshold, so it stays quiet at ordinary sound levels.

Spontaneous activity is very low or absent.

When it does fire, the rate is modest, on the order of single figures up to a few tens of spikes per second, quite unlike the hundreds seen in type one fibres.

Conduction is slow, because the fibre is thin and bare, which places it in the same class as the slow unmyelinated pain fibres of the skin, although the exact human velocity has not been measured.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input one is glutamate released from outer hair cell ribbon synapses, small and few, summed across many cells.

Input two is adenosine triphosphate, abbreviated ATP, the cell's own energy molecule, which becomes a damage signal when injured tissue spills it into the extracellular space and which acts here on purinergic P2X receptors.

Input three is inhibitory, since lateral olivocochlear efferent neurons make gamma aminobutyric acid synapses directly onto these fibres, giving the brain a way to mute its own damage alarm.

Output is onto granule cells and small cells at the edge of the cochlear nucleus rather than onto the main relay neurons, which is why type two signals never produce a clean sound percept.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is unmyelinated along essentially its entire length in humans, peripherally and centrally.

It runs with the type one axons in the cochlear nerve but conducts far more slowly, so its message arrives late.

It is tonotopically loose, because the long spiral along many outer hair cells blurs the fibre across a range of pitches instead of tying it to one.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The fibre itself is glutamatergic.

It expresses peripherin and, in several species, calcitonin gene related peptide and other peptides typical of small pain neurons elsewhere in the body, which is one of the strongest arguments for the nociceptor interpretation.

MYELINATION:

Not myelinated, and this is a defining feature rather than an accident.

The absence of myelin makes the fibre slow, cheap to run, and hard to record from, which is exactly why it took so long to understand.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low, because the cell is small, rarely fires and has no myelin to maintain.

Its vulnerability is not energetic but chemical, since it sits in the outer hair cell region, the first part of the organ of Corti to be poisoned by aminoglycoside antibiotics and loud noise.

PLASTICITY:

Sensitivity to ATP falls after the onset of hearing, which fits the everyday observation that loud sound is uncomfortable rather than agonising in a healthy ear.

After noise damage these fibres sprout and their endings reorganise, and mice lacking their outer hair cell contacts show altered vulnerability to noise induced hearing loss.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The relevant glia here are not classical glia at all but Deiters cells, the goblet shaped supporting cells that cradle the outer hair cells and physically enclose the spiralling fibre.

Deiters cells clear potassium and glutamate from the outer hair cell region and mechanically stiffen the whole structure, so they set both the chemical and the mechanical environment of this fibre in a way no astrocyte does anywhere else.

Type two fibres also lack the satellite glial clustering that surrounds human type one cell bodies.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and the cochlea loses its sense of self preservation.

The ear would still hear, but nothing would tell the brain that the sound now arriving is destroying the organ that detects it, and the reflex that turns the amplifier down in noise would probably weaken as well.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell is a spatial integrator over up to a millimetre of tissue, which is an unusual computation for a peripheral neuron and means its receptive field is a length of cochlea rather than a point.

It reads a chemical damage signal, ATP, not a mechanical one, so part of its input is pure biochemistry.

It receives direct inhibitory efferent control, so the brain can gate its own alarm.

Its role in the medial olivocochlear reflex is genuinely disputed: one study using peripherin knockout mice reported the reflex abolished, while a later re examination of the same mouse line concluded the reflex was intact, and a good simulation should expose this as a switchable assumption rather than hard code it.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: summed glutamate from ten to one hundred outer hair cells, each contributing a small conductance.

• INPUTS: extracellular ATP concentration in the outer hair cell region, treated as zero in health and rising sharply with tissue damage.

• INPUTS: gamma aminobutyric acid from lateral olivocochlear efferents, an inhibitory gain term.

• OUTPUTS: sparse action potentials to granule cell and small cell regions of the cochlear nucleus.

• STATE: membrane voltage, refractory timer, cumulative damage signal.

• PARAMETERS: number of outer hair cells contacted between ten and one hundred, membrane time constant twenty to forty milliseconds, firing threshold several times the maximum sound driven input.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane twenty to forty milliseconds, long refractory tens of milliseconds, damage signal rise and fall over seconds to minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: if it fails, painfully loud sound stops feeling painful, the protective reflexes weaken, and the person keeps exposing an ear that is already being destroyed.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide when a type two fibre fires, by summing tiny inputs from many outer hair cells and adding a damage signal.
Create a number variable called outer hair cell count with initial value 60.
Create a number variable called per cell input with initial value 0.004.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called time since spike with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called total input with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 4000 times.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 0.0005.
    Set time since spike to the sum of time since spike and 0.0005.
    Set total input to the product of outer hair cell count and per cell input.
    If clock is greater than 1, then perform the following actions.
        Set per cell input to 0.01.
        Set total input to the sum of total input and 1.5.
    End the conditional block.
    If time since spike is less than 0.05, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to 0.
        Skip to the next iteration of the loop.
    End the conditional block.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the product of 0.0167 and the difference of total input and membrane voltage.
    If membrane voltage is greater than or equal to 1, then perform the following actions.
        Set spike count to the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set membrane voltage to 0.
        Set time since spike to 0.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because this cell is essentially a slow summing device polled over many inputs and many small time steps, which is exactly the tight fixed step loop C was built for.
#include <stdio.h>
#define N_OHC    60      /* outer hair cells contacted by one fibre */
#define DT       0.0005  /* 0.5 ms step */
#define TAU      0.030   /* 30 ms membrane time constant, deliberately slow */
#define THRESH   1.0     /* firing threshold, arbitrary units */
#define REFRACT  0.050   /* long dead time after each spike */
#define ATP_GAIN 1.5     /* purinergic drive once tissue is damaged */
int main(void) {
    double v = 0.0, sum, t, last = -1.0, per_cell;
    int i, step, spikes = 0, damaged;
    for (step = 0; step < 4000; step++) {   /* 2 seconds of simulated time */
        t = step * DT;
        damaged = (t > 1.0);                /* damage begins at one second */
        per_cell = damaged ? 0.010 : 0.004; /* each hair cell gives very little */
        sum = 0.0;
        for (i = 0; i < N_OHC; i++) sum += per_cell;   /* spatial summation */
        if (damaged) sum += ATP_GAIN;       /* spilled ATP is the alarm signal */
        if (t - last < REFRACT) { v = 0.0; continue; }
        v += DT * (sum - v) / TAU;
        if (v >= THRESH) { v = 0.0; last = t; spikes++; }
    }
    printf("spikes in 2 s: %d\n", spikes);  /* silent for 1 s, then fires */
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the fibre that is bare, slow, small, nearly silent, and wired backwards compared with every other hearing neuron, taking a whisper from a hundred cells instead of a shout from one.

If you find a neuron in the cochlea that spirals for a millimetre past dozens of outer hair cells, carries peripherin, ignores ordinary sound and wakes up only when the tissue is being hurt, there is nothing else it can be.

CHAPTER 230. VESTIBULAR GANGLION NEURONS, ALSO CALLED SCARPA GANGLION NEURONS (NON-COG)

You are never aware of these cells, and yet they are firing about ninety times a second right now, and if they stopped the world would appear to slide off its hinges.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are the primary balance neurons, the first cells in the pathway that tells the brain which way your head is turning and which way is down.

They are sensory afferents, meaning neurons that carry information inward from a sense organ, and they connect the balance organs of the inner ear to the brainstem.

Their most important trick is that they never stop firing.

Because they idle at a high background rate, they can signal motion in both directions with the same fibre, speeding up for one direction and slowing down for the other, which no silent neuron could do.

They report head rotation from the three semicircular canals, which are fluid filled rings, and head tilt and linear acceleration including gravity from the two otolith organs, which are patches of sensory cells loaded with tiny crystals.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Each is bipolar, with one process going out to the balance organ and one going in to the brainstem, and a cell body sitting between them in the bony tunnel of the internal auditory canal.

The peripheral ending is what distinguishes vestibular from cochlear afferents, because it comes in three shapes.

A calyx ending is a large chalice of membrane that completely engulfs a type one hair cell like a hand closing round an egg, and no other synapse in the human body wraps its partner so completely.

A bouton ending is a small conventional swelling on a type two hair cell.

A dimorphic ending has both, one or more calyces plus a spray of boutons, and this is the commonest arrangement.

The cell bodies are grouped into a superior and an inferior part, together called the vestibular ganglion or Scarpa ganglion after the Italian anatomist Antonio Scarpa.

SIZE:

Cell bodies span roughly fifteen to thirty five micrometres, a micrometre being one thousandth of a millimetre, with a wide spread that correlates with function.

Axons are among the thickest in the eighth cranial nerve, commonly two to ten micrometres including myelin, and the largest calyx bearing fibres sit at the top of that range.

A single calyx ending is about ten to fifteen micrometres tall, large enough to be seen easily under a light microscope.

The whole cell spans perhaps ten to twenty millimetres from hair cell to brainstem.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

By ending: calyx only, bouton only, and dimorphic.

By target organ: canal afferents from the three cristae, which are the sensory ridges inside the canals, and otolith afferents from the utricular and saccular maculae, which are the sensory patches of the otolith organs.

By division: the superior division serves the utricle, the superior canal, the lateral canal and a small strip of the saccule, while the inferior division serves the rest of the saccule and, through the singular nerve, the posterior canal.

By discharge pattern: regular and irregular, two classes so distinct that they are treated separately as the next two entries in this book.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The cell bodies sit inside the internal auditory canal in the petrous part of the temporal bone, packed alongside the facial nerve and the cochlear nerve.

The peripheral endings occupy five sensory epithelia per ear: the cristae of the superior, lateral and posterior semicircular canals, and the maculae of the utricle and saccule.

Within each epithelium there are two zones that matter, the central zone, called the striola in the maculae and the crest in the cristae, and the surrounding peripheral or extrastriolar zone.

Two interfaces must be modelled.

The first is the hair cell and calyx cleft, a sealed twenty to thirty nanometre gap in which ions accumulate, which behaves like a chemical and electrical microcompartment rather than an ordinary synapse.

The second is the glial and Schwann junction, the Obersteiner and Redlich zone, which for the vestibular nerve lies unusually far out inside the internal auditory canal and is the site from which almost all vestibular schwannomas grow.

Centrally the axons reach the four vestibular nuclei in the medulla and pons, and a substantial minority run straight past them into the cerebellum.

NUMBER:

Unbiased stereological counting of microdissected human ganglia gave an average of about twenty three thousand six hundred neurons per side in people with normal balance function.

Classic serial section studies of one hundred and six temporal bones from seventy five people describe a steady linear fall from birth towards one hundred years of age, with the superior division losing more than the inferior.

Take about twenty thousand per side as the working figure, which is forty thousand across both ears and fits the vestibular allocation of this section exactly.

Confidence is good for the order of magnitude and fair for the exact value, since counting methods differ by several thousand.

That is roughly five neurons in every ten million in a nervous system of about eighty six billion.

Fibre counts fall measurably from the fifth decade of life and reach about forty percent loss by seventy five to eighty years.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting membrane voltage is around minus sixty millivolts.

Background firing without any head movement is high, roughly sixty to one hundred spikes per second in mammals, with values up to about one hundred and fifteen spikes per second recorded in macaque monkeys and as low as thirteen in a stingray.

Head movement modulates this rate up or down, with sensitivity typically a fraction of a spike per second for each degree per second of head rotation.

Firing cannot go below zero, so a fast head turn in the inhibitory direction silences the fibre completely, which is the cellular reason that the ear on the side being turned away contributes less information, an asymmetry first described by Ewald.

Peak driven rates reach several hundred spikes per second.

Firing regularity is set largely by low voltage activated potassium currents, principally KCNQ, also called Kv7, and Kv1 channels, together with persistent and resurgent sodium currents.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input is glutamate from vestibular hair cells at ribbon synapses, arriving at boutons, at calyces, or at both.

At the calyx there is a second, extraordinary input channel called non quantal transmission, in which potassium leaving the hair cell accumulates in the sealed cleft and depolarises the afferent directly, with no vesicles involved at all.

Efferent input arrives from cholinergic brainstem neurons and acts on nicotinic receptors on hair cells and on muscarinic receptors on the calyx itself, where it closes KCNQ channels and raises excitability.

Output is onto the superior, lateral, medial and inferior vestibular nuclei, and directly onto cerebellar targets in the flocculus, nodulus and uvula.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thickly myelinated and fast, because balance reflexes are the fastest in the body and cannot wait.

Axon diameter correlates with function, with the thickest axons belonging to the irregular calyx bearing afferents of the central zone.

Each axon typically branches to serve one epithelium only, preserving a clean map of which canal or macula is reporting.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The neuron releases glutamate onto vestibular nucleus neurons.

It receives glutamate from hair cells, acetylcholine from efferents, and, at the calyx, a direct potassium mediated signal that is not a transmitter at all.

MYELINATION:

Heavily myelinated by Schwann cells peripherally and by oligodendrocytes centrally, with the handover far out in the internal auditory canal.

The peripheral process loses its myelin just before the ending, so the last stretch and the calyx itself are bare.

Human vestibular ganglion cell bodies are wrapped by satellite glial cells, and reports differ on how much myelin remains over the soma, so a model should treat somatic myelination here as uncertain rather than assumed.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high and permanently so, because a cell that fires ninety times a second for a lifetime never gets to rest its ion pumps.

Blood supply comes through the labyrinthine artery, usually a branch of the anterior inferior cerebellar artery, with no meaningful collateral supply.

PLASTICITY:

The most important plasticity is not in this cell but downstream, and it is the reason people recover from vestibular injury.

When one ear is lost, its silent partner fibres leave the vestibular nuclei permanently unbalanced, and central compensation over days to weeks re tunes the nuclei to the new resting asymmetry.

Peripherally, calyces can retract from damaged hair cells and can partially re form, and in mammals a limited amount of hair cell regeneration follows damage.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The specialised glia of this system are the vestibular supporting cells, which sit shoulder to shoulder with the hair cells and take up potassium and glutamate from the epithelium.

Inside the calyx there is a further specialisation, because the calyx membrane itself is studded with potassium channels and transporters arranged in molecular microdomains, so the afferent partly does its own housekeeping in a space too small for any glial cell to enter.

Schwann cells at the Obersteiner and Redlich zone are unusual in one clinically enormous way: they are the origin of vestibular schwannoma, the common benign tumour also known as acoustic neuroma.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell type and the inner ear still detects motion perfectly and tells nobody.

The immediate cost is not falling over, it is that vision becomes useless during movement, because the reflex that holds your gaze steady while your head moves has no input, and the world jitters with every footstep, a symptom sufferers call oscillopsia.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Non quantal transmission at the calyx is a genuine second physics, in which the afferent is driven by the electrical potential and the potassium concentration in a sealed cleft rather than by transmitter binding, and it is fast enough to explain reflex latencies as short as five milliseconds.

The same sealed cleft allows ephaptic coupling, meaning direct electrical influence between hair cell and afferent without any synapse.

Glutamate spillover within the cleft produces slow responses on top of the fast ones, so one ending runs two timescales at once.

The high resting rate is itself a computation, since it converts a one directional sensor into a two directional signal.

Efferent modulation of KCNQ channels lets the brainstem change the cell's regularity and gain on the fly, for instance during voluntary head movements it has itself commanded.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from hair cell ribbon synapses at calyx and bouton endings, scaled by hair bundle deflection.

• INPUTS: potassium concentration and electrical potential in the calyx cleft, only for calyx bearing cells, driving non quantal transmission.

• INPUTS: acetylcholine from brainstem efferents acting on calyx muscarinic receptors, modelled as a KCNQ conductance reduction.

• OUTPUTS: action potentials to superior, lateral, medial and inferior vestibular nuclei and direct cerebellar targets.

• STATE: membrane voltage, KCNQ and Kv1 activation, cleft potassium concentration, spike adaptation level, time since last spike.

• PARAMETERS: resting rate sixty to one hundred spikes per second, rotational sensitivity zero point two to two spikes per second per degree per second, saturation about three hundred to four hundred spikes per second, floor of zero.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane one to ten milliseconds, cupula time constant about four to six seconds for canal afferents, otolith response essentially sustained, central compensation days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: sudden unilateral loss, as in vestibular neuritis, leaves the two sides unbalanced and produces violent spinning vertigo, nausea and involuntary eye movement until the brainstem rebalances.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn a head rotation profile into the firing rates of a left and right canal afferent pair, including the slow fading of the canal signal and the hard floor at zero spikes per second.
Create a number variable called resting rate with initial value 90.
Create a number variable called gain with initial value 0.4.
Create a number variable called decay factor with initial value 0.99881.
Create a number variable called canal signal with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called previous velocity with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called head velocity with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called left rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called right rate with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 6000 times.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 0.005.
    Set head velocity to 0.
    If clock is less than 20, then perform the following actions.
        Set head velocity to 120.
    End the conditional block.
    Set canal signal to the product of decay factor and the sum of canal signal and the difference of head velocity and previous velocity.
    Set previous velocity to head velocity.
    Set left rate to the sum of resting rate and the product of gain and canal signal.
    Set right rate to the difference of resting rate and the product of gain and canal signal.
    If left rate is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set left rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    If right rate is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set right rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display left rate to the console.
Display right rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the interesting content here is the shape of a signal over thirty seconds of simulated time, and Python lets that be written and probed in a few readable lines.
import math
DT    = 0.005     # 5 ms step
REST  = 90.0      # resting discharge, spikes per second
GAIN  = 0.4       # spikes per second per degree per second of head rotation
SAT   = 400.0     # ceiling on sustained firing
TAU_C = 4.2       # cupula time constant, seconds, human estimate
def simulate(profile):
    """profile is a list of head angular velocities in degrees per second."""
    a = math.exp(-DT / TAU_C)     # per step decay of the canal signal
    canal, prev, out = 0.0, 0.0, []
    for v in profile:
        # the canal is a high pass filter: it follows a change, then fades
        canal = a * (canal + v - prev)
        prev = v
        left  = min(SAT, max(0.0, REST + GAIN * canal))
        right = min(SAT, max(0.0, REST - GAIN * canal))
        out.append((left, right))
    return out
steps   = int(30.0 / DT)
profile = [120.0 if i * DT < 20.0 else 0.0 for i in range(steps)]  # spin, then stop
res     = simulate(profile)
for probe in (0.0, 1.0, 5.0, 19.9, 20.1, 25.0):
    left, right = res[int(probe / DT)]
    print("t=%5.1f s   left %6.1f   right %6.1f   spikes/s" % (probe, left, right))
# note the reversal after 20 s: stopping a long spin feels like turning the other way

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron in the human body that swallows its sensory partner whole inside a chalice of its own membrane, and one of very few that talks to that partner by leaking ions into a sealed pocket instead of by releasing a transmitter.

Add a background rate of ninety spikes per second that never pauses from before birth until death, and there is nothing else it could be.

CHAPTER 231. REGULAR-FIRING VESTIBULAR AFFERENTS (Even-Firing Slow-Sway Neuron) (NON-COG)

Set a metronome ticking ninety times a second and let it run for eighty years without missing a beat, and you have this neuron, the most reliable clock in the human body.

The old label describes the spike train's statistics rather than what the cell is for, so the new name keeps the clock but adds the purpose.

Even-Firing records the one hard fact the old name held, a metronomic resting discharge of around ninety spikes per second with very little jitter between intervals.

Slow-Sway names the motion it is built for, because these fine bouton-ending fibres from the outer rim of the sensory patch respond almost perfectly linearly to head velocity, at roughly half a spike per second for every degree per second of turn, and they hold their firing through sustained tilts and slow constant currents that their irregular cousins cannot sustain.

Neuron marks it as a cell that keeps a calm, unbroken running account of where the head is going.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is one of the two great functional classes of balance nerve fibre, defined not by where it lives or what it looks like but by the astonishing evenness of its firing.

Regularity is measured with the coefficient of variation, written CV, which is simply the standard deviation of the gaps between spikes divided by the average gap, so a value of zero means a perfect metronome.

These fibres have a normalised coefficient of variation below about zero point one, which means the gap between one spike and the next varies by less than one part in ten.

Their job is to carry a faithful, low distortion running commentary on head position and slow head movement.

Because their spike timing is so predictable, a small change in rate stands out clearly against the background, which is why they transmit on average about twice as much information per fibre as their irregular counterparts despite being far less sensitive.

They are the fibres that keep your eyes locked on a target while you walk, and they are the main drive for the vestibulo ocular reflex, abbreviated VOR, the automatic eye movement that counter rotates your eyes when your head turns.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Regular fibres are bipolar vestibular ganglion neurons like all the others, but with a characteristic ending.

The most regular of all are bouton only afferents, which make many small conventional swellings on type two hair cells and never form a calyx.

The rest are dimorphic afferents that happen to carry a large number of bouton endings alongside their calyces, and the more boutons a dimorphic fibre has, the more regular it becomes.

Their terminal fields are broad, spreading over many hair cells in the outer part of the sensory patch, so each fibre averages over a wide area.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are at the smaller end of the vestibular ganglion range, roughly fifteen to twenty five micrometres across.

Axons are thin by vestibular standards, typically two to five micrometres including myelin, which makes them somewhat slower than irregular fibres.

Their peripheral terminal fields can spread over a hundred micrometres or more of sensory epithelium.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Bouton only regular afferents, found almost exclusively in the peripheral or extrastriolar zone, are the extreme case with a coefficient of variation below zero point one.
