Bouton rich dimorphic regular afferents are commoner and sit at slightly higher variability.

Canal regular afferents and otolith regular afferents differ in their dynamics, since canal fibres inherit the slow fading of the cupula while otolith fibres hold a steady rate during a sustained tilt.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The cell bodies lie in the superior and inferior parts of the vestibular ganglion inside the internal auditory canal.

The peripheral endings occupy the peripheral zone of the three cristae, the sensory ridges of the semicircular canals, and the extrastriolar zone of the utricular and saccular maculae, which is the belt of tissue surrounding the central striola.

The important interface is the boundary between striola and extrastriola, an abrupt change in hair cell type, supporting cell chemistry and afferent behaviour that occurs over a few tens of micrometres.

Centrally they end in the vestibular nuclei, with a strong projection to the medial and superior nuclei that serve eye movement.

NUMBER:

These are not additional neurons; they are a subset of the vestibular ganglion population already counted, so nothing is added to this section's budget.

Across mammalian recordings, regular afferents make up roughly half of the vestibular nerve, with intermediate fibres about a quarter and irregular fibres about a quarter.

On a working figure of twenty thousand ganglion cells per side, that is around ten thousand regular afferents per side and about twenty thousand across both ears, all drawn from the forty thousand already allocated.

Confidence is moderate, because the proportions come from animal recordings rather than from human counts, and no one has ever classified a human vestibular nerve fibre by discharge regularity in a living person.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Background rate is high and steady, in the range of sixty to one hundred spikes per second.

The normalised coefficient of variation is below zero point one, so interspike intervals cluster tightly around the mean.

Sensitivity to head rotation is low, commonly in the region of zero point two to zero point five spikes per second per degree per second at one to two hertz.

Their response is tonic, meaning it tracks the stimulus itself rather than its rate of change, and it adapts slowly.

Detection thresholds are excellent despite the low gain, with angular velocity thresholds around half those of irregular fibres, roughly four degrees per second against eight.

The cellular basis is a sustained firing pattern with only a small low voltage activated potassium conductance, so the cell recovers smoothly and predictably after each spike instead of being pushed around by noise.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input is glutamate at many small bouton synapses from type two hair cells, so each individual input is tiny and the total is an average over many independent sources, which is itself a noise reduction strategy.

Efferent cholinergic input from the brainstem is present but produces weaker effects here than in irregular fibres.

Output is onto vestibular nucleus neurons, especially those of the three neuron arc that drives the eye muscles, and onto vestibulospinal neurons for postural control.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Myelinated, moderately thick, and reliably conducting.

Because the fibre is thinner than an irregular fibre, its central spike arrives a fraction of a millisecond later, which matters for the timing of the fastest reflexes.

Terminal arbours in the vestibular nuclei are broad and diffuse.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate is released centrally.

Glutamate is received peripherally from type two hair cells, with no significant non quantal component because a bouton has no sealed cleft in which ions can build up.

MYELINATION:

Present and unremarkable, with Schwann cells peripherally, oligodendrocytes centrally, and the usual loss of myelin at the ending.

Nothing about this fibre's myelin distinguishes it, and that is worth stating plainly so it is not mistaken for a forgotten detail.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Constant and high, since the cell fires roughly eight million spikes per day and never stops.

There is no rest period, no sleep related pause and no adaptation to silence, so the sodium and potassium pumps run at full duty cycle for life.

PLASTICITY:

The fibre itself changes little, but the synapses it makes in the vestibular nuclei are the substrate of vestibular compensation and of motor learning in the vestibulo ocular reflex.

That learning is the reason a person adapts to new spectacles within days, and the regular afferents supply the steady, low noise signal on which that recalibration depends.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Extrastriolar supporting cells differ from striolar ones in their potassium handling and in the composition of the gel and crystal layer above them, so this fibre sits in a chemically distinct neighbourhood from its irregular neighbour a hundred micrometres away.

Because bouton endings are small and open, potassium and glutamate clearance here is done conventionally by supporting cells, unlike the enclosed calyx where the afferent does much of the work itself.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the regular afferents and the balance organ keeps its alarm system and loses its narrator.

Sudden head jolts would still be detected, but the smooth, quiet, continuous report of where the head is and how it is drifting would vanish, and with it the steady gaze and steady posture that make walking feel effortless.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Regularity is an active computation, not an absence of noise, and it is produced by a particular mix of ion channels that includes small low voltage activated potassium currents and specific persistent and resurgent sodium currents that shape the recovery after each spike.

Averaging over many bouton inputs performs a spatial noise reduction that no single synapse could achieve.

The high resting rate plus low variability gives the downstream neuron a very precise reference, so that a change of only one or two spikes per second is statistically detectable within a few tens of milliseconds.

Efferent modulation can shift the operating point without destroying the regularity, so gain and reliability are controlled independently.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from many type two hair cell bouton synapses in the extrastriolar or peripheral zone.

• INPUTS: weak cholinergic efferent drive from the brainstem, modelled as a small excitatory offset.

• OUTPUTS: highly regular spike trains to medial and superior vestibular nuclei and to vestibulospinal neurons.

• STATE: membrane voltage, adaptation level, time since last spike, running list of recent interspike intervals for regularity measurement.

• PARAMETERS: resting rate sixty to one hundred spikes per second, coefficient of variation below zero point one, rotational gain zero point two to zero point five spikes per second per degree per second, membrane time constant about ten milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane about ten milliseconds, adaptation hundreds of milliseconds, canal cupula about four to six seconds, central compensation days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: selective loss of type two hair cells or of these fibres degrades low frequency and static balance signalling, producing unsteadiness and drift that is worst in the dark, when vision cannot substitute.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to generate a regular spike train from a tonic drive and then measure its regularity with the coefficient of variation.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called resting drive with initial value 1.49.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called last spike time with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called intervals with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called interval sum with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called interval count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called mean interval with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 50000 times.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 0.0001.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the product of 0.01 and the difference of resting drive and membrane voltage.
    If membrane voltage is greater than or equal to 1, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to 0.
        If last spike time is greater than 0, then perform the following actions.
            Add the difference of clock and last spike time to the end of intervals.
        End the conditional block.
        Set last spike time to clock.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
For each interval in intervals, perform the following operations.
    Set interval sum to the sum of interval sum and interval.
End the loop.
Set interval count to the total number of items in intervals.
Set mean interval to the quotient of interval sum divided by interval count.
Display the quotient of 1 divided by mean interval to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

JavaScript is chosen because this is a short spiking and statistics demonstration that a reader can paste straight into any browser console with no toolchain at all.
const DT = 0.0001;          // 100 microsecond step
const TAU = 0.010;          // 10 ms membrane time constant
const V_TH = 1.0;           // spike threshold
const NOISE = 0.02;         // deliberately small: regular afferents are quiet
const DRIVE_REST = 1.49;    // tonic drive that sets about 90 spikes per second
const GAIN = 0.004;         // extra drive per degree per second of head rotation
function run(headVelocity, seconds) {
  let v = 0.0, t = 0.0, last = null;
  const isis = [];
  const drive = DRIVE_REST + GAIN * headVelocity;
  const steps = Math.round(seconds / DT);
  for (let i = 0; i < steps; i++) {
    t += DT;
    const noise = NOISE * (Math.random() - 0.5) * 2;
    v += (DT / TAU) * (drive + noise - v);
    if (v >= V_TH) {
      v = 0.0;
      if (last !== null) isis.push(t - last);
      last = t;
    }
  }
  const n = isis.length;
  const mean = isis.reduce((a, b) => a + b, 0) / n;
  const varr = isis.reduce((a, b) => a + (b - mean) * (b - mean), 0) / n;
  // CV below 0.1 is the textbook definition of a regular afferent
  return { rate: (1 / mean).toFixed(1), cv: (Math.sqrt(varr) / mean).toFixed(3) };
}
console.log("at rest        ", run(0, 5));
console.log("head +50 deg/s ", run(50, 5));
console.log("head -50 deg/s ", run(-50, 5));

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Look at the gaps between its spikes.

If they are so nearly identical that the variation is under one part in ten, at a rate near ninety per second, in a fibre that ends only in small boutons out at the edge of a balance organ, you are looking at a regular afferent and at nothing else in the human nervous system.

CHAPTER 232. IRREGULAR-FIRING VESTIBULAR AFFERENTS (Uneven-Beat Sudden-Jolt Neuron) (NON-COG)

This is the nervous system's emergency line: sloppy, noisy, wildly sensitive, and fast enough to move your eyes before you know your head has moved.

Like its partner the old label reports only the scatter of the spike intervals, which is true but stops short of the function.

Uneven-Beat preserves that measured fact, a resting discharge whose intervals vary widely, and pairs it against the even-firing cell so a reader sees the single dimension separating the two.

Sudden-Jolt names the work, because these fibres, fed largely by calyx endings on type one hair cells in the central zone, are fast adapting high-pass detectors whose gain climbs with frequency, and otolith fibres of this class are the ones picked out by roughly five hundred hertz vibration in the clinic.

Neuron marks it as the fibre class most changed by the brain's own outgoing efferent supply, so a jolt is not merely detected but detected under central control.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is the second great functional class of balance nerve fibre, and it is the mirror image of the regular afferent in almost every respect.

Its spikes come at uneven intervals, with a normalised coefficient of variation, meaning the spread of the gaps between spikes divided by the average gap, above about zero point one and often above zero point three.

That untidiness is not a defect.

It goes with very high sensitivity to fast movement, so where a regular fibre changes its rate a little, an irregular fibre changes it a lot, and it does so preferentially for quick jolts rather than slow drifts.

Its job is to detect transients: the stumble, the head impulse, the sudden loud thud transmitted through the skull, the fast unexpected tilt.

It drives the fastest protective reflexes in the body, including the vestibulo collic reflexes that snap the neck muscles into action, and it is the fibre class that clinical tests such as the vestibular evoked myogenic potential, abbreviated VEMP, actually measure.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The defining structure is the calyx, a goblet of afferent membrane that engulfs an entire type one hair cell.

Calyx only afferents form one or a few calyces and no boutons at all, and these are the most irregular fibres of all.

Dimorphic afferents with only a few boutons alongside their calyces are also irregular, and regularity rises as the number of boutons rises.

The terminal fields are compact and concentrated in the central zone of the sensory epithelium, so this fibre samples a small patch intensely rather than a large area gently.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are at the large end of the vestibular ganglion range, roughly twenty five to thirty five micrometres across.

Axons are the thickest in the vestibular nerve, commonly four to ten micrometres including myelin, which makes them the fastest conductors in the eighth cranial nerve.

A single calyx is about ten to fifteen micrometres tall, and the sealed gap between calyx and hair cell is only about twenty to thirty nanometres wide, a nanometre being one millionth of a millimetre.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Calyx only afferents, restricted almost entirely to the striola of the maculae and the central crest of the cristae.

Bouton poor dimorphic afferents, which are irregular but less extremely so.

Canal versus otolith irregular afferents, which differ in their preferred stimulus but share the phasic, high gain behaviour.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The cell bodies lie in the superior and inferior parts of the vestibular ganglion inside the internal auditory canal.

The peripheral endings occupy the striola of the utricular and saccular maculae and the central crest of the three cristae, which are the sensory ridges of the semicircular canals.

The critical interface is the calyx cleft itself, a sealed twenty to thirty nanometre pocket between hair cell and afferent membrane which behaves as a chemical and electrical compartment in its own right, too small for any glial cell to enter.

A second interface is the striolar boundary, the sharp transition to the surrounding extrastriolar zone where regular afferents take over.

Centrally these axons target the vestibular nuclei with a bias towards neurons driving neck and body reflexes, and they project strongly to the cerebellum.

NUMBER:

Like the regular class, these are a subset of the vestibular ganglion neurons already counted, so they add nothing to this section's budget.

Animal recordings put irregular fibres at roughly a quarter to a third of the vestibular nerve, with calyx only fibres a small minority within that.

On twenty thousand ganglion cells per side, that is about five thousand to six thousand five hundred per side, so roughly ten thousand to thirteen thousand across both ears, all drawn from the forty thousand already allocated.

Confidence is moderate, since the proportions come from cat, chinchilla and monkey recordings and have never been established in a human being.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Background rate is similar to the regular class, around sixty to one hundred spikes per second, but the intervals scatter widely.

The normalised coefficient of variation is above zero point one and can exceed zero point five.

Rotational sensitivity is high and rises with frequency, so the fibre is a high pass filter that responds to how fast the stimulus is changing rather than to the stimulus itself.

Responses adapt quickly, dying away within tens to hundreds of milliseconds during a sustained stimulus.

The cellular cause is a large low voltage activated potassium conductance, principally KCNQ, also called Kv7, together with Kv1 channels, which makes the cell phasic, meaning it prefers to fire once at the start of a change rather than to fire steadily.

Detection thresholds for slow rotation are worse than for regular fibres, around eight degrees per second against four, which is the price paid for speed.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Input arrives through two channels at once at the calyx.

The first is ordinary quantal transmission, in which the hair cell releases packets of glutamate onto the calyx membrane.

The second is non quantal transmission, in which potassium flowing out of the hair cell accumulates in the sealed cleft and depolarises the afferent directly, partly by an almost instantaneous change in cleft voltage and partly by a slower change in the potassium equilibrium potential, with no vesicles involved.

Non quantal transmission avoids the delay of chemical release entirely and is the best explanation for reflex latencies as short as five milliseconds and for the very short latency vestibular evoked potentials.

Efferent cholinergic input acts on muscarinic receptors on the calyx, closing KCNQ channels and sharply raising excitability, and this class is far more strongly modulated by efferents than the regular class.

Output is onto vestibular nucleus neurons serving neck, trunk and limb reflexes, and onto cerebellar targets.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The thickest and fastest axons in the vestibular nerve, which is the whole point, because the reflexes they serve cannot tolerate delay.

Central terminal fields are focused rather than diffuse.

Conduction velocity has not been measured in humans, so the human value should be treated as inferred from animal data rather than known.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate is released centrally onto vestibular nucleus neurons.

Peripherally the fibre receives glutamate, and also receives a direct potassium mediated signal that is not a neurotransmitter at all, which makes this one of the very few places in the human nervous system where a synapse works partly by physics rather than by chemistry.

MYELINATION:

Thickly myelinated by Schwann cells peripherally and oligodendrocytes centrally, with myelin ending just before the calyx.

The first heminode, the last insulated patch of membrane before the bare ending, sits immediately outside the calyx and is where the spike is actually launched, which is why the calyx can be electrically driven so quickly.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High, both because of the constant background firing and because the large calyx membrane is expensive to maintain and is crowded with ion channels and transporters.

The calyx also has to manage the ionic composition of its own sealed cleft, which is metabolic work no ordinary nerve ending performs.

PLASTICITY:

Calyces retract from damaged hair cells and can partially re extend, and this retraction is one of the earliest signs of aminoglycoside injury.

The fibre's gain is under continuous efferent control, which is a fast, reversible form of plasticity operating over seconds.

Downstream, the loss of irregular input drives central compensation in the vestibular nuclei over days and weeks.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Striolar supporting cells differ from those in the surrounding zone in their potassium handling and in the thinner, differently structured layer of crystals above them.

The truly unusual arrangement is inside the calyx, where the afferent membrane itself carries potassium channels, glutamate transporters and calcium pumps arranged in molecular microdomains, so the neuron performs its own ion housekeeping in a space no glial process can reach.

That is a genuine departure from the rest of the nervous system, where such clearance is a glial monopoly.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the irregular afferents and the balance organ becomes calm, accurate and too slow.

Steady posture and smooth gaze would survive, but the sharp involuntary catch that stops you falling when you trip, and the reflexes that tests such as the head impulse test and the VEMP probe, would be gone, and a stumble would become a fall.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Non quantal transmission is the headline: this fibre is partly driven by ephaptic and resistive coupling, meaning direct electrical influence through a shared confined space, rather than by any released molecule.

The sealed cleft doubles as a glutamate trap, so spillover within it generates slow responses layered on top of the fast ones, and one ending therefore encodes two timescales simultaneously.

Adaptation is active, produced by potassium conductances rather than by synaptic depletion, so the fibre resets quickly and is ready for the next transient.

Efferent control of KCNQ channels means the brainstem can retune this fibre's gain and even its regularity within a second, for instance during a voluntary head turn it has itself commanded, so the cell's transfer function is not a constant.

The high resting rate lets the fibre report movement in both directions, but its high gain means it silences completely during strong inhibitory movements, which makes the paired opposite ear indispensable.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: quantal glutamate release from type one hair cells at the calyx.

• INPUTS: cleft potassium concentration and cleft electrical potential, driving fast non quantal depolarisation.

• INPUTS: acetylcholine from brainstem efferents on calyx muscarinic receptors, modelled as a reduction in KCNQ conductance.

• OUTPUTS: irregular, high gain spike trains to vestibular nuclei serving neck and body reflexes and to the cerebellum.

• STATE: membrane voltage, KCNQ activation, cleft potassium concentration, spike driven adaptation variable, high pass filtered stimulus estimate.

• PARAMETERS: resting rate sixty to one hundred spikes per second, coefficient of variation above zero point one, rotational gain roughly two to five times that of regular fibres and rising with frequency, membrane time constant two to five milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane two to five milliseconds, non quantal transmission well under one millisecond, adaptation twenty to two hundred milliseconds, canal cupula four to six seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: aminoglycoside antibiotics such as gentamicin concentrate in type one hair cells and cause calyx retraction, and after such treatment most afferents fail to modulate at all while their resting rate looks normal, so the patient loses fast balance responses and suffers oscillopsia while simple clinical tests may look unremarkable.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to generate an irregular, phasic spike train that responds strongly to a sudden head movement and then fades.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called adaptation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called filtered head signal with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called previous head velocity with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called head velocity with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called noise seed with initial value 7.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 100000 times.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 0.0001.
    If clock is greater than or equal to 5, then perform the following actions.
        Set head velocity to 100.
    End the conditional block.
    Set filtered head signal to the product of 0.998 and the sum of filtered head signal and the difference of head velocity and previous head velocity.
    Set previous head velocity to head velocity.
    Set noise seed to the sum of the product of noise seed and 1103515245 and 12345.
    Set noise seed to the remainder of noise seed divided by 2147483648.
    Set drive to the sum of 1.6 and the sum of the product of 0.01 and filtered head signal and the product of 0.3 and the difference of the quotient of noise seed divided by 2147483648 and 0.5.
    Set adaptation to the difference of adaptation and the product of adaptation and 0.005.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the product of 0.025 and the difference of the difference of drive and adaptation and membrane voltage.
    If membrane voltage is greater than or equal to 1, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to 0.
        Set adaptation to the sum of adaptation and 0.35.
        Set spike count to the sum of spike count and 1.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because showing genuine spike timing jitter needs a hundred thousand time steps and a repeatable pseudo random sequence, and C delivers both instantly.
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <math.h>
#define DT        0.0001   /* 100 microsecond step */
#define TAU       0.004    /* fast, phasic membrane */
#define TAU_ADAPT 0.020    /* spike driven adaptation */
#define TAU_HP    0.050    /* high pass filter on head velocity */
#define VTH       1.0      /* spike threshold */
#define NOISE     0.30     /* large: irregular afferents are noisy on purpose */
#define GAIN      0.010    /* drive per degree per second of head rotation */
int main(void) {
    double v = 0, w = 0, hp = 0, prev = 0, drive, head, t, last = -1;
    double sum = 0, sumsq = 0, isi, mean, var;
    int i, n = 0, burst = 0, steps = 100000;   /* 10 seconds */
    srand(7);
    for (i = 0; i < steps; i++) {
        t = i * DT;
        head = (t >= 5.0) ? 100.0 : 0.0;          /* head starts turning at 5 s */
        hp = exp(-DT / TAU_HP) * (hp + head - prev);  /* phasic: reacts to change */
        prev = head;
        drive = 1.60 + GAIN * hp + NOISE * ((double)rand() / RAND_MAX - 0.5);
        w -= DT * w / TAU_ADAPT;                  /* adaptation decays */
        v += DT * (drive - w - v) / TAU;
        if (v >= VTH) {
            v = 0.0;
            w += 0.35;                            /* each spike adapts the cell */
            if (last > 0.0 && t > 1.0 && t < 5.0) { isi = t - last; sum += isi; sumsq += isi * isi; n++; }
            if (t >= 5.0 && t < 5.1) burst++;
            last = t;
        }
    }
    mean = sum / n;
    var = sumsq / n - mean * mean;
    printf("resting rate %.1f spikes/s, CV %.3f\n", 1.0 / mean, sqrt(var) / mean);
    printf("spikes in the first 100 ms of the head turn: %d\n", burst);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only afferent in the body that swallows its sensory cell whole and then talks to it by leaking potassium into the sealed pocket between them, faster than any chemical synapse could manage.

Find a thick, noisy, quick adapting fibre ending in a calyx at the very centre of a balance organ, with spike intervals that scatter by more than a tenth, and you have found it.


PART FOURTEEN: TASTE AND OTHER CRANIAL SENSORY NEURONS

CHAPTER 233. GENICULATE GANGLION NEURONS (Front-Mouth Flavor Neuron) (NON-COG)

Every time a strawberry tastes sweet, the reason you know it is a few thousand cells the size of dust grains, buried in the bone beside your ear.

Geniculate means little knee and describes only the bend the facial nerve makes in the temporal bone, so the old label is an address rather than a job.

Front-Mouth names the territory these cells own, the taste buds of the fungiform papillae on the forward two thirds of the tongue, reached by the chorda tympani nerve, and the taste buds of the soft palate, reached by the greater superficial petrosal nerve.

Flavor names the work, and it is deliberately broader than taste, because these same fibres also report touch and cooling from the tongue surface, so what they deliver upward is a blended mouth report rather than a pure chemical one.

Neuron marks it as a first-order sensory cell whose palate duty is exact, contacting one hundred percent of soft palate taste buds while covering only about thirty five to fifty percent of those on the tongue itself.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is a pseudounipolar sensory neuron, meaning one stalk leaves the cell body and immediately splits in two, so the cell has no branching dendrites at all.

It carries taste from the front two thirds of the tongue along the chorda tympani nerve and from the soft palate along the greater petrosal nerve.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The round cell body is wrapped by satellite glial cells, helper cells forming a jacket one cell thick, and its single stalk forks at a T shaped junction.

At its far end the peripheral branch splits into a small tree of naked fibres that squeeze through the base of a taste bud and lie against the taste cells.

SIZE:

Cell bodies measure roughly 20 to 40 micrometres across, about half the width of a human hair, and the whole ganglion is only about 1 millimetre wide.

One peripheral arbor typically serves one to three taste buds.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Gene surveys sort these cells into a sweet best class marked by Spon1 and a sour best class marked by Penk, with umami depending on Cdh4, bitter on Cdh13 and salt on Egr2 neurons.

A separate non taste class carries touch and temperature and is marked by Piezo2, a mechanically gated pore, rather than by Phox2b, the master gene of the taste cells here.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The ganglion sits at the genu, the sharp first bend of the facial nerve, inside the facial canal of the petrous temporal bone, exactly at the interface between the labyrinthine and tympanic segments of that nerve.

Its endings lie in fungiform papilla taste buds on the anterior tongue, in the soft palate and in a patch of external ear canal skin, and its central terminals reach the rostral gustatory division of the nucleus of the solitary tract in the medulla.

NUMBER:

No reliable modern human count exists, and that must be said plainly rather than dressed up.

The regional budget allots fewer than 5,000 per side, and rodent counts of a few hundred to two thousand taste neurons support a working estimate of 2,000 to 4,000 per side, so 4,000 to 8,000 in total, with low confidence.

Against about 87 billion neurons in the whole nervous system that is roughly one neuron in every 15 million.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage sits near negative 55 to negative 60 millivolts, and many cells carry Nav1.8 and Nav1.9 sodium channels that keep working when the classic blocker tetrodotoxin is applied.

A drop of sugar drives a burst of perhaps 20 to 60 spikes per second that fades within a second or two.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

There are no synapses on the cell body, because a pseudounipolar cell has no dendrites to receive them.

At the taste bud, type II cells push adenosine triphosphate, the cell's energy molecule used here as a messenger, through CALHM1 and CALHM3 channels onto P2X2 and P2X3 receptors, while type III sour cells release serotonin onto 5-HT3 receptors.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The peripheral branch runs in the chorda tympani, a thread of nerve that crosses the eardrum between the tiny ear bones before joining the lingual nerve.

Fibres are 0.5 to 3 micrometres wide and conduct at 2 to 20 metres per second, so taste reaches the brainstem in roughly 10 milliseconds.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The central branch releases glutamate onto second order cells of the nucleus of the solitary tract.

The somatosensory subset also carries the peptides substance P and calcitonin gene related peptide, abbreviated CGRP, while the taste subset is largely peptide free.

MYELINATION:

Myelin is the fatty insulating wrap that makes fibres fast, and here it is patchy: most taste fibres are thinly myelinated A delta fibres and a large minority are bare C fibres.

Schwann cells make the myelin outside the skull and oligodendrocytes take over inside the brainstem.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Energy use is modest because firing rates are low and the axon is short, but the terminal is packed with mitochondria to keep replacing receptors in a saliva bathed environment.

PLASTICITY:

Taste bud cells die and are replaced every ten days or so, so this neuron must keep rebuilding its contacts for a lifetime.

Cut the chorda tympani and the taste buds it served shrink away, patients report phantom tastes, and the transmitter release sites inside surviving taste cells are lost, because the nerve actively maintains them.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Satellite glia buffer potassium around the soma in the ordinary way.

The special partner is at the far end, where type I taste bud cells act as the local astrocyte, carrying the enzyme NTPDase2 that destroys adenosine triphosphate to end each taste signal quickly, without which the tongue would smear one mouthful into the next.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell type and the front of the tongue goes taste blind while touch and temperature survive perfectly.

Food becomes texture, warmth and smell only, and the spit out reflex to a bitter poison is lost from the part of the mouth that meets food first.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The peripheral ending is a chemical laboratory rather than a wire, sensing adenosine triphosphate, watching it be destroyed on a millisecond timescale, and sensing serotonin in parallel.

It collects brain derived neurotrophic factor and neurotrophin 4 at the taste bud and carries them backwards to switch on survival genes.

Because one arbor serves several buds, their signals are summed inside the axon before any spike is generated, which is genuine peripheral computation.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Adenosine triphosphate in the taste bud cleft, 0 to 50 micromolar, from type II cells.

◦ Serotonin in the cleft, 0 to 5 micromolar, from type III cells.

◦ Tongue surface temperature, 5 to 50 degrees Celsius.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Spike train to the rostral nucleus of the solitary tract, 0 to 100 hertz, with glutamate released per spike.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, receptor occupancy per contact, accumulated charge per taste quality, refractory timer.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Capacitance about 30 picofarads, leak conductance about 5 nanosiemens, peak purinergic conductance about 2 nanosiemens, half activating adenosine triphosphate about 8 micromolar, threshold about negative 45 millivolts.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane about 4 milliseconds, receptor desensitisation about 200 milliseconds, adaptation about 1 second, taste bud turnover about 10 days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Varicella zoster virus latent in this ganglion reactivates as Ramsay Hunt syndrome, about 5 cases per 100,000 per year, causing facial palsy, ear blisters and taste loss, and accounting for roughly 12 percent of non traumatic facial paralysis.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn the chemical signal from a taste bud into a labelled taste report from one geniculate ganglion neuron.
Create a list variable called contacts with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called membrane_millivolts with initial value negative 58.
Create a number variable called total_conductance with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called receptor_activation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike_count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called best_charge with initial value 0.
Create a text variable called best_quality with initial value "none".
For each contact in contacts, perform the following operations.
    Set receptor_activation to the quotient of the transmitter level of contact divided by the sum of the transmitter level of contact and 8.
    Set total_conductance to the sum of total_conductance and the product of receptor_activation and 2.
    Check if the accumulated charge of contact is greater than best_charge.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set best_charge to the accumulated charge of contact.
        Set best_quality to the taste quality of contact.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Set membrane_millivolts to the sum of membrane_millivolts and the product of total_conductance and 6.
Check if membrane_millivolts is greater than or equal to negative 45.
If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
    Set spike_count to the sum of spike_count and 1.
    Display best_quality to the console.
Otherwise, perform the following actions.
    Display "no taste reported" to the console.
End the conditional block.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because dictionaries make the labelled line decision readable at a glance.
DT = 1e-4            # seconds per step
C_M = 30e-12         # somatic capacitance, farads
G_LEAK = 5e-9        # leak conductance, siemens
E_LEAK = -0.058      # leak reversal, volts
G_P2X_MAX = 2e-9     # peak purinergic conductance, siemens
EC50 = 8.0           # half activating ATP, micromolar
V_THRESH, V_RESET, REFRACTORY = -0.045, -0.060, 0.003

def p2x_conductance(atp_um):
    """P2X2 and P2X3 activation, Hill slope 2."""
    a = atp_um * atp_um
    return G_P2X_MAX * a / (a + EC50 * EC50)

def taste_response(frames):
    v, spikes, blocked_until, t, charge = E_LEAK, 0, -1.0, 0.0, {}
    for frame in frames:
        g_sum = 0.0
        for quality, atp_um in frame.items():
            g = p2x_conductance(atp_um)
            charge[quality] = charge.get(quality, 0.0) + g * DT
            g_sum += g
        v += DT * (G_LEAK * (E_LEAK - v) + g_sum * (0.0 - v)) / C_M
        if t < blocked_until:
            v = V_RESET
        elif v >= V_THRESH:
            spikes, v, blocked_until = spikes + 1, V_RESET, t + REFRACTORY
        t += DT
    best = max(charge, key=charge.get) if charge else "none"
    return best, spikes / (len(frames) * DT)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(taste_response([{"sweet": 20.0, "sour": 1.0}] * 20000))   # two seconds of sucrose

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron whose cell body sits at the knee of the facial nerve and whose far tip answers to a drop of sugar.

Phox2b positive, lodged in the temporal bone, with an axon that crosses the eardrum: that combination belongs to no other cell.

CHAPTER 234. PETROSAL GANGLION NEURONS (Oxygen-And-Pressure Reporting Neuron) (NON-COG)

Climb a mountain and your breathing deepens within seconds, and the alarm that triggered it was rung by neurons tucked into a shallow pit in the base of your skull.

Petrosal simply means rocky and refers to the petrous part of the temporal bone where these cell bodies happen to sit, which teaches a student nothing about what the cells do.

Oxygen names the first duty, since their peripheral endings wrap the glomus cells of the carotid body, the main peripheral organ that measures the oxygen, carbon dioxide and acidity of arterial blood, and their firing drives the reflex increase in breathing when oxygen falls.

Pressure names the second duty, carried by a separate set of endings in the wall of the carotid sinus that measure how hard the blood is pushing on the artery, the signal behind moment to moment control of heart rate and blood vessel tone.

Reporting Neuron names the direction of traffic, since every one of these cells is a one-way messenger delivering its news to the nucleus of the solitary tract in the medulla.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is the pseudounipolar sensory neuron of the glossopharyngeal nerve, the ninth cranial nerve, with its cell body in the inferior ganglion, called the petrosal ganglion.

It does three unrelated jobs at once: reporting taste from the back third of the tongue, oxygen shortage from the carotid body, and blood pressure from the carotid sinus.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The soma is round with one stalk that forks into a peripheral and a central branch.

Peripheral branches leave in three named nerves, the lingual branches to the tongue, the carotid sinus nerve of Hering to the neck arteries, and the tympanic nerve of Jacobson to the middle ear, and at the carotid body the ending forms a cup around a cluster of glomus cells.

SIZE:

Cell bodies measure about 20 to 50 micrometres across and the ganglion is a swelling only a few millimetres long.

The carotid body it listens to is a speck about 2 millimetres across weighing roughly 12 milligrams.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Chemoreceptor afferents that innervate the carotid body make up only about one fifth of the ganglion.

The rest are baroreceptor afferents on the stretchy carotid sinus wall, gustatory afferents marked by Phox2b serving the circumvallate and foliate papillae, and a general sensory group for pharynx, tonsil and eustachian tube.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The ganglion lies in the petrosal fossula, a dimple on the under surface of the petrous temporal bone just below the jugular foramen, exactly at the interface between skull cavity and neck, with the smaller superior glossopharyngeal ganglion still inside the foramen above it.

Peripheral endings sit in the carotid body and carotid sinus at the fork of the common carotid artery, in circumvallate and foliate taste buds and in the pharyngeal lining, while central endings reach the caudal nucleus of the solitary tract for cardiorespiratory signals and the rostral part of the same nucleus for taste.

NUMBER:

No dependable human count exists, so we know the order of magnitude and little more.

The regional budget places this ganglion below the nodose figure of about 5,000 per side, so 2,000 to 5,000 per side, or 4,000 to 10,000 in total, is the defensible range, with low confidence.

That is about one neuron in every 11 million in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Chemoreceptor afferents are never silent, ticking over at 0.5 to 3 spikes per second while you breathe room air and climbing to 30 to 80 spikes per second when arterial oxygen pressure falls toward 40 millimetres of mercury.

Baroreceptor afferents instead fire volleys locked to each heartbeat, so their message is a rhythm rather than a rate.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

At the carotid body, glomus cells release adenosine triphosphate and acetylcholine onto P2X2, P2X3 and nicotinic receptors, and the junction is reciprocal, so the nerve talks back to the glomus cell.

Dopamine and serotonin from the same glomus cells tune the gain, and the central branch makes glutamate synapses in the nucleus of the solitary tract.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The carotid sinus nerve carries thinly myelinated A delta fibres at roughly 5 to 20 metres per second and unmyelinated C fibres at under 2 metres per second.

The run from neck to brainstem is only a few centimetres, so even slow fibres deliver a hypoxia warning in well under a second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The central output transmitter is glutamate, and subsets also carry substance P and calcitonin gene related peptide, abbreviated CGRP, which they release from their peripheral tips.

On the receiving side they carry purinergic, nicotinic, dopaminergic and serotonergic receptors, an unusually crowded pharmacology for a sensory ending.

MYELINATION:

Mixed, with Schwann cells providing thin myelin to faster fibres and bundling the rest as bare axons in Remak bundles, where one Schwann cell cradles many fibres in shallow grooves.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The ending is ordinary but works inside the most lavishly perfused tissue in the body, since the carotid body receives roughly 2,000 millilitres of blood per 100 grams per minute, about forty times the flow to the brain, precisely so it samples arterial blood before any oxygen has been consumed.

PLASTICITY:

Two days at altitude and the carotid body enlarges while its afferents become measurably more sensitive, which is the cellular basis of acclimatisation.

The same sensitisation turns harmful in obstructive sleep apnoea and heart failure, where an over reactive reflex drives chronic sympathetic overactivity and high blood pressure.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Satellite glia surround the soma in the usual way, but the special partner is the type II sustentacular cell of the carotid body.

That glia like cell, carrying nestin and glial fibrillary acidic protein, releases adenosine triphosphate through pannexin 1 channels to amplify the signal and can act as a stem cell that becomes a new glomus cell during chronic low oxygen, one of very few places where glia turn into sensory cells in an adult.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell type and a person stops responding to suffocation, because the hypoxic ventilatory response depends almost entirely on it.

The carotid baroreflex also fails so blood pressure swings on standing, the gag reflex loses its sensory limb, and bitter taste disappears from the back of the tongue where the body makes its last poison check before swallowing.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The nerve ending and glomus cell form a reciprocal synaptic pair, so the afferent is part of the sensor rather than merely reading it.

Spikes travelling outwards release substance P into the carotid body and dilate its vessels, a local blood flow loop run by a sensory cell.

The terminal also senses carbon dioxide, acid, temperature and circulating inflammatory proteins directly, making the carotid body an immune sensor as well as a gas sensor.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Arterial oxygen partial pressure, 20 to 120 millimetres of mercury.

◦ Arterial carbon dioxide partial pressure, 20 to 80 millimetres of mercury, and arterial pH 6.9 to 7.6.

◦ Carotid sinus wall strain, 0 to 0.2 fractional stretch, updated at the pulse rate.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Spike train to the caudal nucleus of the solitary tract, 0 to 100 hertz, driving ventilation and sympathetic outflow.

◦ Substance P released backwards into the carotid body.

• STATE.

◦ Instantaneous rate, adaptation level, sensitisation gain set by chronic hypoxia, refractory timer.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Baseline rate about 1 hertz, maximum about 80 hertz, half maximal oxygen pressure about 55 millimetres of mercury, curve steepness about 8 millimetres of mercury, carbon dioxide gain about 0.01 per millimetre of mercury.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Hypoxic response onset 1 to 5 seconds, adaptation about 20 seconds, acclimatisation 1 to 3 days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss on both sides abolishes the drive to breathe in low oxygen, sensitisation drives hypertension in sleep apnoea, and irritation of the same ganglion produces glossopharyngeal neuralgia, a stabbing throat pain affecting fewer than 1 person in 100,000 per year that can trigger reflex fainting.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert arterial blood gas values into the firing rate of one petrosal chemoreceptor afferent.
Create a number variable called oxygen_pressure with initial value 40.
Create a number variable called carbon_dioxide_pressure with initial value 40.
Create a number variable called hypoxic_drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called carbon_dioxide_gain with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called adaptation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
While step is less than 300, repeat the following steps.
    Set hypoxic_drive to the quotient of 1 divided by the sum of 1 and the exponential of the quotient of the difference of oxygen_pressure and 55 divided by 8.
    Set carbon_dioxide_gain to the sum of 1 and the product of 0.01 and the difference of carbon_dioxide_pressure and 40.
    Set firing_rate to the sum of 1 and the product of 80 and the product of hypoxic_drive and carbon_dioxide_gain.
    Set firing_rate to the difference of firing_rate and adaptation.
    Check if firing_rate is less than 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing_rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set adaptation to the sum of adaptation and the product of 0.005 and the difference of the product of 0.2 and firing_rate and adaptation.
    Set step to the sum of step and 1.
End the loop.
Display firing_rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the model is a few smooth curves and clarity beats raw speed here.
import math

DT = 0.1                 # seconds per step
BASELINE_HZ, MAX_HZ = 1.0, 80.0
P50_MMHG = 55.0          # oxygen pressure giving half maximal drive
STEEPNESS = 8.0          # millimetres of mercury per e fold
CO2_GAIN = 0.01          # per millimetre of mercury above 40
TAU_ADAPT, ADAPT_FRACTION = 20.0, 0.2

def chemoreceptor(pao2_series, paco2=40.0):
    """Spike rate of one carotid body afferent over time."""
    adaptation, trace = 0.0, []
    for pao2 in pao2_series:
        hypoxic = 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp((pao2 - P50_MMHG) / STEEPNESS))
        co2 = 1.0 + CO2_GAIN * (paco2 - 40.0)
        rate = max(BASELINE_HZ + MAX_HZ * hypoxic * co2 - adaptation, 0.0)
        adaptation += DT * (ADAPT_FRACTION * rate - adaptation) / TAU_ADAPT
        trace.append(rate)
    return trace

if __name__ == "__main__":
    sea_level = [95.0] * 100          # ten seconds of normal air
    summit = [40.0] * 300             # thirty seconds of thin air
    result = chemoreceptor(sea_level + summit)
    print(round(result[50], 2), round(result[110], 2), round(result[-1], 2))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only sensory neuron that never stops firing while you are alive and well, because its resting discharge is the running proof that your blood still carries oxygen.

No other cell type reports at the same moment how much oxygen you have, how hard your arteries are being pushed, and whether the thing at the back of your tongue is bitter.

CHAPTER 235. NODOSE GANGLION NEURONS (Gut-And-Lung Status Neuron) (NON-COG)

The feeling of being full after a large meal is not made in your stomach: it is manufactured by a few thousand neurons in a red, cigar shaped swelling on the vagus nerve in your neck.

Nodose means knotty and describes the swelling of the vagus nerve that holds these cell bodies, which is a landmark rather than a function.

Gut-And-Lung names the two great territories these cells listen to, with separate specialists detecting stomach and intestinal stretch, the arrival of nutrients, and the inflation of the lung during each breath, including the only vagal fibres that reach into the alveolar region where gas exchange happens.

Status names the character of the message, which is a continuous background readout of how the internal organs are doing rather than an emergency signal.

Neuron marks it as part of a traffic overwhelmingly one way and inward, since roughly ninety percent of the fibres in the vagus nerve below the diaphragm carry information toward the brain rather than commands away from it.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is the pseudounipolar visceral sensory neuron of the inferior, or nodose, ganglion of the vagus nerve, the tenth cranial nerve, and like its relatives it has one forked stalk and no dendrites.

It is the body's internal reporter, telling the brainstem how full the stomach is, how stretched the lungs are, what nutrients have arrived in the gut, and how hard the heart is working.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

What makes this cell distinctive is the shape of its endings, which are sculpted structures rather than free tips.

Intraganglionic laminar endings spread as flat plates over nerve bundles in the gut wall to sense stretch, intramuscular arrays run alongside muscle fibres, and mucosal endings fan out inside the finger like villi of the intestine to sample the contents.

SIZE:

Cell bodies span about 20 to 50 micrometres, and the human nodose ganglion is a reddish spindle classically described as about 2.5 centimetres long, the longest sensory ganglion in the head and neck.

Its axons are the longest in the body outside the spinal cord, reaching from neck to transverse colon, close to a metre.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

A single cell survey of the mouse vagal ganglia identified 18 distinct nodose types, each apparently dedicated to one internal measurement.

Named examples include stomach stretch receptors carrying the glucagon like peptide 1 receptor, gut nutrient detectors carrying the acid sensor Gpr65, airway stretch receptors carrying Npy2r that make breathing rapid and shallow, aortic baroreceptors, and taste neurons serving the epiglottis.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The ganglion sits on the vagus nerve just below the jugular foramen, inside the carotid sheath beside the internal carotid artery and internal jugular vein, at the interface between skull base and neck.

Its endings are scattered through the larynx and epiglottis, the lungs and airways, the aortic arch, oesophagus, stomach, pancreas, liver and intestine as far as the transverse colon, and its central terminals map onto the nucleus of the solitary tract with airway inputs in the middle third and gut inputs in the caudal third.

NUMBER:

The regional budget allots about 5,000 per side, so about 10,000 in total.

Direct human counts are lacking and rat nodose ganglia contain roughly 6,000 neurons, so 5,000 to 10,000 per side is defensible, with low to moderate confidence.

That is about one neuron in every 9 million in the nervous system, a startlingly small crew for the entire conversation between your organs and your brain.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Chemosensitive C fibre cells use tetrodotoxin resistant Nav1.8 and Nav1.9 channels and fire broad spikes with a shoulder, while fast mechanoreceptors use tetrodotoxin sensitive channels and fire narrow spikes.

Stomach stretch receptors are slowly adapting, holding a steady 5 to 30 spikes per second for as long as the stomach stays full, which is exactly what a fullness signal requires.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The peripheral endings carry a dense receptor field: PIEZO2 for stretch, 5-HT3 receptors for serotonin from gut enterochromaffin cells, CCK-A receptors for cholecystokinin, and receptors for glucagon like peptide 1, peptide YY, leptin and ghrelin.

Enteroendocrine cells even form synapse like contacts called neuropods that release glutamate onto these endings within milliseconds, and centrally the terminals make powerful glutamate synapses in the nucleus of the solitary tract.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The cervical vagus is roughly 80 percent afferent, meaning it is mostly a cable carrying information up rather than commands down.

Between about 65 and 85 percent of its fibres are unmyelinated C fibres conducting at 0.4 to 2 metres per second, so a gut signal can take a full second to arrive, while the myelinated minority manages 4 to 15 metres per second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The central transmitter is glutamate.

Unlike its neighbour the jugular ganglion, the nodose cell largely lacks substance P, and that single chemical difference is the cleanest way to tell the two apart, though some subsets carry calcitonin gene related peptide, abbreviated CGRP.

MYELINATION:

Mostly unmyelinated and carried in Remak bundles, where one Schwann cell cradles many bare fibres, with the myelinated minority wrapped by Schwann cells outside and oligodendrocytes inside the medulla.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The soma must supply an axon up to a metre long, so axonal transport dominates the energy bill, and proteins made in the cell body take days to reach the intestinal terminal, which is why vagal neuropathy in diabetes recovers so slowly.

PLASTICITY:

A sustained high fat diet blunts the mechanical sensitivity of stomach stretch receptors within weeks, so the same meal reports as less filling, a peripheral contribution to obesity.

Bariatric surgery and drugs acting at the glucagon like peptide 1 receptor work partly by resetting this gain, and electrical vagus nerve stimulation drives the same afferents deliberately.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Satellite glia envelop the soma in the ordinary way, but the distinctive partner is peripheral.

Intraganglionic laminar endings sit inside myenteric ganglia and are physically wrapped by enteric glia, the only glia outside the brain and spinal cord that behave like astrocytes, releasing adenosine triphosphate and taking up glutamate around these endings.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell type and the brainstem goes deaf to the body.

Meals no longer end because fullness never arrives, the Hering Breuer reflex that stops you over inflating your lungs disappears, blood pressure control loses its aortic sensor, and the whole gut to brain axis that shapes mood, nausea and appetite falls silent while the organs keep working.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Gain control here is chemical, since a hormone such as cholecystokinin does not fire the cell by itself but multiplies its response to stretch, so the same stomach volume reports as fuller after a fatty meal.

The ending is also a mechanical transducer in its own right through PIEZO2, converting membrane tension into current with no intermediary.

These cells read the immune system through receptors for interleukin 1 beta, which is how gut infection becomes the feeling of sickness, and they form the sensory arm of the inflammatory reflex that lets the brain damp inflammation down.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Organ wall strain, 0 to 0.4 fractional stretch, from stomach, airway, oesophagus and aorta.

◦ Cholecystokinin 0 to 20 picomolar, glucagon like peptide 1 0 to 100 picomolar, serotonin 0 to 10 micromolar.

◦ Luminal nutrients and pH from mucosal endings, pH 2 to 8.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Spike train to the nucleus of the solitary tract, 0 to 60 hertz, mapped by organ.

◦ Glutamate per spike, with organ identity encoded by terminal location rather than transmitter.

• STATE.

◦ Current strain, previous strain for the rate term, hormonal gain, adaptation level, firing rate.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Static sensitivity about 80 hertz per unit strain, dynamic sensitivity about 15 hertz per unit strain per second, hormonal gain 1.0 to 2.5, maximum rate about 60 hertz, threshold strain about 0.02.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Mechanotransduction under 1 millisecond, slow adaptation 5 to 30 seconds, hormonal gain change 5 to 30 minutes, diet induced change days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Diabetic vagal neuropathy produces gastroparesis, in which the stomach neither empties nor reports, and blunted vagal signalling contributes to overeating.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn stomach wall stretch and gut hormone levels into the slowly adapting fullness signal of one nodose ganglion neuron.
Create a list variable called strain_samples with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called strain with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called previous_strain with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called strain_rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called hormonal_gain with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called static_drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called dynamic_drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called adaptation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 0.
For each sample in strain_samples, perform the following operations.
    Set previous_strain to strain.
    Set strain to sample.
    Set strain_rate to the quotient of the difference of strain and previous_strain divided by 0.05.
    Check if strain is less than 0.02.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set static_drive to 0.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set static_drive to the product of 80 and the difference of strain and 0.02.
    End the conditional block.
    Set dynamic_drive to the product of 15 and strain_rate.
    Set firing_rate to the product of hormonal_gain and the sum of static_drive and dynamic_drive.
    Set firing_rate to the difference of firing_rate and adaptation.
    Set adaptation to the sum of adaptation and the product of 0.005 and the difference of the product of 0.3 and firing_rate and adaptation.
    Display firing_rate to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the whole model is one short state update and readability matters most.
DT = 0.05                 # seconds per sample
THRESHOLD_STRAIN = 0.02
STATIC_HZ = 80.0          # hertz per unit strain
DYNAMIC_HZ = 15.0         # hertz per unit strain per second
MAX_HZ = 60.0
TAU_ADAPT, ADAPT_FRACTION = 10.0, 0.3

def nodose_afferent(strain_series, hormonal_gain=1.0):
    """Slowly adapting stomach stretch afferent with hormone set gain."""
    adaptation, previous, rates = 0.0, 0.0, []
    for strain in strain_series:
        rate_of_change = (strain - previous) / DT
        previous = strain
        static = STATIC_HZ * max(strain - THRESHOLD_STRAIN, 0.0)
        dynamic = DYNAMIC_HZ * max(rate_of_change, 0.0)
        rate = min(max(hormonal_gain * (static + dynamic) - adaptation, 0.0), MAX_HZ)
        adaptation += DT * (ADAPT_FRACTION * rate - adaptation) / TAU_ADAPT
        rates.append(rate)
    return rates

if __name__ == "__main__":
    meal = [0.0] * 20 + [0.02 * i for i in range(20)] + [0.38] * 200
    print(round(nodose_afferent(meal)[-1], 1),
          round(nodose_afferent(meal, hormonal_gain=2.0)[-1], 1))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron whose cell body sits in a finger long red spindle in your neck and whose far tip is draped over a nerve bundle in your intestine.

A vagal sensory cell built from an embryonic placode rather than neural crest, lacking substance P and answering to a gut hormone, is nodose and nothing else.

CHAPTER 236. JUGULAR GANGLION NEURONS (Airway-Irritation Cough Neuron) (NON-COG)

The choking tickle of a cough that will not stop for months usually begins in a ganglion the size of a peppercorn, wedged in a hole in the base of your skull.

Jugular names the throat region the ganglion sits in and nothing more, while these cells have one of the most recognisable jobs in the body.

Airway-Irritation names what they detect, acids, smoke, inflammatory mediators and mechanical insult in the trachea and conducting airways, sensed by neural crest derived fibres whose properties match skin pain fibres rather than the visceral cells beside them.

Cough names the reflex they command, and current work treats this population as the direct target for drugs against chronic cough and cough hypersensitivity.

Neuron marks it as anatomically distinct from its larger neighbour in two hard ways, since the neighbouring nodose ganglion holds roughly five to ten times as many neurons and these cells terminate in the paratrigeminal nucleus rather than the nucleus of the solitary tract.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is the pseudounipolar sensory neuron of the superior, or jugular, ganglion of the vagus nerve, and its defining fact is where it came from.

Unlike its downstream neighbour the nodose ganglion it is built from neural crest cells, the embryonic stock that makes the pain cells of the spinal cord, so it reports damage and irritation rather than routine housekeeping.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The soma is round and jacketed by satellite glia, and in a study of human jugular ganglia the mean cell body cross section measured 1,128.8 plus or minus 99.7 square micrometres with a nucleus of 127.7 plus or minus 20.8 square micrometres.

Peripheral endings are simple bare fibres weaving between the surface cells of the airway lining and ear canal skin, not the sculpted plates of the nodose cell.

SIZE:

That cross section corresponds to a cell body roughly 38 micrometres across with a nucleus about 13 micrometres across.

The ganglion itself is classically described as spherical and about 4 millimetres in diameter, a peppercorn.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Single cell surveys show this ganglion mirrors the spinal dorsal root ganglion rather than the nodose ganglion.

Its classes are C fibre low threshold mechanoreceptors, A fibre low threshold mechanoreceptors, A delta nociceptors, cold nociceptors and mechano heat C nociceptors carrying capsaicin sensitive TRPV1 channels, and most are peptidergic, meaning they carry substance P and calcitonin gene related peptide, abbreviated CGRP.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The ganglion sits inside the jugular foramen, the opening in the temporal bone through which the vagus, glossopharyngeal and accessory nerves leave the skull, often fused with the superior glossopharyngeal ganglion, so it is literally a cell body sitting in a doorway in bone at the interface between cranial cavity and neck.

Its endings reach the external ear canal and eardrum through the auricular branch called Arnold's nerve, the dura of the posterior cranial fossa, and the larynx, trachea, bronchi and oesophagus, while its central terminals go not to the nucleus of the solitary tract but to the paratrigeminal nucleus and spinal trigeminal nucleus, the territory of facial pain.

NUMBER:

This is the one ganglion in this group with a real human count.

A stereological study estimated 2,721.8 to 9,301.1 sensory neurons per side, mean 7,975.1 plus or minus 3,312.8, so roughly 16,000 across both sides, with moderate confidence limited by the wide spread between individuals.

That is about one neuron in every 5 million in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These are high threshold cells that stay silent until something is wrong, then fire slowly and irregularly at 1 to 20 spikes per second from a resting voltage near negative 55 to negative 60 millivolts.

They rely on tetrodotoxin resistant Nav1.8 and Nav1.9 sodium channels, which is why anaesthetics that block ordinary sodium channels are poor cough suppressants.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The peripheral ending has no presynaptic partner and senses tissue directly through TRPV1 for heat, acid and capsaicin, TRPA1 for smoke and irritants, and the purinergic receptor P2X3.

The detail that separates jugular from nodose is that jugular cells carry P2X3 alone while nodose cells add P2X2 to form a more sensitive combined channel, and centrally the terminal releases glutamate with substance P onto neurokinin 1 receptors in the paratrigeminal nucleus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Most fibres are unmyelinated C fibres conducting at 0.5 to 1 metre per second, with a minority of thinly myelinated A delta fibres at about 4 to 8 metres per second.

The airway branches are short, but conduction is so slow that tens of milliseconds pass between tracheal irritation and the first cough.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate is the fast transmitter and substance P is the signature peptide, with CGRP alongside it.

Airway nociceptors with cell bodies here widely express substance P while their nodose counterparts do not, which is the cleanest chemical fingerprint in this whole section.

MYELINATION:

Predominantly unmyelinated and gathered into Remak bundles by Schwann cells, with the thinly myelinated minority handed over to oligodendrocytes on entering the medulla.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Firing rates are low so the electrical bill is small, but peptide production is expensive, since substance P and CGRP must be made in the cell body, packed into large dense core vesicles and shipped the length of the axon.

PLASTICITY:

Nerve growth factor released during airway inflammation raises the amount of TRPV1 and substance P these cells carry, sometimes within hours.

That is the accepted mechanism of cough hypersensitivity syndrome, in which a chest infection clears but the cough continues for months because the sensor has been permanently turned up.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Satellite glia here do something unusual, raising their levels of the gap junction protein connexin 43 after inflammation and electrically coupling neighbouring cell bodies that should be independent.

Out in the airway the functional support cells are not glia at all but mast cells and eosinophils, immune cells that park beside these endings and release histamine, prostaglandin E2 and nerve growth factor onto them.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell type and the cough reflex from larynx and trachea largely dies, so inhaled food and fluid are no longer expelled.

Arnold's reflex, the odd cough some people get when a doctor puts an instrument in the ear, disappears, and the airway loses its alarm system while keeping its stretch sensors intact.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The axon reflex is the headline behaviour, because a spike generated at one branch tip travels back down the others and dumps substance P and CGRP into the tissue, making vessels leak, airways narrow and immune cells gather.

The sensory neuron therefore causes inflammation as well as reporting it, a loop called neurogenic inflammation.

Coupling through satellite glia lets one irritated cell partly excite its silent neighbours, and the same peptides feed back onto mast cells, closing a nerve to immune circuit that runs without the brain.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Capsaicin or equivalent TRPV1 agonist, 0 to 10 micromolar.

◦ Tissue pH 4.0 to 7.4 and tissue temperature 20 to 50 degrees Celsius.

◦ Extracellular adenosine triphosphate, 0 to 100 micromolar, from damaged surface cells.

◦ Nerve growth factor and prostaglandin E2 levels setting the sensitisation multiplier.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Spike train to the paratrigeminal nucleus, 0 to 30 hertz, driving the cough motor pattern.

◦ Substance P and CGRP released backwards into airway tissue.

• STATE.

◦ Generator potential, sensitisation multiplier, slow accommodation term, releasable peptide pool, spike count.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ TRPV1 half activating capsaicin about 0.5 micromolar, heat threshold about 43 degrees Celsius, acid half point about pH 5.4, P2X3 half activating adenosine triphosphate about 12 micromolar, spike threshold about 12 millivolts above rest, sensitisation 1.0 to 4.0.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane about 20 milliseconds, accommodation about 400 milliseconds, peptide refill tens of seconds, inflammatory sensitisation hours to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Persistent sensitisation produces refractory chronic cough, and the drug gefapixant, which blocks P2X3 and P2X2 or P2X3 receptors, was developed to silence these cells, while losing them causes silent aspiration and pneumonia.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether one jugular ganglion airway nociceptor fires and triggers a cough.
Create a number variable called capsaicin with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called tissue_acidity with initial value 0.8.
Create a number variable called atp_level with initial value 2.
Create a number variable called sensitisation with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called generator_potential with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called accommodation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called channel_opening with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called purinergic_opening with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike_count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
While step is less than 3000, repeat the following steps.
    Set channel_opening to the quotient of the sum of capsaicin and tissue_acidity divided by the sum of the sum of capsaicin and tissue_acidity and 1.
    Set purinergic_opening to the quotient of atp_level divided by the sum of atp_level and 12.
    Set drive to the product of sensitisation and the sum of the product of 18 and channel_opening and the product of 9 and purinergic_opening.
    Set drive to the difference of drive and accommodation.
    Set generator_potential to the sum of generator_potential and the product of 0.05 and the difference of drive and generator_potential.
    Set accommodation to the sum of accommodation and the product of 0.0025 and the difference of the product of 0.3 and generator_potential and accommodation.
    Check if generator_potential is greater than or equal to 12.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set spike_count to the sum of spike_count and 1.
        Set generator_potential to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set step to the sum of step and 1.
End the loop.
Display spike_count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C is chosen because a simulator holds thousands of these and the per neuron step must be fixed cost. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>

#define DT 0.001                 /* seconds per step */

typedef struct {
    double v;                    /* generator potential, mV above rest */
    double sensitisation;        /* 1.0 healthy, up to 4.0 after nerve growth factor */
    double accommodation;        /* slow shunting term */
    int    spikes;
} Jugular;

static double trp_open(double capsaicin_uM, double pH, double degC)
{
    double cap  = capsaicin_uM / (capsaicin_uM + 0.5);        /* TRPV1 half point 0.5 uM */
    double acid = 1.0 / (1.0 + pow(10.0, pH - 5.4));          /* acid half point pH 5.4 */
    double heat = 1.0 / (1.0 + exp((43.0 - degC) / 1.5));     /* threshold 43 C */
    double open = cap + acid + heat;
    return open > 1.0 ? 1.0 : open;
}

int jugular_step(Jugular *n, double cap, double pH, double degC, double atp_uM)
{
    double p2x3 = atp_uM / (atp_uM + 12.0);                   /* homomeric P2X3, half point 12 uM */
    double drive = n->sensitisation * (18.0 * trp_open(cap, pH, degC) + 9.0 * p2x3);
    double net = drive - n->accommodation;
    n->v += DT * (net - n->v) / 0.020;                        /* 20 ms membrane */
    n->accommodation += DT * (0.3 * n->v - n->accommodation) / 0.400;
    if (n->v >= 12.0) { n->spikes++; n->v = 0.0; return 1; }
    return 0;
}

int main(void)
{
    Jugular n = {0.0, 1.0, 0.0, 0};
    int i, fired = 0;
    for (i = 0; i < 3000; i++)                                /* three seconds of inhaled citric acid */
        fired += jugular_step(&n, 0.0, 4.8, 37.0, 2.0);
    printf("action potentials in 3 s: %d\n", fired);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the vagus nerve cell that is not really a vagus nerve cell, a neural crest pain neuron sitting in a hole in the skull and reporting to trigeminal pain territory instead of the visceral relay.

Substance P plus P2X3 without P2X2, an ending in the trachea and a terminal in the paratrigeminal nucleus identify it beyond argument.

CHAPTER 237. TRIGEMINAL GANGLION NEURONS (Facial Touch-And-Pain Neuron) (NON-COG)

Touch your cheek, sip something scalding, feel a single eyelash on your cornea: one crescent of cells the size of a thumbnail, lying in a pouch of membrane on the floor of your skull, reports every bit of it.

Trigeminal means three twins and records only that the nerve leaves the ganglion in three branches, so the name is a piece of anatomical bookkeeping.

Facial names the territory, since this single ganglion supplies almost all sensation from the face, the front of the scalp, the teeth, the sinuses, the eye and the lining of the mouth and nose.

Touch-And-Pain names the two duties that dominate the population, from delicate mechanoreceptors that register a hair moving to polymodal nociceptors that fire to mechanical, chemical and heat injury together and form the largest single class in the cornea.

Neuron marks it as one of the twenty thousand to thirty five thousand pseudounipolar cells of the human ganglion, among whose corneal afferents about forty five percent carry the heat and irritant sensor, twenty eight percent a pure mechanical sensor and eight percent a cold sensor.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is the pseudounipolar sensory neuron of the trigeminal ganglion, also called the Gasserian or semilunar ganglion, the largest sensory ganglion in the head, and it supplies the face's entire sense of touch, temperature, itch and pain, including cornea, teeth, sinuses, jaw joint and the membranes covering the brain.

One correction is worth making early: the cells that sense jaw muscle stretch are not here at all, because they uniquely sit inside the brainstem in the mesencephalic trigeminal nucleus.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The ganglion is a flat crescent, convex forwards, from which the three divisions fan out like the ribs of a hand held fan.

Each neuron is a round soma sheathed in satellite glia with one stalk forking at a T junction, and its peripheral endings range from elaborate encapsulated touch corpuscles in the lips to completely bare fibres in the cornea and tooth pulp.

SIZE:

The ganglion measures roughly 15 to 20 millimetres along its convex edge, about 5 millimetres high and 2 to 3 millimetres thick.

Cell bodies span an unusually wide 15 to 100 micrometres, small dark ones feeding pain fibres and large pale ones feeding fast touch fibres, and the sensory root carries about 154,000 myelinated fibres, making it the thickest cranial nerve.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

A combined human and mouse single cell atlas published in 2022 resolved 15 cell types here, about eight of them neuronal and seven support cells: fast A beta low threshold mechanoreceptors for touch, A delta nociceptors for sharp first pain, peptidergic C nociceptors carrying calcitonin gene related peptide, abbreviated CGRP, for burning second pain, non peptidergic C nociceptors marked by MRGPRD, cold neurons carrying TRPM8, and C fibre low threshold mechanoreceptors for pleasant stroking.

A notable species difference is that in humans, but not mice, the CALCA gene encoding CGRP is strongly expressed in an itch related class.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The ganglion lies in Meckel's cave, a pouch of dura mater on the trigeminal impression at the apex of the petrous temporal bone, bathed in cerebrospinal fluid, at the interface between the posterior cranial fossa where the root leaves the pons and the middle cranial fossa where the three divisions depart.
