Its peripheral fields cover cornea and conjunctiva, nasal and sinus linings, all teeth and gums, the anterior two thirds of the tongue for touch, the temporomandibular joint and the dura and large arteries of the front and middle of the brain, while its central branch ends in the principal sensory nucleus in the pons and the spinal trigeminal nucleus running down to the second or third cervical segment.

NUMBER:

Published stereological counts disagree badly, ranging from about 20,000 to about 160,000 neurons per side with reported means from roughly 27,000 to 80,600.

Since the sensory root alone carries about 154,000 myelinated fibres before any unmyelinated ones are counted, the true figure is almost certainly at the upper end, so 100,000 to 200,000 per side is the working number, with moderate confidence in the order of magnitude and low confidence in the exact value.

The regional budget allots up to about 1 million per side, comfortably containing every published estimate, and at roughly 300,000 across both sides this is about one neuron in every 300,000 in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Large touch cells fire narrow spikes and conduct at 30 to 70 metres per second, while small pain cells fire broad tetrodotoxin resistant spikes using Nav1.8 and conduct at 0.5 to 2 metres per second.

The sodium channel Nav1.7 sets the firing threshold, and inherited mutations that make it open too easily cause attacks of scorching facial pain, while mutations that disable it abolish pain altogether.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

In a healthy ganglion there are no synapses between neurons at all, which makes what happens after injury remarkable, because injured cells begin to excite silent neighbours through the shared satellite glial envelope, a phenomenon called cross excitation.

The central terminals make glutamate synapses in the spinal trigeminal nucleus and co release CGRP and substance P during strong or repeated firing.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Each cell has one stem axon that forks, and the fork itself matters because it acts as a low pass filter: at high firing rates some spikes fail to turn the corner and never reach the brain.

Peripheral fibres range from 0.2 to 20 micrometres across and can run 10 centimetres or more to reach a tooth root or the tip of the nose.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate is the fast transmitter for every subtype, and roughly a third to a half of these neurons additionally carry CGRP, with many carrying substance P, somatostatin or pituitary adenylate cyclase activating polypeptide.

CGRP is the most powerful vasodilator known in human tissue, and that is why this ganglion is now a major drug target.

MYELINATION:

Peripheral branches are myelinated by Schwann cells and central branches by oligodendrocytes, with the handover at a short transition called the Obersteiner Redlich zone a few millimetres from the pons.

That zone is the weak point of the whole nerve, because a small artery pressing there strips myelin and lets neighbouring fibres short circuit into each other.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Total demand is high because hundreds of thousands of cells support long axons and continuous peptide manufacture.

One detail matters enormously for medicine: the vessels inside this ganglion are fenestrated, meaning leaky, so there is no blood brain barrier here and antibody drugs that cannot enter the brain reach these cells easily.

PLASTICITY:

Repeated activation drives central sensitisation, in which the brainstem relay becomes so responsive that brushing the hair hurts, the cutaneous allodynia most people report during a migraine.

Herpes simplex virus type 1 lies dormant in these cell bodies for life, with TrkA positive neurons making up about 62 percent of the human ganglion, and reactivation produces cold sores or sight threatening eye disease.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Satellite glia here are unusually active, each soma having its own glial capsule, and after injury these cells raise glial fibrillary acidic protein and connexin 43, forming gap junctions that let one neuron's activity leak into another's.

Resident macrophages sit permanently between the capsules and release inflammatory signals during migraine, and combined with the leaky vessels this makes the ganglion an outpost of the nervous system deliberately left open to the bloodstream.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell type and half the face becomes a numb mask that still moves normally, because the facial muscles are driven by a different nerve.

The corneal blink reflex dies so the eye dries and can be lost to neurotrophic keratitis, teeth feel nothing, hot drinks cause silent burns, and migraine becomes impossible, because the throbbing pain of a migraine is carried by precisely these cells and by nothing else.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The axon reflex is the defining extra behaviour, since a spike arriving at one branch invades the others and releases CGRP into the meninges, dilating arteries and recruiting mast cells, so the pain neuron actively changes the blood supply of the tissue it reports on.

The T junction filters its own traffic, satellite glia cross couple neighbours, herpes viruses hijack the cell body as a lifelong hiding place, and local protein synthesis lets a damaged axon begin repair before the cell body knows.

Corneal fibres also secrete substance P and insulin like growth factor that the corneal surface needs to stay alive, which is why a numb cornea disintegrates.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Mechanical force at the ending, 0 to 100 millinewtons.

◦ Temperature 0 to 55 degrees Celsius, with cold sensing below 25 and heat sensing above 42.

◦ Tissue pH 5.0 to 7.4, plus bradykinin, prostaglandin E2 and nerve growth factor levels.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Spike train to the spinal trigeminal and principal sensory nuclei, 0 to 200 hertz for touch cells and 0 to 40 hertz for pain cells.

◦ CGRP and substance P released backwards into skin, cornea, tooth pulp and dura.

◦ A meningeal vessel calibre signal, since CGRP directly dilates dural arteries.

• STATE.

◦ Generator potential, releasable CGRP store, dural vessel tone, sensitisation level, viral latency flag, spike count.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Mechanical half point about 8 millinewtons, heat threshold about 42 degrees Celsius, acid half point about pH 6.0, spike threshold about 15 millivolts above rest, CGRP released per spike about 2 percent of store, dilation gain about 4.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 8 to 15 milliseconds, CGRP refill about 30 seconds, vessel tone recovery about 2 seconds, central sensitisation minutes to hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ An artery pressing on the root at the Obersteiner Redlich zone causes trigeminal neuralgia, electric shock pain triggered by a breath of wind, while runaway CGRP release causes migraine, now treated with antibodies against CGRP that work because this ganglion has no blood brain barrier.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to fire one trigeminal nociceptor and let its axon reflex dilate a meningeal blood vessel.
Create a number variable called mechanical_force with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called generator_potential with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called cgrp_store with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called dural_tone with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called mechanical_drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called heat_drive with initial value 18.
Create a number variable called total_drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called released with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike_count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
While step is less than 20000, repeat the following steps.
    Set mechanical_drive to the product of 12 and the quotient of mechanical_force divided by the sum of mechanical_force and 8.
    Set total_drive to the sum of mechanical_drive and heat_drive.
    Set generator_potential to the sum of generator_potential and the product of 0.0333 and the difference of total_drive and generator_potential.
    Set cgrp_store to the sum of cgrp_store and the product of 0.0000167 and the difference of 1 and cgrp_store.
    Set dural_tone to the sum of dural_tone and the product of 0.00025 and the difference of 1 and dural_tone.
    Check if generator_potential is greater than or equal to 15.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set released to the product of 0.02 and cgrp_store.
        Set cgrp_store to the difference of cgrp_store and released.
        Set dural_tone to the sum of dural_tone and the product of 4 and released.
        Set spike_count to the sum of spike_count and 1.
        Set generator_potential to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set step to the sum of step and 1.
End the loop.
Display spike_count to the console.
Display dural_tone to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C is chosen because a whole ganglion means hundreds of thousands of instances in a tight inner loop. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>

#define DT 0.0005                /* 0.5 ms step */

typedef struct {
    double v;                    /* generator potential, mV above rest */
    double cgrp_store;           /* releasable CGRP, 0 to 1 */
    double dural_tone;           /* 1.0 is resting vessel calibre */
    int    spikes;
} Trigeminal;

static const double THRESHOLD_MV = 15.0;
static const double TAU_MEMBRANE = 0.015;   /* 15 ms */
static const double TAU_REFILL   = 30.0;    /* CGRP store refills over 30 s */
static const double TAU_VESSEL   = 2.0;     /* vessel calibre relaxes over 2 s */

int trigeminal_step(Trigeminal *n, double force_mN, double degC, double pH)
{
    double mech = 12.0 * force_mN / (force_mN + 8.0);          /* PIEZO2, half point 8 mN */
    double heat = 20.0 / (1.0 + exp((42.0 - degC) / 1.2));     /* TRPV1 threshold 42 C */
    double acid =  8.0 / (1.0 + pow(10.0, pH - 6.0));          /* ASIC3, half point pH 6.0 */
    n->v += DT * (mech + heat + acid - n->v) / TAU_MEMBRANE;
    n->cgrp_store += DT * (1.0 - n->cgrp_store) / TAU_REFILL;
    n->dural_tone += DT * (1.0 - n->dural_tone) / TAU_VESSEL;

    if (n->v >= THRESHOLD_MV) {
        double released = 0.02 * n->cgrp_store;                /* antidromic axon reflex */
        n->cgrp_store -= released;
        n->dural_tone += 4.0 * released;                       /* CGRP dilates dural arteries */
        n->spikes++; n->v = 0.0; return 1;
    }
    return 0;
}

int main(void)
{
    Trigeminal n = {0.0, 1.0, 1.0, 0};
    int i;
    for (i = 0; i < 20000; i++)                                /* ten seconds of noxious dural heat */
        trigeminal_step(&n, 0.0, 45.0, 7.3);
    printf("spikes %d, dural tone %.3f, CGRP left %.3f\n", n.spikes, n.dural_tone, n.cgrp_store);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only sensory neuron that lives in a dural pouch bathed in cerebrospinal fluid, sits behind leaky vessels rather than a blood brain barrier, and can dilate the arteries of the brain covering simply by firing.

A cell body in Meckel's cave whose axon reaches a tooth, a cornea or the dura, and which shelters herpes simplex for a lifetime, can only be a trigeminal ganglion neuron.


PART FIFTEEN: DORSAL ROOT GANGLION NEURONS - BODY SENSATION

CHAPTER 238. PROPRIOCEPTORS (NON-COG)

Close your eyes and touch your nose, and thank a cell that has never shown you a picture, a sound, or a feeling you could name.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A proprioceptor is a sensory neuron whose cell body sits in a dorsal root ganglion (DRG), a bead of nerve cell bodies parked just outside the spinal cord.

Its far end wraps a stretch detector inside a muscle or tendon and reports muscle length, the speed of length change, and tendon force.

Proprioception means the sense of where your own body is, and this cell is its hardware.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

It is pseudounipolar: one stalk leaves the round cell body and splits at a T junction into a muscle branch and a spinal branch, with no dendrites at all.

At the muscle it coils in a spiral around thin fibres inside a capsule called a muscle spindle, or braids among collagen in a Golgi tendon organ.

SIZE:

Cell bodies have a median diameter near 90 to 100 micrometres in the 2025 human DRG reference atlas built from 126 donors, among the largest neurons in the body.

The fibre is 12 to 20 micrometres thick and can run a full metre, serving a spindle only 4 to 10 millimetres long.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Group Ia, the spiral spindle primary, reporting stretch velocity, matching the atlas cluster A-Propr.HAPLN4.

• Group II, the spindle secondary, reporting static length, matching cluster A-Propr.EPHA3, unusually small at about 67 micrometres.

• Group Ib, the Golgi tendon organ ending, reporting force, matching cluster A-Propr.PCDH8.

• Joint capsule and ligament afferents, reporting extreme angles rather than everyday posture.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies fill all 31 pairs of dorsal root ganglia, wedged in the intervertebral foramen, the bony gap between two vertebrae, with cervical ganglia most enriched.

Endings sit in muscle spindles and at the musculotendinous junction, the interface where muscle becomes tendon, and in joint capsules.

Central branches reach lamina IX, laminae VI and VII, Clarke's column from about T1 to L2, and the cuneate and external cuneate nuclei.

The defining interface is the dorsal root entry zone, where Schwann cell myelin hands the axon to oligodendrocyte myelin.

NUMBER:

The body holds roughly 50,000 muscle spindles, and spindles outnumber Golgi tendon organs by one to two to one.

Each spindle takes one Ia and several group II fibres, giving about 250,000 proprioceptors, plausible range 200,000 to 350,000.

That is 2 to 3.5 percent of the roughly 10 million DRG neurons and about 0.0003 percent of all neurons.

Confidence is moderate, because nobody has counted human proprioceptors, and the spindle census rests on Hermann Voss's tally of nearly 22,000 spindles in 139 muscles.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage is about minus 60 millivolts, with a narrow spike and no shoulder on its falling phase.

Group Ia endings hold 20 to 100 spikes per second during steady stretch and burst above 200 during a fast pull.

Conduction reaches 80 to 120 metres per second in cat, but human recordings give 50 to 70 metres per second in the arm.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The Ia fibre contacts motor neurons of its own muscle directly, the only one step reflex in the body, which is the tendon jerk.

It also drives inhibitory interneurons that relax the opposing muscle, and Clarke's column cells feeding the cerebellum.

Uniquely, its own terminals are synapsed upon by GABA releasing spinal cells, so the cord turns its input down before arrival.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

These are the thickest and fastest sensory wires in the body.

The stem axon before the T junction is long, around 230 micrometres against about 60 in small pain cells, and the junction drops spikes at high rates.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, the main excitatory messenger, loaded into vesicles by VGLUT1, the vesicular glutamate transporter type 1.

They carry parvalbumin, and about 90 percent of parvalbumin positive DRG neurons are proprioceptors.

Identity is set by RUNX3 and ETV1, and survival needs neurotrophin 3 acting through the receptor TrkC, also written NTRK3.

MYELINATION:

Roughly 100 wrapped layers of Schwann cell membrane, with a g ratio, the ratio of axon width to fibre width, near 0.6 to 0.7.

Internodes, the insulated stretches between bare gaps called nodes of Ranvier, reach 1 to 1.5 millimetres, which buys the speed.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

The most expensive sensory neuron in the body, firing all day and feeding a metre of thick axon.

Its mitochondria need frataxin to build iron sulphur clusters, which is why Friedreich ataxia, a frataxin shortage, kills these cells first.

PLASTICITY:

The flexibility is mechanical, because gamma motor neurons contract the fibres inside the spindle and reset its sensitivity, a setting called the fusimotor set.

The Ia to motor neuron synapse depresses within a few impulses, which is why a rapidly tapped reflex fades.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Satellite glial cells wrap each cell body about 20 nanometres away and soak up potassium through Kir4.1 channels, and a giant soma needs several.

At the spindle, terminal Schwann cells stop short of the sensory tip, leaving bare membrane where the stretch sensor PIEZO2 sits.

Ganglion capillaries are leaky, with no blood nerve barrier, and in Friedreich ataxia the satellite cells swell into residual nodules before the neuron dies.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this cell and the cord loses its only direct sensory line into motor neurons, and the limb becomes an open loop machine.

Tendon reflexes vanish and muscle tone collapses.

People who lose these fibres still feel touch and pain, yet cannot stand with their eyes shut, and must move by watching their limbs like a puppeteer.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

PIEZO2 opens under membrane tension, but mechanics shape the signal first, because sticky cross bridges inside the spindle create the initial burst and a history dependence called thixotropy.

The brain writes back down the same wire twice, through gamma motor neurons that reset the encoder and GABAergic presynaptic inhibition that gates the terminal.

The afferent keeps its own sense organ alive, since a denervated spindle degenerates, and mouse work shows proprioceptor PIEZO2 is required for normal skeletal shape.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: fascicle length as a fraction of rest length, lengthening velocity in rest lengths per second, tendon force in newtons for Ib units, gamma drive as a gain from 0.5 to 3.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate onto lamina IX motor neurons, Ia inhibitory interneurons, Clarke's column and the cuneate nucleus, at 0 to 320 spikes per second.

• STATE: previous length, adaptation level, thixotropy state, refractory timer, fusimotor gain.

• PARAMETERS: position gain about 60 spikes per second per unit fractional stretch, velocity gain about 12 per square root unit, resting rate about 10, conduction 50 to 120 metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS: transduction under 1 millisecond, initial burst decay 20 to 50 milliseconds, thixotropic recovery seconds to minutes, fusimotor change 50 to 200 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: in Friedreich ataxia and in large fibre sensory neuronopathy from cisplatin or vitamin B6 overdose these cells die first, giving sensory ataxia and absent tendon reflexes, while PIEZO2 loss of function causes distal arthrogryposis with impaired proprioception and touch.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn muscle length and the speed of length change into the firing rate of a group Ia spindle afferent.
Create a number variable called previous_length with initial value 1.0.
Create a number variable called fusimotor_gain with initial value 1.0.
For each sample in the length recording, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the difference of sample and 1.0.
    Set stretch to the result.
    Calculate the difference of sample and previous_length.
    Calculate the quotient of the result divided by 0.001.
    Set velocity to the result.
    Check if velocity is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set velocity to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if stretch is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set stretch to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of stretch and 60.
    Calculate the sum of the result and 10.
    Set firing_rate to the result.
    Calculate the product of the square root of velocity and 12.
    Calculate the sum of firing_rate and the result.
    Calculate the product of the result and fusimotor_gain.
    Set firing_rate to the result.
    Set previous_length to sample.
    Display firing_rate to the console.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: plain arithmetic in a loop, no libraries, and the language most
# simulation agents can already run unchanged.
import math

DT = 0.001        # time step in seconds
K_POS = 60.0      # spikes/s per unit of fractional stretch
K_VEL = 12.0      # spikes/s per square root unit of velocity
BIAS = 10.0       # background discharge at resting length
GAMMA = 1.0       # fusimotor gain set by gamma motor neurons
MAX_RATE = 320.0  # ceiling imposed by the refractory period

def ia_rate(length, prev_length, rest=1.0):
    """Group Ia rate for one time step, Houk and Prochazka form."""
    stretch = max(0.0, length - rest)                  # a spindle cannot be pushed
    velocity = max(0.0, (length - prev_length) / DT)   # unloading silences it
    dynamic = K_VEL * math.pow(velocity, 0.5)          # compressive velocity term
    return min(GAMMA * (BIAS + K_POS * stretch + dynamic), MAX_RATE)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    length, prev = 1.0, 1.0            # ramp and hold, 5 percent over 100 ms
    for step in range(300):
        t = step * DT
        length = 1.0 + 0.05 * min(t / 0.1, 1.0)
        r = ia_rate(length, prev)
        prev = length
        if step % 100 == 0:
            print("t=%.3f s  length=%.4f  Ia rate=%6.1f spikes/s" % (t, length, r))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the biggest cell body, wearing the thickest myelin, on the fastest wire, and the only sensory neuron that speaks to a motor neuron with no relay in between.

It is silent to touch, heat and pain, and busy while you stand perfectly still.
